# Decision Log

Rule 1 requires one place for every non-trivial design choice. A choice belongs here when a competent engineer could reasonably choose another approach. Source-to-target mapping lives in the [traceability matrix](traceability-matrix.md), while open source ambiguities live in [business-rule flags](business-rule-flags.md). Code comments describe behavior and point here for rationale.

**How to read a superseded row.** This log grew as the platform did, and a later decision sometimes replaced an earlier one. A replaced row keeps its evidence and opens with **SUPERSEDED**, which names the row now in force. Only that named row describes the delivered behaviour. Nothing else in this log is superseded: every other row states what the code does today.

## Platform and technology selections

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **C1 — Java 25 with Spring Boot 4.1.0 for all six services** | Node.js with TypeScript; a mixed-language platform | `BigDecimal` preserves fixed-point money and permits explicit truncation. One language also keeps build and deployment behavior uniform. | Java 25 and Spring Boot 4.1 are newer than long-settled enterprise baselines. |
| **C2 — Apache Kafka in the demo, behind publishing ports** | Amazon EventBridge with SQS; direct service calls | Kafka was the requested default and preserves account-key ordering within a partition. Publishing ports keep a managed replacement possible. | The demo operates a broker container and its access-control rules. |
| **C3 — Maven mono-repo under `card-platform/`** | One repository per service | One repository makes the untouched legacy boundary auditable and keeps contracts, fixtures, services, and parity tests versioned together. | Services share a repository release cadence even though their Maven dependencies remain separate. |
| **C4 — JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) Schema Draft 2020-12 with build-enforced compatibility** | Avro with a schema registry; unchecked JSON | JSON remains readable in a console demonstration. Publish and consume validation plus compatibility tests enforce the contract without another runtime container. | A producer outside this build could ignore the compatibility gate. |
| **C5 — Representational State Transfer with JSON** | gRPC | A caller can demonstrate each synchronous route with `curl` and no generated client. | Client types are not compiled from a remote procedure contract. |
| **C6 — Exhaustive tables with short prose** | Exhaustive narrative prose | Rule 1 requires complete coverage, while Rule 5 rejects repetition. Tables carry enumeration without hiding gaps in paragraphs. | Readers who skim tables can miss a narrow exception. |
| **C7 — Simplicity governs presentation, not correctness mechanisms** | Remove the outbox, processed-event markers, or explicit mappers | The demo must remain understandable, but atomic publication and duplicate safety are required behavior. Explicit code is easier to inspect than generated code. | Each service has infrastructure tables and explicit mapping code to explain. |
| **C8 — Interest is verified but not migrated** | Build an interest service; omit interest parity | The user excluded interest migration but required interest parity. Tests verify rate lookup and arithmetic, while account cycle close reproduces only the required counter reset. | Batch interest and the event platform can evolve separately. |

## Migration strategy

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Parallel reimplementation with equivalence verification** | A strangler-fig facade routing traffic to the mainframe | The user required the services to “not call into the mainframe at runtime.” Fixtures and documented rules therefore form the executable comparison baseline. | Behavior absent from both fixtures and documented rules needs a synthetic test or remains unverified. The precision boundary is the concrete instance: no cycle balance in the 300-record daily fixture reaches one billion, so a constructed case carries that proof. |
| **Authorization logic is a declared synthesis** | Invent behavior for `COPAUA0C`; treat the brief’s name as an existing program | `COPAUA0C` and transaction `CP00` are absent. Rules come from `CBTRN02C`, request fields from `COTRN02C`, and the signon fork from `COSGN00C`. | There is no synchronous source program against which to compare the whole request path. |
| **Authorization reads local projections and calls no supporting service** | Synchronous account-service and card-service clients | Existing repositories, freshness fields, and tests already defined event-maintained copies. Outbound clients would add credentials and runtime coupling. | Authorization decisions can reflect projection lag. The account projection records event provenance and observation time. |
| **Two events refresh authorization state, and `CardUpdated` refreshes an observation rather than a mapping** | Consume only `AccountStateChanged`; upsert `card_xref` from `CardUpdated`; call the card service synchronously | Authorization consumes both streams. `AccountStateChanged` replaces every value column of `account_credit_snapshot`, because a decision reads all of them. `CardUpdated` cannot do the same for `card_xref`: it carries a masked card number, while that table is keyed by the full sixteen characters. The message can therefore neither create a row nor move a mapping. It does name the account whose card data was just written, so it refreshes the observation column and nothing else. The per-consumer detail is in [Authorization service](#authorization-service). | Where two cards of one account share their last four digits, both rows are refreshed and one of them was not the card the event named. Bounded and safe: both already name that account and no mapping value changes. |
| **Fraud detection is marked net new** | Manufacture lineage from a nearby transaction program | No source program scores risk, checks velocity, or evaluates fraud rules. Honest traceability distinguishes design intent from migrated behavior. | Fraud tests verify the new contract, not Common Business Oriented Language (COBOL) parity. |

## Arithmetic and numeric fidelity

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Reproduce the tolerant date policy, including its undocumented `2513` acceptance** | Accept a date on severity `'0000'` alone and treat the second branch as a defect; reject the branch and flag it | Four call sites read the tolerant policy: `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L397-L400` and `:L417-L420`, and `app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L396-L399` and `:L416-L419`. Each accepts a date when the severity text is `'0000'` **or** when the message-number text is `'2513'`. No comment in the source explains the second condition. Dropping it would refuse dates the source accepts, which is an equivalence break, and transformation rule T7 requires reproducing a defect and flagging it rather than correcting it quietly. The mask the tolerant policy passes is `WS-DATE-FORMAT PIC X(10) VALUE 'YYYY-MM-DD'` | The platform accepts a date on a condition nobody can explain. `CobolDateValidatorTest` includes a case that passes only because of the `2513` branch, so the tolerance cannot be removed unnoticed. The register carries it as an item a human owner must decide |
| **Carry the strict date policy separately, on its own mask** | Collapse both policies into one method with a flag; apply the tolerant policy everywhere | The source holds two acceptance policies over the same called program and they disagree. The strict one accepts a date only on a numeric severity of zero, and reads its mask as `WS-DATE-FORMAT PIC X(08) VALUE 'YYYYMMDD'` from `app/cpy/CSUTLDWY.cpy:L58-L59`. It has exactly one call site, `app/cpy/CSUTLDPY.cpy:L298`, which holds no `'2513'` branch. Two different masks and two different acceptance conditions are two contracts, so `CobolDateValidator` exposes `isAcceptedByTolerantPolicy` and `isAcceptedByStrictPolicy` and no method takes a policy argument | A caller has to choose a policy, and choosing the wrong one changes which dates are accepted. The two mask constants are public, so a call site names the mask it reproduces |
| **Timestamp shapes come from `CSDAT01Y`, which is a live authority rather than a presentation helper** | Derive the shapes from `CBTRN02C` alone; treat the copybook as presentation-only and excluded | Two 26-character timestamp shapes coexist and they are not interchangeable. `WS-TIMESTAMP` at `app/cpy/CSDAT01Y.cpy:L42-L55` fixes the inbound origin shape, separator by separator, including the space its `FILLER` at `:L48` puts between the date and the time. `DB2-FORMAT-TS` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L159-L174` fixes the processing shape, with hyphens and dots and two significant fractional digits. `BillPaymentEquivalenceTest` reads the copybook as the oracle for the first shape, so classifying it as presentation-only was wrong about a document the suite depends on | A copybook the presentation layer also uses is in scope for one section of its content. The traceability matrix lists its live consumers so the reason is discoverable |
| **Truncate every monetary result toward zero** | `RoundingMode.HALF_UP`; `RoundingMode.HALF_EVEN`; floor | `ROUNDED` appears zero times across all 28 programs in `app/cbl/`. COBOL therefore truncates stored arithmetic results. | A future “cleanup” of the rounding mode can change cents without a type error. Equivalence tests detect that change. |
| **Serialize money as a two-place decimal string** | JSON number | Common JSON clients decode numbers through binary floating point. Decimal strings preserve the fixed-point contract across languages. | Consumers must parse the value as a decimal rather than as a native floating-point number. |
| **Derive precision and scale from each Picture clause** | Store cents in integers; use one wide decimal type everywhere | `PIC S9(10)V99`, `PIC S9(09)V99`, and `PIC S9(04)V99` map to `NUMERIC(12,2)`, `NUMERIC(11,2)`, and `NUMERIC(6,2)`. | A source clause read incorrectly can propagate into entities, schemas, migrations, and tests. |
| **Keep display identifiers as fixed-width text** | Numeric identifier columns | Account, customer, merchant, and category identifiers retain leading zeros and are compared as display fields. | Text storage does not provide numeric ordering, which these identifiers do not need. |
| **Reproduce tolerant numeric input parsing** | `new BigDecimal(String)` | `FUNCTION NUMVAL-C` accepts separators and currency notation at `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L383`; `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L2201` gates the same conversion. | Inputs rejected by stricter systems remain acceptable for parity. |

## Data model and persistence

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line)**. A full card number travelled only in an authenticated request body, never in a path or a query string | Keep `GET /cards/{cardNumber}` and `PUT /cards/{cardNumber}` as the plan sketched them | Every access log, reverse proxy, trace and browser history on the way records a path and a query string, and so does the `instance` member of a default error document. A Primary Account Number placed there is therefore copied into stores nobody chose. The substitute routes were `POST /cards/detail` and `PUT /cards`. They are withdrawn. The plan names one transaction per route and each addresses exactly one card, and publishing two shapes for one row left the two disagreeing about which member names the card. `POST /authorizations` still takes its card number in the body, because that route names no card in its path at all | Held while a number stood in those paths, and no longer: the delivered paths carry a card token, so no card number reaches a request line |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line)**. Neither the masked number nor the card token can key a route that names one card | Key them on the masked number; key them on the card token | Twelve of a masked number's sixteen characters are mask characters, so two cards sharing their last four digits collapse to one value. A route keyed on it would merge two histories and admit a caller to a card it does not hold. The reasoning recorded against the token was that the cross-reference and the card table are keyed by the full number and resolving a token back would need a stored mapping. That was wrong on its own evidence: `card.card_token` is `CHAR(64) NOT NULL UNIQUE` and `statement_transaction` is keyed on the token already, so the mapping was in the schema the whole time | The masked-number half of this row still holds and no route is keyed on a masked value. The token half is withdrawn |
| **Use `DATE` for card expiry and `VARCHAR(10)` for account expiry** | Use one date type for both fields | Card expiry is decomposed into calendar parts at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L115-L123`. Account expiry is compared as text at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L414`. | Similar business terms have different database types, so a later unification could break parity. |
| **Drop trailing filler bytes** | Persist filler columns | Six record layouts end in filler that carries no business data. The widths are 178 bytes at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L17`, 168 at `app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy:L23`, 59 at `app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L11`, 22 at `app/cpy/CVTRA01Y.cpy:L10`, 20 at `app/cpy/CVTRA05Y.cpy:L18`, and 14 at `app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy:L8`. The [traceability matrix](traceability-matrix.md) records every omission. | Fixed-width loaders must still account for bytes that no database column stores. |
| **Record each area code once, with a band column naming the condition name that declares it** | Three tables, one per condition name; one table holding only the tested band; a boolean column per band | `app/cpy/CSLKPCDY.cpy` declares 490 area codes under `VALID-PHONE-AREA-CODE` at `:L30`, then re-lists the same codes split across `VALID-GENERAL-PURP-CODE` at `:L521` with 410 and `VALID-EASY-RECOG-AREA-CODE` at `:L931` with 80. The two subsets are disjoint and their union is the broad band exactly, so a code belongs to precisely one of them. One row per code with a band discriminator is the smallest shape that answers both which codes exist and which band declares each, and `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L2298` tests only the general-purpose band. | A validator that tested the broad band would have to accept both band values, so the discriminator has to be read rather than assumed. Adding a third band to the source would need a new value, not a new table. |
| **Store 786 reference rows against 1,276 source literal occurrences** | Store all 1,276 occurrences; store only the 490 codes the tested band admits | The five condition names of `app/cpy/CSLKPCDY.cpy` carry 1,276 literals in total: 490, 410, 80, 56 and 240. The 410 and the 80 re-list codes the 490 already holds. The distinct set is 490 area codes, 56 state codes at `:L1013` and 240 state-and-zip-prefix combinations at `:L1073`, which is 786. `V3__reference_data.sql` seeds exactly that. | The two counts differ by 490, and a reader comparing a row count against a literal count will see a gap unless the reconciliation is stated. [Data model](data-model.md) and the [traceability matrix](traceability-matrix.md) both carry it. |
| **Omit `LAST-3-OF-ZIP` from the reference data** | Persist it as a fourth reference table; model it as a column of the state-and-zip table | `app/cpy/CSLKPCDY.cpy:L1314` declares `LAST-3-OF-ZIP PIC X(3)` as the second part of the group whose first part carries the validated combinations. It declares no condition name of its own, and nothing under `app/` references it, so there is no list of permitted values and nothing to validate against. | A reader expecting every field of the copybook to reach a table finds this one absent. The [traceability matrix](traceability-matrix.md) records it as a sanctioned omission. |
| **Adopt `CVCUS01Y.cpy` as the canonical customer layout** | Adopt `CUSTREC.cpy`; merge the two copies | Six programs bind to `CVCUS01Y`; `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L55` binds to the fork. The copies differ in one field name. | Code that depended on the fork’s identifier spelling now follows the canonical name. |
| **Correct `EXPIRAION` in target field and column names** | Preserve the source misspelling | Identifier text does not affect behavior. The mapping records both renames for source searches. | A direct search for the corrected target name finds no source identifier. |
| **Name the date-validation condition for what it tests** | Carry the source name `FC-INVALID-DATE` forward | `app/cbl/CSUTLDTC.cbl:L62` names a condition for an invalid date and tests the all-zeros feedback token, which the date service returns on success. Identifier text does not affect behavior. | A reader searching the target for the source condition name finds nothing. The [traceability matrix](traceability-matrix.md) records the rename. |
| **One private database and schema per service** | A shared schema; cross-schema reads | Private ownership enforces service boundaries. A service needing foreign state holds a projection or seeded reference copy. | Replicas and repeated reference rows can lag or drift. |
| **Store the fraud velocity window in PostgreSQL** | Redis; in-memory state | PostgreSQL avoids another demo container and preserves state across restarts. The repository boundary permits a later replacement. | Database reads and writes cost more than an in-memory cache at high volume. |
| **Seed small reference lookups into each owning schema** | Shared lookup tables; a seventh service | Seven transaction types and 18 categories are cheaper to duplicate than to fetch over the network. | Repeated seed data must change consistently. |
| **Persist the card verification value but never emit it** | Drop the source field; expose it with the card record; field-encrypt it under a managed key | `app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L7` defines the field, so storage preserves the layout, and sections 0.4.1 and 0.6.4 of the plan require it to be persisted and never emitted. Section 0.2.2 places payment-card industry controls beyond the documented masking deviation outside the engagement, which rules out encryption as well. Entities, responses, events, and logs expose no accessor or value. | The sensitive value still exists in the card service database, in the clear, and a database, volume, snapshot or backup disclosure exposes it. Retaining it after authorization is prohibited by the payment-card industry data security standard, and no business path reads it, so the column is held by the record layout alone. A security review recorded this as a critical finding. It is answered by departure D4 in `business-rule-flags.md` and the removal task in `suggested-next-tasks.md` rather than by a change, because the plan mandates the column. |
| **Withhold the customer Social Security Number and government-issued identifier from every response** | Answer both as the source displays them; answer them masked | `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L496-L504` builds the number in hyphenated form and `:L519` moves the identifier, so the source discloses both to a terminal operator. `GET /customers/{customerId}` answers 16 of the 18 fields `app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy:L5-L22` declares, and `CustomerControllerTest` measures the wire form of a fully populated view. | A caller that needs either value has no route to it, and a later requirement reopens the question. |
| **`GET /customers/{customerId}` takes the customer identifier directly** | Nest the route under an account; resolve the customer from an account identifier | `ACCOUNT-RECORD` at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L4-L17` declares no customer identifier. The source reads one from the cross-reference record at `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L739` and then keys the customer file at `:L708`. `9400-GETCUSTDATA-BYCUST` at `:L825` reads by customer identifier, which is the shape this route reproduces. The account service does hold a private pairing table, `account_customer_link`, created by `V7__account_customer_link.sql` in place of an earlier `card_xref` replica. It is not a resolver: `AccountUpdateService` reads it to confirm a pair the caller has already named, and nothing on this platform refreshes it. | A caller holding only an account identifier makes two calls, the first against the service that owns the cross-reference record. |
| **Use encoded HTTP Basic passwords instead of the source plaintext comparison** | Reproduce the direct comparison at `app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L223` | Every service already applies the same default-deny security chain. Startup refuses a hash without its `{bcrypt}` prefix, and the decision path receives only authenticated identity. | Signon credential behavior is a deliberate security deviation rather than an equivalence claim. |
| **Notification owns an account-keyed cardholder projection, refreshed by a delivered `CustomerContextChanged` event** | Blank renderer context; synchronous account lookup; a shared customer table; leave the projection fixture-only | Notification's renderers need stable cardholder context and cannot call another consumer. The projection is seeded from sanctioned fixture sources and then kept current: the account service publishes `CustomerContextChanged` on every customer mutation, and `messaging/CustomerContextChangedConsumer` applies it under the group `notification-customer`. The seeded rows carry a Unix-epoch provenance stamp so the first real event supersedes them. | A cardholder field changed before the projection has received its first event is rendered from the fixture value. |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The subject of a decision comes from a stored row" in the security-review section below, which withdrew the table in `V20__unresolved_card_attempt_withdrawn.sql`. It read:** Authorization unresolved-card attempts have their own table | Discard the failed request; force a false account identifier; record the outcome only on the topic | The cross-reference read of `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L383` resolved no row, so the platform learned nothing about the card beyond the fact that it reached this service, and that fact is what this table records: the masked card number, the amount, the reject code and, since `V19__unresolved_decline_names_its_account.sql`, the account the caller declared as the subject. It is keyed on the transaction identifier because it records one attempt. No identifier is forced: the account is the validated eleven digits the request carried, and a call carrying none is refused before a decision. The `authorization_decision` row beside it names the event the same transaction published. Together they are the durable record `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L446-L465` writes on the batch path and `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L620-L636` writes nowhere on the synchronous one. | The table holds one row per refused attempt on a card the platform does not carry, so a caller repeating unknown card numbers grows it until the decision horizon expires those rows. The row itself is readable only from the database: a consumer learns of the decline from the event and reads the masked card number off it, so the amount-and-card history behind a velocity pattern is assembled from the event stream rather than from this table. `account_id` is nullable and its check is `NOT VALID`, because a row written before that migration recorded no subject and none can honestly be invented for it. |
| **Store the refused transaction as one 350-character column** | Decompose the block into thirteen columns; keep a chosen set of diagnostic fields | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L447` moves the whole arriving record into `REJECT-TRAN-DATA PIC X(350)` without reshaping a field, and `app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl:L36` allocates the 430-byte result. `TransactionEntity` already models that layout field by field for accepted transactions. The card number inside the block arrives masked, and `ck_rejected_transaction_masked_card_number` reads offsets 263 through 278 to hold that boundary at the column. | A reader slices a field out at its copybook offset, and no index covers a field inside the block. |
| **Give the reject row a surrogate `UUID` primary key** | Key the row on the transaction identifier; add a database-generated sequence | `app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl:L34-L38` allocates DALYREJS as a sequential generation data group with no `KEYS` parameter, so no source field carries a key. One transaction reaches this table more than once across redeliveries, which a unique key on the transaction identifier would turn into a crash. | The application assigns the identifier, so two rows recording one refusal cannot be told apart by key alone. |
| **Hold `reject_reason_code` as four-character text** | Store it as an integer; store it as an enumerated type | `WS-VALIDATION-FAIL-REASON PIC 9(04)` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L181` is a display numeric that keeps its leading zeros, and the declined event contract carries the same zero-padded string. Text keeps the row and the event identical. | An arithmetic comparison on the code needs a cast, and the column accepts four digits the platform defines no reason for. |

## Event contracts and messaging

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Use a transactional outbox in each producing service** | Publish inside request handling; distributed database-broker transactions | The source performs three writes without rollback at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L440-L442`. Domain state and the event row now commit together. | Publication occurs after the domain transaction and can be delayed until the relay succeeds. |
| **Every outbox writer declares `MANDATORY` transaction propagation** | Leave the default `REQUIRED`; document the expectation in a comment | `REQUIRED` starts a transaction when the writer is called without one, so an overlooked caller would commit an event row on its own and leave an event no aggregate write stands behind. `MANDATORY` refuses that call instead, which turns the atomicity claim into something the container enforces rather than something a comment asserts. | A caller that legitimately wants to write an event outside an aggregate transaction has no path to it, which is the intent. |
| **Use a processed-event marker inside the consumer transaction** | In-memory deduplication; rely on broker delivery alone | A replay reaches the source duplicate-key abend path at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L562-L579`. Durable markers survive restarts and guard side effects. | A marker has to outlast every window the record can return through, and no configured horizon bounds that window. The claim is therefore permanent: nothing deletes a `processed_event` row, and the table grows by one small row per consumed event. The security-review section below records why the horizon was withdrawn. |
| **In force. Restated by "One event for the outcome whose card resolves nothing" below.** Use the account identifier as the Kafka key on every event whose subject is an account, and the transaction identifier on the one event whose subject is not | Random key; transaction identifier throughout; omit the key; one key form for every event, which the previous revision held | The ledger must preserve per-account order, and Kafka orders records only within a partition, so every event about an account keys on that account and a dead-letter diagnostic names the account it concerns. Reject code 0100 has no such subject: it follows a cross-reference read that resolved nothing, and the only eleven digits within reach are the ones a caller declared, which no stored row corroborates. Its key is the sixteen-character identifier this platform minted for the decision, which is a value it issued rather than one it was given. Every key still comes from something the platform knows. | One unusually active account concentrates work on one partition. Two key widths reach `ck_outbox_event_aggregate_id`, which admits both, and `outbox/OutboxWriter` is what holds each contract to the one width its document declares. A decline that resolves no account is ordered against nothing, which costs nothing, because it changes no balance. |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The subject of a decision comes from a stored row" in the security-review section below, which withdrew it. It read:** Resolve the subject of every decided call, and publish reject code 0100 like the other three: `transaction-declined-v3`, keyed on the eleven-digit account | Publish nothing, which one earlier revision did; publish an account-less version keyed on the transaction identifier, which the next revision did; invent or derive an account identifier for the key; publish on a separate topic | The AAP names the account identifier in `TransactionDeclined` and makes it the key of every event, and the reject record of `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L446-L465` cannot be built from a payload carrying six properties. Both earlier positions read `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L385-L387` as meaning the decision has no account, when what that ordering settles is which read resolved an identifier. The subject is knowable: `VALIDATE-INPUT-KEY-FIELDS` at `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L196-L209` takes an account identifier or a card number from the caller, so where the card resolves no cross-reference row the declared account is the subject, validated at the eleven digits `XREF-ACCT-ID PIC 9(11)` holds. Nothing is invented, and the value is not a synthetic key. A call that establishes neither is refused before a decision with `Card Number NOT found...` at `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L625-L626`, which is what the source itself answers, so it allocates no identifier, records no decision and publishes nothing. | The refusal is a new outcome on this route: a caller that sends only an unknown card number now receives that message instead of reject code 0100 with a decline body. Entitlement is compared against the resolved account and never the declared one, so a caller owning an account cannot use it to probe card numbers; `domain/CallerEntitlement` and one named test hold that. `transaction-declined-v2` is retained rather than deleted, so a record already on the topic stays readable while no producer writes it, and `contracts/released-contracts.json` is where that posture is declared. |
| **Correct an applied migration with a later migration rather than by editing it** | Edit the earlier file in place; squash the history | Flyway compares a migration checksum on every start, so editing an applied file refuses to start against any database that already applied it, and the demo stack keeps its database on the `postgres-data` volume across `docker compose down`. `V10`, `V11`, `V14`, `V15`, `V19`, `V20` and now `V24` each restate catalogue comments an earlier file wrote, and ledger `V6` does the same for ledger `V4`. On the one question of the unresolved-card decline the folder therefore holds six files arguing successive positions, and `V24` is the current one: it restates the comments `V20` wrote, to the position where the outcome is decided and published against no account. | A reader following the migrations in order meets a correction some files later than the statement it corrects, and the header of a superseded file remains the historical record of a change that was made. Every such header names the decision log as the authority for why. |
| **In force. Scopes "Correct an applied migration with a later migration rather than by editing it".** Text a migration only carries in a `--` comment is corrected in that file; text a migration executes, a `COMMENT ON` that lands in the catalogue, is corrected by a later migration. Card `V5` cited the status test as lines 1861 to 1863 of `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl`, a file that ends at line 1560, so its header is corrected where it stands and card `V8` restates the constraint comment | Correct only the file and ship no migration; ship only the migration and leave the file naming a range past the end of the file it cites; leave both and keep the wrong locator as the historical record, as ledger `V4` keeps its own | A citation exists to be followed, so a shipped file may not name a range its target does not contain, and `DocumentationContractTest` now reads every `path:Lx-Ly` in the tree and fails on one that does not resolve. Editing the file cannot reach a database that already applied it: Flyway compares the checksum of every applied file at start-up, and the one remedy that keeps rows, `flyway repair`, rewrites checksums without re-running anything, so the catalogue keeps the earlier comment. A later migration is the only statement that corrects that database, which is what ledger `V6` did for the two column locators of ledger `V4`. Onboarding pitfall 13 carries the reset the checksum change asks of an older demo volume | Two files execute the same constraint comment, and a reader of card `V5` alone cannot tell that `V8` restates it. `V8` names `V5` in its header and the card README lists both. A fresh database applies the two in order and ends where one would, because a comment statement replaces whatever text the constraint carried |
| **In force. Supersedes "`CardUpdated` is governed at one version".** Both released `CardUpdated` documents stay governed and on the classpath, and a producer publishes version 2 | Keep only version 1 and delete version 2, as the previous revision did; restore `embossedName` to version 2 so the chain is additive; publish a third version identical to version 2 | Deleting a released document is the one change that cannot be undone by a consumer. A record already retained on `card.updated` under version 2, and an `outbox_event` row already written under it, become unreadable the moment the document goes, and no argument from "nothing was published yet" can be verified across independently deployable environments, which is exactly what AAP 0.8.6 requires of the contract. Restoring `embossedName` to version 2 would put the cardholder name back on the wire, which the data-minimisation decision removed on purpose. A third identical version adds a number that means nothing. So both documents stay exactly as they were released, version 2 remains the shape a producer writes, and the one thing that was really wrong — that nothing recorded the released set or measured its immutability — is fixed by the row below | The released chain carries one non-additive step: version 2 stopped requiring `embossedName`, so a consumer holding version 1 refuses a version-2 record. That is a released fact rather than a new one. It is recorded in `contracts/released-contracts.json` under `nonAdditiveOver`, `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the migration that closes it, and `SchemaBackwardCompatibilityTest` refuses any further undeclared step |
| **Pin the released contract set and the wire contract of each version in `contracts/released-contracts.json`** | Trust review to catch a deleted or rewritten document; compare only the documents currently on the classpath, as the previous revision did; hash the whole file byte for byte; run an external schema registry | A compatibility test that reads only what is on the classpath cannot see what left it: deleting a version and its registry entry lowered a hard-coded pair count and the build stayed green. The baseline states the set, so a deletion fails as a set difference and an addition fails until an owner records the new version with its digest. The digest is taken over the document with `description`, `$comment`, `examples` and `title` removed and the rest serialised with sorted keys, so a correction to prose moves nothing and a change to what the document asserts about an instance moves everything. A byte digest would have made every prose fix a false alarm, and a false alarm is how a control gets switched off. A registry service would add a container to a demo the user asked to keep to five minutes | The baseline is a second place to keep in step, and it is deliberately the only one: it is checked against the registry rather than trusted, on every build. A digest that ignores prose cannot catch a description that lies about the contract, which is why `KafkaDeliveryGuaranteeContractTest` reads the dead-letter descriptions separately |
| **State the compatibility direction the platform actually holds, rather than the one the schema text implied** | Claim that an older document accepts a newer record; drop `additionalProperties: false` so it could; dual-publish every version | Every document closes its property set and pins its own `schemaVersion` with `const`, both for a security reason: an undeclared property is where a Primary Account Number travels. Those two keywords also mean an older document refuses a newer record, so the honest guarantee is the one `contracts/released-contracts.json` now states — a released document is never deleted or rewritten, a new version only adds, and a consumer validates each record against the version that record declares. Rollout is consumer-first, which the mono-repo makes automatic because every service depends on one library version. Opening the property set to make the wider claim true would remove the control that keeps a card number off a topic; dual publication doubles every event for a demo that has no independently deployed consumer | A newer record reaching a consumer that predates its version is refused rather than read, and lands on the dead-letter topic. That is fail-closed and diagnosable, and it is a real operational cost: the record has to be replayed after the consumer is upgraded |
| **`schemas/dead-letter-v1.json` governs the shared fallback topic alone, and the fixed-width `<source>.DLT` contract is defined in `docs/event-flow.md`** | Standardise all six dead-letter topics on the governed envelope; standardise all of them on the fixed-width record; leave the document claiming it is the one shape every dead-letter topic carries | Two forms are already released and each has a reason. The fixed-width record is the four fields of `app/cpy/CSMSG02Y.cpy:L21-L29` at their declared widths, which is the closest thing this platform has to the source's own abend report, and the governed envelope carries broker coordinates a JSON reader can filter on. What was wrong was the claim, not the forms: tooling generated from the document would have failed on three topics. Narrowing the claim and defining the other form costs no behaviour change; standardising either way would rewrite a released wire form | A reader needs one parser per topic rather than one per platform. `KafkaDeliveryGuaranteeContractTest` fixes which module writes which form, so the two cannot drift into each other |
| **Refuse a sensitive property name by scheme alias, and read an extension member's own name as the label its value lacks** | Screen values only, as the previous revision did; allowlist every permitted extension key; forbid the `extensions` object entirely | A verification value is three or four digits, and three or four digits carry no shape a screen can recognise: a merchant category code holds four. The value screen therefore needed a label beside the digits, and `{"extensions":{"cvc":"123"}}` carried none — an unrecognised key and a value too short for the long-run screen, so it passed both ends. Each card scheme abbreviates that value differently, so the name screen now holds `cvv`, `cvv2`, `cvc`, `cvc2`, `cv2`, `cid`, `csc`, `cvn`, `cvd`, `cav2` and `cavv` as whole names, and inside the extensions object a member named for a code refuses a bare three- or four-digit value while a member named for a credential refuses any value. A full key allowlist would close the class completely and would also close the additive evolution the schemas promise; forbidding the object contradicts the documents | A name nothing anticipated can still carry three digits. That residual is recorded in `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` as the extension-key allowlist, and the two-letter and three-letter aliases are compared whole so `incidentId` and `cscReference` are not refused |
| **Keep the widened `outbox_event.aggregate_id` column and correct the catalogue instead** | Narrow it back to `CHAR(11)`; edit `V4__outbox_transaction_key.sql` in place | Flyway compares a migration checksum on every start, so editing `V4` refuses to start against any database that already applied it. The demo stack keeps its database on the `postgres-data` volume across `docker compose down`. `V14`, `V15`, `V19` and `V24` each restate the comments that describe which key form a live contract writes, which is the same forward-correction `V10` and `V11` used. Narrowing the check to the account form was tried and withdrawn: a PostgreSQL `CHECK` marked `NOT VALID` skips the rows present when it is added and is still enforced on every `UPDATE` of them, and the relay writes a column on every row it claims, retries or abandons, so the narrowed rule would have stranded every stored transaction-keyed row. | The header text of `V4` argued for publishing the decline under a transaction key, and `V24` records that one contract does exactly that again, so the column now admits two widths a producer writes rather than one. Which width belongs to which contract is an invariant of `outbox/OutboxWriter` rather than of the column, because one column cannot express a rule about the payload beside it. |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Both notification listeners account for a contract version they cannot act on and acknowledge it" under [What a consumer does with a version it cannot act on](#what-a-consumer-does-with-a-version-it-cannot-act-on), which replaced its last column. It read:** Publish `TransactionPosted` schema version 2 with statement detail | Pad nine columns in notification; break version 1 | Ledger already receives the authoritative fields on `TransactionAuthorized`. An additive version carries them forward while version 1 remains readable. | Consumers that require statement detail must reject version 1 rather than invent values. |
| **Share `fraud.assessed` between `FraudFlagged` and `FraudCleared`** | One topic per verdict | The envelope’s `eventType` discriminates the payload, and one topic keeps the demo routing surface smaller. | A listener interested in one verdict still reads both event types. |
| **Receive `fraud.assessed` as `Object` before type discrimination** | A `FraudFlagged`-typed listener | `FraudCleared` is structurally narrower. Typed conversion could fail before application code recognizes a valid cleared event. | The listener must reject every object outside the two governed record types. |
| **Mask the card number only at publication and response boundaries** | Mask before authorization; emit the full number | Authorization needs the full 16-character key. Payloads and logs need only the masked value. | The full card number remains in memory and in owning-service tables. |
| **Derive card identity as a keyed, versioned code rather than a plain digest** | An unkeyed SHA-256 digest of a labelled card number; a random surrogate issued by a vault | A card number is sixteen digits, so anyone holding a token can recompute an unkeyed digest for every candidate. This platform puts tokens in routes, cursors, responses and authorities. `HmacSHA256` under a deployment key removes that search, and folding the version into the covered message makes a rollover a configuration change rather than a code change. A vault surrogate needs a service the user excluded from a demo, and AAP section 0.2.2 excludes payment-card industry controls beyond the documented masking deviation. | The key is deployment state that has to reach every service, and turning it over re-derives every stored token and granted authority. `CardTokenKeyContractTest` holds the four artifacts that carry one to a single key and version, and `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the turnover procedure. |
| **Supply no default card-token key, and refuse to derive a token without one** | Compile a demo key into the helper; fall back to an unkeyed digest when no key is set | A key compiled into the repository is a key every reader holds, which is the property the key exists to remove. A silent fallback is worse, because the weaker derivation would be invisible. A refusal names the property that supplies the key. | Every runtime path and every test run has to be given the key. `pom.xml` supplies it to Surefire and Failsafe, and the Compose stack, the ConfigMap and the Secret template supply it at run time. |
| **Report a card token as present or withheld in a log rendering, never in full** | Log the token; log a prefix of it | The token discloses no card number, but it names one card for as long as its key stands. A log line carrying it lets any reader of that log follow the card across every request that touched it. A prefix of a keyed code is equally linkable. | An operator diagnosing a paging or read-model problem locates a row by transaction identifier rather than by card. |
| **Emit currency as constant `USD`** | Omit currency; infer it in every consumer | The required event field has no source equivalent, and the source system is United States only. | Supporting another currency requires a contract and domain change. |
| **Treat reason 109 as an observable infrastructure failure** | Reproduce the source’s unread reason code | The source sets reason 109 at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L556` after a write and never checks it. The target retries, then dead-letters. | A condition the source swallowed now becomes operationally visible. |
| **Replace the single abend path with typed outcomes** | One fatal error path | Expected declines return HTTP 422 with `approved=false` and publish an event. Infrastructure failures retry, and terminal failures produce governed dead-letter metadata. | More outcome paths require separate tests and monitoring. |
| **Publish supporting-service events only after state changes** | Publish read events; publish nothing | The user limited supporting services to mutation events. Account updates and cycle close publish `AccountStateChanged`; customer changes publish `CustomerContextChanged`; card updates publish `CardUpdated`. | Authorization and notification depend on their local projections staying within the configured freshness bounds. |
| **Decide each account-update event on its own record, not on the pair** | One event per applied update; both events on every applied update | The two rewrites at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4066` and `:L4086` run unconditionally. Each writes back the values the caller submitted. A record whose submitted values equal its fetched values is therefore rewritten with what it already held, and it has no state change to announce. Publishing regardless makes a reader of the topic read a change that never happened, and it contradicts the AAP's limit of supporting services to mutation events. The comparison is the one `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1684-L1768` already draws, applied per record. | A consumer cannot treat either topic as a heartbeat for every update. Reaching the rewrites requires the submitted pair to differ from the fetched pair, so at least one event is always published and no committed write is silent. |
| **Govern eight business event types and one dead-letter envelope** | Limit schemas to the five initial core events | The delivered platform also publishes account, customer-context, and card state changes, as required for supporting-service mutations and notification projection freshness. All runtime wire forms use the same validation gate. | The contract library governs service-owned records that it does not bind to Java classes itself. |

## Concurrency and identifiers

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Use a database sequence for transaction identifiers** | Browse backward from high values and add one | Both online source paths perform a read-modify-write race at `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L444-L449` and `app/cbl/COBIL00C.cbl:L212-L217`. | Generated values differ from source browse order, so parity tests do not assert their exact value. |
| **Use field-level compare-and-swap for account updates** | A version column; unconditional overwrite | `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4109-L4193` compares a specific field set with case folding and date decomposition. A version column detects a different conflict set. | The compared field list must stay synchronized with the entity and request mappings. |
| **Wrap the account and customer writes in one database transaction** | Preserve two independent file rewrites | The source rewrites two files at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4066` and `L4086` while recovery is disabled. One transaction removes the partial-write state. | No material behavior risk was accepted; atomicity is an additive correction. |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The card update path reads no cross-reference row, and the divergence series is withdrawn" under [Card service](#card-service). Surface card cross-reference divergence without repairing or rejecting** | Repair from the card row; reject the update; ignore divergence | A card row lacks customer identifier data, and the source update never checks the cross-reference. A meter and redacted log were to expose the anomaly without changing the outcome. The comparison was then measured against `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl`, which reads no cross-reference row at all, and both the comparison and its meter were withdrawn. | Superseded twice. The reading of the source still stands, and the reconciliation this service now runs on its own copy is recorded under [Card ownership, source-fidelity and status-domain remediation decisions](#card-ownership-source-fidelity-and-status-domain-remediation-decisions). |
| **Use a `CardOwnership` bean seam in route authorization** | Method-security annotations; route constants | The seam permits ownership checks while preserving literal route rules scanned by cross-module security tests. | Route security remains partly coupled to a source-text contract test. |

## Defects reproduced rather than fixed

Equivalence is the acceptance contract. The target reproduces the following outcomes and exposes each issue for human review rather than silently changing it.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Reproduce the credit-limit precision narrowing** | Widen the temporary value | `WS-TEMP-BAL` is `PIC S9(09)V99` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L187`, while the limit and accumulators use `S9(10)V99` at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L8` and `L13-L14`. | A cycle balance at or above one billion loses its high-order digit. The comparison approves on greater-or-equal, so that loss turns a decline into an approval. |
| **Reproduce the refund sign convention** | Treat a refund as reducing cycle debit | The source adds a negative amount to cycle debit at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L551`, then subtracts that accumulator at line 404. | A refund tightens the next authorization instead of restoring credit. |
| **Reproduce the overlimit formula without current balance** | Include `ACCT-CURR-BAL` | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L403-L407` uses only cycle credit, cycle debit, and the incoming amount. | Stated balance and tested available credit can disagree. |
| **Preserve the message-2513 date tolerance** | Accept only severity zero | `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L397-L400` accepts one undocumented validator message as well as ordinary success. | The tolerated condition may be a source defect rather than intended policy. |
| **Preserve processing timestamps at hundredths** | Emit full Java fractional precision | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L173-L174` stores two significant fractional digits and line 701 fills four zeros. | Raw byte comparison needs the documented normalization. |
| **Reject impossible calendar dates at the card database boundary** | Preserve every ten-character source date | PostgreSQL `DATE` cannot store a text such as `2028-02-31`. The API reports this additive validation explicitly. | One source-accepted text can no longer be persisted, a forced platform divergence. |

## Validations deliberately not added

Adding any rule below would change authorization results and break the required source parity.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **No Luhn or checksum validation** | Validate the card-number checksum | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L194` checks only 16 numeric digits. | A checksum-invalid card can pass the same structural rule as the source. |
| **No card-status authorization check** | Reject inactive cards | `CBTRN02C` never opens the card file, and `POSTTRAN.jcl` never allocates it. | An inactive card can authorize if every implemented source rule passes. |
| **No account-status authorization check** | Reject inactive accounts | `ACCT-ACTIVE-STATUS` exists at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L6` but is not tested before posting. | A closed account can authorize under source-equivalent rules. |

## Equivalence evidence decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Use stateless Model A for authorization parity and cumulative Model B for posting parity** | Force one model across both suites | Authorization evaluates each fixture against its loaded account snapshot. Posting must apply approved records cumulatively in source order. | Counts differ by model and must always be labelled. |
| **Treat the first daily transaction as `504.77`** | Use the inherited `50.47` figure | The 11-byte `S9(09)V99` overpunch decodes only to `504.77`. | Existing prose that repeats `50.47` is stale. |
| **Treat the posting result as 100 category rows** | Use the inherited 99-row total | Fifty seeded type-01 rows remain, and 50 approved type-03 refunds create 50 more rows. | Existing prose that repeats 99 rows is stale. |
| **Report both the 99 keys posted to and the 100 final table rows** | Publish one figure and leave the other implicit | The two count different things. Forty-nine type-01 keys and 50 type-03 keys receive a posting, and the seeded key for account `00000000037` receives none because all five of its type-01 records reject. | A reader who compares only one figure across documents can read a true difference as an error. |
| **Omit the seeded balance row for a category key absent from the fixture** | Write a synthetic `0.00` seeded value for every posted key | Only 49 of the 99 posted keys exist in `app/data/ASCII/tcatbal.txt`. The other 50 take the create branch at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L503-L510`, which initializes the record before the add. | A reader expecting one uniform row set per key must branch on the recorded upsert branch. |
| **Assert the type-01-to-cycle-credit and type-03-to-cycle-debit relation beside the category balances** | State the relation only with the final account state | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L549` and `:L551` route one amount to one accumulator, and every fixture record carries category `0001`, so each accumulator equals its category balance. | Two expected files must change together whenever either side changes. |
| **Record 28 DOWN-versus-HALF_UP differences and zero DOWN-versus-FLOOR fixture differences** | Use inherited 56 and 49 counts | Actual DEFAULT rates include zero for every negative type-03/category-0001 row. Measured chained rows produce the stated counts. | Fixture data alone cannot distinguish DOWN from FLOOR on a negative non-zero rate. |
| **Add a synthetic negative FLOOR discriminator** | Claim the fixture feed covers every rounding direction | `-763.00 × 25.00 ÷ 1200` uses a real fixture rate and separates DOWN from FLOOR. | The input combination is additive rather than a delivered transaction. |
| **Add synthetic cases for reasons 100, 101, 103, equality, overwrite order, and precision narrowing** | Report only fixture-reachable reason 102 | The 300-record feed reaches only reason 102 under Model A. Synthetic records close specified rule boundaries. | Synthetic coverage proves documented rules, not their prevalence in production data. |
| **Use an in-test COBIL00C reference for bill payment** | Create a bill-payment service; omit the comparison | Bill payment is not a target service, but its separate balance rule is part of the parity claim. | The reference is executable test logic rather than a migrated runtime component. |
| **Use a synthetic matched disclosure group beside fixture fallback cases** | Test only the fixture’s blank groups | All account fixtures trigger `DEFAULT`. A matched group is required to prove the primary lookup branch. | The matched account-group assignment is additive test data. |
| **Chain category balances before interest comparison** | Compute each transaction in isolation | The source interest program reads accumulated category balances, not individual transactions. | A wrong posting order can contaminate the interest comparison. |

## Documentation, configuration, and presentation

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Write OpenAPI documents by hand** | Add a documentation generator | Hand-written contracts add no unmanaged dependency and remain reviewable beside each controller. | A controller and its description can drift, so contract tests scan both. |
| **Take the JSON library at Jackson 3 under `tools.jackson`, and keep annotations under `com.fasterxml.jackson.annotation`** | Jackson 2 coordinates under `com.fasterxml.jackson.core`; a different JSON library | Spring Boot 4.1 resolves Jackson 3, which moved databind and core to `tools.jackson` but left the annotation package under its original name. Following that default keeps one Jackson generation in every module's main code. Every event serializes through `libs/event-contracts`, so a single mapper family matters more here than elsewhere. It does not keep one generation on the classpath, and an earlier version of this row said it did. `spring-kafka-test` resolves `kafka-server`, which brings `com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-databind` onto the test classpath of the six services and of `equivalence-tests`. That path is floored at 2.21.5 in `pom.xml` and checked by `enforce-transitive-security-floors`. | The two package roots read like a mixed-version mistake, so a well-meaning import rewrite can break the build. Two generations really are present, so a reader who assumes main code and test code share one mapper family is wrong about the test classpath. |
| **Use explicit records and mappers, without Lombok or MapStruct** | Generate boilerplate and mappings | Explicit code keeps copybook-to-field transformations visible during review. | More mapping code is maintained by hand. |
| **Author legacy and target diagrams fresh in Mermaid** | Convert or edit files under `diagrams/` | Rule 2 governs new documentation, while the legacy tree remains read-only. | Two representations of the legacy architecture remain in the repository. |
| **Build presentation styles from Rule 4’s enumerated values** | Read the missing `blitzy-reveal-theme.css`; omit required classes | The repository contains no `blitzy-deck` directory or theme file. Rule 4 supplies the required palette, fonts, gradients, properties, and pinned libraries. | The inline result cannot be compared with the absent canonical file. |
| **Show delivery as dependency order, not a calendar schedule** | Dates, durations, or a Gantt chart | The implementation priorities describe code dependencies, not calendar commitments. | Readers seeking delivery dates will not find them. |
| **Use measured repository counts when the plan prose differs** | Repeat inherited counts | Direct inspection finds 8 Customer Information Control System (CICS) files, 17 mapsets, 18 programs, 18 transactions, 1,318 `CSLKPCDY` lines, 18 customer fields, 324 README lines, and 59 contribution-guide lines. Two locators also move: the cycle accumulators sit at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L13-L14` rather than lines 12 and 13, and the six card-validation messages span `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L188-L200` rather than lines 190 to 202. | Readers comparing this tree with older specification prose will see documented discrepancies. |
| **Target configuration overrides only at services that read them** | Inject every key into every container | `.env.example` and Kubernetes document shared defaults. Compose passes retention and paging keys only to actual consumers, while health probes reach all services. | Per-service environment blocks are longer than one global block. |
| **Keep existing security route literals in `SecurityConfig`** | Replace paths with constants | `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest` verifies shipped route text and required authorities. Literal paths also make the policy visible in one file. | Source-text tests constrain harmless refactoring. |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The whitespace policy is carried by the check invocation, not by a file at the repository root" under [Completeness review](#completeness-review)**. It read: declare the root guide's carriage-return line endings in a root `.gitattributes`, rather than converting the file to line feeds | Convert `README.md` to line-feed endings; add `text eol=lf` and let git rewrite it on checkout; leave the policy undeclared and accept the reported defect | Every one of the guide's 440 lines ends with a carriage return and a line feed, and it did so before this engagement. `git diff --check` reads the whitespace attribute of the file it reports on, and with none declared it called each of the added lines trailing whitespace and exited 2. Converting the file is not available: the legacy content has to stay byte for byte, which `RuleThreeDocumentationContractTest` asserts against a hash of the 324-line original, so a conversion would fail the build it was meant to clean. The one entry declares the ending and changes no byte: no `text`, `eol` or `working-tree-encoding` attribute is set anywhere, so every file is stored and checked out exactly as committed | The two line-ending conventions are still here and still correct, and the reading of `git diff --check` above still holds. What changed is where the policy lives: the file it named is withdrawn, and `everyCarriageReturnTerminatedFileIsCheckedUnderADocumentedInvocation` now reads the same bytes and holds the documented command instead |

## Runtime, configuration and infrastructure decisions

### Platform and runtime wiring

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Publish the event through a transactional outbox rather than from the request handler | Publish directly after the write; publish inside a distributed transaction across the database and the broker | The decision row and the event row commit in one local transaction, so a decision can never exist without its event or an event without its decision. The source has an ancestor for the shape: `app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L517-L518` writes to a queue and lets a separate reader pick the work up. It also has the gap: `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L440-L442` performs three writes with no rollback, and every file in `app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD:L3-L9` declares `RECOVERY(NONE) JOURNAL(NO)` | An event is published after the commit rather than with it, so a consumer sees it late rather than never. Two writes per call instead of one |
| All five producer services use one claimed-row relay state machine | Let each relay poll unlocked rows; rely on one replica; keep the older pessimistic-lock query without durable claim state | A rolling deployment or manual scale-out can run two relay instances over one table. `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` gives each transaction a disjoint due batch, `claimed_by` and `claimed_at` make the winner durable, and the claim timeout recovers work from an instance that stopped. The attempt count, due time and bounded backoff prevent one refused row from being retried on every sweep. Explicit `TransactionTemplate` boundaries also keep counters and timers outside the database transaction, so a rollback cannot report success | A broker round trip holds a database transaction and row locks. A send that succeeds before the database commit fails can be repeated, so consumers still need processed-event idempotency |
| Every service applies one bounded `service` common tag to application and framework meters | Tag only hand-written meters; rely on scrape-target labels; leave services inconsistent | One collector receives application, Java Virtual Machine, HTTP, and database meters from all six services. A registry customizer applies the same dimension before any meter is registered, so every series can be grouped with one query and no service-specific exception | The service name is present both in the metric prefix and as a tag on hand-written series. The duplication is bounded to six values |
| Readiness checks the database, Kafka, expected listener containers and terminal outbox state, while liveness checks process state alone | Keep Boot's generated `readinessState` group; put external dependencies in liveness; probe the aggregate health endpoint | A process can stay alive after its database or broker is lost, a listener is stopped, or a relay abandons an event. Those states cannot serve the platform contract and must withdraw the instance without restarting a healthy Java virtual machine. Database acquisition and Kafka discovery have explicit short deadlines, and Compose and Kubernetes probe the same readiness group | A transient shared dependency outage can withdraw every replica at once. An abandoned outbox row keeps a producer unready until an operator resolves it; that is deliberate because later events must not overtake the failed row |
| Compose reads `.env` for interpolation but every application container receives an explicit environment map | Attach `.env` to every service with `env_file`; split one dotenv file per service; put all credentials in the shared environment anchor | Compose interpolation can select one service's database and broker credentials without injecting the source variables themselves. Removing `env_file` prevents the PostgreSQL superuser, Kafka administrator and five sibling service credentials from entering each application container, while one checked-in map keeps the demo runnable from a single operator file | The six service blocks repeat their own settings, and adding a new application property requires adding it to the owning block. A build contract checks the active relay, consumer and retention keys so an omission fails before deployment |
| Dotenv bcrypt values are single quoted | Leave generated hashes unquoted; escape every dollar sign as `$$` | A generated bcrypt value contains several dollar signs. Compose expands dollar-prefixed text in unquoted and double-quoted dotenv values, corrupting a valid hash before Spring Security reads it. Single quotes preserve the generated value character for character and are easier to paste correctly than escaping each dollar sign | The quotes must remain around the replacement value. The example and a Compose runtime check demonstrate that requirement |
| Kubernetes stores the single demo broker's KRaft log, topic records and committed offsets on a `ReadWriteOnce` persistent claim | Keep `emptyDir`; rebuild broker state on every Pod replacement; require an external managed broker | A Deployment replacement is routine maintenance, not an instruction to erase every topic and every consumer position. A claim also keeps the configured KRaft cluster identifier paired with the metadata it names. The standard-cluster manifest remains self-contained and uses the cluster's default storage class | The 2 GiB claim is single-node demo storage, not a highly available broker design. A cluster without a default storage class leaves the claim pending, and deleting the claim remains the explicit destructive reset |
| Each service mounts its own server keystore while all services share one trust-only Secret | Mount one shared keystore in all six Pods; duplicate the same truststore into six Secrets; terminate Transport Layer Security outside the services | The six Services have different Domain Name System names, so each certificate must identify its own endpoint and each private key must stay within that workload's boundary. The certificate authority and truststore are public trust material rather than service identity, so one trust-only Secret avoids six copies without sharing a private key | Operators must issue and rotate six certificates and six keystore passwords. The checked-in Secret file contains placeholders only and must be replaced before deployment |
| Kubernetes uses `imagePullPolicy: Never` for the six locally built demo images | `IfNotPresent`; `Always`; require a registry before the demo can run | The repository defines local Docker builds and no image registry. `Never` makes that contract explicit. A node either has the exact image the operator built, or the Pod fails. The alternative is silently pulling a same-named image from a default registry, or running a stale cached tag | Every node that can schedule a service must have the image preloaded. A multi-node or remote cluster needs a registry and a deliberate pull-policy change |
| Every service image tag in Kubernetes is the Maven project version | Keep the stale `1.0.0` literal; use `latest`; version the deployment independently | The build produces `1.0.0-SNAPSHOT`, so a manifest naming `1.0.0` asks Kubernetes for an artifact this reactor did not build. Deriving the tag from `project.version` keeps the build command, the six manifests and the deployment contract on one version | A snapshot tag is mutable and is suitable only for this demo. A production release should publish immutable versioned images, preferably with a digest |
| Producer services enforce outbox and processed-marker retention with scheduled, explicit transactions | Keep the settings as documentation only; remove the settings; run one platform-wide database job | A configured horizon with no caller is false configuration. Each service owns its schema, so each service deletes only its own published rows and markers. Two explicit `TransactionTemplate` calls avoid the self-invocation trap that would leave modifying queries outside a transaction | Five small scheduled components repeat the same shape. A failed sweep delays deletion until the next interval but never blocks business traffic |
| Every consumer records processed event identifiers | Rely on broker exactly-once semantics; deduplicate in memory | Kafka delivers at least once, and a rebalance or a restart mid-batch redelivers. The source has no duplicate detection at all: a replayed feed drives the transaction write at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L562-L579` into a duplicate key and straight to the abend routine. An in-memory guard loses its contents on the restart that causes the redelivery | One extra table and one extra read per delivery. The marker table grows and needs a retention horizon |
| Money is a decimal string on the wire, never a JSON number | JSON number; integer minor units | Most readers deserialize a JSON number into a binary floating-point double, which reintroduces the representation this platform exists to avoid. A string with a two-decimal pattern in the schema cannot be widened into a double by accident | Consumers must parse rather than read a number directly |
| All monetary arithmetic truncates toward zero | `HALF_UP`; `HALF_EVEN` | The `ROUNDED` phrase appears zero times across all twenty-eight programs in `app/cbl/`, so every arithmetic store in the source truncates. `HALF_UP` is the reflexive choice in Java and it breaks equivalence by one cent, silently | A reader unfamiliar with the source will find `RoundingMode.DOWN` surprising, which is why one test asserts that half-up rounding produces a different answer |
| The account identifier is the Kafka message key on every event a producer writes | A single key form plus an exception for the one reason that resolves no account; no key | Kafka guarantees order within a partition only, and the ledger's balance updates for one account must not be reordered, so an account key is what keeps them on one partition. A reason-0100 decline resolves no account, so no key can be built for it from a stored row and the call is refused instead; `transaction-declined-v2.json`, which fixes both its code and its text to `0100` and declares no account, is retained rather than published. Inventing an account identifier to fill a key would put a false value on the wire, and omitting the key would spread one account's events across partitions | A stored `outbox_event` row written before `V19__unresolved_decline_names_its_account.sql` still carries the sixteen-character form, so a reader of that column meets two widths even though a reader of the topic meets one |

### Authorization service

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Keep `card_xref` and `account_credit_snapshot` current by consuming `account.state-changed` and `card.updated`, rather than calling the account and card services synchronously per authorization | Synchronous REST reads on the authorization path; a shared database; no replication at all | The plan states that `account_credit_snapshot` is kept current by account events, and replication keeps the response path free of two network calls that would each be able to fail or hang. A synchronous read would put two more services in the blast radius of every authorization | The replica can lag. That risk is what `carddemo.replica.lag-ceiling`, the `replica_gap` table and the refusal below exist to bound. Two more consumer groups to operate |
| Refuse an authorization this instance cannot vouch for, as an infrastructure fault answering 503 | Decline the transaction; approve anyway and log; add a fifth reject reason | A replica behind its owner fails silently, which is what makes it dangerous. The credit-limit rule at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L403-L407` computes an answer from obsolete numbers and approves what the current numbers would decline. A fifth reject reason was rejected outright: `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L385-L420` defines exactly four, `DeclineReason` holds exactly those four and the published schema enumerates them, so a fifth value breaks every consumer. It would also be untrue — the card and the account did nothing wrong, this service is the component unable to answer | A replica that stops being fed refuses traffic instead of serving it. That is the intended trade: refusing is visible, and approving against unknown numbers is not |
| Measure replica currency as consumer lag plus a per-account gap record, not as the age of a row | Keep the row-age window and shorten it; keep it and lengthen it; drop the check entirely; query the broker for group lag on every call | The row-age window was the shipped rule with a one-day default, and it refused the ordinary case rather than the failing one. Both owners publish on a state change and on nothing else. The observation stamp of an untouched card therefore recedes for ever while the copy stays exactly correct. A day after seeding, every authorization refused with nothing wrong and no way to recover. Shortening the window makes that arrive sooner and lengthening it only postpones it. Consumer lag inverts the reading. An idle topic with a caught-up consumer reports no backlog, so an untouched card stays authorizable indefinitely, and a change published and not yet applied is what registers. Lag alone is not enough, because a delivery that failed has its offset advanced once its diagnostic is away. `messaging/*Consumer` therefore writes a `replica_gap` row for that account in a transaction of its own, and the decision reads both. The lag comes from the listener containers this process already runs, so no broker request is issued on the decision path | `carddemo.replica.lag-ceiling` defaults to `0`, so any measured backlog on either replica stream refuses every decision until it clears, which is a wider refusal than one account's gap. A consumer holding an assignment that has completed no fetch publishes no lag sample, and that is read as nothing waiting rather than as unknown. Otherwise an idle topic would refuse for ever, which is the defect this replaced. A permanently unapplicable change leaves one account refusing until an operator clears its gap row, by design |
| Bind the two consumed events to authorization-local records rather than importing the producer's class | Import `com.carddemo.account.messaging.AccountStateChanged`; promote both records into `libs/event-contracts` | No service module may depend on another, so the import would not compile. That restriction is what makes "no synchronous coupling between consumers" a build-verified property rather than a review comment. `libs/event-contracts` deliberately holds the schema document for these two events and names no class to build, because the record belongs to the service owning the aggregate. The shared JSON Schema is the contract, and the deserializer validates every message against it | Two small records describe the same payload in two modules. The schema is what keeps them in step, and a divergence fails validation rather than passing silently |
| A `CardUpdated` message refreshes the observation on `card_xref` and writes no mapping field | Upsert the row; match on the last four digits and rewrite `account_id`; consume nothing | The message carries a masked card number because no full card number travels on any topic. `card_xref` is keyed by the full sixteen characters, so the message cannot name a key: it can neither create a row nor safely move a mapping. It does carry the account identifier, so it does confirm the owning service just wrote that account's card data, which is what the observation column records | Where two cards of one account share their last four digits, both rows are refreshed and one of them was not the card the event named. Bounded and safe: both already name this account and no mapping value changes, so no authorization can be misrouted |
| Record every metric of a decision outside the transaction that commits it | Annotate the method `@Transactional` and count inside it; count inside and compensate on rollback | A meter takes no part in a database transaction, so an increment inside one survives the rollback that discards the decision. The approval count would then exceed the events the outbox holds, and the difference would be invisible. The timer is described as running to commit, and a sample stopped inside the transaction excludes the flush and the commit, which is where a slow decision spends its time | The transaction boundary is an explicit `TransactionTemplate` call rather than an annotation, which is one more line and no proxy subtlety |
| Serialize published events with the string serializer, not `JsonSchemaValidatingSerializer` | Name the validating serializer on the producer | The value reaching the template is already the JSON text stored in `outbox_event.payload`. It was validated against its schema at write time inside `OutboxWriter`, where a rejected event can still roll back its own transaction. The validating serializer writes one of the registered event *records* and refuses every other value, so it would refuse this text outright. Every publish would raise a serialization exception and the outbox would retry for ever.
