The service would look healthy throughout | The producer re-reads the stored text rather than the record, so it cannot check what a record would have written. `EventContracts.publishViolationsOf` applies the same six checks to that text as `PublishGate` applied to the record, and the write-time gate is still the one that matters, because it is the only one that can still roll back |
| One schema name for the datasource URL, Flyway and Hibernate, all defaulting to `authorization_service` | Leave the Hibernate default at `authorization`, since deployments set the shared variable anyway | Flyway migrated one schema while Hibernate validated another, and a deployment that set `AUTHORIZATION_DB_SCHEMA` made the two agree and hid it completely. The failure therefore appeared only when the service ran on its defaults, which is exactly what onboarding asks a new developer to do first | None. The values were already meant to be the same |
| Route a replica record that can never be applied to the dead-letter topic after bounded retries | Retry for ever; drop it; stop the container | A record whose bytes break the schema breaks it on every attempt, so retrying only stalls the partition behind it. Dropping it loses the evidence. The dead letter carries the four diagnostic fields of `01 ABEND-DATA` at `app/cpy/CSMSG02Y.cpy:L21-L29` and no payload value, because the record may hold a credit limit or a pair of cycle balances | The replica misses that one change until an operator replays it |

### Notification service

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Acknowledge a rolled-back delivery only when a committed processed-event marker proves the work was already done | Treat every integrity violation as a concurrent duplicate and acknowledge | `statement_transaction` and `notification_log` raise the same exception type on a constraint of their own, and acknowledging those would discard the event silently. Re-reading the marker after the rollback establishes which collision it was rather than assuming | One extra read on the failure path |
| Count failed attempts and dead-lettered records on two separate series | One counter for both | One record retried three times and then dead-lettered incremented a single counter four times, while a record refused at deserialization incremented it once. The number answered no question. `failures` now counts one attempt and `records.dead.lettered` counts one record | Two series to read instead of one |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Notification consumes `transaction.authorized` directly, under `notification-authorized`" under [Event topology and fan-out](#event-topology-and-fan-out)**. Register no `TransactionAuthorized` metric series in this service | Keep the series for symmetry with the other consumers | When this row was written the service read `transaction.posted` and `fraud.assessed` only, and a series that can only ever read zero states something about the topology that is not true. The service then took a direct subscription on `transaction.authorized`, so the series now has a listener behind it. `config/ObservabilityConfig` registers `TransactionAuthorized` among the `event.type` tag values it publishes, and the broker grants all four topics this service subscribes to | The six services' meter sets are still not identical, which is correct: their topologies are not identical either |
| Enforce the shipped retention horizons with a scheduled sweep | Leave the horizons documented and unapplied; drop the settings | Three tables grow by one row per consumed event, and nothing else removed a row. Every horizon in `application.yml` and in the `COMMENT ON TABLE` statements was therefore a claim about intent rather than behaviour. A horizon nothing enforces is worse than none: a reader takes the table for bounded and it is not | A scheduled deletion runs against financial records. Each sweep is bounded by an index and runs in its own transaction, and a failure is logged and retried on the next pass rather than failing a delivery |
| Open each retention delete's transaction explicitly rather than annotating the three sweep methods | `@Transactional` on each method, called from the scheduled method | The scheduled method reaches its neighbours on `this`, which does not pass through the proxy that would start the transaction, and a modifying query outside a transaction fails. That failure arrives as a data-access exception, which the sweep logs and swallows, so the annotation would have left every horizon unenforced while reporting nothing louder than a warning | None. The explicit boundary cannot be bypassed that way, and one test counts the transactions opened |
| The `processed_event` retention window is stated once, in `application.yml`, and the table comment points at it | Restate the number in the `COMMENT ON TABLE` text | The comment said thirty days while the setting shipped one hundred and sixty-eight hours. Two numbers for one policy drift, and the one an operator reads is not the one the sweep applies | An operator reading the catalogue must follow one reference to get the value |
| The fraud-assessed listener declares `java.lang.Record` as its payload type | Declare `Object`; receive `ConsumerRecord<String, Object>` and extract the value; add a shared event interface | Spring supplies the raw Kafka `ConsumerRecord` as an invocation argument. An `Object` parameter matches that argument before payload resolution runs, so both valid assessment events reached the listener as the transport record and were rejected. `Record` is the narrow common Java type of `FraudFlagged` and `FraudCleared`, does not match Kafka's transport class, and keeps transport handling in the listener adapter | A future assessment implemented as a normal class would require a shared interface or a deliberate listener signature change |

### Ledger posting service

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A missing account balance row fails the consumer and reaches the dead-letter topic | Record it as a reject; ignore it | This is reject reason `0109` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L556-L558`, which the source assigns after the category balance has already been written and then never inspects. In the source it changes nothing. Here it is a real failure, because the alternative is a posting that silently updated one table and not the other | A deliberate behaviour change from the source, made because the source behaviour is a defect rather than a rule |
| Apply `spring.kafka.listener.auto-startup` in the hand-built container factory | Leave the factory as it was | The hand-built factory replaced the auto-configured one and never read that setting, so tests that set it to false were ignored and opened a real consumer. A setting a configuration file names and a factory silently discards is worse than an absent setting | None |
| Assert in the build that every relay destination has a matching broker grant | Rely on review; discover it at runtime | The relay published `TransactionDeclined` to a topic its principal had no producer entry for, so every decline stalled in the outbox while the service reported healthy. A test that reads the shipped topic names and both provisioning scripts turns that into a build failure | The test must be extended when a service gains a destination, which is the point |

### Fraud detection service

This service has no COBOL ancestor. Searching `app/cbl/` for `fraud`, `velocit`, `risk` and `scoring` matches none of its 28 programs, so every value below was chosen rather than derived, and each row says so.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A rule that triggers does not by itself flag; the summed score is compared against `carddemo.fraud.risk.flag-threshold` | Treat any triggered rule as a flag and delete the threshold; keep both and let the threshold stay unread | The threshold was bound and validated and then never read, so a single 25- or 30-point rule published `FraudFlagged` while the shipped configuration said 50. Two readings of the same file disagreed. A threshold is also the only way a score means anything: without one, the score is decoration and every rule is equally final | A single rule now clears. That is the intended reading of a threshold of 50 against rules worth 30, 30 and 25, and the score and the rules that objected are still recorded on the row |
| The threshold travels inside `RiskAssessment` and `flagged()` derives from it | Store a computed boolean; pass the threshold to each caller | A stored verdict can contradict the score beside it, and a threshold passed around is a policy in several places. Deriving from the two values it depends on makes disagreement unrepresentable | The record carries one more component, so its construction sites all name the threshold |
| `flag-threshold` starts at 1, not 0 | Keep `@Min(0)` | Zero flags a transaction no rule objected to, and `fraud-flagged-v1.json` requires a flagged event to name at least one rule. Zero would therefore produce an event the contract refuses and a row the schema refuses. Rejecting it at start-up names the property; accepting it fails later, per message | A configuration that set 0 now fails to start. No shipped file sets it |
| `fraud_assessment` refuses a flag with no rule, and permits rules without a flag | Keep the constraint as a biconditional; drop the constraint | The biconditional made a below-threshold row unstorable, so applying the threshold would have failed at the database. The remaining half is still worth enforcing. Every rule scores above zero and the threshold is at least one, so a score that reached it must name a rule. A flagged row naming none states a conclusion with no reason behind it | `flagged` alone no longer tells a reader whether a rule triggered. `triggered_rules` does, and it is on the same row |
| Count deserialization and schema failures in the recoverer, not in the listener | Count them in the listener; count every recovered record there | `ErrorHandlingDeserializer` records the failure and the container raises it before any listener is invoked, so the listener line that counted it could never run. The recoverer is the only place that sees one. Counting *every* recovered record there instead would double-count a business failure, which the listener already counts once per attempt | The two counts must stay disjoint. They are, because a payload that did not read never reached a listener |
| Register a dead-letter topic override on the validating serializer | Send dead letters through a second plain template; stop validating on publish | The relay sends its `DeadLetterEnvelope` through the same template as the two assessment events. The validating serializer refuses an event whose type is not bound to the topic it was sent to. It knows only the default name, so any deployment that renamed the dead-letter topic had every dead letter refused. The row that could not be published became unpublishable too. Every sweep retried it, failing identically for as long as the service ran | Three event types now share one factory, so a fourth destination needs a fourth override. The build asserts it |
| Fraud validates on publish while the other services publish text | Publish text here as well | This relay reads the outbox payload back into the event record before sending, so it hands the template a record and the validating serializer can check it. The other relays hand over the stored JSON text unchanged, which that serializer refuses by design, and their publish-side gate runs at write time instead | Two publish shapes exist across the platform. Each service's producer configuration states which one it uses |
| The four risk values are demonstration values | Derive them from the fixtures; omit them | No measurement stands behind any of them, and `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` carries no fraud outcome to fit against. Naming them as chosen is honest; deriving a threshold from data that contains no labels would not be | The numbers are illustrative. `docs/business-rule-flags.md` is where a reader is told which platform values are measured and which are chosen |

### Account service

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Account-update and cycle-close success meters record only after `TransactionTemplate` returns | Increment inside `@Transactional` methods; compensate on rollback; register transaction synchronizations in each branch | A meter is not part of the database transaction. Recording after the explicit boundary returns means the account row, customer row, and outbox row have committed before a success counter moves. A commit failure reaches a separate bounded failure series | The service methods carry an explicit wrapper and a small result record instead of relying on an annotation |

### Card service

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Register a card-update series only where a production class records it | Keep a zero-valued series for symmetry with the other services; expose a series an operator can read before any code moves it | `PUT /cards/{cardToken}` is delivered, so `domain/CardUpdateService` records `update.applied`, `update.conflicts`, and `update.latency`, and the relay records `events.published`, `failures`, `publish.latency`, `outbox.abandoned`, and `dead.letters.failed`. A series no class increments states something about the module that is not true, which is the reason the earlier zero-valued series were withdrawn rather than kept | The six services' meter sets are not identical. That is correct, because their surfaces are not identical either |
| `PUT /cards/{cardToken}` collapses the source's validate-then-confirm cycle into one call | Model the three confirmation states and require a second call; expose a draft resource | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl` is pseudo-conversational: it validates, answers `Changes validated.Press F5 to save` at `:L167`, ends the task, and waits for a second terminal interaction before `9200-WRITE-PROCESSING.` runs. `CCUP-CHANGES-NOT-OK`, `CCUP-CHANGES-OK-NOT-CONFIRMED` and `CCUP-CHANGES-OKAYED-AND-DONE` exist to carry that state across the two interactions, and transformation rule T6 drops presentation and navigation state rather than mapping it. A stateless request has no second interaction to carry state into | A caller cannot preview the edits before committing them. The text at `:L167` is emitted nowhere, and `CardValidationMessagesTest` asserts its absence together with the other five `WS-INFO-MSG` texts so the collapse stays visible |
| The card update detects a concurrent change by comparing fields, with no version column | Add a `@Version` column to `CardEntity`; overwrite unconditionally | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1503-L1508` compares a named set of values on the row it locked, and refreshes exactly that set at `:L1512-L1517`. A version column detects a different conflict set. It reports a race after any write to the row, including one that changed nothing this comparison names, so it would refuse updates the source applies | The compared value list has to stay in step with `CardEntity#applyUpdate`. `CardUpdateServiceTest` pins the list, and the entity exposes exactly one mutator, so a field can only become mutable by changing that method |
| The embossed name is folded on both sides of the concurrency comparison | Fold only the re-read value, as an earlier revision did; compare both sides unfolded | `9000-READ-DATA.` folds the fetched name at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1356-L1358` before storing the snapshot at `:L1360`, and `9300-CHECK-CHANGE-IN-REC.` folds the re-read name at `:L1499-L1501` before comparing at `:L1504`. Folding one side reported a lost race for every mixed-case name, and `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt` carries `Aniya Von` | A writer that changed only the letter case of a stored name is not reported as a concurrent change, which is what the source does. The refreshed snapshot returns the folded name, matching the move at `:L1513` |
| The concurrency comparison covers five values, not the source's six | Expose an accessor for the card verification value so the sixth can be compared; carry the value in the refreshed snapshot | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1503` opens the comparison on `CARD-CVV-CD`. `entity/CardEntity` publishes no accessor for it, and `CardEntity#applyUpdate` never writes it. No other path of the service writes a card row, so no writer can change it between the read and the lock. There is also no caller-held value to compare against, since the value is emitted nowhere. Five values cover every field a writer can change | A future writer of that column would not be detected by this comparison. `CardholderDataExposureTest` asserts the accessor stays absent, so such a writer cannot appear without failing the build |
| **SUPERSEDED by "`domain/CardCrossReferenceReconciler` reconciles this service's `card_xref` row against the card row on every update that commits" under [Card ownership, source-fidelity and status-domain remediation decisions](#card-ownership-source-fidelity-and-status-domain-remediation-decisions)**. The card update path reads no cross-reference row, and the divergence series is withdrawn | Keep the comparison and its counter, as an earlier revision did; keep the counter registered and unincremented | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L356` reads `*COPY CVACT03Y.` commented out, and the program's three file operations at `:L1383`, `:L1428` and `:L1478` touch only the card file, so the source update path holds no cross-reference access to reproduce. That reading of the source still stands. What it did not settle is what this service owes its own copy of the mapping, and the later row answers that: the card row is the authority, and the replica is brought into step inside the transaction that writes it | Superseded. This service's `card_xref` copy now has a reader and a writer, and the three outcomes are metered as `carddemo.card.xref.agreed`, `carddemo.card.xref.corrected` and `carddemo.card.xref.missing`. A card nobody updates is still never compared, and `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries that residual |
| The card update path uses two `cobol-compat` classes, `PicClause` and `PanMasker` | Route the numeric components through `NumvalParser`; validate the assembled expiry with `CobolDateValidator` | A census of the three card programs finds no call to `CSUTLDTC`, `NUMVAL`, `TEST-NUMVAL`, `NUMVAL-C`, `CSUTLDPY` or `CSUTLDWY`, and no `EXEC CICS LINK`. Every numeric input arrives as `PIC X(n)`, is class-tested at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L740` and `:L784`, and is read through a `PIC 9(n)` redefine that performs the conversion. `app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy` carries no monetary field, so no fixed-point arithmetic arises | The card path and the money paths validate numbers by different means. Each cites the source construct it reproduces |

### How long a duplicate-suppression marker is kept

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **SUPERSEDED by "A duplicate-delivery claim is permanent" in the security-review section below, which withdrew the horizon and the setting that named it. It read:** `PROCESSED_EVENT_RETENTION_HOURS` is 720 hours against 168 hours of broker log retention, and each service refuses to start with a marker horizon under twice the broker figure | Keep the two equal, as shipped; keep them equal and rely on the sweep running late. Add a permanent uniqueness constraint on the business transaction and drop the horizon question; make the horizon unbounded | The two were equal, so the guarantee held only if a swept marker and an unreadable record happened at the same instant, and three ordinary events break that. Segment cleanup is not instant. A restored backup carries a record older than the broker would still hold. An operator resetting a consumer group replays whatever the log still has. Any one leaves a record readable after its marker is gone, and for `account-posted` that means one transaction amount reaching a balance and a cycle accumulator a second time. A permanent business-transaction constraint is the stronger answer and a wider change. The ledger writes a category balance and an account balance from one event, so uniqueness would have to cover a compound the schema does not carry today. An unbounded horizon turns a table every message passes through into one that only grows | The marker table holds roughly four times as many rows before a sweep reaches them, which is one small row per consumed event. Twelve times the platform's own retention margin still would not survive an operator replaying a year-old backup, so the relationship is a floor rather than a promise. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the permanent-uniqueness option for a deployment that needs one |
| **SUPERSEDED by the same decision, which removed the `ProcessedEvent` record from all six properties classes. It read:** The relationship is enforced in each service's own `ProcessedEvent` properties record, at start-up | Assert it in the contract test alone; check it in the retention sweep and log a warning; document it in `.env.example` and rely on review | Both numbers arrive from configuration. A deployment that raises broker retention without raising the marker horizon has changed the safety of the platform without touching any code, and no test here would see it. A start-up refusal names the two values and stops the service. A warning from the sweep arrives after the window has already opened, and documentation is what the shipped pair already had | Six copies of one rule, because a properties record per service is the shape section 0.3.1 of the plan fixes. Each service's own `*PropertiesTest` holds the refusal, the boundary and the raise-broker-alone case, so a copy that drifts fails its own module |
| **SCOPED by the same decision: the figure now reaches the broker alone, because no service compares anything against it. It read:** `KAFKA_LOG_RETENTION_HOURS` reaches all six services from the shared Compose block and the ConfigMap, rather than being restated per service | Give each service its own broker-retention key; read the value from the broker at start-up through an admin client | One value describes the broker, so two copies of it can disagree and the disagreement is exactly what the check exists to catch. Reading it from the broker would tie start-up to a broker round trip, and to a topic-level override the service cannot see anyway. This platform sets retention at the broker rather than per topic | A deployment applying retention per topic rather than at the broker has to set this key to the shortest topic value by hand. The key's comment in `.env.example` says so |

### What a schema comment is allowed to promise

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every retention horizon a `COMMENT ON TABLE` declares now has a caller, and every purge a sweep performs is now declared | Delete the horizon text from the comments; leave the comments and treat them as aspiration; move all retention policy into one platform document | A security review found three business tables declaring a window nothing applied: `fraud_assessment` at ninety days, `velocity_window` at seven, `rejected_transaction` at ninety. Each grows by one row per event and none ever shrank, so a reader took a bounded table for granted and got an unbounded one. Deleting the text would have removed the only statement of intent anyone had written. The reverse defect existed too: `authorization_decision` said `retention=relationship` while a 365-day timer deleted it, and `unresolved_card_attempt` carried no comment at all while the same timer deleted it | Seventeen horizons across six schemas are now load-bearing configuration rather than prose. A migration that adds a table with a horizon has to add a sweep call in the same change. `RetentionSweepContractTest.everyDeclaredHorizonHasASweepThatAppliesIt` fails the build in both directions rather than leaving that to review |
| The horizon is declared as a span or as the setting that carries it, and never as a number restated beside a setting | Always a number; always a setting name; drop the convention and let each migration choose | A number in a comment drifts from the number the sweep reads, and the notification marker comment had already drifted from thirty days to one hundred and sixty-eight. Naming the setting cannot drift. A span is still permitted where no setting exists, which is how `retention=relationship` and `retention=reference` stay distinguishable from a real window | The contract test has to recognise both forms, which is one regular expression rather than a lookup. A comment that names a setting sends a reader to `application.yml` for the value |
| A reject row expires by age, which is faithful to the source rather than an addition | Keep the table unbounded; make it unbounded and say so; expire by row count, as the source expires by generation count | `app/jcl/DALYREJS.jcl:L24-L28` defines the source's reject generation group with `LIMIT(5)` and `SCRATCH`, so writing a sixth generation deletes the oldest. Every one of the six bases in `app/jcl/DEFGDGB.jcl` carries the same two parameters. The source's reject set is bounded and self-expiring. The deletion lives in a catalogue definition rather than in a `PERFORM`, which is why `RepositorySurfaceTest` had classified the table as one no source paragraph deletes from | A row count is not a generation count, so the bound is expressed in days rather than in files. `RepositorySurfaceTest.PERMITTED_RETENTION_DELETE` now names one exception among the ledger repositories and cites why, which is a weaker blanket rule than "no business repository exposes a delete" |
| The fraud velocity horizon is refused at start-up when it does not outlast the velocity window | Validate it in the sweep and log a warning; document the relationship; derive the horizon from the window | A bucket is counted into for the length of the configured window. A shorter horizon deletes the row a live authorization is incrementing, and the count is lost with no error anywhere. The two settings sit in different records, `carddemo.retention` and `carddemo.fraud.risk`, so neither can validate the other and the check belongs in the component that holds both. A warning from the sweep arrives after the window has already opened | One cross-record check in `domain/RetentionSweep`'s constructor rather than in a properties record, which is where every other check of this kind lives. Deriving the horizon would have removed the setting a deployment may need to raise for a legal reason |

### What a stored row is allowed to claim

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `notification_log` records an alert that was rendered and not sent, and says so in a column rather than in prose | Keep the delivery-attempt wording; delete the table; build the delivery port now; rename only the prose | A security review found rows described as delivery attempts while the service reaches no mail, message, webhook or push gateway. An operator counting rows in an incident review would have concluded that cardholders had been told about transactions and fraud flags. That is worse than a missing feature, because nobody looks for it. `outcome` carries `RENDERED_NOT_SENT`, `ck_notification_log_outcome` permits nothing else, and `NotificationLogEntity` writes the constant rather than accepting it, so no caller can claim a delivery even by mistake | A column every row shares a value with is normally worth removing. This one is a contract: adding a transport means widening the constraint and the entity together, in a commit a reviewer can see. Section 0.1.1 of the plan specifies this service as rendering an alert, so the delivery port stays a next task rather than a fix |
| A comment correction ships as its own migration named `subject_request_posture`, one per schema that carried a claim | Edit the original migration; fold the correction into the nearest unrelated migration; correct only the schema the review cited | An applied migration is not edited, so the correction has to be a new file. A file whose name states what it settles is findable by the next person asking the same question. The review cited one comment. A sweep of every live comment found six statements of the same promise, so the correction reaches all of them rather than the one that was quoted | Four schemas gain a migration that declares no table, column, index or row, and Flyway's history grows a row per schema. `SchemaMigrationTest`, `AccountControllerIT` and `CardRepositoryIT` each pin that history, so a future correction of this kind has to update its pin, which is the cost of the history being asserted at all |
| `attempted_at` becomes `rendered_at`, through a forward migration rather than an edit to V1 | Leave the column name and change only the comment; drop and re-add the column; edit V1 | The value written was always the renderer's own clock reading, so the new name is the one the value already had and no data changes. An applied migration is not edited, which is the same rule `V7__account_customer_link.sql` and `V4__marker_retention_margin.sql` follow. A column rename leaves the index definition correct, because PostgreSQL indexes columns by number, so only the index name needed renaming | The rename reached one entity field, one accessor, one Jakarta Persistence Query Language statement, one repository method name, one index name and thirteen test sites. A deployment that had already applied V1 gets the rename as a metadata-only operation |
| No schema comment on this platform promises an export or an erasure workflow | Leave the promises and build the workflow later; leave them and add a footnote; remove the sentences without recording the capability | Six statements promised one, in four schemas. The account service's `customer` table said "erase on request", and its `social_security_number` column said an erasure request cleared the value with the row. `account_customer_link` said an erasure has to reach the other replicas. The authorization service's `card_xref` said erasure followed the card service deleting the card, which neither service does. The card service's `card_xref` said an erasure request has to reach it. The notification service's `cardholder_context` said `observed_at` served a purge of erased accounts. None was implemented anywhere, and a reader planning for a subject request would have found a sentence and no code. Each now states the position outright, in capitals, and points at the task | The capability is still absent, and now conspicuously so. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` names all five stores an erasure has to reach across four schemas, which is the honest answer rather than an erasure that reports success after reaching four of them |

## Runtime QA-remediation decisions

A runtime test pass over the consumer-configuration surface raised eight findings: two major, two minor and four informational. Each fix below could reasonably have been made another way, and each informational item is a choice a reader could question. Both kinds are recorded here rather than in a code comment.

### Terminal delivery and offsets

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| All five consuming services set `commitRecovered` on their `DefaultErrorHandler`, and each one refuses to start unless the effective acknowledgement mode is `MANUAL_IMMEDIATE` | Have the recoverer acknowledge the record after a successful publication; leave the offset uncommitted and accept the redelivery. Switch the containers to an automatic acknowledgement mode; state the required mode in a comment and trust a deployment to keep it | Every container acknowledges by hand, so nothing acknowledges a record its listener never accepted, and the offset of a dead-lettered record stayed uncommitted. A restart, a deployment or a plain rebalance then read the record again and published a second dead letter for the same coordinates. The dead-letter topic and the per-record counter grew without new input. A recoverer holds no acknowledgement, so the framework setting is the only seam. An automatic mode would acknowledge a record whose writes had not committed, which is the guarantee the manual mode exists for. The framework applies the setting under `MANUAL_IMMEDIATE` alone. Under `MANUAL` it reports the setting as ignored and commits nothing. A comment naming the requirement was not enough: `spring.kafka.listener.ack-mode` is bindable from the environment, and moving it withdrew the guarantee in silence. Authorization, ledger, notification and account now read the effective mode back and stop the context on any other value. Fraud pins the mode and refuses a bound value that names another | A deployment that names any other acknowledgement mode fails to start, rather than running with a dead-letter route that is not terminal. That is a louder failure than the one it replaces. One test per service asserts the setting, the commit and the refusal of every other mode |
| Every relay publishes one governed diagnostic for an outbox row it gives up on, and counts the abandonment | Log the tenth failure at ERROR and leave it there; drop the row silently; keep retrying for ever; store a terminal flag and expect an operator to query the table | A row that spends its ten attempts is an event no consumer will ever see. The four services that already published a diagnostic made that visible, and authorization did not. Its tenth attempt wrote the same warning as the nine before it, so a permanent loss and a transient retry read identically on the topic and on the metrics endpoint. Retrying for ever would hold the partition of one account behind a row nothing can fix. The diagnostic is published inside the sweep that abandons the row, and waited for. A broker that refuses it rolls the abandonment back, so the row is offered again rather than left terminal with no record anywhere | One row cannot be named this way. A decline whose card resolved to no cross-reference row is keyed on its sixteen-character transaction identifier, and `schemas/dead-letter-v1.json` keys a diagnostic on eleven account digits. Publishing one would fail validation on every attempt and roll the sweep back for ever. That row is abandoned, reported at ERROR with its identifier, and counted as a diagnostic that did not reach the topic |
| Authorization, ledger and fraud count a broker-acknowledged publication as well as an outbox write | Read the outbox table instead; count only the write and describe it as publication; add a gauge of unpublished rows | The write count and the publication count answer different questions, and only the second one shows a relay that has stopped. While three services counted the write alone, a broker no relay could reach left every produce-side series rising exactly as it does on a healthy stack. Account and card already counted the acknowledgement, so three services were describing outbox insertion with the word published | Each new series is untagged, so it adds one time series per service. A publication the broker acknowledged whose row mark then rolled back is not counted, because every count is taken after the sweep commits |
| The relay pass budget bounds the whole sweep, and a send waits only for what is left of it | Wait the whole budget for every send and accept the overrun; rename the setting a per-send ceiling; give each send its own separate budget | `carddemo.outbox.relay.max-duration-ms` names a wall-time limit on one pass, and waiting the full value per send made it behave as a per-send ceiling. A send admitted a millisecond before the deadline held the scheduled thread for another whole window. A pass could therefore run for close to twice its stated bound. The account relay already read the setting as a remaining budget, so the two now agree and the name matches the behaviour. Renaming the key instead would have left five relays with two different meanings for one concept | A send abandoned with the budget spent may still reach the broker while the next pass republishes the row, so one event can appear twice. That is the at-least-once publication this platform already documents, and every consumer's processed-event marker absorbs it |
| The readiness administrator carries pinned connection settings, and metadata recovery is off | Set a log level to hide the output; create and close an administrator per health check; leave the settings inherited and accept the output; drop the Kafka readiness check | A broker unreachable at start-up left the client rebootstrapping with no interval at all. `metadata.recovery.strategy` defaults to `rebootstrap`, and the client logs one INFO line every time it looks for a node and finds none. Measured before the fix, one service wrote over a thousand lines a second and spent measurable processor time doing it. That fills a disk and buries the line naming the degraded dependency. Turning metadata recovery off removes the log site itself: rebootstrap re-reads `bootstrap.servers` for a client whose known brokers have all moved, and this client knows the bootstrap addresses and nothing else. Failing the call is what a probe wants, and the health indicator already reports it as down. A log level would hide the symptom and the diagnosis together; an administrator per check would churn a client every ten seconds | The client no longer re-reads `bootstrap.servers` after a total broker loss. Reconnection was verified across a broker restart with no application restart. The four backoff settings and two connection timeouts are pinned values rather than tuned ones |

### Failure taxonomy and listener settings

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One predicate decides what a persistence failure is, and it names a transaction fault as well as a data-access fault | Test for `DataAccessException` at each site, as before; test for `SQLException` alone; add a new tag value for a connection failure | A paused or unreachable database surfaces as `CannotCreateTransactionException` from the connection pool, which is a `TransactionException` and is not a `DataAccessException`. The registered `persistence` series therefore stayed at zero for the most common database failure there is, while `rendering` counted faults that never reached a renderer. The rule was written five times in the notification service, so correcting it in one place was the only correction that could not drift. A new tag value would split one integration across two series | The predicate walks a cause chain, so a fault that merely wraps a database fault counts as one. That is the reading an operator wants: the degraded integration is the database either way |
| The fraud container factory applies the listener concurrency it reads | Delete the inert key from the shipped file and pin concurrency in the Javadoc; leave the key inert | The shipped file declares `spring.kafka.listener.concurrency`, and a hand-built factory that read the key and did nothing with it left the declaration inert. The shipped value equals the framework default, so a deployment raising it got one consumer thread anyway with no indication. The ledger factory already applied the same value the same way, so this makes two hand-built factories answer a bound listener block alike | A deployment can now raise concurrency on a service whose velocity window is a database row. The shipped value stayed one at the time, so that change altered no shipped behavior. A later performance review raised it to the partition count, which the row under [what reads a partition](#what-reads-a-partition-and-what-runs-on-the-scheduler) records, and this factory is what carries it |
| A ledger dead letter carries the same four diagnostic headers as its notification and fraud counterparts | Leave the ledger route with broker coordinates alone and treat the fixed-width value as the whole contract; remove the headers from notification and fraud instead | A reader of all six dead-letter topics had to parse the value of one service and read the headers of the others. Every value is generated here from a constant or a failure type. The outgoing set is rebuilt from an allowlist reading the last value of each name. One test asserts a spoofed inbound copy of each name is replaced. Uniform headers cost four generated values per record | The four components now travel twice, in the value and in the headers. The value remains the authority, and both are built from the same constants |

### Informational items confirmed rather than changed

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Readiness stays down while the broker is unreachable, and the Compose health check keeps probing the readiness group | Move the Kafka check out of readiness so a broker outage leaves a container healthy; probe liveness instead; drop the restart policy | A consumer that cannot reach the broker cannot consume, so reporting it ready would route traffic to an instance that renders no alert and posts no balance. Liveness stays up throughout, which is the signal that decides a restart. Docker restarts an exited container rather than an unhealthy one, so the shipped `restart: unless-stopped` cannot loop on a broker outage. Both endpoints answered throughout the outage in testing | Every container reads unhealthy during a broker outage while remaining alive and answering. A reader of `docker compose ps` sees the outage as six unhealthy services |
| Each service keeps its own failure-metric discriminator, and the naming is documented rather than unified | Rename notification's `failure.kind` to `stage`; rename the ledger and fraud `stage` to `failure.kind`; add a second tag to every series | The three services count different things. Notification tags by what failed — a schema document, a database, a renderer — because it renders and persists. The ledger and fraud tag by the stage that failed, because their pipelines have stages an operator reasons about separately. A rename would break the series a running deployment already collects, and no requirement asks for one scheme. The observability requirement asks each service for a failure count with bounded tags, which all three have. The names are recorded in [event flow](event-flow.md) so one query per family can be written without reading the code | A platform-wide dashboard needs one query per discriminator rather than one across all services. A follow-up task in [suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) carries the unification |
| A ledger dead letter keeps abend code `DEAD` and culprit `POSTTRAN` while notification and fraud use `0999` and their service names | Use `0999` everywhere; use a service name everywhere; use the job name everywhere | The two fields exist to name what failed. `POSTTRAN` is the job at `app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl` whose step the ledger container replaces. `0999` is the abend code `MOVE 999 TO ABCODE` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L710` carries, in the four-digit form the status formatter renders. Making them uniform would erase the provenance each was chosen for. The envelope schema admits one to four characters of `[0-9A-Z]`, so both are valid | A reader learns two literals. Both are documented, and the surrounding record layout is identical |
| Rendered text stays restricted to printable United States ASCII | Transliterate a non-ASCII value; widen the pattern and let the renderer emit it | The renderers reproduce a column-oriented statement layout at `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L86-L159`, where a field occupies a fixed number of character positions. A multi-byte character breaks that alignment, and a transliteration invents a cardholder-facing value the source never held. The pattern is `^[ -~]*$`, documented on the contract, and a record that breaks it is dead-lettered rather than rendered wrong | An accented merchant name or cardholder description is refused rather than rendered. This is a product decision a reader should confirm, and it is carried as a review item in [business-rule flags](business-rule-flags.md) |

## Review-remediation decisions

An integration review of the delivered platform raised eighteen findings. Every fix below could reasonably have been made another way, so each records its alternatives here rather than in a code comment. The source rules a fix restores are cited; the rules a fix leaves alone are recorded in [business-rule flags](business-rule-flags.md).

### Event topology and fan-out

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **In force. Supersedes "Register no `TransactionAuthorized` metric series in this service" under [Notification service](#notification-service)**. Notification consumes `transaction.authorized` directly, under `notification-authorized` | Count notification's `transaction.posted` subscription as the third consumer; add a fourth service whose only purpose is to consume; have the ledger call notification | The requirement sets a floor of three independent services consuming *that event*. A consumer of a downstream event is not one: it cannot run until the ledger commits, so the ledger's failure is its failure and the independence claim is false. Notification now holds its own offsets on the authorization topic and reaches a cardholder alert without waiting for posting | Notification writes for two points in one transaction's life, authorization and posting. The composite key on `statement_transaction` and the per-group `processed_event` marker make the second write an update rather than a duplicate |
| The ledger consumes `AccountStateChanged` under `ledger-account-state`, its own group | Read the account service synchronously when a projection row is missing; let `V2__seed.sql` remain the only writer; subscribe under `authorization-account-state` | `account_balance_projection` had two writers and neither was the owner of the data: the seed loaded the fifty fixture accounts and the posting path added deltas. An account opened after deployment therefore had no row, and a cycle close at `app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl:L353-L354` never reached the ledger's copy of the two accumulators. Sharing authorization's group would split the partitions between two services, and each would see half the events | A second replica of account state to keep current. Each apply is monotonic against the producer clock, so a redelivery discards itself rather than moving a cycle balance backwards |

### Semantic correctness

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The ledger cannot reject an authorized event, and `RejectRecorder` takes a `FeedTransaction` instead | Keep the projection-miss reject; accept either type through a common interface; keep the reject but suppress its event | A reject publishes `TransactionDeclined` for a transaction another service already approved, which reverses a decision this service does not own. Making the input a different record type removes the mistake from the type system rather than from a review checklist: no conversion exists from an authorized event to a feed record. `2500-WRITE-REJECT-REC` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L446-L465` refuses feed records, which is exactly what the type now names | The reject path has no runtime caller in the delivered platform, because no delivered entry point supplies a feed record. It is retained because that paragraph is inside the parity claim |
| An authorization request may name a card number, an account identifier, or both | Require the card number, as before; require both; resolve an account-only request by scanning cards | `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L196-L209` accepts either identifier and refuses only a request carrying neither, with its own message. An account-only request resolves through the account-to-card cross-reference, and a request carrying both must agree | One more repository path on the decision route. A both-supplied mismatch is refused rather than silently preferring one value |
| A missing required projection row is never manufactured | Render with blank cardholder fields, as `CardholderDetails.blank()` did; render a partial alert; skip the alert | A blank alert is silently wrong on the cardholder-facing path: no name, no address, no credit score, and no error anywhere. `V2__seed.sql` now bootstraps `cardholder_context` from `app/data/ASCII/custdata.txt`, so a first alert is correct, and a genuinely absent row fails and retries instead of rendering | An account with no projection row blocks its own alert until the account service publishes its context. That is visible, where a blank alert was not |

### Security

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The authorization dead-letter route overrides `createProducerRecord` rather than supplying `setHeadersFunction` | Configure `setHeadersFunction` on the plain recoverer; publish the failed record unchanged; drop the record silently | `setHeadersFunction` *augments* the inbound header set instead of replacing it, so every inbound header still travels and the failed payload and key travel with it. A poison record on a replica stream can hold a credit limit, a pair of cycle balances, or a card number. Overriding record construction is the only seam that removes all three: the diagnostic is bounded and sanitized, the key is the record's coordinates, and only an allowlist of headers survives | An operator cannot replay the original bytes from the dead-letter topic. The governed envelope names the topic, partition, offset, attempt count, and reason, which is what a diagnosis needs |
| Card detail authorizes on the derived card token and then verifies the row's own account | Keep the masked last-four comparison; keep the masked comparison and add the account check; authorize on the full card number | A masked card number is not an identity. Only its last four digits vary, so two cards can share one masked form, and an authority granted over one would reach the other. The token is unique by construction. The account comparison closes the second half: a caller holding a card number must also name the account that card belongs to. `app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L739` shows the source contemplated the same comparison and left it commented out | A departure from strict source equivalence, recorded as entry D1 in [business-rule flags](business-rule-flags.md). It is a departure in this platform's own additive authorization layer, not in a source rule. The source reached that program only through a terminal signon that granted every user every card |
| The card token is a keyed, versioned code, and no key means no token | An unkeyed digest; a vault-issued surrogate; a compiled-in default key | Three rows under [Event contracts and messaging](#event-contracts-and-messaging) carry this decision and its risks | Recorded there |

### Reliability of the terminal paths

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Three relays record the dead-letter obligation as durable state; two rely on the abandoning transaction rolling back | Use one model in all five; use neither and accept the loss; add a `dead_letter_state` column to all five relays | Every relay publishes asynchronously and none runs its sweep in one transaction: each claims a batch in one transaction, dispatches outside every transaction, and settles each row in another. The four publisher ports answer `CompletionStage<Void>`, card's at `messaging/EventPublisherPort.java:L66`, and the ledger relay sends through its own `KafkaTemplate`. What decides the model is where a refused diagnostic would go. Authorization, fraud detection and account catch a failed row and continue their sweep, so a refused diagnostic would be lost with the stage that reported it, and `outbox_event.dead_letter_state` holds `REQUIRED` until an acknowledgement moves it to `PUBLISHED`. Ledger posting and card open exactly one transaction that spans a send, and only on the attempt that gives up on a row: the diagnostic is published and awaited inside it, so a refusal rolls back both the abandonment and the attempt just recorded, and the next sweep offers the row and its diagnostic together. Durability comes free there, and a column would add state nothing reads | Two models to understand instead of one, and the rollback model holds a database transaction across a broker round trip on that one attempt. A diagnostic acknowledged just before its commit fails is sent again on the next sweep, which is why every consumer keeps a processed-event table. Each relay states which model it uses, and one test per relay proves the diagnostic is not lost |
| The destination lookup happens inside the per-row failure boundary | Resolve every destination before the loop, as before; validate destinations at start-up | An unknown event type threw before `recordFailure` ran, so the row's attempt count never moved and every later sweep claimed the same row and failed identically. Resolving inside the boundary lets the row record its attempt and progress to abandonment, and an unresolved destination becomes the placeholder `no-configured-topic` on the diagnostic | A row of an unconfigured type still cannot be published. It now says so once and stops, instead of failing silently for as long as the service runs |
| The placeholder for an unresolved destination is `no-configured-topic`, not a bracketed phrase | Keep `(no configured topic)`; omit the field; use an empty string | `schemas/dead-letter-v1.json` constrains `sourceTopic` to `^[a-zA-Z0-9._-]{1,249}$`. A parenthesised phrase fails that pattern, and account validates its envelope before publishing. The diagnostic that explains an unpublishable row would itself have been unpublishable, retried by every sweep with the obligation never discharged | The value is less readable than a sentence. It is a topic field, so a topic-shaped value belongs in it |
| The ledger's terminal listener route sends bytes through its own template | Keep the JSON-validating template bound to the business topics; send the diagnostic as a governed envelope like authorization does | The recoverer addresses `<sourceTopic>.DLT` and the fixed-width record is bytes, which the validating serializer refuses by design because those bytes are not a registered event. A `KafkaTemplate<String, byte[]>` with `ByteArraySerializer` is the only binding that accepts them. A static contract test now holds the recoverer's destination and its serializer together | Two producer templates in one service. The test names the pairing, so a fourth destination fails the build rather than the broker |
| Readiness derives the expected listener count from the container registry | Keep a literal count per service; drop the listener check; check only that at least one listener runs | The literal said two while notification declared three, so a service that had lost a listener still reported ready. Deriving the expectation from the registry makes the check self-maintaining: adding a listener cannot leave readiness behind. Applied to all six services, because the same literal pattern was in all six | The check can no longer detect a listener a developer forgot to declare at all. One test per service asserts the derivation instead of a number |
| Consumers validate against the schemas on the `event-contracts` classpath; the account service alone ships one local copy | Ship a byte-identical local copy in every service, as an earlier revision described; read every document from the library at run time and ship none; let copies diverge | Every service depends on `libs/event-contracts`, so `schemas/*.json` is already on its classpath and duplicating thirteen documents six times would create seventy-eight files to keep in step for no gain. The one exception is `services/account-service/src/main/resources/schemas/account-state-changed-v1.json`, which the account outbox writer validates against before insert so a malformed event is refused inside the transaction that created it. A contract test compares that copy against the canonical document and fails on any drift | One document exists twice. The test is what makes the duplication safe, and a reader looking for six local copies will not find them |
| The account outbox validates each of its two event records against its governed schema before insert | Validate on publish only; validate one of the two and not the other | An event that cannot be published is better refused inside the transaction that created it, because that transaction can still roll back. The publish-side gate cannot: by then the business change has committed. The card writer already validated the one record it writes, `CardUpdated`, so the account writer matching it removes an inconsistency as well as a gap | Two validations per event on the write path. Each is a schema check over a small document |

### Observability of terminal outcomes

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A failed publish has exactly one metric owner, and `publishTimeout` bounds the active send | Keep both the publisher and the relay counting it; delete the unused property; keep the unused blocking helper | One failure incremented two series, so neither number answered a question. The relay is the owner because it is what decides a row failed. `carddemo.outbox.relay.publish-timeout` had no reader at all, and it now bounds the send the relay actually performs. The blocking helper nothing called was removed rather than left as a second way to publish | The publisher no longer reports its own failures. It reports them to the relay, which counts them once |
| A terminal dead-letter outcome is counted on a series distinct from the per-attempt failure series | Add a fourth `stage` tag value to the existing `failures` series; count nothing terminal; reuse the deserialization stage | A fourth stage on `failures` would overlap the stages already there, because a record spent at deserialization is also a terminal record. Summing the stages would then double count, which is precisely the confusion the finding named. A separate name with an `outcome` tag cannot overlap. The ledger relay is the one place a `stage=abandon` tag is safe, because an outbox row is never a consumed record | Two names to read per service rather than one. Each Javadoc states which series carries retries and which carries spent work |
| The terminal hooks are invoked from the real recoverer and abandon paths, not from a wrapper that only counts one of them | Count in the listener; count before routing; leave the hooks uninvoked | A counter that increments before the route reports a dead letter the broker refused. Counting after the delegate returns, and separately before a rethrow, makes the two outcomes distinguishable. `DefaultErrorHandler` swallows a rethrown recoverer failure, so the count is the only observable evidence that the diagnostic did not land | A refusal is visible as a count and a log line rather than as a propagated exception. That is the framework's behaviour, not a choice made here |

## Fraud vertical-slice QA-remediation decisions

A runtime test pass over the fraud detection service raised ten findings: three minor and seven
informational. Every fix below could reasonably have been made another way. Every informational item
is a choice a reader could question. Both kinds are recorded here instead of in a code comment.

### Velocity window semantics and capacity

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The velocity span start is truncated to the bucket unit | Leave the span start at `occurredAt` less the configured width; keep the behaviour and document the granularity | The writer stored whole hour buckets while the rule compared an untruncated instant against bucket **starts**. A bucket holding events inside the configured width was dropped whenever its start preceded the boundary. The evaluated span was whatever remained of the current clock hour. One millisecond of difference in `occurredAt` flipped the verdict on identical history, a burst spanning 02:55 to 03:01 was not counted, and any width below sixty minutes evaluated nothing. Truncating keeps the whole bucket that holds the configured width, which makes the verdict a function of the history rather than of the wall-clock position | The evaluated span now covers the configured width and at most one bucket more, so a sixty-minute setting reads between sixty and one hundred and twenty minutes of history. That is a stated property rather than an accident, and a finer bucket unit is a follow-up task in [suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) |
| The bucket unit is declared once, on `VelocityWindowEntity` | Make the bucket unit configurable so the width is honoured literally; declare it in both the writer and the rule | Two classes disagreed about the shape of the history, and the disagreement was possible only because each carried its own notion of the unit. One declaration is what stops them drifting apart again | A configurable unit is not available without touching both classes, which is the point |
| `velocity_window.total_amount` is `NUMERIC(15,2)`, and fifteen digits rather than thirteen | Keep `NUMERIC(11,2)`; widen to `NUMERIC(13,2)`; saturate the accumulator at its ceiling instead of failing; store the count alone and drop the total | The column accumulates every magnitude an account books inside one bucket, and its precision had been taken from `TRAN-AMT PIC S9(09)V99` at `app/cpy/CVTRA05Y.cpy:L10`, which is the width of **one** amount. Two authorizations at the maximum magnitude the event contract admits therefore overflowed, rolled back the consumer transaction and dead-lettered the authorization. The account became unassessable exactly while it was booking large amounts, which is the traffic a velocity rule exists to notice. Thirteen digits move the ceiling to one hundred such authorizations, still inside the burst the rule watches for. Fifteen holds ten thousand, and `authorization_count` is an `INTEGER`, so the count reaches its own ceiling first. Saturating would report a total that is not the total | The stored width matches no copybook field, and none is behind it: nothing in `app/cbl/` counts velocity, so no equivalence claim depends on it. The scale stays two |
| The widening ships as `V3__velocity_total_headroom.sql` rather than as an edit to `V1__schema.sql` | Edit `V1__schema.sql`; number the new migration `V2` | `spring.flyway.validate-on-migrate` is true, so editing an applied migration stops start-up on every database that already ran it. `V2` is the seed slot the other five services use and this service ships none, which two tests assert, so taking that number would make the numbering say something untrue. The migration was applied against a database already carrying the original column and left every stored row unchanged | The service's migration numbering skips a version. The migration header says why, and the version-two absence is still asserted |

### Outbox publication window

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The producer resolves one send inside the relay tick that issued it | Leave `delivery.timeout.ms` at 120000 and accept the duplicate; raise `carddemo.outbox.relay.max-duration-ms` above the producer window | The tick bounded one send by the time left in the pass, while the producer held the record for 120 seconds. A send the relay had given up on could therefore be delivered much later, while the relay's retry published a second copy. Measured: freezing the broker while three pending rows relayed produced seven records, one `eventId` three times. `enable.idempotence` cannot suppress that, because the two copies come from two `send` calls with their own sequence numbers. `max.block.ms` plus `delivery.timeout.ms` is now 4000 against a 5000 pass budget. Measured after: three records, each `eventId` once | A broker outage is noticed in about two seconds instead of two minutes, so a row spends its ten attempts sooner — about 140 seconds of outage survived against about 170 |
| The relay waits out a send it has already issued rather than cancelling it | Cancel the abandoned send; drop the pass deadline altogether | A Kafka send cannot be cancelled, so the deadline is read before a send rather than used to bound one already in flight. Dropping the deadline would restore the case it was written for: a broker that accepts a connection and never answers, holding the scheduled thread | A tick that has issued a send can outrun its own budget by that send's window, so the thread is bounded by the pass budget plus one send. A duplicate can still arrive when a broker appends a record and loses the acknowledgement. No producer setting removes that and only transactional publication would, so publication stays at least once by design. [Event flow](event-flow.md) separates the two paths |
| The relationship between the two settings is asserted in the build rather than left to a comment | Comment the pairing in the shipped file alone; validate it at start-up; derive one value from the other | Two values in two blocks of one file have to agree, and nothing about either one says so where a person changes it. A test that reads the shipped document fails the build when one moves without the other, which is the same shape as the relay-destination-versus-broker-grant assertion already in this service. Deriving one from the other would hide the producer's own constraint that `delivery.timeout.ms` is at least `linger.ms` plus `request.timeout.ms`, which the test also asserts | The test has to be extended when a new timing value joins the budget. That is the point of it |

### Velocity magnitude

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The velocity accumulator holds amount magnitude, so a refund raises the total a burst is measured against | Accumulate the signed amount; keep a signed total and a magnitude total side by side; skip a negative amount altogether | Velocity asks how much activity an account had in a window, and a refund is activity. Nothing in `app/cbl/` counts velocity, so no source rule settles it. The reading is consistent everywhere it appears: `RiskScoringService` records `amount().abs()`, `VelocityRule` and `AmountAnomalyRule` compare magnitudes, and `ck_velocity_window_total_nonnegative` refuses a negative total. Signed accumulation would let alternating charges and refunds cancel each other out and raise no amount signal at all, which is the pattern the rule most needs to see. Two totals would double the write and leave a reader asking which one a threshold means | A refund tightens the next authorization instead of loosening it, which reads as unfair to a cardholder who returned a purchase. That is a scoring-policy call rather than a correctness one, so [suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) asks a person to confirm it. The convention was previously readable only in `V1__schema.sql` and in Javadoc, which is why it is written here |

### Dependency floors above the versions the plan names

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Four dependency versions are pinned above the versions the imported bill of materials resolves. Two of those, `postgresql` and `jackson-databind`, are versions the plan names, so the deviation is recorded here rather than only in a `pom.xml` comment | Resolve what the bill of materials manages and accept the advisories; wait for a bill of materials that manages the fixed versions; pin per module; exclude the affected artifact | A published advisory affects each managed version. `GHSA-5gvw-p9qm-jgwh`, recorded as `CVE-2026-59889`, affects `jackson-databind` 3.1.4 and is fixed in 3.1.5. Every event on this platform serializes through `libs/event-contracts`, whose records use `@JsonUnwrapped`, and that is one half of the affected combination, so the floor is raised rather than argued away. `GHSA-xx22-p4ch-683r`, recorded as `CVE-2026-59949`, is the advisory behind the `lz4-java` floor. Byte-array arguments reach the Java Native Interface XXHash implementations unvalidated in 1.11.0 and earlier, which lets a caller crash the virtual machine, and 1.11.1 fixes it. That identifier was previously written as `CVE-2026-7053` in the `pom.xml` comment, which named an advisory that says nothing about this library. A property alone changes nothing, because a bill of materials imported with scope `import` ignores the importing project's properties, which is why `dependencyManagement` re-declares every affected artifact. `jackson-core` moves with `jackson-databind` because the two ship as one release train and a mixed pair is unsupported | The pins sit above the table in the plan until that table is revised. Each one has to be withdrawn once the bill of materials manages that version or higher, and the `pom.xml` comment says so where a person changes it. A version the framework did not test with is a compatibility risk, which the aggregator build and both suites are what answer |
| The `net.logstash.logback:logstash-logback-encoder` pin is withdrawn, leaving `json-schema-validator` as the only artifact this build pins outside the bill of materials | Keep the pin and let a module declare the encoder; keep the pin and add a comment saying nothing uses it. Add the encoder to the six services and ship a Logback configuration file per service | The pin governed nothing. No module declared the artifact, so `dependencyManagement` had no coordinate to manage and the library never reached a classpath. Structured JSON log output already comes from the framework: each service sets `logging.structured.format.console` to `logstash`, which selects the Logstash format Spring Boot implements, and no service ships a Logback configuration file. The plan names the encoder for structured JSON output, and that requirement is met by the framework rather than by the third-party library. The deviation is therefore the dependency and not the behaviour. The plan also asks that the hand-pinned set stay as small as possible, because every hand-pinned version is a future conflict | A reader who expects the plan's two unmanaged artifacts finds one, which this row is what explains. A module that later wants the encoder has to pin it again. `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest` fails if one declares it without a pin, so the omission is reported rather than resolved to whatever version a transitive path supplies |

### Fraud items the pass confirmed rather than changed

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| An event identifier reused for a different transaction is dropped, and the consequence is written down rather than guarded against | Key the marker on the transaction identifier as well; read the claimed payload back and compare it; refuse a reused identifier with a diagnostic | The marker exists so a redelivery has no second effect, and `ProcessedEventRepository.claimEvent` is one conditional insert whose row count decides. A second payload arriving under a claimed identifier is indistinguishable from a redelivery at that moment, and telling them apart would mean reading the first payload back on every consume. Identifier uniqueness belongs to the producer, and `libs/event-contracts` mints one identifier per event | A publisher that reuses an identifier loses the second transaction silently, with no dead letter and no counter to notice it by. It cannot arise from this platform's own publishers, and a producing service's own outbox rows are where it would show |
| The framework's producer listener keeps logging the message key and the opening of the payload when a send fails | Replace `LoggingProducerListener` with one that logs neither; lower its logger below ERROR; disable it | The key is the account identifier and the payload prefix is the governed event document, which carries a masked card number and never a card verification value. Checked against a real failure log: the entry stops at `schemaVersion=1, ...` because the listener truncates what it prints. Removing it would remove the one line that names which send failed, since the outbox row records only the failure class | An account identifier and an event's opening fields reach the log at ERROR while a broker is unreachable. Both are pseudonymous, and the platform's masking rule is about card data |
| The upper score clamp stays although the three shipped rules cannot reach it | Remove the clamp; assert the rule total in a test instead; raise a rule's weight until the clamp is reachable | `FraudFlagged` bounds `riskScore` at 100, and the clamp is what keeps a scored event inside its own schema document. The three rules total 85 today, so nothing on the wire reaches it. A fourth rule or a raised weight would. The failure it prevents is a refused publish at the serializer with the assessment already written. `RiskScoringServiceTest` drives two sixty-point rules through it, so the branch a caller cannot reach is still covered | A branch no end-to-end path exercises is one a reader may take for dead code. The unit test and this row are what say otherwise |
| `HEAD` on the assessment route is refused, because the permit names `GET` | Permit `HEAD` beside `GET`; permit any method and let the controller refuse; drop the method from the matcher | The chain ends in `denyAll`, so a method no rule names is refused instead of allowed. `GET` is the only method the route serves and no requirement asks for `HEAD`. Permitting a method the controller does not implement would answer from inside an authorized chain with 405 rather than refusing at the edge | A client probing with `HEAD` sees 403, which reads as an authorization problem rather than an unsupported method. Default deny is worth that |
| The environment mismatch a test pass reported is stale environment state rather than a repository defect, so no shipped Compose or Kubernetes file changed | Weaken `docker-compose.yml` to match the broker that happened to be running; add a plaintext profile; commit a `.env` | Raising the stack from the shipped `docker-compose.yml` provisioned everything the report named as missing. That is database TLS, `SASL_PLAINTEXT` with the standard authorizer, the six per-service logins including `carddemo_fraud_svc`, and the topics the fraud service reads and writes. The `.env` the file needs is generated from `.env.example`, and `.gitignore` keeps it untracked because it holds passwords. The file describing the environment is therefore correct, and the environment that was running had been raised another way | A reader who starts a broker by hand still meets the mismatch. [Onboarding](onboarding.md) carries the sequence that does not, and no repository file can govern a container started outside it |

## Container packaging decisions

`services/*/Dockerfile`, the six `Dockerfile.dockerignore` files beside them, and the health probe in `docker-compose.yml` are identical across the six services, so each decision below applies six times.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every `Dockerfile` builds its own module in a Java Development Kit 25 builder stage, from the `card-platform` context | Keep both stages on the Java Runtime Environment image and copy an archive the host packaged; add a seventh "builder" image the six copy from; build in the image but from the module directory | The Agent Action Plan requires a Java Development Kit 25 builder stage, and the previous shape had none: both stages were the runtime image and the archive arrived from a host `mvn package`, so the image was reproducible only to the extent the machine that ran Maven was. The premise the earlier log entry rejected it on — that an in-image build puts a compiler and the source tree into a runtime image — is false for a multi-stage build, and it is measured false here: the shipped image carries `/app/app.jar` and no `mvn`, no `javac` and no `.java` file anywhere on its filesystem. The context has to be `card-platform` rather than the module directory because `mvn -pl services/X -am` reads the aggregator descriptor and builds `libs/event-contracts` and `libs/cobol-compat`, and none of the three is inside a module. A shared builder image would be a seventh artifact to version and would still need this context | An image build now needs network access to Maven Central, where before it needed only a local archive; the BuildKit cache mount means one download serves all six builds in a run, and the pipeline's job budget went from thirty-five minutes to sixty to hold it. `docker build` without BuildKit ignores the cache mount and re-downloads, so the pipeline and `deploy/k8s/load-images.sh` both name `DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1`. The host `mvn package` step stays in the pipeline as the fast failure, not as an input to any image |
| The builder stage refuses to continue unless exactly one archive arrived under its own `target/` | Keep `COPY target/*.jar app.jar`; name the archive with a build argument; copy the whole `target/` directory and select inside the image; check the archive is a boot archive rather than counting | Copying a pattern onto a name is ambiguity-tolerant. Measured, with two matches BuildKit keeps whichever sorts last and reports nothing. A decoy archive named after the real one therefore shipped in place of it, and the build still exited 0. Counting is one shell test and it turns that silence into a build failure naming what it found. A build argument would move the version into a second place that has to be raised with `pom.xml`. Copying all of `target/` would send the class files and the reports to the daemon for no gain | The guard runs in the builder stage, which carries a shell, so the runtime image no longer needs one for it. A single archive with the wrong contents still ships: the guard counts archives and does not read them |
| Each module carries `Dockerfile.dockerignore` beside its `Dockerfile` | Rely on the root `.dockerignore`; exclude nothing and accept the transfer; keep the per-module `.dockerignore` the module context used | The context is now the platform directory, which every service shares, so a root ignore file could not say which of the six services a build is for. BuildKit reads `<dockerfile>.dockerignore` in preference to the context's own file, which lets one file per service exclude the other five services' sources: a build of one image cannot read another module's source at all. Measured: 3.81 megabytes transferred, and inside the builder only this module carries a `src/` directory while the other five contribute a `pom.xml` each, which Maven needs to build the project graph. A module-context `.dockerignore` would govern nothing here, so keeping one would be a file that reads as active and is not | The two ignore files are not merged, so everything the root file excludes has to be repeated in each of the six.
