Each file lists paths rather than excluding everything and re-including what is needed, because an inverted pattern that goes wrong fails by shipping nothing rather than by shipping too much |
| The health probe keeps a curl form and a bash form, and the `wget` form between them is removed | Keep all three; keep `wget` and drop `bash`; install `wget` into the image | `bellsoft/liberica-openjre-debian:25.0.4-9`, the image every `Dockerfile` pins by digest, carries curl and bash and no wget, so that branch could never run: it read as a fallback and was dead text. The bash form stays because it needs no package at all, and it is what would answer if a future base image dropped curl. Installing wget would add a package to an image that installs none | A base image carrying neither curl nor bash would report every container unhealthy. The digest pin is what makes that a deliberate change rather than a surprise |
| The unhealthy tolerance is twelve retries at a ten-second interval, inside a sixty-second start period | Keep twenty-four retries; drop to six; add `restart: on-failure`; leave the start period to absorb it | Twenty-four retries meant a service that stopped answering kept reporting healthy for 240 seconds, which is longer than the whole demonstration it serves. Failures inside `start_period` do not count and the cold start measures 45 to 47 seconds, so the 60-second grace is untouched and the change costs no startup margin. Halving the retry count keeps a wide tolerance for a transient pause, while making an outage visible in a time a person watching will notice. Measured, a service reported unhealthy 176 seconds after its database was stopped. It recovered 15 seconds after the database returned, with no restart | A service unreachable for about three minutes is reported unhealthy, and `restart: unless-stopped` does not restart on health, so an operator still acts. A pause longer than 120 seconds under a heavy load would be reported unhealthy while the process is alive |

## Configuration truthfulness and published-key decisions

Each row below removed something that was inert, or made something that was silent say what it is. A configuration statement nobody can act on is worse than no statement, because a reader plans against it.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SUPERSEDED by "Both deriving services refuse the two card-token keys this repository publishes, and the build-scope key only when it arrives through `CARD_TOKEN_SECRET`" under [Where the card-token key comes from](#where-the-card-token-key-comes-from). Every service refused to start on the published card-token key unless its own configuration stated that it meant to use it, through a new `CARD_TOKEN_ALLOW_PUBLISHED_KEY`. The four demonstration paths each carried the statement. They no longer do. `.env.example` and the Secret template carry a placeholder, the provisioning script generates a key per install, and `CardTokenReconciler` re-derives the seeded literals under it. Only `pom.xml` still states the intent, for the build key it supplies | Leave the key as a working default and rely on the documented rotation; ship a `REPLACE` placeholder as every other credential does. Generate a key per install; refuse the key outright with no way to state intent | `.env.example`, `deploy/k8s/31-secret.example.yaml` and `pom.xml` all name one working key, and they have to. The fifty `card_token` literals the card service seeds and the `SCOPE_CARD` authority the demonstration grants were derived under it. A placeholder or a per-install key would therefore leave every seeded row keyed to nothing and every card route answering 403. What was missing was any way to tell that configuration apart from a rotated one. A derivation site cannot: the published key derives perfectly good tokens, and their only defect is that anyone holding this repository can recompute them for a candidate card number. `PanMasker.requireCardTokenSecretFitForUse` therefore recognises the published value and refuses it unless the deployment states its intent, and the four demonstration paths each carry that statement in their own configuration. A deployment that assembles its own configuration cannot inherit the statement by copying a key | Five configuration surfaces now carry one more entry, and a rotation has to remove it in the same change, which `suggested-next-tasks.md` records and `CardTokenKeyContractTest` asserts for the demonstration paths. Only the literal `true` states the intent, so a typed `yes` reads as unstated and stops the service — deliberate, because a guard that accepts approximations of consent accepts none. No start-up log line reports that the published key is in use, because no service's credential guard owns a logger. A line in a log nobody reads is weaker than a line in the configuration a deployment must write |
| `net.logstash.logback:logstash-logback-encoder` is removed from `dependencyManagement` and its version property with it | Keep the entry and the comment explaining it is available; declare the dependency in the six services and configure a Logback file; keep the property and drop only the managed entry | The entry pinned version 9.0 of an artifact no module declared, so it reached no classpath. Measured on the running image: no `logstash-logback-encoder` archive is present, and every service emits `@timestamp`, `@version`, `logger_name` and `level_value`, because `logging.structured.format.console: logstash` is answered by the framework's own logging support. Declaring the library in six services to justify a pin would add an artifact and a Logback file per service to reproduce output the framework already produces. This is a divergence from the planned dependency inventory, which lists the encoder as one of two unmanaged dependencies. The requirement it served, structured JSON logs, is delivered by the framework instead | The build now manages one unmanaged artifact rather than two, so a module that later wants the encoder has to pin it deliberately. Anyone reading the plan's dependency table will not find the encoder in the build, which is why this row exists |
| `GROUP_AUTHORIZATION` is removed from `.env.example`, from `docker-compose.yml` and from the authorization service's own fallback | Keep it as documentation of the default group; keep it and grant it a broker entry; rename it to one of the two active groups | The key reached nothing. The access-control matrix grants entries to `GROUP_AUTHORIZATION_ACCOUNT` and `GROUP_AUTHORIZATION_CARD`, and both authorization listeners name their own group through `carddemo.kafka.groups`. The authorization container was never given `GROUP_AUTHORIZATION` at all, so setting it changed no consumer and granted no entry. A reader scoping the groups per stack would have set eleven values, one of which does nothing. The service's `spring.kafka.consumer.group-id` keeps a literal default, because a consumer factory built without one refuses a listener that names no group. A comment beside it now says no listener joins it | The dead key is gone from the inventory a reader scopes per clone, so a stack that set it must stop. Nothing consumed it, so nothing breaks |

## Demo bootstrap and cluster image decisions

`scripts/start-demo.sh`, `scripts/generate-env.sh` and `deploy/k8s/load-images.sh` are the three committed procedures a reader has to run rather than assemble. Each replaced a documented sequence that was correct and that nobody could execute in one step.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One committed script, `scripts/start-demo.sh`, is the advertised path from a clean clone to a running stack | Keep the four documented steps and rely on the reader to follow them; add a `Makefile`; add a Maven profile that runs Compose; commit a set of demo credentials so `docker compose up` works alone | `.env.example`, `docker-compose.yml` and the README each advertised `cp .env.example .env` followed by `docker compose up -d --build`, and that pair could not work from a clean clone: at the time every `Dockerfile` copied an archive out of its own module's `target/`, which a fresh checkout has none of, and Compose reads nineteen credentials no file in this repository supplies. A reader following the advertised command reached a `COPY` failure, and a reader who fixed that reached a refusal about a placeholder password. The archive half is now gone — each `Dockerfile` compiles its own module in a builder stage, recorded under [container packaging](#container-packaging-decisions) — and the credentials remain, which is what the script is for. The script performs the steps in the one order that works and asks nothing. Committing credentials was rejected because a credential published here is a credential everyone holds | A script is one more thing to keep true, so `DemoBootstrapContractTest` reads it and the commands the documents advertise, and the compile stage of the workflow parses all three scripts. The script is bash, so a Windows reader needs a POSIX shell, which the Docker prerequisite already implies |
| `scripts/generate-env.sh` reads the credential names out of `.env.example` instead of holding a list of them | Hard-code the fourteen names as the workflow does; keep the list in a fourth file both read; generate every assignment rather than only placeholders | The names exist in `.env.example` already, marked by the values `REPLACE-WITH` and `REPLACE-THIS`, so a second copy of them can only be a copy that falls behind. Deriving them means adding a credential to `.env.example` extends the script with no edit. Filling only placeholders is what makes re-running safe: a value a reader set by hand survives, and the script is idempotent rather than destructive | The derivation depends on those two markers staying the shape they are, which the contract test pins. The workflow keeps its own inline list, because two of its stages generate credentials before any Maven run and the identity hashes need the `spring-security-crypto` archive that a Maven run resolves |
| An existing `.env` is reconciled against `.env.example` on every run, and the run fails when an assignment the example declares is still absent | Leave a reused file alone, as before; overwrite it from the example and regenerate everything; refuse a file that is out of date and make the operator start again | The example is the declaration of what a run needs and `.env` is one machine's answer to it, so a key added to the declaration afterwards is simply absent from the answer. An absent key leaves no placeholder, so the placeholder count this script ended with could not see it: it reported success on a file missing `ACQUIRER_PASSWORD_HASH`, and Compose then refused to interpolate a variable it requires without a default, which stopped the documented demo at its first command. Overwriting would destroy credentials already in use and, with them, the database volume that was initialised under the old superuser password. Refusing outright would make every upgrade a manual merge of a file of 136 assignments. Appending the example's own value for each missing name is the smallest change that makes the file complete, and the generation steps that follow fill any placeholder it just added, in the same run | A value the example changed its default for keeps the old value on a reconciled file, because an existing assignment is never rewritten. Assignments the example no longer declares are reported and kept: the script reads two files and cannot tell a retired setting from one an operator added. `GROUP_AUTHORIZATION` is now exactly such a name: this platform retired it from the example and from Compose, so a reused `.env` still carrying it is reported and kept even though nothing reads it any more, and deleting it on the script's own judgement would delete an operator's own setting just as readily. `PROCESSED_EVENT_RETENTION_HOURS` is a second, retired with the marker horizon it set, and a reused file keeps it on the same terms |
| A value this repository publishes is regenerated on reconciliation, and a value this machine overrode is reported rather than changed | Reconcile names only, as the row above did; rewrite every value the example changed its default for; reimplement each service's validation rules in the script | Reconciling names left the other half of an upgrade open, and the delivered workspace proved it twice: `CARD_TOKEN_SECRET` held the demo key this repository publishes, which `SecurityConfig.requireUsableCardTokenKey` refuses outright, and `PROCESSED_EVENT_RETENTION_HOURS` held a week, which was no longer twice the broker window a duplicate-delivery marker then had to outlive. Neither is a missing key and neither is a placeholder, so every check the script had passed while four of six containers could not start. A published value is nobody's secret and no running deployment can hold one, so it is regenerated, with the name read out of the file rather than listed in the script. An overridden value may be a deliberate choice, so it is named with both values and left alone. Reimplementing the services' own rules in bash was rejected: the rules would drift from the code that enforces them, which is the defect this row exists to close | An overridden value still stops a container until an operator acts on the report, which is the cost of not overwriting a choice. Regenerating a card-token key invalidates every token already derived under the old one, so a `SCOPE_CARD` authority and any stored token have to be re-derived; `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the rotation procedure |
| The three identity passwords are generated, and their plaintext is written to `card-platform/.demo-credentials` at mode 600 | Prompt for them, as the guide did with `read -rsp`; use one fixed demo password; print them to the terminal only; store them in `.env` beside the hashes | `.env` stores a bcrypt hash, and a hash cannot be sent to a service as a password, so a generated password that is never recorded leaves a stack nobody can authenticate against. A prompt makes the bootstrap interactive, which is the property this script exists to remove. A fixed password is a published credential. Printing without storing loses the value as soon as the scroll-back does. The file is git-ignored beside `.env`, and `CARDDEMO_ADMIN_PASSWORD`, `CARDDEMO_USER_PASSWORD` and `CARDDEMO_MONITORING_PASSWORD` let a reader choose the values instead | A second file now holds credentials for the local stack. It is owner-only and ignored, and the script prints its path rather than its contents so a captured terminal log carries no password |
| `.demo-credentials` is owner-only from creation rather than narrowed after the write: the script sets `umask 077` before its first write, fills a temporary file `mktemp` created at mode 600, renames that onto the destination, refuses a destination that is a symbolic link or not a regular file, and brings an existing file back to owner-only on every run | Keep the redirection and the `chmod 600` after it; copy a temporary file into place with `install -m 600`; rely on `umask` alone and set no mode explicitly; leave the mode of an existing file as it is | A redirection creates its file under the umask in force at that moment, 0644 on a default account, and all four plaintext logins were written before the `chmod` narrowed it, so another local account could read them in between. That is CWE-732 with the temporary-file exposure of CWE-377 beside it, and the only fix is a mode that is right at creation rather than corrected afterwards. The rename is atomic inside one directory, so a reader sees the previous file or the finished one and never a partial write, and it carries the temporary file's mode onto the destination, which is why nothing is narrowed after it. The symbolic-link refusal is what keeps the write from landing somewhere else entirely. An existing file's mode is repaired on every run because the exposure belongs to the file rather than to the run that made it, and a run that generates nothing would otherwise leave a wide file wide | A run interrupted between `mktemp` and the rename can leave a `.demo-credentials.XXXXXX` file behind. It carries mode 600 like the destination, the shell trap removes it on any failure `set -e` sees, and `.gitignore` covers the pattern so it cannot enter history. `umask 077` also governs every other file this script writes, which is intended: each one carries a working credential |
| `deploy/k8s/load-images.sh` builds the six images and loads them into kind, minikube or Docker Desktop | Leave the manifests as they are and document the step in prose; push to a local registry and change the pull policy to `IfNotPresent`; parameterise the image reference so a registry can be named; use `docker save` and `ctr images import` per node | The six Deployments name `carddemo/<service>:1.0.0-SNAPSHOT` with `imagePullPolicy: Never`, nothing publishes those images, and no file in this repository loaded them, so a clean cluster reported `ErrImageNeverPull` on every service Pod. That status names no cause, which is what made this worth a script rather than a sentence. A registry would add a container and a credential to a demonstration the user asked to keep minimal, and changing the pull policy without one would make a missing image a silent pull attempt against Docker Hub. The script reads the tag out of `pom.xml`, so it cannot drift from the manifests, and it reads the node's image list back afterwards rather than assuming the load worked | Three runtimes are covered and any other cluster is refused by name rather than half-supported. A remote cluster still needs a registry, which `suggested-next-tasks.md` records. The images stay mutable snapshots, as they are in Compose and in the pipeline |

## Continuous integration decisions

`.github/workflows/ci.yml` is the repository's only workflow, and the repository had no pipeline before it. Rule 1 keeps rationale out of the workflow file, so the choices behind its ten stages are recorded here. Six of the nine predate the security review. The three it added, and the two controls it added to the container stage, are recorded under Supply chain and delivery decisions below. [Onboarding](onboarding.md) carries the local form of every command the workflow runs.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The equivalence suite is its own stage, so nine named stages replace one `mvn verify` | Fold every check into a single `verify` stage; run the parity checks only on the default branch; run them on a schedule | The user asked that equivalence testing never be deferred, so the suite needs a stage whose name appears in the run summary and whose failure cannot be read as a compilation error. Two further stages exist for the same reason: the `*IT.java` classes in the six service modules run only under Failsafe — 24 of them today, counted off disk by `ContinuousIntegrationWorkflowContractTest` so the figure here cannot drift from the tree, while the stage itself selects by naming pattern rather than by count, so adding one needs no edit to the workflow — and the event schema gate is what keeps a new consumer from breaking an existing one. A single stage would report all of that as one reactor log | Nine stages each check out the repository, and the eight that run Maven install the toolchain again, which costs runner minutes that one stage would not |
| The toolchain is pinned to one exact build in three places: `JAVA_VERSION: "25.0.4+7"` in the workflow, `[25,26)` and `[3.9.16,3.10.0)` in the aggregator's enforcer, and the same two versions in the onboarding table | Keep `JAVA_VERSION: "25"` and the open-ended ranges; pin the workflow and leave the enforcer open; pin the enforcer and leave the workflow open; add a toolchains file | The three had drifted apart. The workflow installed the release line, so it took whichever Temurin patch Adoptium had published that morning; the enforcer admitted JDK 26 and every future Maven 3.9; onboarding told a reader to install 25.0.4+7. A build that pins its dependency versions by bill of materials, its images by digest and its actions by commit was choosing its compiler by date. The workflow now also reads both versions back out of the installed toolchain, so an installer that answered with something else fails the compile stage by name rather than surfacing later as a class-file or enforcer failure | A new JDK or Maven patch is a deliberate edit to three files rather than an automatic upgrade, and a developer who has 25.0.5 installed is refused by the enforcer until the range is raised. That refusal is the point: nothing here has been exercised on it |
| Every archive carries one fixed entry timestamp, `project.build.outputTimestamp`, so two builds of one commit produce byte-identical archives | Leave the build time in each entry; derive the value from the commit time; set it per module | Without it the Jar plugin and the Spring Boot repackage stamped every entry with the moment the build ran, so nothing could tell a rebuild from a change: not a reader comparing two images, and not the provenance statement the pipeline records over the six archives. Measured after the change: two `mvn clean package` runs three seconds apart produced the same SHA-256 for all nine archives, and every entry reads 2026-08-10 00:00 rather than the build time. A commit-derived value was rejected because a Maven property cannot read git, and a per-build value restores exactly the problem it removes | The date is raised by hand when a release is cut, and an archive therefore claims a modification time that is not when it was built. That is what the Maven specification for this property intends, and the version in the archive name is what identifies the contents |
| Apache Maven 3.9.16 is downloaded and checked against its published SHA-512 sum in every stage | Accept the Maven the runner image ships; add a Maven wrapper to the repository; relax the enforcer range | The build requires `[3.9.16,3.10.0)`, and a hosted runner ships whatever patch its image carries. A runner below the floor would fail the enforcer for an environment reason that reads like a project defect. No wrapper exists in this repository, so the archive is fetched and verified instead | Each stage depends on the Apache archive host being reachable, and the pinned version has to be raised here as well as in `card-platform/pom.xml` |
| Each stage assembles the checksum line before verifying it, rather than handing the published file to `sha512sum --check` | Compare the two digests with a shell string equality test; skip verification; keep a checksum file in this repository | `apache-maven-3.9.16-bin.tar.gz.sha512` holds 128 hexadecimal characters, no file name and no closing newline, and GNU coreutils reads a checksum line as digest, two spaces, file name. Measured: handing the published file to `sha512sum --check` exits 1 with "no properly formatted checksum lines found", which failed every job at its second step, so nothing after it ever ran. Assembling the line keeps the verification inside the tool that owns it, and `--strict` then refuses any other shape. Taking the first field with `awk` also accepts the two-field form other projects publish. A shell equality test would work and would move the comparison out of that tool, where a typo reads as a passing string comparison rather than as a failed checksum | The step now depends on the published file's first field being the digest. `ContinuousIntegrationWorkflowContractTest` pins the assembled form and asserts the bare-digest form is absent, so the defect cannot return unnoticed |
| Every Maven stage builds the modules it needs with `-am` rather than installing the reactor first | Run `install` in the first stage and resolve from the local repository in later stages; publish the archives between stages | `card-platform/equivalence-tests` declares test-scope dependencies on all six services at `${project.version}`, and the aggregator does not manage those coordinates. Measured: `-pl equivalence-tests test` without `-am` fails with "Could not find artifact com.carddemo:event-contracts". Each stage runs on its own runner with its own workspace, so `-am` makes every stage self-sufficient | Upstream modules are compiled again in each stage instead of once, which trades runner time for stages that do not depend on an earlier stage's local repository |
| The container stage builds all six images, starts them, and publishes none | Push to a registry; sign the images; keep the images only as a build cache; build without starting | The user framed the deliverable as a demonstration rather than a deployment, so the check that matters is that each `Dockerfile` builds **and** that what it builds runs. Publishing would need a registry credential the workflow does not hold and a package write scope no job of it takes: every job holds `contents: read`, and the one job that adds a write scope is the push-only provenance stage, which takes the two an attestation needs and nothing wider. Building without starting proved compilability only: measured, a stage that ends at `docker build` stays green while every image fails to boot, migrate or serve health. The stage now raises the whole stack from `docker-compose.yml`, reads `/actuator/health` on all six management ports, and removes the stack whether the check passed or not | The stage costs the time a cold stack takes to converge, roughly two minutes, and it needs the fourteen credentials and three encoded identity secrets generated on the runner. Nothing downstream can consume the images, so a deployment target still builds them again |
| One image tag serves the workflow, Compose and Kubernetes: `carddemo/<service>:1.0.0-SNAPSHOT`, the Maven project version | Keep the workflow's own `:ci` tag; use `latest`; let Compose keep its default `<project>-<service>` name; derive the tag from the commit | Three names for one artifact meant the stage's output was consumed by nothing: the workflow built `:ci`, `deploy/k8s` named the project version, and the six Compose services declared no `image:` key at all, so Compose built a seventh name. One tag makes the images the workflow builds the images Compose starts in the same job and the images a cluster expects, and `KubernetesDeploymentContractTest` and `ContinuousIntegrationWorkflowContractTest` both derive it from `card-platform/pom.xml` so the three places cannot drift | A snapshot tag is mutable, which is acceptable for a demonstration and not for a release. The version has to be raised in `pom.xml`, in the six Compose entries and in the six manifests together, and the two contract tests are what report a mismatch |
| Every stage that writes a test report keeps it as a named artifact | Retain nothing and read the log; publish a test-report annotation with a third-party action; upload the archives and the images too | A run previously discarded every Surefire and Failsafe document, so a red run left nothing to download and a failure had to be reproduced locally before it could be read. A third-party annotation action would add a supply-chain dependency to a workflow that uses first-party actions alone. The six 82-megabyte archives and the six images are deliberately not uploaded: they are rebuilt by one documented command | Four artifacts per run at a fourteen-day retention consume storage |
| The upload sets `if: always()` and `if-no-files-found: error` | Upload on success only; treat an empty upload as a pass | `if: always()` is what makes a failing stage upload rather than skip, which is the run a reader most needs the report from. `if-no-files-found: error` turns an empty upload into a failure, because a stage that wrote no report did not run its tests | A stage legitimately producing no report would have to declare so explicitly |
| `-DskipTests` appears on the compile and packaging stages, and the tests belong to the three stages between them | Run the tests in the compile stage; skip integration tests with `-DskipITs` and rely on one later stage; package with the tests enabled | Those two stages exist to produce class files and archives. Every test still runs, and now runs once: Surefire across all nine modules in stage 2, Failsafe over the six services in stage 3, and the `*EquivalenceTest` classes in stage 4, each stage skipping only what a stage before it already ran and reported. Packaging with tests enabled would run the whole suite a second time for no new information | A reader who greps the file for `skipTests` sees two hits and has to read three more stages to confirm nothing is skipped overall |
| The stage that raises the stack generates the fourteen credentials with the command `.env.example` documents, immediately after copying it | Commit a set of credentials for the pipeline; relax the provisioning refusal when a continuous-integration variable is set; add a second Compose file for the pipeline; drop the stage and rely on Testcontainers alone | Copying the example file is not enough on its own. Measured: `cp .env.example .env` followed by a Compose start leaves the database unhealthy and the stage fails, because the provisioning script refuses a credential the example file publishes. Generating them in the stage keeps the refusal intact and puts no credential in the repository, and reusing the documented command means one place defines it. Measured after: every container reports healthy and every health endpoint answers with an up status. The container stage is the only stage that reads the Compose file, because the two test stages that once raised a database and a broker from it were reached by no test | Each run holds credentials that exist only for that runner, so a failed run cannot be reproduced by connecting to its database afterwards. The variable list is repeated in the workflow and in `.env.example`, and a new credential has to be added in both |
| The class-file check reads every class file the reactor wrote, collected with `mapfile` and read by one `javap` invocation | Sample one class file with `find -print -quit`; pipe `find` into `head`; run `javap` once per file; read the release from the compiler settings instead | A step declared as `shell: bash` runs under `bash -eo pipefail`, so no form of this check may contain a pipeline whose reader closes early: measured, `find` piped into `head -n 1` exits 141 on 10 of 10 runs because `find` takes SIGPIPE, and the step fails on the shell's status rather than on a class file. Sampling one file avoided that and checked 1 of the 534 files this reactor writes, always inside the first module, so a service compiled at another release passed the stage four other stages depend on. `mapfile` with process substitution has no pipeline, and `javap` accepts every path in one invocation: measured all 534 files in 1.8 seconds, against roughly two minutes for one invocation per file | The step holds the whole collection in a shell array and one temporary file, which is bounded by the reactor's own output. It reads main class files only; test classes compile under the same property and the enforcer rule covers them |
| Every action runs at a full commit SHA, with the release that commit carries named in a comment beside it | Keep the major tag `@v4`; move to the newest major tag `@v7`; name a full release tag such as `@v7.0.1`; add an automated update tool and keep tags; vendor the three actions into this repository | A major tag is a movable reference: the owner of an action repoints it at every release inside that line, so what a run executes changes with no change in this repository, and a compromised or withdrawn release reaches this build without review. A full release tag is movable too, because a tag can be deleted and recreated. A commit cannot move. Measured against the three action repositories: the newest releases are `actions/checkout` v7.0.1, `actions/setup-java` v5.7.0 and `actions/upload-artifact` v7.0.1, so the workflow's `@v4` keys were three, one and three major lines behind. Each `uses` key now names the commit and carries the release beside it, because a bare forty-character digest tells a reader nothing about which version they are reading. `ContinuousIntegrationWorkflowContractTest` reads both halves and refuses any reference that is not a forty-character commit, so a tag cannot return unnoticed | A pinned commit receives no fix, so raising the pins is deliberate work rather than something that happens on its own, and no automated update tool is configured — [suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) carries that as an item. The comment is the only human-readable trace of the version, and an edit that changes one half without the other is a lie the contract test is there to catch |
| Every job names the runner image `ubuntu-24.04` | Keep `ubuntu-latest`; move to `ubuntu-26.04`; run the jobs inside a container image this repository controls | `ubuntu-latest` moves to the next Ubuntu release on GitHub's own schedule, which replaces the runner's kernel, its Docker version and its preinstalled toolchain under a build that pins its Java to 25 and its Maven to 3.9.16 by hand. A build that pins two toolchain versions and leaves the machine under them floating is pinned in name only. `ubuntu-24.04` is the generally available image this workflow was verified against. `ubuntu-26.04` was in public preview at the time of writing, and a preview image is the wrong place to discover a Docker or Compose difference. A container job would give the strongest control and would mean maintaining and publishing an image for a demonstration pipeline | The image reaches end of life on GitHub's schedule and the label then has to be raised, in six places at once. `everyJobNamesThePinnedRunnerImage` asserts all six agree and refuses `ubuntu-latest`, so the raise is one edit the test verifies rather than a silent divergence |
| The workflow header draws the dependency graph rather than describing a sequence | Leave the ordered description; drop the description and let a reader read the six `needs` keys; make the stages genuinely sequential by chaining each `needs` to the one before | The header claimed six stages run in order, and four of them name the compile stage alone, so the runner starts all four together. A reader planning a change trusted a straight line that does not exist. Chaining the stages would have made the description true and would have turned four parallel gates into a serial run, roughly tripling the wall-clock time of a red build for no extra signal. The header now draws the fan-out as ASCII and says the four gates start together, and `theHeaderDescribesTheDependencyGraphTheJobsDeclare` reads the `needs` keys out of the parsed document and compares them with the graph the header draws | The drawing has to be redrawn whenever a stage is added, and the contract test fails until it is. That is the intended cost: the description cannot drift from the keys without a red build |
| The compile release is verified by an enforcer `requireProperty` rule in every module | Add `extra-enforcer-rules` and its `enforceBytecodeVersion`; trust the aggregator property alone; check the release in the workflow only | The plan requires the language level to be verified by the enforcer, and `requireJavaVersion` verifies the running JDK, which says nothing about the release a module compiles at. Measured before the rule: setting `maven.compiler.release` to 21 in one service compiled silently at exit 0 and wrote class files at major version 65. `requireProperty` reads the effective property in every module, ships inside `enforcer-rules` already on the classpath, and therefore keeps `mvn -o` working. `enforceBytecodeVersion` would read the class files themselves, and adding it means a new artifact to resolve and pin | The rule reads a property rather than the bytes, so a compiler argument passed on the command line could still override the release. The workflow's class-file sweep is what reads the bytes |

## Web-surface QA-remediation decisions

A runtime test pass over the six web surfaces raised eighteen findings, six major and twelve minor,
plus one informational item against the ledger balance route. The finding is what a caller saw; the
row below is the choice made in answer to it, and every one of these could reasonably have been made
another way. Where a finding's suggested fix was not taken, the row says so and says why.

### Request strictness on the authorization route

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| An amount arriving as a JSON number is refused at deserialization rather than coerced | Coerce the number to text and carry on; accept it and record a warning; leave the framework's coercion in place | Section 0.3.1 of the plan makes money a decimal string and never a JSON number. Most parsers deserialize a JSON number into a double, which reintroduces binary floating point into the one place this platform cannot afford it. Refusing at the boundary is the only answer that cannot silently round | A client that sent a number now receives 400 where it received 200. The refusal text names the member, so the change is diagnosable from the response alone |
| The openapi `amount` pattern was widened to the grammar the parser accepts, and the parser was left alone | Narrow the parser to the published pattern; leave the two disagreeing; publish two patterns and pick by content type | Requirement I3 of the plan mandates the tolerant grammar of `FUNCTION NUMVAL-C`, which admits a currency sign and grouping commas. The code was right and the document was wrong, so the document moved. Narrowing the parser would have broken equivalence with `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L2201` | A published pattern that admits `$1,234.56` looks permissive beside a plain decimal. The description names the source function, so a reader can see the tolerance is deliberate |
| An unknown member in the request body is refused | Ignore unknown members; ignore them and log; accept a declared alias set | The document already publishes `additionalProperties: false`, and a body carrying a member this service ignores is a caller believing it sent something it did not. Refusing is the only answer that keeps the published contract true | A caller that padded its body now receives 400 |
| Each width constraint carries its own message naming its own field | Keep the framework's generic size text; name the field in a single shared text; report every violation at once | The framework text names a bound and not a field, so a caller with several fields could not tell which one was too wide. The source composes each message from the field label it moved, at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1215`, so a per-field text is also the source's own shape | Ten more literals to keep in step with ten column widths. Each one is asserted against its `PicClause` constant |

### Error shape and status on the query surfaces

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A page size outside its published bounds, and a page size that is not a whole number, are told apart by two texts rather than one | One text for every refusal of the parameter; report the received value; clamp silently to the nearest bound | The two are different mistakes and call for different corrections. Clamping was rejected outright: a response claiming success while serving a page size the caller did not ask for is the defect this finding was raised against, one level up | Two texts per parameter rather than one. Neither names the value received, so neither can echo a caller's input back |
| A value the binding could not convert is answered by the same handler that answers a constraint, at 400 | Let the framework answer it; answer 422; answer 400 with the framework body | A query string is text, and the page size is the one value of these routes that is not. It converts before any constraint runs and reaches no constraint at all. That is why these routes answered 500 to `pageSize=abc`: a conversion failure is a caller's mistake and 500 says it is the service's | The handler now names a framework exception type. The type is asserted in a unit test, so a framework rename fails the build rather than the route |
| The refusal text on the notification history route names the card number, not an account identifier | Keep the one shared text; name both values; name neither | The route reads a card number and holds no account identifier, so the previous text described a parameter the route does not have. A text that names the wrong parameter sends a caller to correct a value it never sent | One text for the one value the route reads |
| `route` in the card error body resolves from the handler mapping that failed rather than from a fixed literal | Keep the literal; omit the member; carry the resolved request path | A fixed literal reported `/cards` for a failure on `/cards/{cardToken}`, which is a body that misdescribes its own request. The mapping template is the honest answer and, unlike the resolved path, it carries no value the caller sent | A request matching no mapping has no template to report. That case answers the service-level text, which names no route at all |

### Mandatory-field enforcement on the account update

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| An absent required member of a present block is refused exactly as a blank one is | Fill an absent member from the stored row; treat absence as "leave unchanged"; refuse only a blank | The map area a 3270 screen sends is fixed width, so an operator who cleared a field sent spaces and the edit at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1470-L1676` read spaces. Filling from the stored row would answer 200 to a caller that dropped a field and write a value it never sent, which is a silent write | A caller that omitted a member now receives 422. The change-detection gate at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1460-L1467` still runs ahead of every edit. A request that changes nothing answers "No change detected" and reaches no edit at all. That is why omitting a member the record does not compare still answers 200 |
| The optional members stay optional and keep their stored value | Make every member required; make every member optional | Six customer members and the group identifier carry no mandatory edit in the source, and one of them carries the comment `Not mandatory to enter a phone number`. Making them required would refuse a request the source accepts | The required and optional sets have to be read from the document rather than inferred from the record. Both sets are asserted against `openapi.yaml` |

### Documented answers reconciled with the source text

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The two account read routes declare their own 404, and each publishes the identifier-bearing text its source paragraph composes | Strip the identifier from the detail to match the shared response; keep the shared response and leave the two disagreeing | The text is the verbatim concatenation `9300-GETACCTDATA-BYACCT` builds at `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L796-L806`, and equivalence with that text outranks the privacy convention the shared response follows. The caller already knows the identifier: it put it in the path | Two routes answer a 404 detail that carries a value the caller sent. The shared response's description says so explicitly and disclaims governing them, so the exception is visible in the document rather than only in the code |
| **In force.** It supersedes an earlier revision of this platform, which echoed the stored day back and is no longer delivered. The submitted expiry day is persisted, and a year, month and day that name no calendar date is refused | Ignore the submitted day as the screen does, which is what the earlier revision did; echo the stored day back and refuse a mismatch; accept the day without a calendar check | The plan requires the update to carry the expiry decomposed into year, month and day, and the document publishes the day as required with a calendar rule. Answering 200 while discarding the day is the one outcome that satisfies neither | The 3270 screen protected the day field, so this is a departure from the screen's behaviour. It is recorded as departure D3 in `business-rule-flags.md` |
| The conflict snapshot carries the stored value as stored, and the fold is used only for comparison | Return the folded value; return both; return no snapshot | The fold exists to make the comparison at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4109-L4193` case-insensitive. A snapshot is what the caller must review before resubmitting, and a folded snapshot resubmitted verbatim would rewrite the column in the wrong case | Two accessors on the same entity, one folded and one not. Their names say which is which and a unit test asserts the pair |

### Readiness under a dependency outage

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every readiness indicator that reaches a dependency catches its own failure and reports that dependency down | Let the failure escape and shape the response afterwards; drop the indicator; probe the dependency on a schedule and serve the last verdict | An indicator that throws leaves the actuator with no document to render. The request then leaves through the error path, and the poll answers a business error body instead of a health document. Catching inside the indicator is what keeps the endpoint answering its own document, and it follows the `kafkaHealth` indicator that already did this | The detail carries the root cause's class name and nothing else. A class name names no row and no credential, and `management.endpoint.health.show-details` withholds it from an anonymous caller in any case |
| No exception advice names a base package, in any of the six services | Scope each advice to its `api` package; scope only the four that were unscoped; scope them and add an actuator-path guard | Scoping was implemented across all six and reverted after measurement. Spring selects advice from the resolved handler type. A request with no matching route has no handler, so scoped advice is skipped. The framework then answers unsupported media types, unacceptable responses, and unsupported methods instead of the documented problem body. Scoping was also insufficient: the fraud advice was already scoped and its health poll still answered the framework body | An advice that names no package could shape a failure of the management port. It cannot, because no indicator lets a failure escape, and an equivalence test asserts that property over all six services rather than trusting it |

### Bounded wait on a contended write

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The lock wait is bounded transaction-locally with `set_config('lock_timeout', :ms, true)` | Set `jakarta.persistence.lock.timeout` as a query hint; use `NO WAIT`; set `lock_timeout` on the datasource; set `statement_timeout` | PostgreSQL ignores a positive JPA lock-timeout hint, so the hint would have read as a fix and changed nothing. `NO WAIT` gives up on the first contention, and would turn the ordinary concurrent write into a lock refusal. The plan verifies that write answers "Record changed by some one else" five times over. A datasource-wide or statement-wide setting would also bound a migration and the relay sweep, neither of which should give up | A bound is a number, and three seconds is a judgement. It is configurable, and a container-backed test asserts that a held row is given up on well inside it |
| A bound is passed as a bind parameter to `set_config` rather than written into a `SET LOCAL` statement | Build the statement by concatenation; hard-code the value in the query | PostgreSQL admits no placeholder in a `SET` statement, so `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = :ms` cannot be parameterised. Concatenating a configured value into SQL is the shape of an injection even when the value is a number from a validated property | One native query per repository. Each is asserted by a test that holds a real row lock |
| A refused lock answers the 409 the document already published, and records latency but neither a failure nor a validation refusal | Answer 503; answer 500; count it as an update failure | The document already published `Could not lock record for update` for this outcome. The source set that condition name whenever its `READ UPDATE` came back with anything other than a normal response. Counting it as a failure would attribute a caller's contention to the service | The metric now distinguishes three outcomes where it distinguished two. A unit test asserts which counters a refused lock touches and which it leaves alone |

### The ledger balance route's error contract

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The ledger answers every failure with the same problem document the other five services answer | Keep the framework default body; publish the framework body in the document; answer a bespoke shape | The framework body carries the resolved request path, so a caller naming an account identifier read that identifier back and copied it into its own log. It also gave one answer, 500, to a malformed value, an absent dependency and a defect. One shape across six services means one shape for a client to parse | A sixth error type to keep in step. Its members are asserted against the record's own components |
| An unreachable datastore answers 503 and the detail invites a retry; the arm names three types and no wider | Catch every `DataAccessException` and answer 503; answer 500 for all of them; answer 504 | A connection that cannot be opened, a transaction that cannot be begun and a statement that ran out of time are the datastore being away. A constraint violation or a broken query is not, and answering 503 for a broken query would invite a caller to retry something that cannot succeed | Three named types will not cover a fourth way the datastore can be away. A test asserts that a broken query answers 500 and an unreachable connection answers 503, so merging the arms fails the build |
| The ledger document gained the security scheme and the 401 and 403 answers it was already giving | Leave them undocumented; remove the security rule | The route requires an identity that holds the account, and the document published neither the requirement nor the two refusals. A document that omits an answer the service gives is a document a client cannot code against | The description now duplicates what `config/SecurityConfig` expresses. A contract test compares the published statuses against the handler's arms |

## Cycle-accumulator chain QA-remediation decisions

A runtime test pass measured six consecutive authorizations of 100.00 against an account whose credit
limit is 120.00. All six were approved, taking the account five times past its limit, and the two
billing-cycle accumulators read zero afterwards. The same pass found `GET /accounts/{id}` and
`GET /balances/{id}` reporting balances that differed by every amount ever posted. Both findings had
one cause. No service consumed `transaction.posted` into the account record, so the only copy of
`ACCTDAT` the credit-limit rule can reach was never moved by a posting.

### Where the posted amount is applied

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The account service consumes `transaction.posted` and applies the amount to its own `account` row, reproducing `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L545-L560` | Have the authorization service consume `transaction.posted` and write its own snapshot; have the ledger publish the accumulators it already holds. Have the ledger accept the value columns of the account service's `AccountStateChanged` | The account service owns the record, and the amount belongs in it whether or not any rule reads it. Applying it here fixes the balance `GET /accounts/{id}` reports and the accumulators reason code 102 reads with one change, through the `AccountStateChanged` path authorization already consumes. Authorization consuming the posted event would fix the rule and leave the account read wrong, and would make authorization derive state it does not own. The ledger publishing accumulators would put two producers on one contract and still leave the account read wrong | One more consumer group to operate, and a chain of three asynchronous hops between a posting and the snapshot the next authorization reads. Onboarding pitfall 5 documents the spacing that implies for a live demonstration |
| The listener applies the event's `amount` and never its `newBalance` | Copy `newBalance` into the account row; copy it and reconcile on divergence | A balance copied across a service boundary has two owners and no way to settle a disagreement. An amount applied to a locally held balance keeps one owner, and the processed-event marker makes the repeat harmless rather than the arithmetic having to be idempotent | The two balances can diverge if a posted event is ever lost outright rather than redelivered. The marker plus the dead-letter route make that visible instead of silent |
| The ledger continues to ignore the value columns of an `ACCOUNT_UPDATED` event on a projection row it already holds | Apply them like any other state change; apply them only when they are newer | The ledger applied the same amount itself a moment earlier. Taking the account service's copy would overwrite a correct value with one that is behind by every posting the account service has not yet heard about. The two services would then chase each other. `messaging/AccountStateChangedConsumer` in that module documents the reasoning at length | The two copies of the accumulators can differ transiently. Neither is read by a decision the other makes, so the difference has no consumer |

### The demo overlay had to be mirrored

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `account-service` gained its own `db/demo/V900__demo_expiry_extension.sql`, mirroring the authorization one, and both default to `db/migration` alone | Extend only the authorization replica, as before; drop the overlay and edit `V2__seed.sql`; have the account service omit the expiry from its event | `AccountStateChanged` carries `expirationDate` and the authorization listener applies it. The first posted transaction would therefore have written the fixture's 2025 expiry back over the extended replica, and reason code 103 would have declined every later call on that account. Extending one copy was not a partial fix but a broken one. Editing `V2__seed.sql` would move the oracle the equivalence suite measures against | Two overlays to keep in step. `AuthorizationDemoDataTest` fails the build if either module's shipped default carries the demo location or if the composition enables only one |
| Both deployment paths ship the demo overlay, and both read it from one key that names the base-profile opt-out | Remove `db/demo` from the two Deployments so Kubernetes is fixture-faithful; keep the value fixed in each Deployment; add a second overlay-free manifest set | The manifests exist so the same demonstration runs on a standard cluster, and a cluster that declined every authorization with reason 0103 would demonstrate nothing, so fixture-faithful was the wrong contract for this path. Fixing the value inside each Deployment was what made the two paths disagree in the first place: three authoritative texts said Compose alone enabled the overlay while `40-authorization-service.yaml` and `44-account-service.yaml` hard-coded it, so an operator could not tell which profile a cluster ran. `30-configmap.yaml` now declares `AUTHORIZATION_FLYWAY_LOCATIONS` and `ACCOUNT_FLYWAY_LOCATIONS` with the overlay and both Deployments read them, which is the same shape and the same two key names Compose already used. A second manifest set would double every future edit | Both paths mutate the seeded expiries, so neither is the oracle: an equivalence run sets both keys to `classpath:db/migration`. The two keys must change together, and `AuthorizationDemoDataTest` fails the build when either path enables one overlay alone or when a Deployment fixes the value instead of reading the key |
| The overlay writes the literal `2099-12-31` into every expiry it touches, in both copies | Compute a rolling date at start-up; choose a nearer year such as 2030; vary the value per account; leave the fixture expiries alone and have the demo send an older origin timestamp instead | The column holds ten characters of text and reason code 103 compares it lexically against the first ten characters of the origin timestamp at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L414-L420`, so a four-digit year sorts correctly against any timestamp a caller sends. A fixed literal is deterministic, needs no maintenance and reads as synthetic, so no reader mistakes it for a fixture value. A date computed at start-up would let two databases that ran the overlay on different days disagree about the same record. Sending an older origin timestamp instead would mean the demonstration could not send the current date, which is the case the overlay exists to make work | The literal appears in each overlay twice, in the `UPDATE` and in the assertion that follows it, so a change touches four places. Both overlays raise rather than commit when a row was missed, and `AuthorizationDemoDataTest` fails the build when one module ships the overlay without the other |

### What the new listener is held to

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A record the listener cannot apply routes to the shared `carddemo.dead-letter` topic as a governed envelope, not to a `transaction.posted.DLT` destination | Publish to a source-specific destination like the ledger and notification listeners do; publish the refused record unchanged | The notification listener already routes this topic's failures to `transaction.posted.DLT`, and a second writer of one destination makes a diagnostic ambiguous about which consumer refused the record. The shared topic carries the refusing service in the envelope, and needs no new topic and no new access-control entry | A reader of the shared topic sees envelopes from several services and has to read the field to tell them apart |
| The `@SpringBootTest` contexts of this module pin both `spring.kafka.listener.auto-startup=false` and a resolvable broker address | Pin the flag alone; run an embedded broker for every context; point the shipped default at localhost | The flag alone was not enough. The test context cache stops and restarts a cached context when it evicts around it. A restart starts every registered container whatever the flag says, so a context that had booted cleanly failed later in the same run. A resolvable address makes that restart harmless | A restarted context does attempt a connection and retries in the background. The attempt is to port 1 and logs at warning level |

## Cumulative decision state and caller entitlement

### Making an approval visible to the next decision

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Approved cycle exposure is reserved on `account_credit_snapshot` in the same local transaction as the decision row and the outbox row, in two new columns bounded by `pending_expires_at` | Sum approved rows of `authorization_decision` since the last refresh; hold the outstanding exposure in memory; accept the propagation lag as a demo limitation | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl` posts each record before it validates the next, because paragraph `2000-POST-TRANSACTION` at `:L424-L444` runs inside the read loop. `2700-UPDATE-ACCOUNT` at `:L545-L560` has already rewritten the account by the time `:L403-L405` reads it again. Two 60.00 transactions against a 100.00 limit therefore approve once and decline once. Here the account service owns those accumulators and reports them back four asynchronous hops later, so a reservation is what restores the source's own timing. It answers in constant time and is exact. | Two numeric columns and one timestamp are added to a table this service owns, and every approval now writes to that table as well as reading it. |
| Reading the account row under `PESSIMISTIC_WRITE`, with a transaction-local `lock_timeout` from `carddemo.decision.lock-wait-ms` | Optimistic version column; serializable isolation; no lock at all | The lock has to cover the figures the credit-limit rule computes from, as well as the reservation write. A lock taken after the comparison would serialize two calls that had both already decided to approve. A transaction-local bound governs one decision and never a schema migration or a relay sweep. | One account's decisions are taken one at a time, so a contended account answers more slowly and a refused lock answers 503. |
| A summed aggregate over `authorization_decision` was rejected | Add `posted_at` to the decision row and sum the unposted ones | Deciding which decisions are still outstanding would compare this service's clock against the account service's. A decision taken microseconds before the account service's own timestamp would drop out of that window with its posting still in flight. That is the same over-approval the reservation exists to stop. The aggregate also grows with a table that only grows. | The reservation has to be released explicitly, which `applyStateChange` now does. |
| The release is driven by the advance the authoritative event reports, in the same statement that applies it, and an event that lowers a credit accumulator clears the reservation outright | Release in a second statement; release the whole reservation on any event; release nothing and rely on the expiry | Releasing in a second statement leaves a window carrying the new authoritative figure and the old reservation, and every decision in that window declines against exposure counted twice. Releasing the whole reservation would drop a second approval still in flight. Only a cycle close lowers a credit accumulator, because every other source statement adds, so clearing there carries no exposure from the closed cycle into the new one. | The release arms are `CASE` expressions inside `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, which no mapping check can verify; `NativeStatementIT` reads every arm back from a real database. |
| Each reserved accumulator is stored at the `PIC S9(10)V99` width of the accumulator it mirrors, through `CobolDecimal.truncateToPictureField` | Let the sum grow and rely on the column; widen `pending_cycle_credit` and `pending_cycle_debit` past ten integer digits; refuse an approval that would overflow the reservation | `pending_cycle_credit` and `pending_cycle_debit` mirror `ACCT-CURR-CYC-CREDIT` and `ACCT-CURR-CYC-DEBIT` at `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L12-L13`, so the reservation must hold what the accumulator would hold and nothing wider. Ten integer digits admit two approvals of the widest `DALYTRAN-AMT PIC S9(09)V99` amount, and a run of maximum approvals inside one reservation lifetime needs an eleventh: relying on the column raised SQLSTATE 22003, so a valid call answered 500 and committed neither a decision nor an event, which breaks the one-call-one-event guarantee. A COBOL `ADD` with no `ON SIZE ERROR` phrase discards the digit that does not fit and continues, and reproducing that store keeps the reservation equal to the figure the account service will later report. Widening the columns would make the reservation disagree with the accumulator, and refusing the approval would invent a decline the source has no reason for. | A reservation that has truncated its high-order digit under-counts exposure, exactly as the source accumulator does at the same magnitude, so the credit-limit rule reads the same under-counted figure the source reads. That is register item 7's narrowing seen from the reservation side. |
| A reservation expires, and the expiry is refreshed on each reservation rather than swept | No expiry; a scheduled sweep that clears stale reservations | An approval whose event is dead-lettered would otherwise hold its exposure for ever, and available credit would shrink monotonically until every call declined — the failure `docs/onboarding.md` already warns about for the accumulators themselves. Writing the effective figures plus the amount retires an expired reservation on the next write, so no sweep is needed. | An approval whose posting takes longer than `carddemo.decision.reservation-ttl` releases its exposure early, and a second call inside that window could exceed the limit. |
| The source's truncation, the narrowed `WS-TEMP-BAL PIC S9(09)V99` working field and the refund sign convention are untouched | Correct the refund sign while touching this code | Every accumulation runs through `CobolDecimal` at the accumulator scale, matching the `ADD` statements at `:L549` and `:L551` that the ledger already reproduces, and the one narrowing store stays in the rule where the source has it. Equivalence is the contract; the refund defect is a human decision recorded in `docs/business-rule-flags.md`. | A refund still tightens the next authorization, exactly as the source does. |

### Who may authorize against which subject

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `POST /authorizations` gains an ownership comparison inside the decision, applied after the card and account are resolved and before a transaction identifier is allocated | A distinct acquirer role on the route; an ownership rule in the filter chain; leaving the route on role alone | Role alone let an ordinary credential authorize against every account the platform holds. A request may name an account instead of a card, and the decision path then reads that account's first card, so no card number had to be known. A filter-chain rule cannot express it, because the route names its subject in the request body and neither identifier exists until the cross-reference has been read. The route rule that runs before it is stated one section further down, under "Who may authorize a transaction". The two gates are independent: the role rule bounds who may ask, and this comparison bounds which subject the asker reaches. | The comparison runs later than every other ownership check on the platform, so `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest` has to hold its placement rather than a route rule. |
| SUPERSEDED — a distinct acquirer role was rejected here, and the later security review reversed it: see "Who may authorize a transaction" | Add `ROLE_ACQUIRER` to the route rule and to the entitlement check | The reasoning recorded at the time was that the administrator authority already means "reaches every subject" on every ownership-scoped route. A role no shipped identity holds is configuration with no reader. The security review that followed found the route decided on role alone for a subject named in the request body. It added `ROLE_ACQUIRER`, an acquirer identity that holds it, and `anyRequest().denyAll()` beneath. `config/SecurityConfig` now declares `hasAnyRole(ROLE_ACQUIRER, ROLE_ADMIN)` on the route, and the ownership comparison above stayed. | An acquirer and an administrator are no longer the same authority, so the two shipped identities have to be kept apart in the environment template and in the demo guide. |
| A refused caller receives 403 carrying the same fixed detail the filter chain writes, and no decision row and no event | 404; a fifth reject reason published as `TransactionDeclined`; a detailed 403 | A fifth reject reason would tell every consumer a transaction was declined when none was decided, and would put an account identifier the caller does not own onto a topic. A detailed 403 or a 404 would confirm the subject exists. A card that resolves no account is refused as well, and the entitlement comparison runs first, so neither answer is a card-existence oracle. | A caller cannot tell a role refusal from an ownership refusal, which is the intent. |
| `RequestCaller` carries the caller's authorities as plain text, and the vocabulary is declared in the domain | Read the security context inside the domain; pass the framework `Authentication` into the service | An access-control type in the domain layer would put authorization in two places, and `config/SecurityConfig` is the one file that declares it. `SecurityConfigTest` holds the two copies of the vocabulary in step, so a rename cannot pass the build alone. | The vocabulary exists twice, guarded by a test rather than by the compiler. |

### The recorded actor names one identity

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `authorization_decision.actor` widens from `VARCHAR(8)` to `VARCHAR(64)`, the principal is recorded whole, and `config/SecurityConfig` refuses a wider configured username at start-up | Keep the width and enforce unique eight-character usernames; hash the principal; keep shortening | The column does not hold `SEC-USR-ID PIC X(08)` at `app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy:L18`; it holds the authenticated principal of an HTTP request, which no source field bounds. Shortening made two identities agreeing in their leading characters share one recorded actor, and the shipped nine-character `monitor01` reached the column as `monitor0`. An audit row that cannot name one identity does not audit. | The audit column is wider than any source field, and a deployment configuring a name over sixty-four characters fails at start-up rather than at its first decision. |
| The unauthenticated sentinel becomes `ANONYMOUS` | Keep `ANONYMOU` | The truncated spelling existed only to fit the eight-character column, and leaving a truncation artefact behind while removing truncation would invite the same question again. | A row written by an earlier run carries the old sentinel, which no code reads. |

## How one relay pass is divided, and what a row nobody can publish leaves behind

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One pass of the authorization relay is several transactions: one committed claim, no transaction at all around the sends, and one short transaction per result | Keep the whole pass in one transaction; move the sends onto separate threads | The single transaction held a connection and every row lock of the batch for the sum of its broker waits. With a hundred rows and a ten-second wait each, that is roughly a thousand seconds. Worse, the claim was uncommitted work of that transaction, so a process that died mid-pass rolled its own claim back. `recoverStrandedClaims`, written for exactly that case, could never observe anything. Committing the claim first is what makes the recovery real. | A pass now takes several transactions where it took one, so a row can be claimed and then left claimed by a crash. That is the case the claim timeout covers, and it is observable where it previously was not. A pass that runs out of time is no longer one of those cases: it releases what it did not attempt, which [what one pass may drain](#what-one-pass-may-drain-and-what-a-pass-that-runs-out-of-time-leaves-behind) records. |
| Every send is awaited against one monotonic deadline shared by the whole pass, from `carddemo.outbox.relay.max-duration-ms` | A per-send timeout only; no bound at all, as before | A per-send timeout bounds one send and not the pass, so a batch of slow sends still runs for the batch size times the timeout. The pass deadline bounds what the scheduler thread can cost, and `await` cancels the stage it stops waiting on rather than leaving it outstanding. | A pass that runs out of budget leaves the rest of its batch to the next pass, which shows up as one relay interval of extra latency under a slow broker. |
| The producer window is aligned under that deadline: `max.block.ms` and `delivery.timeout.ms` at two seconds each against a five-second pass, asserted by a test | Leave `delivery.timeout.ms` at the shipped 120000 and rely on the caller wait | A delivery timeout twenty times the caller's wait meant the relay could stop waiting on a send the broker went on to deliver. The next pass then published a second copy of the same event, and the relay state recorded a failure that had not happened. Consumers deduplicate, so the copy was survivable — the wrong relay state was not. | A genuinely slow broker now fails a send in two seconds where it previously had two minutes, so a transient outage costs more retries. Each retry is cheap and backs off. |
| `EventPublisherPort.publish` answers `CompletionStage<Void>` instead of returning nothing | Keep the void seam and block inside the adapter; return the Kafka `SendResult` type | A seam that returns nothing has to block inside itself, for a duration it chooses, and the caller is the one component that knows how much of its pass remains. Returning the broker's own result type would put a Kafka type in the one abstraction that exists so Kafka can be replaced. | Every implementation and every test stub of the seam changed with it, which the compiler enforced rather than a reviewer. |
| An abandoned row records a durable obligation to name itself on the dead-letter topic, in the same transaction that abandons it, and the obligation clears only against a broker acknowledgement | A best-effort send at the moment of abandonment; a readiness signal alone; a scheduled reconciliation job | A dispatched-and-unawaited diagnostic makes an unreachable broker look exactly like a healthy one, and the row is terminal either way. The claim query never returns it again, so the sweep that gave up on the row was the last sweep that ever saw it. The obligation is what brings the relay back. Readiness says something is wrong and names nothing. | Two columns and a partial index exist for a case that should be rare. An owed diagnostic is retried at the head of every later pass for as long as it takes. |
| A diagnostic for a row keyed by a transaction identifier travels under the eleven-zero sentinel | Widen the `aggregateId` pattern of `schemas/dead-letter-v1.json` to admit the sixteen-character form; skip the diagnostic for such rows | `schemas/dead-letter-v1.json` accepts eleven digits, so the real key would fail the serialize gate on every attempt and the obligation could never clear — an unpublishable diagnostic retried for ever. Widening the document would let a dead letter be keyed on a partition no account owns. `config/KafkaConsumerConfig` already makes the same substitution for a record it could not read, and the row is still named exactly by `failedEventId`. | Only a row stored before `V19__unresolved_decline_names_its_account.sql` can reach this substitution, because no producer writes that key form now. Such a diagnostic lands on the sentinel partition rather than an account partition, so it is found by event identifier rather than by account. |
| The claim query of every one of the five relays returns the due head row of each account rather than every due row | Claim every due row and stop the pass at the first failure; claim every due row and continue; hold the rule in the two relays whose ordering is most visible, as an earlier revision did | Recording each outcome separately means an older row could fail while a newer row of the same account succeeded, and the retry would then reach the topic behind it — reordered for every consumer of that account, because the account identifier is the message key. Retry backoff produces the same reversal with no batch involved at all: the older row waits on `next_attempt_at` while the newer row of that account is due now. Stopping the pass at the first failure would preserve order and let one unpublishable row block the whole table. Holding the rule in the authorization and account relays alone left the ledger, fraud and card streams reorderable, and a rule that holds in two of five services is a rule a reader cannot rely on. | A burst of events on one account drained at one per pass, which is one relay interval each. A later performance review removed that cost without touching the claim: a pass repeats the claim, and each cycle takes the head that account has once the previous head is published. See [what one pass may drain](#what-one-pass-may-drain-and-what-a-pass-that-runs-out-of-time-leaves-behind). The absence check names `PENDING` and `CLAIMED` alone, so an abandoned row releases its account rather than holding every later event of it; that is asserted per relay rather than assumed. |
| `DefaultErrorHandler.setCommitRecovered(true)` is set on the replica error handler | Acknowledge inside the listener before rethrowing; change the acknowledgement mode | Both replica listeners acknowledge with `MANUAL_IMMEDIATE`, and a record that exhausts its attempts is never acknowledged, because the listener method threw. The offset therefore stayed uncommitted: the diagnostic was published, the offset did not move, and the next assignment read the same record and published a second diagnostic — indefinitely, since nothing about the record changes. Acknowledging before rethrowing would acknowledge records that had not been applied. | The offset of a record this service could not apply advances, so the record is only recoverable from the dead-letter topic. That is what the dead-letter topic is for. |
| A `406` answer carries no body, while `415` keeps one | Answer both with the same error body; answer `406` with a plain-text body | The refusal exists because no response media type could be selected, so attaching a body asks the framework to select one a second time for the same request. There is none, and the attempt turns a legible `406` into a second failure. `src/main/resources/openapi.yaml` documented a bodyless `406` before the code produced one, and the document was right. | A caller receives a status with no explanation, which is the only thing a caller accepting nothing this endpoint writes can receive. |
| `TransactionDeclined` gains contract version 3, carrying the nine descriptive values of the daily transaction record | Widen version 1 to admit the nine; relocate the reject row to the authorization service; leave the reject path unreachable | `2500-WRITE-REJECT-REC` at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L446-L465` writes `REJECT-TRAN-DATA PIC X(350)`, the whole daily record, ahead of the reason code. Versions 1 and 2 carry six payload properties, so a consumer holding either cannot render those 350 bytes. It would have to store spaces where the source stores merchant and description values. Widening version 1 would change a contract consumers already read. Relocating the row would contradict the data-ownership table of AAP 0.3.1, which assigns `rejected_transaction` to the ledger. `transaction-posted-v2` is the shipped precedent for exactly this addition, so version 3 lifts the same nine properties at the same widths, and every published reject reason travels under it | Version 2 of this event type is the account-less variant released for reason 0100 rather than a superset of version 1, so the version axis encodes two orthogonal facts for one event type. The document's own `$comment` says so, and a reader who assumes monotonic enrichment will be wrong. Version 2 is retained and unpublished, so it is a version a consumer can still receive and no producer writes |
