| The ledger consumes `transaction.declined` under its own group and publishes nothing there | Publish a second decline from `RejectRecorder` and let the version gate stop the loop; give the reject row to the authorization service | The authorization service is the sole writer of the decision under AAP 0.1.1. A second `TransactionDeclined` for one refusal would therefore put two differently shaped events for one decision on a topic this service reads. The pairing AAP 0.4.1 describes still holds: the event exists, the row exists, and the arriving event's `processed_event` marker commits in the same transaction as the row | The reject row and the decision row now live in two schemas, so diagnosing one refusal reads two services. The transaction identifier is the only join, and it is copied rather than derived at every hop |
| A decline at contract version 1 or 2 is acknowledged with no reject row written | Refuse the delivery and dead-letter it; write a row holding spaces in the nine positions | Neither version carries the nine values `REJECT-TRAN-DATA` needs, and inventing what was not sent is the one outcome equivalence forbids. Version 2 names no account at all. All 38 reject records the fixture `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` produces are reject reason `0102`, which resolves an account and travels at version 3, so the boundary costs the parity evidence nothing | A producer that regresses to version 1 would silently stop producing reject rows. The listener logs the version it saw at `INFO` on every such delivery, which is the only signal |
| The three outcomes of one declined delivery are counted apart, and a delivery carrying no detail raises neither the reject nor the duplicate counter | Report a no-detail delivery as a duplicate; add a fourth counter for it | A delivery suppressed by a marker and a delivery carrying no renderable detail are different facts. Reporting the second as a duplicate would put a number in the demo that means nothing, and the events-consumed counter already accounts for it | A no-detail delivery is visible only in the log line, not in a metric. A deployment that started producing them would see the reject count fall without a counter rising |
| The ledger relay bounds one sweep with `carddemo.outbox.relay.max-duration-ms`, and the producer window is aligned below it | Leave `join()` unbounded and rely on `delivery.timeout.ms`; bound each send separately | The sweep ran inside one transaction when this was decided, so an unbounded wait held the scheduled thread, the database connection and every row lock of the sweep for the whole `delivery.timeout.ms`, which was two minutes against a half-second schedule. A later performance review divided that sweep into a claim, the sends and one transaction per result, so the transaction is gone and the budget it justified remains. A send the sweep abandoned can still be delivered afterwards and the retry would publish a second copy, so `max.block.ms` plus `delivery.timeout.ms` has to stay below the sweep budget and the expired wait is cancelled | The producer window is now two seconds rather than two minutes, so a genuinely slow broker fails sends that would previously have succeeded. Those rows are retried with backoff, and a contract test fails the build if either value is raised alone |
| Both ledger retention deletes are bounded, ordered and drained under a finite per-table ceiling | Keep one unbounded delete; bound without draining | Both tables are on the hot write path: the relay sweeps `outbox_event` every half second and all three listeners write `processed_event`. One unbounded delete holds every expired row under one lock for a duration the configuration cannot predict. A single bounded pass leaves a permanent backlog whenever rows arrive faster than one batch an hour, so the drain repeats until a batch comes back short | A table whose backlog cannot clear inside the thirty-second ceiling keeps the remainder for the next pass, so a very large backlog takes several hours of passes to clear rather than one long statement |
| The unbounded `deletePublishedBefore(Instant)` and `deleteMarkersProcessedBefore(Instant)` are removed from the ledger repositories | Keep both as an escape hatch | An unbounded delete left on the interface is the defect itself, one call away from being reintroduced. Neither had a caller once the sweep drained in batches | A future operational need for one whole-table delete has to add it back deliberately, which is the intent |
| `LedgerProperties.Kafka.Topics` gains `accountStateChanged`, and `StreamNameReport` reports every bound topic | Leave the key unbound and report four of the six names | The key was already present in `application.yml` and bound to nothing, so a renamed or dropped variable produced no error and the listener read the built-in default silently. That is the exact defect `StreamNameReport` exists to prevent, and its own javadoc claimed one entry per bound topic while two were missing | The record now refuses a blank account-state topic at start-up, which is stricter than before. A deployment that relied on the empty value failing later now fails at start-up instead |
| Every checked-in expected-output file has one owning test that closes its rows, through a shared `ExpectedOutcomes` reader that tracks which rows were consumed | Leave the files as reference data; assert only the aggregate summary rows; let closure be shared across readers | Twelve of the fourteen files were never opened, so a value could drift from the code it described and nothing would fail. One closing owner per file makes "no row left unread" enforceable, because the consumption ledger lives on one instance; two instances cannot share one ledger | A closing owner is a wide test class. | `ExpectedOutputBindingContractTest` resolves each reader call's argument to the file that call opens, following a `String` constant through the declaring source | Require `ExpectedOutcomes.load("<file>")` verbatim; accept a file name and a reader call co-present in one source | Every binding names its file in a constant so the name can appear in its own display names, which the verbatim form forbids. Co-presence was the form first shipped, and a review found it could not fail: a class naming ten files and opening one passed, as would a constant renamed while an old name survived in a Javadoc string | An argument computed at run time resolves to nothing, so the test refuses it rather than skipping it. That forbids a data-driven reader call inside an equivalence class; the two classes that choose a file at run time do so for a presence check through `getResource`, which reads no rows |
| The reject record is rendered as a verbatim copy of the fixture's own 350 bytes plus a built 80-byte trailer, and the masked card number lives only on the stored row | Keep substituting the masked number into the rendered bytes; drop the masked representation entirely | `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L447` is `MOVE DALYTRAN-RECORD TO REJECT-TRAN-DATA`, a byte copy, and `dailytran-reject-records-model-b.csv` expects the full sixteen digits. Masking is this platform's addition and belongs on the persisted row, not in the equivalence bytes | Two representations of one card number now coexist. A dedicated test asserts they are different and that the stored row carries the masked form |
| The stale claim that the rendered amount carries plain digits is corrected rather than the overpunch removed | Remove the sign overpunch to match the prose; leave the prose as it was | The fixture carries `0000007154D` for 715.44, so the overpunch is the source's own encoding and the prose was simply wrong. The earlier test hid it by comparing absolute values | None. The corrected test compares the signed value, so a future change to either the encoding or the sign fails |
| `synthetic-boundary-cases.csv` declares one consuming test rather than four, and its `consuming_test_classes` row is corrected from four to one | Keep four consumers each filtering on `record_seq`; leave the row at four while the harness has one consumer | Consumption is tracked per reader instance, so four readers cannot prove no case was left unread. That row describes the harness rather than the COBOL, so the truthful value follows the harness | A single class now spans authorization, narrowing, rate resolution and credit-score bounds. Each section resolves through its own method, and every outcome is still derived rather than restated |
| `posting-summary.csv` gains two rows for the declined stream instead of changing the row that measures the authorized stream | Change `ledger_rejected_transaction_count` from zero to thirty-eight; leave the declined stream unmeasured | The existing row states a true fact: the authorized consumer writes no reject rows. The declined consumer writes them, so the honest change is additive. Idempotency markers are counted per consumed topic for the same reason | The summary grew by two rows. Both name the consumer that produces them, so the provenance of each number is unambiguous |
| The whole-feed comparison drives the real `TransactionDeclinedConsumer` over all thirty-eight refusals rather than only building their events | Assert the events were built and stop; assert reject rows from a hand-written fixture | The refusal path of `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L446-L465` was the one posting outcome no test exercised end to end. Driving the shipped consumer reproduces 262 posted and 38 declined through production code with no test-only state | The comparison now depends on two consumers and one database. Each consumer acknowledges on its own counter so either count stays independently measurable |
| `CobolSourceEvidence.containsStatement` reads normalised lines, and `contains` continues to read the raw file | Normalise inside `contains`; write the source's own column padding into every caller | Fixed-format COBOL puts arbitrary spacing between words, so `ADD DALYTRAN-AMT  TO ACCT-CURR-BAL` is invisible to a raw search. Callers that need the comment text still need the raw form, so both exist | Two similar helpers invite the wrong choice. Each javadoc names what it reads and why |
| The fraud velocity horizon is a validated setting the scheduled sweep drains, rather than a bounded delete no production path called | Leave `deleteWindowsStartedBefore` unreferenced; drop the method as dead code; sweep on a fixed row age written into the query | The method, its ordered `LIMIT` and its documentation all existed and nothing invoked them, so `velocity_window` grew one row per account per scoring span for the life of the schema. Its own Javadoc named two surfaces that do not exist, `carddemo.retention.velocity-retention` and `outbox/RetentionSweeper`, which is how a reader was left believing the sweep was wired. Dropping the method would have made the growth permanent; a row age written into the query would have put the safety bound out of a deployment's reach | The horizon must outlast the scoring window or the sweep deletes the bucket a live authorization is counting into. That relation crosses two sub-records, so no per-field annotation can express it and the compact constructor of `FraudProperties` refuses start-up instead |
| The velocity horizon is compared strictly against the window span, not for equality or greater-or-equal | Accept a horizon equal to the span; compare against the span plus one bucket; leave the comparison to the sweep | A window that started exactly one span ago is still the current window for an authorization arriving in the same instant, so equality is not safe. Binding is the only place both settings are visible and start-up is the only moment at which refusing costs nothing | A deployment that sets the two to the same value is refused rather than silently losing velocity counts. The failure text names both settings, so the fix needs no source reading |
| Every fraud retention delete drains in bounded batches under a per-table deadline, matching the shape the ledger service already ships | Keep one bounded delete per hourly sweep; raise the single bound; drop the bound and delete the whole horizon in one statement | One bounded pass an hour leaves the surplus behind for ever once rows arrive faster than one batch. A table then grows without limit even though every statement against it was small. An unbounded statement holds every row it removes under one lock while the relay, the listener and the scoring path all wait on the same tables | A backlog too large for one pass is carried to the next rather than held on the scheduled thread. The per-table ceiling is what stops one large table from starving the other two, which is why the three are swept in sequence with a deadline each |
| Abandoning a fraud outbox row records a durable dead-letter obligation in the same write, rather than taking one unawaited send | Publish the diagnostic without recording anything; rely on the readiness indicator and the container log; treat `ABANDONED` as sufficient evidence | `ABANDONED` says the relay stopped attempting a row, not that anyone was told. The claim query returns `PENDING` rows only, so the pass that abandoned the row was the last pass to look at it. A log line is the record that disappears with the container. This service is ADDITIVE, so a lost assessment has no batch job to re-run and no reject dataset holding what was missed | Two columns on the hot write path, and a partial index that stays the size of the backlog rather than the table. The obligation and the abandonment cannot commit separately, and only a broker acknowledgement clears it |
| The abandonment diagnostic is discharged at the head of every later pass, and an abandoned row is never marked published | Clear the obligation on the attempt; mark the row published once its diagnostic is sent; retry the diagnostic from a separate scheduled task | Clearing on the attempt would lose exactly the diagnostic the column exists to keep, since a broker outage is the case it is for. Marking the row published would claim an event reached a consumer when the whole point is that it did not, and the entity refuses that write. A separate task would need its own transaction boundary and its own failure handling for work one existing pass already has both for | A refused diagnostic is delayed rather than lost, at the cost of one indexed read of no rows on a healthy pass. A second relay instance offering the same diagnostic publishes one duplicate on a dead-letter topic instead of losing one |
| The abandoned count is a meter of its own rather than derived from the two dead-letter series | Infer abandonment from the dead-letter series; count it under the existing `failures` name with a fourth stage value | The denominators differ: `failures` counts attempts, so ten refused attempts on one row read as ten there and once here. An abandonment whose diagnostic was refused raises this and the `failed` series. The later pass that finally names it raises `published` and not this one, so comparing the two over time is what answers whether every row given up on has been named | One more series to scrape, and the reading that matters is a comparison rather than a single number |
| The two fraud integration tests that own their containers stop scheduling and listeners in an `@AfterAll`, rather than disabling scheduling in the profile | Disable scheduling for the integration profile; drive the relay by hand in those tests; accept the noise | JUnit runs a class's `@AfterAll` methods before the extension callback that stops a `@Container` field, and the Spring context is cached beyond the class. The relay therefore kept ticking every half second into a database that had gone. Disabling scheduling would have removed the thing several of those classes assert on, which is that the schedule reaches the relay at all | One shared helper in test scope and two `@AfterAll` methods. The two closed-connection stack traces logged under `Unexpected error occurred in scheduled task` on an otherwise green run are gone. That matters, because noise shaped like a failure is worse than a failure |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Every service requires a non-simple request header on every state-changing method" under [What makes a state-changing request first-party](#what-makes-a-state-changing-request-first-party)**. Cross-site request forgery was answered by the shape a request must have, not by a token | Enable token-based CSRF protection with a cookie repository; enable it with a session repository; require a bespoke custom header; leave it as it was | This chain creates no session, so there is nowhere to keep a token, and every caller of this platform is a machine that could not obtain one. A cookie repository is the pattern for a browser application reading its own cookie, and there is no browser application here. The rest of the reasoning was that a browser cannot set `Content-Type: application/json` on a cross-origin request without a preflight, and that this service answers none. The later security review kept that fact and rejected the conclusion drawn from it. A media type is a property of a body, and `POST /accounts/{accountId}/cycle-close` reads none, so the control rested on a `consumes` declaration any route could lose by changing its signature. The bespoke header this row dismissed is now required, in a filter ahead of every handler, and no route can forget it | Every state-changing caller sends `Content-Type: application/json`, including on a route that reads no body. That reads as odd until the reason is known, so the route, the interface description and the README all give it. The protection was recorded here as depending on this service never gaining a cross-origin policy. That dependency is gone with the row: the delivered filter refuses a foreign `Origin` and foreign fetch metadata whenever a request carries either, whatever the policy says |
| `POST /accounts/{accountId}/cycle-close` requires `application/json` although it reads no body | Leave the route with no media type and rely on the security check alone; require a header instead; accept a body nobody reads so the media type looks natural | The route was browser-simple: a POST with no body, no declared media type and ambient HTTP Basic credentials is exactly what a cross-origin form can send. The card service already declares `consumes = application/json` on both its state-changing routes, so this is the shipped shape of the platform rather than a new idea. Inventing a body would be a fiction the interface description would then have to document | A caller that omits the header reads 415 rather than 200, which is a caller-visible change to a route that previously accepted anything. The 415 is now in `openapi.yaml`, and the difference between it and the chain's 403 is stated. A missing header is a mistake; a form encoding is the shape of an attack |
| The browser-simple content types are refused in `SecurityConfig` as well as by each route's `consumes` | Rely on each controller's `consumes` alone; write a servlet filter; check the header inside each controller method | A per-route declaration is one annotation away from being forgotten, and the next state-changing route added to this service would be unprotected with nothing failing. The refusal sits ahead of every route rule, so coverage is the default. It belongs in `SecurityConfig` because a contract test holds every mention of a Spring Security type to that one file, and because a reviewer looking for the control looks there | Two answers now exist for a wrongly typed request, 403 from the chain and 415 from the route. That is deliberate and documented, but it does mean a caller reading only the status has to know which it hit |
| Both account retention deletes became bounded and drained in one change, rather than bounded first | Bound the statements and keep the single pass; raise a single unbounded delete's frequency; leave both unbounded | Both statements were genuinely unbounded here, unlike the fraud service where the bound already existed. Bounding alone would have replaced one unpredictable stall with a permanent backlog above one batch an hour, which is a different defect rather than a smaller one. The shape is the ledger's, already shipped and already covered by a cross-service contract test | One more parameter on two repository methods, and the two native queries now name their key columns explicitly. `processed_event` is keyed by the event identifier and the topic together since V6, so a delete matching on one of them would remove a marker another delivery still needs |
| The account README's claim that the authorization service reads its routes synchronously is corrected rather than the code changed | Reintroduce a synchronous read so the README becomes true; leave the README as it was | The authorization service holds no HTTP client at all and answers from an `account_credit_snapshot` projection kept current by this service's events. `docs/architecture-before-after.md` already states that no service calls another synchronously, so the README was the only document disagreeing with the code. Adding a synchronous read to make a sentence true would put two more services in the blast radius of every authorization | None to the code. The README also claimed this module registers no Kafka listener and holds consumer rights on no topic, both false since `TransactionPostedConsumer` shipped, and claimed five migrations where six run |
| `NotificationLogRepository.deleteRenderedBefore` becomes a bounded ordered delete, and all three notification sweeps drain under a finite per-table ceiling | Bound the statement and keep one pass an hour; leave it unbounded and raise the sweep frequency; page the rows into memory and delete them by identifier | The log grows once per rendered alert. It was the one notification delete still matching every expired row in a single statement, so a long-idle deployment could stall the whole sweep on its first table. The marker and read-model deletes were already bounded, so what was missing was the repetition rather than the bound. The shape is the ledger's, already shipped in four services, and `ORDER BY rendered_at` is what makes successive batches converge instead of revisiting the same rows | A pass that reaches its thirty-second ceiling leaves a remainder for the next hour, so the table shrinks in steps rather than at once. The ordering reads an index the table already carried, and nothing else reads `notification_log`, so a bounded delete cannot starve a query |
| The notification sweep keeps the name `sweepExpiredRows` and the key `carddemo.history.sweep-interval-ms` rather than being renamed onto the shape the other five services share | Rename the method to `purgeExpiredRows` and the key to `carddemo.retention.sweep-interval-ms` so one contract test covers all six; add notification to that test's service list under its present names | The key is already published in `.env.example`, `docker-compose.yml` and the configuration map as `RETENTION_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS`. This module groups its settings under `carddemo.history`, because what it retains is history rather than an outbox. Renaming a published deployment key to satisfy a test lets the test dictate the deployment surface. `RetentionSweepContractTest` names five services deliberately, and this one is absent for that reason | A reader comparing the six services finds one whose scheduled method and property prefix differ, and no cross-service contract test holds this one to the shared shape. The drain itself is identical, so the divergence is naming only |
| The schema and entity comments that named things which do not exist are corrected, and the drift is recorded where it happened rather than quietly overwritten | Delete the wrong sentences outright; leave them and change the code they describe; move every retention statement out of the migration into one document | `V1__schema.sql` named a `purge_op` of `purgeAttemptedBefore` that no repository declares, and claimed twenty-four months against a configured four hundred days. Four javadoc lines plus one entity column row named a `card_number` column on a table that holds `masked_card_number`. A comment naming a method that does not exist is worse than no comment, because a reader greps for it and concludes the sweep is missing. Naming the configuration key as the authority, and recording that this comment did drift, is what stops the next drift being silent | The migration comment now points at a configuration key rather than restating its value, so a reader wanting the number opens `application.yml`. The constraint `ck_notification_log_card_number` keeps its name, because renaming it would cost a migration for no behavioural gain, so one legacy identifier still reads as though a raw column exists |
| The notification README's runtime statements are regenerated from the registered instrumentation rather than edited only where they read wrongly | Correct the three meters the table already named and stop there; drop the table and point at the actuator endpoint; generate the table at build time | The table named three meters where `ObservabilityConfig` registers six. The two it omitted are the pair a demo needs: the failure counter and the dead-letter counter that carry the same `failure.kind` tag values. A partial list is read as a complete one. Reading each name, tag and description from the class that registers them is the only way the document cannot disagree with the process. It caught a value written as `schema-validation` where the registered literal is `schema_validation` | The table is hand-written, so a seventh meter added without touching the README puts the document back where it started. Two service READMEs still name none of their meters, carried to the pass that owns the remaining documentation findings rather than fixed here |
| A test configuration nested in a test class is named on that class rather than left for Spring to detect | Move each configuration to a top-level class in the same package; leave the four warnings until the framework line actually moves; silence them through logging configuration | Spring Framework 7.0 detects a configuration nested in a test's hierarchy, ignores it, and warns that 7.1 will stop ignoring it. In the account relay test that change is not cosmetic. A nested group had declared a subclass of the enclosing configuration purely because a group resolves the classes declared inside itself. Under 7.1 both would contribute one bean name twice. Naming the class outright removes the detection entirely, and three sibling tests here already do it that way, so this is the shipped idiom rather than a new one | `@ContextConfiguration` on a class annotated `@DataJpaTest` changes the context cache key, so those three repository tests build a context of their own rather than sharing whatever a bare slice had cached. The subclass workaround is deleted, so the nested group now depends on the enclosing class's annotation reaching it, and its one assertion passing is what proves it does |
| A contended card update answers 409 for a lock refusal or a statement timeout only, and every other database fault answers 500 | Keep the wide catch and accept that a broken grant reads as a conflict; inspect the SQL state inside the existing catch; map the wider family to 503 | `applyUpdate` grouped `JpaSystemException` with `PessimisticLockingFailureException`, and `JpaSystemException` is where the persistence layer puts every Hibernate error it cannot translate: a revoked privilege, a full disk, a lost connection. All of them answered `Could not lock record for update` at 409, which invites a retry that cannot repair any of them. All of them moved `carddemo.card.update.conflicts`, so a broken grant read as ordinary contention. `CardRepositoryIT.aBoundedWaitGivesUpOnAHeldCardLock` already proves real contention arrives as `CannotAcquireLockException`, which is a `PessimisticLockingFailureException`, so narrowing the catch keeps the outcome the source documents at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl` | A fault this deployment has not met, and that the persistence layer classifies as neither a lock nor a timeout, now answers 500 where it answered 409. That is the intended change and it is still a change a caller can see. One shipped test had encoded the old grouping and asserted a permission error was a conflict. It was rewritten onto two real lock outcomes, and a second test now proves such a fault escapes and moves no conflict count |
| The applied-update counter and its log line are taken after the transaction commits, through a transaction synchronization | Count in the controller after the service returns; wrap the update in an explicit `TransactionTemplate` and count after `execute`; publish an application event and count on its listener; accept the drift | The counter sat at the foot of a `@Transactional` method, so it ran inside the transaction and before the proxy committed. A constraint violation or a connection loss at commit therefore left `carddemo.card.update.applied` counting an update that never happened. Beside it sat a log line claiming an event was produced when the outbox row had rolled back with it. `afterCommit` runs on the successful path only and needs no restructuring of the method or its transaction boundary. No service on this platform carried an after-commit hook before this one, so the pattern is established here rather than copied | `afterCommit` runs outside the transaction, so a failure inside the callback can roll nothing back. It is one counter increment and one log line, which is why that is acceptable here and would not be for a database write. The method also runs in unit tests with no transaction bound, so it counts in place when no synchronization is active. A reader has to know that fallback exists to read the two telemetry tests |
| Card retention drains its bounded batches under a finite per-table ceiling instead of removing one batch per run | Raise the single-batch ceiling until a plausible backlog fits inside it; drop the ceiling and delete without a limit; shorten the sweep interval so the batches come round faster | Both deletes were already bounded and already ordered, so each run removed at most a thousand rows and stopped. On an hourly schedule a backlog built up while the broker was unreachable shrank by one batch an hour, and could grow faster than it cleared. That is unbounded storage growth reached through a bounded statement. Repeating a batch until it comes back short clears the backlog in one run, and a thirty-second ceiling for each table stops one sweep holding a connection indefinitely. Four sibling services already ship this exact shape, so the card service now matches them rather than inventing a sixth variant | An arrival rate sustained above the drain rate still leaves rows behind, and a large backlog needs several runs. That is deliberate: a sweep that never yields is worse than one that finishes late. The batch callback is held in a local, so the source carries exactly one `transactionTemplate.execute`, which a contract test counts. A later edit that inlines it fails the build for a reason that reads as stylistic until the test message is read |
| One send resolves inside the sweep that issued it. The producer window, the publish bound and the whole-pass deadline are set as one arithmetic relationship, and the relay watches a send on a `CompletionStage` it can stop watching | Raise the relay's wait to the producer's hundred and twenty seconds; keep the wait and accept the mismatch. Cancel the underlying send rather than the observation; hold the pass open until every send answers | The publisher blocked for ten seconds on a `get` while `delivery.timeout.ms` gave the producer a hundred and twenty. A slow broker therefore made the relay abandon a send that was still live, and the row was swept again, a duplicate the relay itself created. Aligning the windows so the broker gives up first is what removes that duplicate, because dropping an observation cannot recall a record already on the wire. Handing back a stage rather than blocking lets one deadline bound a whole pass instead of one bound per row, which is the shape the other four relay-owning services already carry | A degraded broker now fails a send in two seconds that would previously have succeeded in thirty, so a row is retried more often while the broker is slow. A property test asserts the relationship, and the comment above the producer block states it in full |
| Card integration tests stand scheduled work down where they assert nothing about it, and stop the scheduler before container teardown where they do | Stand the relay down everywhere and drive every sweep by hand; raise the Awaitility windows; treat the teardown errors as noise; give every container-owning class the teardown guard | Three of the eight container-owning classes left the relay at its five-hundred-millisecond default. Two of the three assert nothing about the schedule, so pushing the interval beyond the run is the smaller change. The third waits for the scheduled sweep to put a record on the topic, and standing it down timed out twelve of its assertions. It keeps its tick instead, and stops the scheduler and the listeners before teardown, using the helper the fraud service already ships | The property remedy is invisible to whoever adds a ninth container-owning class, so a new test could reintroduce the race in silence. A contract test now walks the test tree and demands one remedy or the other, and it was proven to fail by restoring the old interval before being kept. | The dead-letter topic of the declined stream is created, and a test now derives each deployment path's creation list from its own access control entries | Create the topic and rely on review to keep the two lists together; let the broker create a topic on demand. The platform guide counted nine listeners and listed nine of eleven. The environment template said one command generates seventeen credentials, where its loop generates fourteen and three hashes are generated elsewhere. Both provisioning files counted eleven topics and named four dead-letter topics while creating thirteen and five, which is what the inventory then held. It holds fourteen and six today, after the ledger took a third listener on the declines. A count that is wrong in one direction is read as complete in both, and each of these was written when it was true | Every one of these statements is hand-written, so the next listener added without a documentation edit puts the files back where they started. | Every stream and group name a service binds is reported at start-up, including the ones two services were leaving out | Report only the names a listener reads; leave the diagnostic as it was and rely on the broker refusing an unknown group; log the whole bound properties record | `StreamNameReport` exists because every topic and group name carries an in-image default, so a dropped or mistyped key in a ConfigMap produces no error at all. Its own javadoc said the module registers three listeners. The fraud service omitted `TOPIC_DEAD_LETTER_SUFFIX`, which decides a dead-letter destination. Both now report every name their properties record binds, which is the convention the ledger service already follows | The diagnostic is longer, ten lines for the notification service, and a reader has to know that the suffix is composed with a consumed topic rather than being addressable itself. | Dead code is removed rather than annotated: three unused public constants, two factory aliases, one unused private helper and forty-six unused imports | Leave them and rely on a linter to report them; deprecate rather than delete; delete only the members the review named | Each removal is measured rather than judged. A composite action keeps both installs in the repository and pinned by the commit. A third-party action would add a supply-chain dependency to a build whose whole point is that it verifies what it downloads. Both versions are declared as required inputs rather than read from the workflow environment, so the action states its own contract | A local action is not pinned to a tag, so the versioned-action rule had to admit the path form. | The two contradictory expiry-day decisions are reduced to the one the code implements, and the update route is named as it ships | Keep both rows and date them; keep the earlier row and change the code back to it; delete both and describe only the behaviour | Two rows decided opposite things. One said the expiry day is carried through from the stored row and never taken from the caller. The other said the submitted day is persisted and an impossible calendar date refused. The code implements the second, and `domain/CardUpdateService` explains why at length. A request body has no protected field, so reading the stored day would leave the submitted day in no comparison and in no column. A caller changing the day alone would read the no-change text for ever. A log holding both is worse than a log holding neither, because a reader cannot tell which is current. The surviving row already listed the earlier decision among its alternatives, so deleting the superseded row loses no history. Both rows also named `PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, which the update no longer declares. `api/CardController` maps the write with `@PutMapping(path = CARD_PATH)` under the class-level `/cards`, and `CARD_PATH` names the card token, decided under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line) | The reversal is now visible only in the surviving row's own wording, not as two dated entries. The card service guide names the same route, so the guide, this row and the mapping agree |
| Each service guide names every meter its service registers, and a test holds it there in both directions | Name the meters the review found missing; drop the tables and point at the actuator endpoint; generate each table at build time | Four guides named every meter they register and two named none, while registering fourteen and eight. A metric table is read as a complete list, because nothing about it says otherwise. A reader looking for the counter that says an outbox row was abandoned, and finding no such row, concludes the service does not count it. `MetricDocumentationContractTest` reads every `carddemo.<service>.<name>` literal out of a service's main sources and requires each in that service's guide. It requires the reverse too, because a documented meter an operator cannot find is the same defect backwards. A service's own configuration keys share that prefix and are read out of its `application.yml` and excluded, rather than being listed in the test where the list would go stale | The tables stay hand-written, so the test reports a divergence rather than preventing one. It matches the service segment exactly, so a meter named under a prefix no service owns would go unchecked |
| Two findings whose premises did not reproduce are recorded as measured rather than acted on | Change the numbers to the values the review reported; treat the absence as evidence the review was wrong; say nothing | The review reported that the equivalence guide claimed 266 Surefire tests where the logs showed 255. Measured against the state the review examined, the guide and the logs agreed at 266 and 214, so nothing was corrected on that basis. The counts have since moved for a real reason, which is that this pass and the one before it added tests. The review also recorded a `sun.misc.Unsafe::invokeCleaner` warning from the Kafka client, and a sweep of the current verify log finds no occurrence of `sun.misc`, `invokeCleaner`, `restricted method` or `terminally deprecated`. The Byte Buddy warning it recorded beside that one does reproduce, thirteen times. Writing a number to match a report rather than a measurement is how a document stops being evidence | A premise that did not reproduce here may reproduce on another machine or another Java release, so neither is closed. Both are carried in `suggested-next-tasks.md` with the sweep that would confirm them |
| The account service's producer window is aligned with its relay pass budget, so all five relay-owning services now hold one send inside the pass that issued it | Leave it as the one unaligned service and keep the follow-up task; raise its pass budget to 125 seconds instead; remove its pass budget so nothing is abandoned | The account service already bounded a whole pass at five seconds and cancelled its watch when that lapsed, while its producer was allowed 120 seconds to deliver. A send abandoned at five seconds could therefore land afterwards, and the next pass published the same event again. That is the defect the review raised against three other services, in a fourth it did not name, and it was already there before this work. Raising the pass budget instead would let one unanswering broker hold the scheduled thread for two minutes, which is what the budget exists to prevent | The account service now abandons a send after two seconds where it previously waited thirty, so a degraded broker causes more retries. Delivery stays at least once, and every consumer records processed event identifiers. `AccountPropertiesTest` now asserts both inequalities, as the other four services do, and the follow-up task that asked for this decision is removed rather than left standing |

## Interface-review remediation decisions

These cover the notification history route, the account surface and the card surface. The review that
prompted them found four faults in the notification route. It was keyed on a value no requirement
names, and its body was wider than the required shape. Its page size truncated a per-card total, and it
described a caching header no response carried.

On the account surface it found six. A read schema
narrower than the rows it returns, required lists shorter than the bodies written against them, and
request schemas the reader did not enforce. An identifier promised as nine digits and checked as nine
characters, and a complete Social Security Number in two published examples. Prose about the error body
that two of its own answers contradict.

On the card surface it found six as well. Two required routes
absent with body-driven substitutes in their place, a retryable write answered as a defect, and a named
filter accepted and then dropped. An expiry range the schema did not hold, a read that refused a card
over a relationship the source never tests, and eight complete seeded card numbers in published
examples.

### The notification history route

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line). The route carried the card number and the read model stayed keyed on the token derived from it | Key the route on the card token, as before; key the read model on the card number too; publish both and let a caller choose | The path value was read as `TRNX-CARD-NUM PIC X(16)` at `app/cpy/COSTM01.CPY:L22`, the first part of the key `KEYS(32 0)` at `app/jcl/CREASTMT.JCL:L30` declares. `PanMasker.cardToken` resolved it to the stored key on every request, so that no card number was stored, logged or returned. Storing the card number instead would have put a full Primary Account Number in a key column, a primary-key index and every seed file. That half of the reasoning still holds | The delivered route carries the token the read model is keyed on and derives nothing, so the request line holds no card number either. The read model is unchanged |
| SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line). The ownership rule derived the token from the path value before it looked for an authority | Grant on a `SCOPE_CARD_` authority naming a card number; add a second authority kind for a card number; move the check into the handler | A configured authority names a card token, which `.env.example` already ships and which no card number can be recovered from. Deriving inside the rule kept the authority list free of card numbers and kept the check in the filter chain, ahead of every handler, where no handler can forget it | The path value is now the token itself, so both ownership managers compare it against the caller's `SCOPE_CARD_` authorities directly and derive nothing. The check is still in the filter chain and the authority list still names no card number. A missing deployment key no longer reaches the rule at all |
| The response covered the whole history of one card and declared no paging parameter, until a performance review bounded the page | Keep `pageSize` and add a cursor and a next-page flag; keep `pageSize` and refuse a value above its maximum; keep the parameter and total every row anyway | `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL` reads every row of one card between two key breaks and `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L429` totals all of them, so a count and a total over part of a history describe a statement the source never produced. A bounded page also left older rows unreachable, since the route offered neither cursor nor offset. `carddemo.history.default-page-size` and `carddemo.history.maximum-page-size` were retired with the parameter, along with the two environment variables behind them: configuration nothing applies documents a bound that is not there. **The first alternative in this row is what the platform now ships**, because the reasoning above was answered rather than overturned: the count and the total still cover the whole card while the entries are paged. A later review replaced the aggregate that read them with a maintained row, and neither figure changed. See [what a history response reads](#what-a-history-response-reads-and-what-one-posted-event-writes) and [what a page of history costs](#what-a-page-of-history-costs-and-what-keeps-a-stored-total-honest) | One response grew with a card's history for as long as this held, which is the finding the review raised. The retention sweep bounds the table, `NotificationRenderer.MAXIMUM_STATEMENT_ROWS` still bounds one rendered alert, and a stray `?pageSize=` was neither read nor refused while the route declared no query parameter |
| The body names the card once, on the envelope, and carries four members with twelve per entry | Keep the card token on the envelope; keep the masked number on every entry; keep both and mark them optional | The required shape is four envelope members and twelve entry members. The card token is a storage key: it discloses no card number but names one card for as long as its key stands, so a log line carrying it lets a reader follow that card across every request that touched it. Repeating the masked number on every entry restated one value per row of a history that is already keyed by that card | The route now names the card by its token, so a caller already holds the value the body withholds and loses nothing by the omission. The masked number in the envelope is read from the first row rather than derived from the path, which is why a token holding no row is answered 404 |
| The successful response documents the four caching directives Spring Security writes, rather than `no-store` alone | Set `Cache-Control: no-store` on the response and leave the document; document `no-store` and accept the mismatch | `headers().cacheControl()` installs `CacheControlHeadersWriter`, which writes `no-cache, no-store, max-age=0, must-revalidate` together with `Pragma` and `Expires`. Measured against that writer directly: a response with no header of its own receives all four directives, and one that sets `no-store` first keeps it, which is why the 401 and 403 the chain writes by hand still document `no-store`. The document moved because the code was right | A reader of the document sees a longer value than the intent behind it. The description names the writer, so the four directives read as one policy rather than four decisions |

### The account and customer surface

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The read schema bounds `ficoCreditScore` at 0 and 999, and the source range 300 through 850 stays on the update path alone | Keep 300 and 850 on the read schema; drop the bounds from the read schema entirely; clamp a stored score on the way out | `88 FICO-RANGE-IS-VALID VALUES 300 THROUGH 850` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L848-L849` is reached from `1275-EDIT-FICO-SCORE`, which only `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` performs. `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L505-L506` moves a stored score to the screen and tests nothing. Columns 330 to 332 of `app/data/ASCII/custdata.txt` hold scores from 1 to 793, with 21 of the 50 rows below 300. A read schema bounded at 300 declared 21 seeded rows unrepresentable, and its own description said so. Zero and 999 are the bounds `PIC 9(03)` holds, so the schema is narrower than nothing the column can carry | A caller reading the schema alone no longer learns the update range from the read property. `CustomerDataRequest.ficoCreditScore` carries it, and the read property names where |
| Every property of a response schema is required, and `AccountUpdateResponse.account` is required and admits `null` | Leave the four always-serialized properties optional; omit `null` from the wire with `NON_NULL` inclusion; reference `AccountView` alone and let the null go undeclared | All eighteen columns of table `customer` are `NOT NULL`, `AccountRecordMapper.viewOf` reads each of the sixteen the projection carries, and a record serializes every component. A property left out of `required` therefore told a caller it might be absent when it never is. `AccountController.answerOf` passes `null` for the account when the second read finds no row, and Jackson writes that member either way. A bare `$ref` therefore declared a body this service can write invalid. `oneOf` with `type: 'null'` is what JSON Schema 2020-12 defines and what `openapi: 3.1.0` admits; `nullable` is a keyword of an earlier version and appears in no document of this platform | A caller must handle a null account on a 200. The property description says when it happens and what to do about it, and `OpenApiExampleValidationTest` holds the schema to both halves |
| The account service gets the same request-reader strictness the authorization service has | Leave the reader at its defaults and rely on the schemas being documentation; add `@JsonIgnoreProperties` to each record; validate each body against its schema at runtime | `additionalProperties: false` on all three request schemas and `type: string` on every property of them were promises the reader kept neither of. A body naming `accountId` bound and was ignored, although the path is the one place that identifier is named. A misspelled `groupId` was dropped and the column kept its stored value with nothing said. A JSON number in a monetary property became the text of that number, through a binary floating-point type, in a platform whose correctness rests on fixed-point arithmetic with truncation pinned | A caller that had been sending an extra member now reads 400. That is the point. Neither setting is a second validator: both act before binding completes, so `AccountUpdateService.editMapInputs` remains the one validator and one message still travels |
| The nine-digit rule for a submitted `customerId` is enforced in the controller, and the record carries the matching bean constraint | Add the bean constraint alone, as the review suggested; add `@Valid` to `AccountUpdateRequest.customerData` so the constraint runs; check the width inside `AccountUpdateService` | `AccountUpdateRequest` declares no cascade marker, and that absence is the contract: `WS-RETURN-MSG PIC X(75)` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L479` holds one text, so one ordered validator runs and stops at the first failure. A cascade would run a second, unordered pass, which `DtoValidationWiringTest.theRequestDeclaresNoCascade` exists to prevent. The bean constraint alone would therefore have documented the rule and enforced nothing. The controller checks it beside the existing 'must be supplied' gate and before any row is read, mirroring `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1429`. That paragraph performs the search-key edit at `:L1434-L1435` and leaves at `:L1446` before a field edit runs | The rule now lives in two places: the record declares it for a caller validating the record directly, and the controller applies it. Both read one pair of constants, `CustomerDataRequest.CUSTOMER_ID_PATTERN` and `CUSTOMER_ID_MESSAGE`, which `CustomerController` also uses for its path variable. The refusal text is ADDITIVE: `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1222` calls the field 'actually not editable' and the source emits no text for it |
| Both request examples publish zero placeholders for the three Social Security parts and the government identifier | Publish the three parts of the seeded row; publish a different synthetic number; drop the parts from the examples | The parts were `111`, `11` and `1111`, which recombine into a complete Social Security Number in a document meant for a customer-facing reader. The government identifier read as a serial number. Neither was the seeded value, so the summary claim that every value came from the seeded row was already untrue. Every value the source edits accept is a full nine-digit number. `1245-EDIT-NUM-REQD` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L2109` refuses a blank, a non-numeric value and a zero, so no passing value can be visibly a placeholder. A zero placeholder of the declared width is the pattern the authorization request examples already use for a card number | The examples as printed answer 422 with the detail `SSN: First 3 chars must not be zero.` Each summary says so. A caller substitutes the customer's own parts, which is what a request example is for |
| The `ApiProblem` prose distinguishes the fixed details from the two read-miss details | Leave the prose and accept the inaccuracy; stop echoing the identifier so the prose becomes true; move the identifier into an extension member | Both read misses reproduce a source text built around the identifier the caller put in the path: `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L796-L806` for an account and `:L846-L856` for a customer. Removing the identifier would drop a verbatim source text this platform exists to reproduce, and the 404 responses already documented it. The prose was the only thing wrong, so the prose moved. Every other detail is one of the fixed constants, and nothing carries the request path, the query string, a header or a submitted body value | A reader has to read one more sentence to learn which details name an identifier. The identifier in question is the path segment the caller sent, so nothing reaches it that it did not already hold |
| The account document declares 406 and 415, and the `title` enum admits the two reason phrases the protocol arm writes | Leave 405 as the one documented protocol refusal; give the protocol arm its own titles as constants; drop the enum | `AccountApiExceptionHandler.onUnsupportedRequest` answers a media type this service cannot write with 406, and a body it cannot read with 415. It reads the title from the status the framework named rather than from a constant. The account document was the one of six that declared neither status. Its published 405 example carried a title the enum did not admit, so the example was invalid against its own schema. The enum now reads the two phrases from `HttpStatus` in the test, so a title this document publishes cannot drift from the one the framework supplies | The last two enum values follow reason-phrase capitalization and not the house style of the first seven, which the description states. Status 406 carries no body, so it is the one status of this document whose response declares no content, and `OpenApiContractTest.everyFailureAnswersOneProblemShape` exempts it by name |

### Determinism of the card-verification-value scans

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A scan for a three-digit card verification value reads the members a record supplied, never the members a run generates | Pick a three-digit value no identifier can hold; scan the whole envelope and accept an occasional failure; widen the sentinel past three digits | `eventId` is a random universally unique identifier and `occurredAt` is the moment of the run, and a three-character run appears inside one of them roughly once in a hundred renderings. No three-digit value is free of that collision, and a wider sentinel is no longer the width `CARD-CVV-CD PIC 9(03)` at `app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L7` declares. `card-service/OutboxRelayDeadLetterTest` already dropped those members before scanning; `authorization-service/DeadLetterSanitizationTest`, `account-service/KafkaConsumerConfigTest` and `card-service/CardEventPublicationTest` now do the same | A leak that reached only a generated member would go unreported. Neither member is written from a record: one is minted per envelope and one is read from the clock, and each scan asserts the trimmed document still carries members |

### Which status answers a method a route does not serve

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Both the `405` and the `403` description state which of the two answers a method a route does not serve | Document 405 alone, as the first pass did; drop 405 from the documents; add a rule per unruled method so the dispatcher answers 405 | Every chain ends in `denyAll()` and declares one rule per method and path. A request whose method carries no rule is refused with 403 in the filter chain and never reaches the handler. Measured against all six running services: a write method on a read-only route answers 403 with the problem document. A document that declared 405 alone promised an answer a deployed caller does not receive, and dropping 405 would have hidden the answer a request the chain admits does receive | A reader has to read two statuses to learn one behaviour. `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest.everyDocumentStatesWhichStatusAnswersAWrongMethod` holds all six documents to both halves, so neither can drift from the chain |

### The card surface

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line). The card number was a path variable of the read and the update, and the substitute routes are withdrawn | Keep `POST /cards/detail` and `PUT /cards` and add the path routes beside them; keep the substitutes and document them as the contract. Keep the body and accept a path variable that shadows it | The required routes are `GET /cards/{cardNumber}` and `PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, and each reproduces one transaction: `CCDL` at `app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD:L347-L348` and `CCUP` at `:L367-L369`. Each addresses exactly one card, so the identifier belongs to the resource and not to a body. Keeping both shapes would have published two ways to reach one row, and the two disagreed about which member names the card | Held while a number stood in those paths, and no longer. The withdrawal of the substitute routes still stands. The identifier the two paths carry is now the card token, so no access log, reverse proxy log, distributed trace or browser history receives a number from this service |
| The read keys on the card number alone and tests no relationship to an account | Keep the account cross-check and refuse a mismatch; keep the account as an optional filter; move the check into the read query | `app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L740` moves the card number into the read key and the account move above it at `:L739` is commented out, so the source reads by card and tests nothing else. Refusing a card because it belongs to another account is a rule the source does not hold, and AAP item I9 forbids adding one. Entitlement is a separate concern and stays in the filter chain, where `ownsCardNumberPathVariable` derives the card token before any handler runs | A caller holding the card scope of one card reads that card without naming its account. That is what the source does, and the filter chain still refuses a card the caller holds no scope for |
| A write that failed after the lock, and a card store that cannot be reached, both answer 503 | Keep 500 for both; answer 409 for the failed write; answer 500 and set `Retry-After` | `Update of record failed` at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L210` is set at `:L1491`, on the path where the lock was taken and the rewrite did not complete. That is a condition a second attempt can clear, and 500 tells a caller the opposite. The three data-access types the new arm names are the same three the ledger service answers 503 for, so one platform reads one way | A caller that retried on 500 now reads a different status for the same condition. The document declares 503 on every operation of this service and names the writer of each |
| Each update status references its own outcome schema, and every member of each is required | Keep one schema for the four statuses; keep one schema and drop the members from `required`; omit null members from the wire | One schema admitting every outcome told a caller that a 200 might carry `CONFLICT`, and that `refreshedCard` might arrive on a success. The four schemas name the outcomes their status carries, type the members that are always null as `type: 'null'`, and require all three, because a record serializes every component. A 503 on this operation carries the error body the handler writes, not an outcome | Four schemas replace one, so a reader follows four references. Each is named for its status and `OpenApiExampleValidationTest` holds every one to its status |
| The card service reads a request body strictly | Leave the reader at its defaults; annotate each record with `@JsonIgnoreProperties`; validate each body against its schema at runtime | `additionalProperties: false` and `type: string` on every property were promises the reader kept neither of. A body naming `cardNumber` bound to nothing and was ignored, although the path is the one place that identifier is named. A body naming `cardVerificationValue` was absorbed silently. An `expiryMonth` of `07` arriving as the JSON number `7` became the text `7`. The source edit at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L95` reads that as a different value than the two characters the map transmits | A caller that had been sending an extra member now reads 400. Neither setting is a second validator: both act before binding completes, so the ordered edits stay the one validator and one message still travels |
| The list route refuses an `accountId` of eleven zeros at its boundary, and the query service keeps reading it as absent | Refuse it inside `CardQueryService`; accept it and return an unfiltered page, as before; treat it as a filter and return no rows | `1230-EDIT-ACCOUNT` at `app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L739-L760` treats a zero account as not supplied, which is the behaviour the query service reproduces for a direct call. A route that names a required filter and then serves every card is a different matter: the caller asked for one account and read all of them. `CardController#ACCOUNT_ID_PRESENT_PATTERN` refuses the value with the source text at `:L745`, so the boundary refuses and the domain stays source-faithful | The same value means two things at two depths, which the javadoc of both states. No account of the seeded data is zero, and no route reaches the query service without passing the boundary |
| An explicitly empty `direction` or `pageSize` is refused, and a default applies only when the parameter is absent | Keep `defaultValue` on both; refuse an empty value in the filter chain; treat an empty value as the absent case deliberately | `@RequestParam(defaultValue = ...)` applies its default to an empty string as well as to an absent parameter, so `?direction=` browsed forward and `?pageSize=` served seven. A caller that sent an empty value asked for something and was served something else. Both parameters now bind as text with no default, and three cases stay apart: absent, empty, and a value | A caller that had been sending an empty parameter now reads 400. The document states the refusal for both parameters, and the two texts name what a value has to be |
| **SUPERSEDED by "No published contract carries a card number in any form" under [Which card number a guide and a published contract may carry](#which-card-number-a-guide-and-a-published-contract-may-carry). The card routes were later keyed by card token, so no example of this document names a card number at all. It read:** Every published example carries one sixteen-digit placeholder for a card number | Publish the seeded numbers, as before; publish a masked number; drop the examples | Eight examples carried complete numbers from `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt`, in a document meant for a customer-facing reader. A masked value cannot be sent, because the read and the update both key on the full sixteen digits. The placeholder holds the declared width and satisfies the declared pattern, which is the pattern the authorization request examples already follow | The placeholder names no seeded row, so a caller substitutes a card of its own before sending the example. `OpenApiExampleValidationTest` scans every rendered example for a sixteen-digit run and admits only the placeholder |
| `CardList.nextCursor` is required and admits `null` | Leave it out of `required`; omit the null from the wire; report the last page with an empty string | The record serializes the component either way, so a page with no successor carries `nextCursor: null`. Leaving the property out of `required` told a caller it might be absent, which no response of this route makes true. `oneOf` with `type: 'null'` is what JSON Schema 2020-12 defines and what `openapi: 3.1.0` admits | A caller must read a null rather than a missing member to learn it holds the last page. The description says so and the published example of the last page carries the null |
| The expiry year range 1950 through 2099 is encoded in the schema | Keep `^[0-9]{4}$` and leave the range to the runtime; add `minimum` and `maximum`; publish the range in prose alone | `88 VALID-EXPIRY-YEAR VALUES 1950 THRU 2099` at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L99` is the range `1260-EDIT-EXPIRY-YEAR` tests, so a schema admitting any four digits declared 1900 sendable and the runtime refused it. The property is text, and `minimum` does not apply to text, so the range is a regular expression over the two centuries it spans | The expression is longer than the four-digit one it replaced. `OpenApiExampleValidationTest` drives six accepted years and seven refused ones through it, so the expression cannot drift from the source range |
## Security-review remediation decisions

### Who may authorize a transaction

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `POST /authorizations` requires `ROLE_ACQUIRER` or `ROLE_ADMIN`, and a new acquirer identity carries that role | Keep `hasAnyRole(USER, ADMIN)`; require the caller to hold `SCOPE_ACCOUNT_<id>` or `SCOPE_CARD_<token>` for the card it presents; resolve the card and then compare it against the caller's scopes inside `AuthorizationService` | The route names its card in the request body, so no path variable carries an identifier an ownership scope can be compared against, and role alone therefore decided the request. An ordinary cardholder identity holding `ROLE_USER` could authorize against any account in the cross-reference and leave a durable decision and a published event behind it. Scoping the route to the presented card would contradict what the route is for: an acquirer legitimately authorizes every card the platform holds, and a point-of-sale network holds no cardholder entitlement. Separating the workload identity from the cardholder identity states that distinction in configuration rather than in a comment | One more identity to configure and one more password hash to generate. A deployment that grants `ROLE_ACQUIRER` to a human identity re-creates the finding, so the role is documented as a machine credential in `.env.example`, `30-configmap.yaml` and the service README |
| The entitlement check inside the decision path passes `ROLE_ACQUIRER` as well as `ROLE_ADMIN` | Grant the acquirer identity `ROLE_ADMIN`, as the entitlement rule originally assumed; grant the acquirer an ownership scope over every seeded card; leave the check admitting the administrator alone | Two additions met here. `domain/CallerEntitlement` was written while an acquirer client was configured as an administrator, so it passed one authority. The security review that followed gave the acquirer a role of its own, so that it reaches this route and no other. Passing only the administrator would have left the route admitting the acquirer at the chain and refusing every call it made, which is a route nobody can use. Granting the acquirer `ROLE_ADMIN` instead would undo the separation the review made. Scoping it to every card would put fifty ownership authorities into configuration, and would still fail for a card the platform issues next. `RequestCaller.reachesEverySubject` therefore admits both names, and `SecurityConfigTest` holds them in step with the route rule | The acquirer reaches every card the platform holds, which is what the route is for and what `.env.example`, `30-configmap.yaml` and the service README each state. A deployment granting that role to a human identity re-creates the finding the review closed |
| The role check is not replaced by the decline rules that follow it | Rely on reason 0100, which declines a card the cross-reference does not carry | Reason 0100 says a card resolves nothing, which is not the same as saying the caller may not ask, and all four reasons leave a durable decision row, a published `TransactionDeclined` event and downstream consumers doing work. The rules bound which cards resolve, not who may ask | The rules and the role check overlap for a card that does not exist, which costs one comparison |
| The authorization description publishes its security scheme, its requirement and its 401 and 403 answers | Leave the contract undocumented, as before; document the statuses without the scheme | Five of the six services already published a scheme, and this one — the only route a client calls directly — did not. A document that describes a route and omits its authentication tells every reader, and every generated client, that the route is open | The description now restates what `config/SecurityConfig` expresses. `OpenApiContractTest` compares the two, so the duplication is checked rather than merely written |

### What the sensitive-value screen reads

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The screen removes any run of characters standing between two digits, rather than a named set of separators | Add the decimal point and the solidus to the existing space-and-hyphen set; strip every non-digit; keep the value as written and rely on the schema patterns | A named set can only hold the separators somebody thought of, and free text admits every printable character. `4111.1111.1111.1111` and `020.97.3888` passed both the publish and the consume screen, and reached Kafka, an outbox row, a ledger row and the notification renderer. Removing whatever stands between two digits is independent of the spelling. Stripping every non-digit would instead join the digit groups of ordinary sentences. The two lookarounds requiring a digit on each side are what keep `REF 1234 SEQ 5678 LOT 9012` in three runs | A free-text value that legitimately holds twelve or more digits with nothing but punctuation between them is now refused. A monetary amount reaches twelve digits only at ten dollars of magnitude with cents, which no merchant name, city or description carries. A refusal is a 400 rather than a silent disclosure |
| The value is also read under Unicode NFKC normalization | Read the value as written alone; reject every non-ASCII character in every screened value | A full-width digit renders as a digit to a reader, and a compatibility separator renders as a separator. A value that looks like a card number was one to everybody except the screen. Normalizing first costs one pass and closes the whole class. Refusing non-ASCII outright is already what a typed free-text field does, since its record constrains the value to `^[ -~]*$`. The `extensions` subtree is open by design, and is the path these values arrive on | Normalization is not free on a hot publish path. It runs only after the value as written has already been screened and found clean, so an ordinary event pays one pass over one short string |
| Both screens keep the twelve-digit floor rather than bounding the run at nineteen | Bound the run to the twelve-to-nineteen digits a card number occupies | An upper bound tells a caller how to smuggle a card number: pad it. A floor with no ceiling refuses the padded form too | A digit run longer than nineteen characters is refused as well, and no property this platform screens carries one |

### The card token had to be described as what it is

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `transaction-authorized-v2.json`, `transaction-posted-v2.json` and the notification description now name HMAC-SHA-256 and state that the key is deployment-supplied | Leave the descriptions as they were; describe the primitive nowhere and point at `PanMasker` | A consumer implements the derivation from the published contract and from nothing else. All three described an unkeyed SHA-256 digest, so a consumer coding from them would have derived a different token and, worse, one that any reader can invert over a sixteen-digit space. The code was already correct; the specification was not | The contract now names a primitive, so changing the primitive is a contract change. `CardTokenKeyContractTest` compares the description against the constant `PanMasker` applies, so the two cannot drift silently |
| The contract test matches the shape of an unkeyed claim rather than one exact phrase | Compare the exact sentence, as before; drop the check and rely on review | The check compared one phrase, the two schemas spelled the claim differently, and both went on describing an unkeyed digest while the check passed. A pattern that looks for the primitive with no `HMAC` in front of it fails on either spelling | A description that discusses SHA-256 for an unrelated reason within forty characters of the word `key` passes. No token-bearing contract does |

### Where the account service keeps the customer relationship

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The account service replaces its `card_xref` replica with `account_customer_link`, holding the `XREF-ACCT-ID` and `XREF-CUST-ID` pair of `app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy:L6-L7` and no card number | Keep the replica and encrypt the column; keep the replica and store the derived card token in place of the card number. Drop the replica and call the card service for the pairing; drop the replica and trust the customer identifier the caller submits | The replica had one reader, `AccountUpdateService.authoritativeCustomerId`, and it asked one question: which customer does this account belong to. The card number was the source primary key and nothing more, so fifty full Primary Account Numbers sat in a schema whose every query names an account. Section 0.3.1 of the plan lists the tables this service owns and no cross-reference table is among them. Holding the pair instead answers the same question with none of the exposure. It also removes the ordering the card-keyed query needed: there is one row to find rather than a lowest card number to choose between. Calling the card service would put a synchronous hop inside an update transaction, and trusting the submitted identifier is the exact substitution the source refuses at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl` | The pair is a copy and can still go stale, exactly as the replica could, and nothing on this platform refreshes it yet. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries that ownership question, and now records that one fewer copy needs reconciling. An operator who wants to know which cards an account holds has to ask the card or authorization service, which is where a card-keyed replica belongs |
| `V7__account_customer_link.sql` seeds the fifty pairs as literals and drops the old table, rather than copying rows across and editing `V4` | Copy from `card_xref` with `INSERT ... SELECT` before the drop; edit `V4` so the replica is never created; leave the replica in place and stop reading it | An applied migration is not edited, so `V4` stays as it ran and `V7` corrects the schema forward. Nothing refreshes the rows `V4` seeded, so the seeded pairing and the live pairing are the same fifty rows and a literal seed loses nothing a copy would have kept. Writing the pairs out also keeps the seed readable in the migration that owns the table, which is where `ProjectionBootstrapContractTest` compares a seed against `app/data/ASCII/cardxref.txt`. Leaving the table in place unread would have kept the disclosure surface and removed only the justification for it | A deployment that had written its own rows into `card_xref` would lose them. No code path on this platform writes that table, and the drop is recorded in the migration, the service README and the data model |
| The authorization and card services keep their card-keyed replicas | Give all three services the account-keyed pair; give all three a card-keyed replica | Both of those services answer questions asked about a card. Authorization resolves a presented card to its account at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L385-L387`, and the card service compares a card's own row. An account-keyed table cannot serve either. The asymmetry follows the query, which is the same reason the account copy holds no card | Three schemas now describe the same source record in two shapes, and a reader has to know which shape a given service holds. The data model and the traceability matrix state it per service |

### What a log line may carry

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every Kafka template installs a `SafeProducerListener` that records a failed send by topic, partition and failure type | Leave the framework default in place; call `setIncludeContents(false)` on the default listener; install one listener through auto-configuration | A template installs `LoggingProducerListener` unless told otherwise, and that listener writes the message key and the first hundred characters of the payload on every failure. The key here is the account identifier, because per-account ordering requires it, and the payload is a domain event. A broker outage is exactly when sends fail and every retry writes another line, so the default turns one outage into a durable copy of production traffic wherever logs are retained. Trimming the contents is not enough: it keeps the key and still passes the throwable.
