Auto-configuration reaches only the template the framework builds, and all ten of these are built by hand | One small class per service rather than one shared class, because `libs/event-contracts` carries `kafka-clients` and not `spring-kafka`. Adding the framework to a contracts library to save sixty lines would change what every consumer of that library depends on. `ProducerFailureLoggingContractTest` counts templates against installations, so a template added later cannot quietly keep the default |
| A failure is logged as its type and the types of its causes, never as the throwable | Pass the throwable, as before; log the message and not the throwable; route stack traces to a second, access-controlled sink | A throwable attached to a log event is rendered with its message, and a message quotes what caused the failure. That can be the value a unique constraint refused, the statement a timeout cancelled, or the data-source URL with the user it connected as. The type is the half that diagnoses and the message is the half that discloses. Three levels of cause reach the framework wrapper, the driver exception and the cause underneath, which is what tells a timeout from a constraint from a broken connection. A second sink is a deployment this demo does not have, and section 0.2.2 of the plan excludes that kind of hardening | A stack trace is no longer available from the ordinary sink. A defect inside this platform's own code is diagnosed from the failure type, the route and the metric rather than from a frame list. That is a real loss of detail, accepted because the alternative discloses row values to whoever reads logs, and `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the redacted diagnostic sink as the way to get both |
| The two authorization listener lines name the event and drop the account identifier | Keep the account identifier; mask it as a run of asterisks, as the ledger listener does; hash it under a deployment key | The event identifier is a value this platform generates and it leads a reader to the event on the topic, where the access controls are. A run of asterisks carries no information at all, so printing one is the same disclosure as printing nothing while being longer to read. A keyed hash would be a new secret to manage for a correlation value the event identifier already supplies | A reader who wants the account has to fetch the event rather than read it from the line. The ledger listener keeps its asterisk form, because no finding was raised against it and rewriting it would change nothing about what is disclosed |
| Every service defaults `com.carddemo` logging to INFO | Leave the DEBUG default and rely on compose and Kubernetes to override it; default to WARN | Compose and the manifests both set INFO, so the DEBUG default was reached only by an operator starting a service directly. That is exactly the path with no log policy behind it. WARN would hide the outcome lines the fan-out is demonstrated with, and the demonstration is a stated requirement | A developer tracing one message has to raise the level for that run. `.env.example` documents the variable and says to lower it again afterwards |
| `scripts/redact-report-artifacts.sh` rewrites every test report before the workflow uploads it, and fails the stage while any seeded value survives | Rely on the framework logger being off in all six services; stop uploading reports; redact inside a test; match card numbers by shape | A report is an artifact: the workflow keeps every Surefire and Failsafe report for `REPORT_RETENTION_DAYS`, so anything a test wrote to standard output is readable by everyone who can read the run, which is the whole of CWE-532. One instance was found and its shape behind it: a test provoking a check-constraint violation on `card` produced a line carrying PostgreSQL's own `DETAIL`, and that detail holds the entire failing row — number, verification value and token together. Turning the failed-statement logger off closes the source; this closes the artifact, so a leak from a source nobody has thought of yet is still caught before the upload. Reports are not dropped because a failure with no report is a failure nobody can read. A shape rule for card numbers is deliberately not used: a transaction identifier here is sixteen digits, so "any sixteen-digit run" fires on ordinary traffic, and a check that fires on everything gets switched off. The one shape rule applied is the failing-row detail, because that phrase is never legitimate in a report | The script reads the seeded values out of the fixtures at scan time and writes none of them down, so a value the fixtures stop carrying stops being scanned for. A leak of a value that is not seeded is not caught here, which is why the framework logger stays off at the source |

### Where the card-token key comes from

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| No deployment path ships a usable card-token key: `.env.example` and `31-secret.example.yaml` carry placeholders and the Compose stack requires the variable rather than defaulting it | Keep the published demo key and document that it is a demo key; ship a key and rotate it on first run. Derive the key from another secret already in the deployment | A card token is an `HmacSHA256` code over a sixteen-digit card number, so the key is the whole of what makes the token more than a rename. One token plus the key is enough to recompute the token of every candidate card number offline. The published key was the effective default of the build, the Compose stack and the Kubernetes Secret template at once. Documenting a published key does not make it unpublished. Deriving it from another secret would tie two rotations together for no gain | The stack no longer starts on a fresh clone without one generated value. That is the point, and it is one `openssl` command that `.env.example` and the Secret template both carry. `CardTokenKeyContractTest` fails the build if a usable key returns to any of the three |
| Both deriving services refuse the two card-token keys this repository publishes, and the build-scope key only when it arrives through `CARD_TOKEN_SECRET` | Refuse nothing and rely on the placeholders; refuse both keys unconditionally; refuse any key shorter than a threshold and no specific value | A placeholder stops a fresh clone but not a copy-paste from an older `.env` or from this repository's history, and the published key reached both. Refusing the build-scope key unconditionally would stop every test that starts a context, because the build supplies exactly that value. The variable a deployment sets and the system property a build sets are different paths, so the path the value arrived on is what separates the two cases. The length floor stays as well, because `PanMasker` already had one | An operator who passes the build-scope key as a JVM system property is not refused. Nothing deploys that way here — Compose and the manifests both set the environment variable — and `CardTokenKeyContractTest` asserts the value appears in no deployment artifact |
| The key is a Secret of its own, mounted into the authorization and card services alone | Leave it in the shared identity Secret; give each of the two its own copy; move it into the ConfigMap because a token is not a password | Two services derive a token: the card service for every row it writes, and the authorization service for the decision it records. The other four read a token that arrives in an event or in a request. The shared identity Secret is pulled whole by all six Deployments. The key therefore reached four services that had no use for it, and widened what a compromise of any one of them reached. Two copies of one key double the rotation work and change no boundary, and a ConfigMap is not encrypted at rest in a cluster that encrypts Secrets | One more Secret to create, named in the template and in both manifests. `CardTokenKeyContractTest` asserts exactly two Deployments pull it, so a third cannot be added quietly |
| The fifty seeded `card_token` literals stay checked in, derived under a build-scope key, and `CardTokenReconciler` re-derives them under the deployment's key at start-up | Derive the tokens in SQL inside the migration; make the column nullable and fill it lazily. Pass the key to Flyway as a placeholder; drop the seed and load cards through the API | The column is `NOT NULL` and `UNIQUE`, so the seed has to supply a value. A checked-in literal is what lets `CardRepositoryIT` and `CardSeedEquivalenceTest` compare a literal against the one derivation this platform holds. PostgreSQL cannot take an `HmacSHA256` code without an extension, and a Flyway placeholder would write the key into the migration history. A nullable column would let a service answer for a card with no identity. Re-deriving at start-up is what makes the literals a bootstrap rather than a live identity. After the runner has run, no token in the database belongs to a key this repository publishes | Start-up now reads the card table before readiness accepts traffic, in pages of 500 with a ceiling of 200,000 rows. The fixture is fifty rows. A run that rewrites nothing logs at DEBUG and costs one page read, and `CardRepositoryIT` proves both the rewrite and the second run that changes nothing |
| The `SCOPE_CARD` authority is no longer shipped in `.env.example` or `30-configmap.yaml` | Ship a placeholder authority; ship the authority derived under the build-scope key; grant the ordinary identity every card | An authority names a token, and a token belongs to one key, so an authority checked in beside a placeholder key names a card under a key no deployment holds. It would answer 403 for the card it was meant to open, which reads as a broken demo rather than as a configuration gap. A placeholder authority does the same thing with more ceremony, and granting every card is not an entitlement | The ordinary identity reaches no card route until an operator derives one token and appends it. `.env.example` carries the command, and the administrator identity reaches every card route without it |

### Which password encodings authenticate

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service builds its own `DelegatingPasswordEncoder` over an allowlist of `bcrypt` and `pbkdf2@SpringSecurity_v5_8`, in place of Spring Security's stock factory | Keep the stock factory and refuse unwanted prefixes at start-up only; keep the factory and set a fallback encoder; encode with one algorithm and map no others | The stock factory maps ten identifiers, and eight of them exist so that a deployment holding legacy hashes can migrate off them. `noop` stores a password in plain text, and `MD4`, `MD5`, `SHA-1`, `SHA-256`, `sha256` and `ldap` are unsalted or single-pass digests. This platform holds no hash to migrate, so those mappings could only ever let a weak stored value verify successfully. A start-up check alone would leave the encoder able to verify one, which matters because the check reads configuration and the encoder reads whatever reaches it. Mapping a single algorithm would refuse a hash generated under the other approved one for no reason | Two encodings rather than ten, so a deployment arriving with a legacy hash has to re-encode before it starts. That is the intended outcome, and the message names the identifier it refused and the ones it accepts |
| `argon2` and `scrypt` are not on the allowlist, although both are adaptive | Add both; add both and add Bouncy Castle to every service | Both implementations call Bouncy Castle, which is not a dependency of this platform and is not among the artifacts section 0.5.1 of the plan names. Mapping them without the dependency would move the failure from start-up to the first authentication, where it reads as a wrong password. Adding the dependency to six services to widen a choice nothing needs is scope this engagement does not have | A deployment that standardises on Argon2 cannot use its hashes here. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` records the dependency and the two lines it would take |
| A `bcrypt` hash must declare a cost of at least ten, checked by parsing the value at start-up | Accept any cost; require twelve; check the cost only in review | Cost is logarithmic, so a hash at cost four is 64 times cheaper to guess than one at ten, and nothing in the encoder refuses it. Ten is what the generation recipes in `.env.example` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` produce. The floor therefore accepts every hash this repository tells an operator to make, and refuses one made by an older or hand-rolled recipe. Requiring twelve would refuse the hashes those two recipes generate, which is a coordinated change across both and the onboarding guide for a step of one | The floor is a constant in six files rather than one, because a security configuration per service is the shape section 0.3.1 of the plan fixes. `PasswordEncodingContractTest` holds all six to one value |
| Test fixtures configure a real bcrypt hash instead of `{noop}` | Keep `{noop}` in tests and exempt them from the allowlist; disable the encoder in tests; encode at test run time | Ninety-one properties across twenty-nine test sources configured a plaintext password, and each of those contexts is the same context a deployment runs. A test that shortcuts the encoder stops exercising the verification path, and an exemption keeps the refused encoding alive in the repository where the next author copies it. A hash cannot be computed at run time in an annotation, which has to carry a constant, so one `TestIdentityPasswords` per module holds the four plaintexts and the four hashes together | Seven small classes of constants, and a hash that has to be regenerated with its plaintext. Each module's `SecurityConfigTest` asserts that every pair still verifies, so a hash changed on its own fails one test rather than every authentication in the module |

### What makes a state-changing request first-party

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service requires a non-simple request header on every state-changing method, and refuses foreign fetch metadata or a foreign `Origin` when either is present | Enable Spring Security's synchronizer-token protection; move the business surface onto a bearer token a browser does not attach by itself. Grant no cross-origin policy and call the reads safe, as before; require the header and ignore the other two; require fetch metadata and `Origin` outright | HTTP Basic is the credential this platform authenticates with, and a browser attaches a cached one to a request a page made without being asked. An auto-submitting form on any other site could therefore reach `POST /accounts/{accountId}/cycle-close` and zero both cycle accumulators. The absent cross-origin policy does not stop it: a form submission is sent whether or not the page may read the answer. Token protection needs a prior safe request to hand the token out, and a session or a cookie to bind it to. This surface is stateless by design. Replacing Basic with a bearer token is the better answer, and a larger change than this platform's identity model has. `app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L223` compares a stored password directly, and the migrated signon reproduces that comparison. The three conditions `config/CrossSiteRequestFilter` tests are therefore not equal, and only one of them is mandatory. The configured header is the condition that always holds, so a request that omits it or leaves it blank is refused on that alone. A form cannot set a header at all, and a cross-origin script that set one turns the call into a preflight no chain here grants. Fetch metadata and `Origin` are read only when the request carries them, and each refuses a forged browser call earlier than the header does. Neither can be demanded, because a legitimate client that is not a browser sends neither | A non-browser client has to send one header it did not send before. Every `curl` example in this repository carries it, and the five state-changing operations declare it in their OpenAPI parameters. The refusal is documented in each service README and in onboarding pitfall 11. The header's value is never checked, so it is not a secret and authenticates nothing. It is a capability check on the caller, exactly as strong as the browser rule that a form cannot set a header. A client that sends the header and also sends cross-site fetch metadata is still refused, which is deliberate: only a browser sets that metadata |
| **SUPERSEDED by "Four filters declare an explicit rung ahead of the security chain" under [A refusal runs ahead of the password it would otherwise pay for](#a-refusal-runs-ahead-of-the-password-it-would-otherwise-pay-for), which ordered both filters and added the deferral that keeps every answer where it was. It read:** The filter carries the default component order, so it runs after the security chain | Order it ahead of the chain so a forged call never reaches authentication; order it with `RequestRateCeilingFilter` | A forged cross-site call is by definition authenticated, so the check has to precede the state change and not the credential check. Running after the chain also keeps the answers in the order a caller expects. A request with no credential still receives 401 rather than a cross-site refusal, which is what `config/RequestBodyCeilingFilter` already does | An authenticated forged call pays one bcrypt verification before it is refused. The rate-ceiling filter bounds how many such calls one source may make, which is the control that limits the cost Superseded: the cost this row accepted is the finding a later security review raised, and ordering the filter ahead of the chain no longer changes the answer, because it stands aside for a request carrying no credential. |
| Safe methods pass untouched and no path is exempted | Exempt the actuator base path explicitly; apply the check to every method | `GET`, `HEAD`, `OPTIONS` and `TRACE` change nothing, and no chain on this platform grants a cross-origin policy, so a browser withholds a read's answer from the page that asked for it. Every management endpoint this platform exposes is a read, so exempting a path would add a list to maintain and change no outcome | A state-changing management endpoint added later would need the header like any other write. Only `health`, `metrics` and `prometheus` are exposed, and all three are reads |
| The refusal is 403 carrying the same fixed problem document as every other refusal, naming no route, header or identifier | Name the condition that refused, so a caller can correct it; answer 400 because the request is malformed rather than forbidden | A body that says which of the three conditions refused tells an attacker which one to work on next. One body for all three discloses nothing, and it matches what `config/SecurityConfig` already writes for an unauthorized or forbidden request. The 403 is the truthful status: the request was well-formed and this client may not make it | A first-party client that forgot the header reads a refusal that looks like a role refusal. Onboarding pitfall 11 and the six service READMEs name the header as the first thing to check. Every `429` and `403` this control writes is counted separately, so the cause is visible in the metrics |
| The cross-cutting web and messaging plumbing stays as one small copy per service — `config/SecurityConfig`, `CrossSiteRequestFilter`, `RequestRateCeilingFilter`, `CorrelationContextFilter`, `SafeProducerListener`, `ReadinessHealthConfig` and `StreamNameReport` — rather than moving into a shared library or an auto-configuration module | Extract them into a third library beside `event-contracts` and `cobol-compat`; publish them as a Spring auto-configuration; keep one copy in one service and depend on it from the other five | A review counted 84,000 lines across the six main trees and asked for the uniform plumbing to be extracted, and the plan does not admit it. AAP 0.3.1 enumerates a `config` package inside each service and names these classes there; AAP 0.4.2 fixes the module graph at two libraries plus six services with no service depending on another, and `card-platform/pom.xml` enforces that with a banned-dependency rule; AAP 0.3.3 abstracts exactly one seam, the event publisher, and says no other seam is abstracted; and AAP 0.1.3 C7 rejects generated or hidden code because a reader cannot see it, which is what an auto-configuration module is. Depending on one service from five would break the property that makes the consumers independent. What the review's own measurement supports is the other half of its resolution, removing duplicated rationale prose, and that is what was done: 349 comment lines came out of the six trees and the arguments they repeated are in this log | The six copies still drift unless a build check holds them together, which is what `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest`, `RequestSurfaceControlContractTest`, `ProducerFailureLoggingContractTest`, `ReadinessListenerExpectationContractTest` and `CommonMetricTagContractTest` do, each asserting the same property in all six services. A reader walking one service still reads its own copy. Extracting the plumbing remains available to a human who is willing to amend the plan, and `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` records it |

### How much of a service one caller may consume

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service applies five ceilings in `config/RequestRateCeilingFilter`. They are 20 failed authentications and 600 requests a minute per source address, 600 a minute per identity, 120 state-changing requests a minute per source, and 64 requests in flight | Put the ceilings in an ingress controller or a gateway; use a rate-limiting library with a token bucket. Bound authentication attempts by locking an account after a number of failures; add none, as before | A guessing burst costs this platform one bcrypt verification per attempt, and an accepted write costs a row, an outbox row and three consumers doing work. An unbounded surface is therefore both a credential-guessing surface and a denial-of-service surface. A gateway is a component this demo does not deploy and would leave a service reached directly unbounded, which is how `docker compose` reaches it. A library would add a dependency section 0.5.1 of the plan does not name for an algorithm that is a counter and a window. Locking an account turns a guessing attempt into a way to deny a real cardholder service, so the source address is bounded rather than the identity's ability to authenticate at all | A fixed window admits up to twice the nominal rate across a boundary, which a token bucket would not. The ceilings are set for a demonstration rather than a measured load, and doubling 600 for one minute is still bounded. A shared client behind one address — a corporate proxy or a test runner — consumes one ceiling for everyone behind it, and `API_RATE_*` raises it |
| The filter runs at `SecurityFilterProperties.DEFAULT_FILTER_ORDER` minus one, ahead of the security chain | Run it after the chain, where the authenticated identity is available; register it as a chain filter | A refusal has to cost less than the attempt it refuses. An attempt that reached the chain has already paid for a bcrypt verification, which is the cost the credential-guessing ceiling exists to avoid. Running first also means the concurrency bound refuses before a request occupies a datastore connection | The identity is therefore the username read from the `Authorization` header rather than an authenticated principal. It is used as a counter key alone, is never trusted and is never logged, and a caller who varies it only moves onto the per-source ceiling |
| Failed authentications are counted separately from successful traffic, and only when the request carried a credential and was answered 401 | Count every 401; count every 4xx; count nothing separately and rely on the blanket ceiling | A caller that simply forgot to authenticate is not guessing, and counting its 401 would throttle an honest client learning the API. Counting only a presented-and-refused credential makes 20 a guessing budget rather than a mistake budget, which is what lets it sit thirty times below the blanket ceiling | A guesser that varies the source address is bounded per address rather than in total, which a shared counter would fix. That limit is recorded in `card-platform/docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` together with the per-instance one |
| The counter map is bounded at 10,000 keys, expired windows are removed first, and a key that still finds no room shares one overflow window | Grow the map with the traffic; use an eviction cache; key the ceilings by something an attacker cannot vary | An attacker varying the source address or the username is varying the counter key, so an unbounded map is the denial-of-service the filter was added to prevent. Sharing one window past the ceiling degrades toward refusing rather than toward exhausting memory. Nothing keyed to a request is unvariable, which is why the bound is on the map instead | Past 10,000 distinct keys inside one window, unrelated callers share a window and can refuse one another. Ten thousand distinct sources against one replica of a demo stack is already the attack this bound exists for |
| A refusal counts `carddemo.<service>.requests.throttled` tagged with the stage that refused, and all five stages are registered at zero when the service starts | Count refusals without a stage tag; log each refusal; expose no metric | The review asked for monitoring, and a control nobody can see is a control nobody trusts. The stage tag is what separates a guessing burst from a load test from a concurrency wall, which is the difference an operator acts on. Registering at zero means a dashboard shows the series before the first refusal, rather than a gap that reads as a missing service. Logging each refusal is what an attacker would use to fill the log | Five series per service rather than one. The tag values are a closed set, so the cardinality is fixed, and the fraud service's meter inventory test holds the whole set |

### Where a version floor lives, and what proves it still holds

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Five `com.fasterxml.jackson` coordinates are managed at 2.21.5, beside the Jackson 3 entries they have nothing else in common with | Exclude the transitive edge from `spring-kafka-test`; wait for a Spring Kafka release that resolves a newer `kafka-server`; accept it because the scope is test | Nothing managed the Jackson 2 coordinates, so `spring-kafka-test` resolved `kafka-server`, which brought 2.21.4 onto the test classpath of all six services and of `equivalence-tests`. 2.21.4 carries GHSA-5gvw-p9qm-jgwh, GHSA-5jmj-h7xm-6q6v and GHSA-mhm7-754m-9p8w. Excluding the edge would have broken the embedded broker every consumer test starts. `jackson-databind` and `jackson-core` are the two the advisories name. `jackson-dataformat-csv`, `jackson-dataformat-yaml` and `jackson-datatype-jdk8` arrive on the same edge and are raised with them, because a graph holding 2.21.4 beside 2.21.5 makes the claim that the vulnerable version was removed untrue | Two Jackson generations are now declared in one descriptor, which reads like a mistake. The comment beside the entries states the resolved path, and `equivalence-tests` `SupplyChainContractTest` asserts that path is still described |
| A floor is enforced against the resolved graph by `enforce-transitive-security-floors`, and not only declared in `dependencyManagement` | Trust the management block; assert the declared versions in a test; run a scanner in the pipeline and nowhere else | The two answer different questions. Management sets the version a coordinate resolves to; the rule asks whether anything still resolves below the floor, which is the question nobody had asked about Jackson 2. It is a `bannedDependencies` rule with a range per coordinate, `searchTransitive` true, and it runs at `validate` in every module of every build | Thirteen ranges have to be raised whenever a floor is, and a raise that misses one leaves a floor unenforced rather than loud. `SupplyChainContractTest` compares the two sets, so a missing range fails a test rather than going quiet |
| `com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-annotations` is left where it resolves, at 2.21 | Raise it alongside the other four; exclude it; hold all five at one version | The 2.x line of that artifact stops at 2.21. There is no 2.21.5 to raise it to and no advisory above names it | A reader who expects the release train to move together will look for a sixth entry and not find one, which is why the comment says so |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The Tomcat pin names the advisory it does not answer" under [Final acceptance gate remediation decisions](#final-acceptance-gate-remediation-decisions), which was written when CVE-2026-66299 was published. It read:** `tomcat.version` is described as precautionary rather than as a security floor | Leave it described as a security floor; drop the raise and take the bill of materials version; name an advisory | No advisory affecting the 11.0.22 the bill of materials manages was identified when the row was written | The description went stale the day an advisory reached the 11.0.x line, which is what the superseding row records |
| GHSA-xx22-p4ch-683r is recorded as CVE-2026-59949 | Leave the identifier as it was; cite the GHSA identifier alone | The authoritative advisory maps it to CVE-2026-59949, and it had been recorded as CVE-2026-7053. A wrong identifier sends a reader to the wrong advisory, which is worse than no identifier | None. The fixed-version floor of 1.11.1 was correct either way |

### Which artifacts a module declares for itself, and which it reaches through a starter

Eleven module descriptors declare `org.apache.kafka:kafka-clients` or `tools.jackson.core:jackson-databind`
directly, and each declaration carried its own paragraph of justification. Rule 1 puts that
justification here, and the descriptors now state the mechanical fact and name this row.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A module that imports `org.apache.kafka.clients` or `org.apache.kafka.common` declares `org.apache.kafka:kafka-clients` itself. The eleven descriptors are `libs/event-contracts`, the six services and `equivalence-tests` | Reach the artifact through `spring-kafka`, which resolves it transitively; declare it once in the aggregator's `dependencies` rather than in `dependencyManagement`; add a starter to `libs/event-contracts` so every consumer inherits the edge | A module that compiles against an application programming interface it does not declare compiles only while something else keeps supplying it, and `libs/event-contracts` carries the client without carrying `spring-kafka` at all. Declaring the edge makes the dependency a fact of the descriptor rather than a property of another module's graph | Eleven declarations move together when the coordinate changes. No version travels with them: the imported bill of materials resolves 4.2.1, which is the tag `docker-compose.yml` and `deploy/k8s/10-kafka.yaml` run the broker at, and `SupplyChainContractTest` holds the two to each other |
| A module that imports `tools.jackson.databind` declares `tools.jackson.core:jackson-databind` itself, on the same terms | Reach it through `spring-boot-starter-web`, which every service already declares; declare it in `libs/event-contracts` alone and let the services inherit it | The rule is the same rule, and applying it to one artifact and not the other would leave a reader guessing which edges a descriptor states. `libs/event-contracts` declares no web starter, so for that module there is no starter to inherit from | `jackson-core` and the Jackson 2 annotations arrive with `databind` and are not declared, because neither can be absent while `databind` is present. The floors that govern their versions are the row above |

### What the pipeline checks besides compiling and testing

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Three new stages carry six controls: a secret scan over the tree and the history, static analysis, a bill of materials, an advisory review of that bill, an image scan, and a provenance statement | Add one combined security stage; run the scanners on a schedule rather than per run; rely on the platform's own alerting | A review found the pipeline had no static analysis, no secret scan, no advisory review, no image scan, no bill of materials and no provenance. Splitting them by what they read keeps a failure legible: a secret in the tree, a result in the sources, an advisory in the graph, a vulnerability in an image | Ten stages take longer than six, and the three new ones each install a tool. The container stage now waits on seven stages rather than four, so a slow scan delays the images |
| An action is pinned to a full commit identifier with its release in a trailing comment | Keep the major-version tag; pin to the release tag; use a bot to update the tags | A major-version tag is a moving reference: whoever controls the action repository can point `v4` at other code and every run afterwards executes it. A commit identifier names the bytes, which is the same reasoning the upstream images under `deploy/k8s` are digest-pinned for | A pinned action does not update itself, so a security fix in an action needs a commit here. The trailing comment is what makes the pin readable enough to update |
| A downloaded tool is pinned to a version and verified against a digest its own project published | Install from a package manager; use each tool's own action; fetch the latest release | An action is pinned by commit and an archive cannot be. Every archive this workflow installs is checked with `sha256sum --check --strict`, which is the shape the Maven install step has always used. A tool that arrived unverified would be a hole in the pipeline that checks for holes | Three digests have to change whenever a tool version does. A digest that is wrong fails the stage loudly, which is the failure mode to prefer |
| A bill of materials describes the resolved graph, test scope included, and the advisory scan reads that rather than the descriptors | Scan the descriptors; scan the repository directory; scan only the runtime classpath | The finding this stage answers lived on a test classpath and was named by no descriptor. A tool reading `pom.xml` would not have seen it. The file has to be named `bom.json`: the scanner recognises a bill of materials by the file name its specification defines and refuses any other | The bill of materials describes what one build resolved, so a scan is as current as the run. It is kept as an artifact, so a later advisory can be checked against what shipped |
| Two stages state a severity threshold: static analysis fails at security severity 7.0 and above, and the image scan fails on a high or critical vulnerability that has a fix | Fail on every result; fail on nothing and report; set the thresholds per service | The runtime base image the six services share carries 41 high or critical findings today and every one of them is unfixable at any version. A gate that can never pass is a gate somebody switches off. Both stages count and print what they do not fail on, and both keep the full report | A high or critical finding with no fix does not stop a release. It is printed in the stage log and kept in the artifact, and the unfixable count moving is the signal to look |
| `card-platform/.gitleaks.toml` allowlists SHAPES of known-safe value and no paths, and sits under `card-platform/` so the change stays inside the two directories this engagement adds to | Scan with the default rules and accept the failures; scope the allowlist by path; keep the path exception and add `condition = "AND"` beside it; drop the history scan | The default rules fire 23 times on this repository, on card tokens, refused template values, test keys and a documented `curl` example, and every one of the 23 was measured rather than assumed. Two of the entries were then found to be wide enough to hide a real credential and are withdrawn. The first named `deploy/k8s/31-secret.example.yaml` by path, which dropped every finding in the one file this repository fills with credentials before any detector ran: measured, a private key committed into it was reported clean. Adding `condition = "AND"` beside that path does not narrow it on gitleaks 8.30.1 — measured the same way, with the same result — so the path is gone and the three placeholder shapes stand alone. The second excused any 64-character lowercase hexadecimal value anywhere, which is a card token and equally an unrelated key; it is replaced by three expressions that require the name `CARD_TOKEN` or `cardToken` beside the value, which is the part an unrelated secret does not have | An allowlist can still hide a secret that takes an allowed shape, so each entry is listed here with the risk it accepts, and the canary step of the secret-scan stage plants a real credential on every run to prove the remaining entries are not too wide. **1. A card token named as one**, because publishing a token is the design; a 16-to-64 character hexadecimal key assigned to a name containing `CARD_TOKEN` or `cardToken` would pass, and nothing else of that shape does. **2. The two `PanMaskerTest` keys**, exact literals in one test source; a real key of the same text would pass, and `CardTokenKeyContractTest` fails the build if either reaches a deployment artifact. **3. A 16-character hexadecimal literal repeated four times**, which is how a test writes a token without a 64-character line. **4. Version 4 identifiers**, published in every event envelope; a secret formatted as one would pass, and no secret on this platform is. **5. The build-scope card-token key**, one exact literal that both deriving services refuse to start on. **6. The three refused placeholder shapes** this repository publishes, `REPLACE-WITH-`, the `{bcrypt}` template hash and the base64 whose bytes decode to `REPLACE-WITH-THE-`; a credential that happened to begin with one of those literals would pass. **7. The phrase `the password you chose`**, which is instruction text a reader replaces rather than a credential |
| The secret-scan stage plants a credential on every run and requires the scanner to find it | Trust a clean scan; assert the allowlist shape in a test and stop there; run the canary on a schedule | A clean scan says the repository holds no secret. It does not say the scanner would still fail on one, and for the file most likely to receive one it would not have: the withdrawn path exception dropped every finding in `31-secret.example.yaml` and this stage stayed green. Reading the configuration is not enough either, because an entry that reads as scoped can behave as unscoped, which is exactly what `condition = "AND"` beside a `paths` key does on gitleaks 8.30.1. The step plants two secrets in turn — a private key inside the Secret template and an unrelated hexadecimal key beside it — and fails the run if either scan comes back clean. Both plants are removed and the tree is checked back against git before the step ends | The stage runs the scanner twice more, which costs about a minute. A plant left behind would hand the rest of the run a modified Secret template, so the step restores on every exit path and then proves the restoration with `git diff --quiet` |
| Provenance is a stage of its own that runs on a push, and it is the only job holding a write permission | Keep the attestation inside the container stage as it was; drop the attestation; gate the step on the event and leave the permissions at the job | The attestation needs an OpenID Connect token and the scope that records the statement. The container stage holds those on every event, and it runs on a pull request, where the local composite action, the Maven descriptors, the sources, the Compose file and the six Dockerfiles all come from the proposal and all execute before the attestation step. Gating the step changes nothing about that: a permission is granted to the job, not to the step. The container stage now holds `contents: read` alone, and the new stage runs on a push, where the content is what was merged. Its checkout sets `persist-credentials: false`, so no later step reaches the token through the local git configuration | The archives are packaged a second time, which costs about a minute, because a statement has to name bytes the attesting job produced rather than bytes it downloaded. A pull request records no provenance, which is what it recorded before |

### What a manifest can promise about the bytes it runs

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The six platform images keep `imagePullPolicy: Never`, and the manifests now say the tag is mutable | Switch to `IfNotPresent` so a digest could be pulled; publish the six to a registry; drop the tag for a digest | This project publishes no image anywhere, so `carddemo/<service>` is a Docker Hub name it does not own. A policy that permitted a pull would let a node fetch bytes from whoever does own that name, under a name that reads like ours. `Never` removes that path; what it does not do is make the reference immutable, and the manifests had not said so | The tag stays mutable. An operator who tags different bytes under it on one node gets those bytes and no manifest notices. `deploy/k8s/README.md` states this as the residual risk rather than leaving it to be inferred |
| The six references live in `deploy/k8s/kustomization.yaml`, with the digest-pinning command beside them | Edit six Deployments to pin; add a templating tool; leave the references where they were | Pinning by digest is then one edit rather than six, and the procedure sits next to the values it changes. `kubectl apply -k` is a stock command, so nothing new has to be installed to use it | A second entry point exists, so a reader who applies the directory file by file misses the image rewriting. `deploy/k8s/README.md` names `apply -k` as the way in |
| `31-secret.example.yaml` is deliberately absent from the kustomization resource list | List it and let an operator overwrite the values; rename it out of the folder; generate it | Every value in that file is refused at start-up. Applying it would create Secrets no service can start on, which is a confusing failure rather than a safe default | An operator who expects `apply -k` to create everything finds no Secrets. The README says where they come from |
| Signing and admission-time verification are recorded as a task rather than built | Sign with a key in the repository; add an admission policy that cannot be tested here; claim the control and defer it | Both need a registry, a signing key and an admission controller. Section 0.2.2 of the plan places production hardening out of scope and section 0.8.5 records the instruction to keep infrastructure configuration minimal and swappable | The images carry a provenance statement from the pipeline and no signature on the image itself. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the task and names every file it touches |

### Which card number a guide and a published contract may carry

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A guide reads the card number out of `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt` into a shell variable instead of printing one | Print a redacted form such as `05000244********`; print a synthetic number; drop the example | The two guides exist so a reader can run the call. A redacted number is not runnable, and a synthetic one resolves to no cross-reference row, so `curl` returns a decline for reason 100 and the guide teaches the wrong outcome. `cut -c1-16 app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt` yields the value the seed migration loaded, so the command works and the repository still carries no number a reader could replay | The command has to run from the repository root, which both guides now state. A reader on a machine without the fixture has no card number, and the same sentence tells them where it comes from |
| **SUPERSEDED by "No published contract carries a card number in any form" below, which withdrew both literals. It read:** A published contract example carries one of two synthetic sixteen-digit numbers, neither of which is seeded | Read the fixture, as the guides do; omit the example; describe the shape in prose only | An example value in an OpenAPI document is a literal and cannot be computed. Both numbers are absent from `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt`, from `app/data/ASCII/cardxref.txt` and from all three seed migrations, so replaying one against the demo stack resolves nothing. Each example sits beside a sentence naming the command that reads a real number | An example that resolves to nothing declines for reason 100 if a reader pastes it. That is the safe direction, and it is stated in the description rather than left to be discovered |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The cardholder-example guard refuses a card-number shape across the delivered surface" below, which widened the sweep and added a shape rule. It read:** `CardholderExampleContractTest` reads the customer-facing surface and exempts `src/test` | Sweep every file; exempt by file extension; assert only the four files the review named | A fixture-derived expected result and a test that constructs a seeded card are the tests that prove equivalence. A sweep including them would have to allowlist most of the suite, and would stop meaning anything. The three seed migrations that load the fixture are named individually, and a further test asserts each one still carries a number so the exemption cannot outlive its reason | A card number added to a test source is not refused. It never reaches a reader, and the review scoped this finding to documentation and API examples |
| No published contract carries a card number in any form, and the one request example that named a card is withdrawn | Keep the two synthetic literals, as before; publish a masked number; keep the example and let its body name a number | A sixteen-digit literal in a delivered document is a card number shape, whatever it resolves to. A reader copies the shape, and the habit outlives the value. A masked number cannot be sent, because the cross-reference read at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L382-L383` keys on all sixteen digits. An example naming a card therefore has to carry sixteen digits to bind, and `OpenApiContractTest#everyPublishedRequestExampleBinds` requires every published request example to bind. The only body satisfying both rules names no card, so the card branch is documented on the `cardNumber` property instead, which names `cut -c1-16 app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt` as the source of a value. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` reads its smoke card out of `app/data/ASCII/cardxref.txt` the same way and masks it out of the log | A reader who wants the card branch runs one command before sending a body. Five request examples remain and every one names an account, so nothing published demonstrates the card branch end to end. The refusal that branch produces is still published, as a response example |
| The cardholder-example guard refuses a card-number shape across the delivered surface, `.github` included, and still exempts `src/test` | Keep the sweep inside `card-platform` and match on seeded values alone, as before; sweep every file including test sources; assert only the four files the review named | Matching on seeded values alone catches only numbers the fixture holds today. A synthetic literal passed that rule for as long as it was published, and the smoke step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` held a seeded number where the sweep never looked, because the walk started at `card-platform`. The guard now applies two rules to one widened surface: no seeded number anywhere outside the three seed migrations, and no sixteen-digit literal that the artifact itself labels a card number. A label is read on the same line, and in YAML also in the six lines above it, because a YAML value sits under its key. Prose is same-line only: measured over the whole tree, a window read one sentence about the width of a transaction identifier as a labelled card number. Test sources stay exempt, because a fixture-derived expected result has to name the row it compares and no reader copies an assertion into a request | A card number added to a test source is still not refused. The label list is a list, so a field named something new has to be added to it; fourteen negative controls in the same class state what each rule catches, which is what makes an omission visible |
## Repository and instrument surface decisions

### The reference-data repository surface

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `DisclosureGroupRepository` extends `ListCrudRepository` with an empty body | Keep the bare `Repository` base and declare `findById` and `findAll`; extend `JpaRepository`; extend `CrudRepository` | The declared pair duplicated two inherited signatures exactly, so the base interface already supplied the whole read surface. `JpaRepository` adds a pagination surface no account paragraph justifies, and neither `app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl` nor `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl` contains a browse verb. `CrudRepository` returns `Iterable` from `findAll`, and this package returns `List` | The base brings `save` and four delete signatures onto a table only a Flyway migration writes. A caller can now reach them. `RepositorySurfaceTest` and `DisclosureGroupRepositoryTest` hold the interface to declaring nothing of its own, which is the guarantee that replaced the narrower base |

### The account-service meter surface

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The seven instruments the account service was asked for register inside one `AccountMeters` facade, exposed by two beans | Declare one `@Bean` per instrument and inject six counters and a timer by qualifier; split the facade across several configuration classes | All seven register at start-up and carry the requested names: `carddemo.account.events.consumed`, `carddemo.account.update.latency`, `carddemo.account.publish.failed`, `carddemo.account.validation.failed`, `carddemo.account.update.applied`, `carddemo.account.cycle.closed` and `carddemo.account.outbox.published`. Five of the six services register their meters through one named facade, so the shape is the platform convention. The facade keeps one recorder per path, which is what lets the relay be the sole caller of its own series | A reader looking for one bean per metric finds two beans instead. `ObservabilityConfigTest` asserts each of the seven by name and instrument type, so the surface is build-enforced rather than asserted here |
| The `config` package of each service holds more than the two files its earliest file brief named | Consolidate every service's configuration into two classes; delete the later additions | The services acquired security, readiness, consumer, and request-ceiling configuration in later checkpoints, and the account service acquired a `@KafkaListener` consumer that the earliest brief recorded as absent. Measured file counts are 10, 7, 7, 6, 9 and 8 across the six services, so the two-file shape describes none of them | The earliest per-file briefs and the delivered packages disagree on file count. A reader trusting a brief over the tree would expect two classes and find more |

## Synchronous-surface, validation and marker-boundary decisions

Each row below records a choice that a reader could reasonably have made differently, and that
previously survived only as an argument inside a source comment. The code now states the behaviour
and points here.

### The synchronous surfaces

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The authorization exception advice names no base package | Scope `@RestControllerAdvice` to the API package of the service | Spring selects an advice by the type of the handler it resolved, and a request matching no mapping resolves none. A `consumes` condition the request content type misses is the common case. A scoped advice is therefore skipped for exactly the failures raised before a handler is chosen. The documented answers to an unsupported media type, an unacceptable media type and an unsupported method would each revert to the framework body this class exists to replace. Scoping is also not sufficient on its own: `fraud-detection-service` was scoped throughout and still answered a paused datastore with the framework body, because its health indicator threw | The advice also sees failures raised outside the API package. `config/ReadinessHealthConfig` keeps management-port failures away from it by making every indicator report its own dependency down rather than throw |
| SUPERSEDED by "Every route that names one card names it by that card's token" under [What names one card on a request line](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line). The two card routes that name one card took the card number in a request body, so a read was a `POST` | A path variable; a query parameter; a `GET` with a request body | The reasoning recorded at the time was that a card number in a path or a query string is written to access logs, proxy caches and browser history by default. No service here can undo that. That reasoning was sound and the conclusion drawn from it was not. The delivered routes became `GET /cards/{cardNumber}` and `PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, because each addresses exactly one card and two shapes for one row disagreed about which member names it | Both routes now carry the card token, which addresses exactly one card and puts no number in the request line. Neither the body shape nor the number in the path survives |
| The account update takes its identity only from the path, and refuses a submitted identifier | Accept the identifier in the body as well; prefer one over the other on disagreement | Carrying it in both places raises a question no source rule answers, namely which value wins when the two disagree. Declaring it once removes the question instead of answering it. `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1222` calls the identifier `actually not editable` | A caller migrating from the source screen layout has to move one field out of the body. `AccountUpdateService.editSearchKey` still applies the identifier edit from `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1802-L1803` to the path value |
| One ordered validator runs over an update request, and no bean-validation cascade runs beside it | Mark the nested records with `@Valid`; report every violation; report a field-error map | `WS-RETURN-MSG PIC X(75)` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L479` carries one text, and the guard `WS-RETURN-MSG-OFF` at `:L480` means the first failure is the only failure reported. A bean validator answers with an unordered set, so a form with two bad fields would produce two messages and no rule for which the caller sees first. `AccountUpdateService.editMapInputs` runs the edits of `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1205-L1280` in source order and stops at the first | Validation order is Java control flow rather than annotations, so a reader has to read the service to see it. `AccountDataRequest` and `CustomerDataRequest` keep their own width bounds as documentation of field shape, and a test holds the two sets to agreement |
| SUPERSEDED by the row above. The card detail and search routes applied their field edits in source order in the controller rather than through `@Valid` | Annotate the body and let the framework answer; keep both and accept either message | The source tests each field for absence before its character class, and an unordered violation set answers the wrong one of four texts. With the number in the path there is no search body left to edit. `api/CardController#CARD_NUMBER_PATTERN` holds the path value to the sixteen digits `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L784` tests, and `domain/CardUpdateService` runs the remaining edits in source order | The shape is declared twice, on the mapping pattern and in the service. A test holds them to agreement |
| `AccountDataRequest` components declare a width bound and nothing else, and the group identifier admits printable characters only | Add presence, digit and format constraints to the record; leave the group identifier unconstrained | The field edits and the tolerant numeric parse belong to `domain/validation` and `domain`, and duplicating them as constraints would create the competing pass the row above rejects. The money widths deliberately admit a currency sign and grouping commas so `FUNCTION TEST-NUMVAL-C` at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L2201` still gates the parse. A control character in the group identifier would corrupt the fixed-width comparison that `ConcurrentChangeDetector` folds to lower case | A caller reading the record alone sees widths and infers no edits. The printable-character restriction is additive, so a value the source screen could not have delivered is refused rather than stored |
| The fraud collection route declared `page` and `size` as its own bounded parameters and refused `sort` outright, until a performance review replaced the page number with a cursor | Accept a framework `Pageable` argument; accept `sort` and ignore it; page by a cursor and refuse `page` as well | A `Pageable` coerces an unparsable size to the default, clamps an oversized one down and reads a negative page as page zero, each with a `200` and no statement of what it did. A caller asking for 5,000 rows would receive the first 2,000 and be told nothing. The finder orders by assessment time descending in its own name, so any caller order would be silently ignored while the response claimed success. Reading `size` as text and refusing what it cannot serve still holds, and **the third alternative in this row is what the platform now ships**: bounding a page number was not the same as bounding the work it caused, so `page` is refused for exactly the reason `sort` is. See [what a page costs](#what-a-page-costs-at-the-far-end-of-a-history) | A caller cannot choose the order, and a caller that sends `sort` receives a client error rather than a best-effort answer. While `page` was served, a valid request could walk two hundred million entries to answer with two hundred, which is the finding the review raised |
| The notification history route is keyed by the card and its refusal text names the card number | Key the route by account identifier; key it by card token; reuse the source statement heading text | `COSTM01.CPY:L20-L36` keys the read model by card and transaction, and `app/jcl/CREASTMT.JCL` builds it by sorting into that order, so the card is the key this service holds. The delivered route is `GET /notifications/{cardNumber}`, and `CARD_NUMBER_MESSAGE` names the shape rather than the value submitted. The message text has no source ancestor and is marked additive where it appears | A caller holding only an account identifier cannot use the route. The additive text is not an equivalence claim |

### Field edits with no source ancestor

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A value wider than the source field it edits is refused, and the refusal names the width | Truncate to the declared width and edit the truncated copy; accept and store the wider value | A 3270 screen field cannot deliver a value wider than it declares, so the source defines no behaviour for one. Truncating would edit characters the caller did not intend to submit and could pass a value the caller never sent. `AlphabeticRequiredValidator`, `AlphabeticOptionalValidator` and `UsStateZipPrefixValidator` all take this arm | Additive: a caller receives a refusal the source never issued. Each message constant is marked as having no source literal |
| Trailing spaces past the declared width are padding rather than content | Treat any character past the width as content; trim before measuring | The source moves a fixed-width screen field into a fixed-width edit field, so the move drops padding and nothing else. Reporting padding as over-width would refuse values the source accepted | The over-width test inspects every character past the width, so a very long run of spaces is scanned before it is accepted |

### Repository surfaces and duplicate-delivery markers

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Repositories over read-only or append-only aggregates declare only the operations the service performs | Declare finders and writes freely on every interface | `CBSTM03B.CBL:L99-L114` dispatches on a fixed operation code, so the reachable operations were enumerable in the source and are enumerable here. `RejectedTransactionRepository` extends `Repository` and exposes the append, the counts and one bounded retention delete, because the source only ever appended a reject record. `DisclosureGroupRepository` declares no method of its own at all, since no program in `app/cbl/` writes a disclosure group and every read it needs is inherited | Adding an operation is a repository change rather than a call-site change. A reader looking for a finder has to read the interface to learn it is absent by design, and `RepositorySurfaceTest` is what keeps one from appearing |
| The outbox claim query is written in the Jakarta Persistence Query Language rather than as native SQL | A derived query name; a native `FROM outbox_event` statement | The predicate names a state, a due time and an ordering over two columns, which a derived name cannot express readably. A native statement carries no schema, and `spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.default_schema` does not apply to a native string, so it fails wherever the search path does not already name the right schema | The query is validated when the persistence context starts rather than at compile time |
| The processed-event marker is keyed by event identifier and consumed topic together | Key on the event identifier alone; add the consumer group or the consumer class name | An event identifier is assigned by the publishing service, and different producers assign them independently, so two different events can carry one identifier without either producer being at fault. Keyed on the identifier alone, the second of two same-identifier events arriving on two topics loses its claim and its listener writes nothing. That is right for a redelivery, and a dropped side effect for a different event. The topic is carried in the delivery's own `RECEIVED_TOPIC` header, so a listener records what it observed, while a group name is configuration that a rename would change | A consumer that legitimately reads one event from two topics records two markers and performs its side effect twice. No listener in this platform does that. Every service carries the wider key even where it reads one topic, because a contract inherited narrow reintroduces the defect silently on the day a second listener is added |

### Net-new scoring and per-event rendering

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The amount-anomaly rule scores the absolute transaction amount, so a refund scores like a purchase of the same size | Score the signed amount; skip negative amounts | Risk follows magnitude, and a large refund is as unusual as a large purchase. This service is net new, so the choice alters no source arithmetic; the ledger's own refund handling stays source-compatible and is flagged separately | No parity baseline exists for the choice, so it is a design position rather than a verified behaviour |
| An authorization alert renders its balance field as spaces and builds one detail row from the event | Read the balance from a projection; read the read model for the detail rows | An authorization establishes no balance, and the ledger derives one only when it posts. The read model holds posted transactions, so reading it would report every earlier transaction and omit the one the alert is about. `INITIALIZE STATEMENT-LINES` at `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L459` leaves the field in exactly this state | A reader of an authorization alert sees a blank balance and has to know why. The posted alert, which does carry a balance, follows within the same fan-out |

## Integration-reconciliation remediation decisions

A reconciliation pass over the six services read the relay, dead-letter and producer surfaces against
each other rather than one service at a time. It found five places where one service's contract
differed from its four siblings with nothing recording why. Each row below either aligns that service
or states why the difference stands.

### What a relay depends on to reach a broker

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The fraud relay publishes through `messaging/EventPublisherPort`, so no relay on this platform holds a `KafkaTemplate` and also publishes more than one event type | Leave the relay holding the template and document the exception; introduce the port and keep the template beside it for the dead-letter route | The port is the one seam this platform declares so a managed event service can replace Kafka. The fraud relay was the only place a broker type was a compile-time dependency of business code. It reaches the broker from three call sites: the assessment, the unknown-record-type diagnostic and the abandonment diagnostic. The substitution the seam exists for therefore meant editing the relay rather than one adapter. The two producer reliability settings and the eleven-digit key are now checked once, in the adapter, at construction | The port's `publish` takes the event record rather than serialized text, which differs from the authorization, account and card ports. That is deliberate and stated on the interface. This producer is built with `JsonSchemaValidatingSerializer`, so serialization, schema validation and topic binding all happen inside the send, and taking text would serialize twice and validate in two places |
| The ledger relay keeps its `KafkaTemplate` and declares no port, and the divergence is recorded as a next task rather than argued from a property | Introduce the port in the ledger too, so all five producing relays match. Delete the port from fraud again, so the two relays that publish only from a sweep match each other | Four of the five producing services now declare the port and the ledger does not, which is a difference a reader will notice. The reason previously given — that nothing there publishes outside the relay — is equally true of the fraud service, so it no longer distinguishes the two. Aligning the ledger was not in the scope of this pass, and inventing a fresh justification for a difference that has none would be the unexplained deviation Rule 1 forbids | One producing service reaches its broker client directly. [Suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) carries the alignment, and the ledger README states the position rather than defending it |
| Every relay instance identifier defaults to the container hostname, in all five producing services | Keep a per-service literal and require a deployment to override it; generate a random identifier per process | A literal default is one shared identity, and two replicas sharing one make `claimed_by` unreadable. The stranded-claim recovery reads rows a live sibling holds and returns them to `PENDING`, so the same event publishes twice. A Kubernetes container's hostname is its Pod name and a Compose container's is its container identifier, so both are already distinct per replica. A random identifier would be distinct too and would lose a restarting instance its own stranded claims | A deployment that pins one hostname across replicas reintroduces the collision. `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest.everyRelayInstanceIdentifierFallsBackToTheHostname` reads the effective default of all five services, and the Kubernetes ConfigMap deliberately declares no key for it, because an empty key would win over the Pod name |

### What a row nobody can publish is recorded as

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A permanent failure ends a fraud outbox row `ABANDONED` with a dead-letter obligation, and never `PUBLISHED` | Keep marking the row published once the diagnostic landed; spend the remaining nine attempts on a payload that fails identically each time, then let the ceiling abandon it | `RelayState.PUBLISHED` means one thing on this platform: the broker acknowledged the event on its business topic. A payload the schema document refuses, or one no record type reads, reached no consumer. Recording it as published made it indistinguishable in the table, in the retention sweep and in every metric from an assessment that had. The only thing published was the diagnostic saying it had not been. `OutboxEventEntity.abandon` writes the terminal state and the obligation in one write, before anything is attempted, so a refused diagnostic leaves `REQUIRED` standing and a later pass offers it again | `carddemo.fraud.outbox.abandoned` now moves for a permanent failure as well as for a spent one, so a series compared against a reading taken before this change steps up. That is the correction: the counter was silent for exactly the rows an operator most needed to see |
| A shared governed dead letter is keyed on the envelope's own `aggregateId`, and the record it came from is named in the payload | Keep the broker coordinates as the message key; carry the coordinates in both the key and the payload | `dead-letter-v1.json` states that `aggregateId` **is** the message key of the envelope. The authorization and account recoverers keyed on `topic-partition-offset` instead, so the key and the payload disagreed about what the record was. Coordinates are also unique per record, so two refusals of one account spread across partitions and no consumer of the dead-letter topic could read them in order. `sourceTopic`, `sourcePartition` and `sourceOffset` are declared payload fields and still carry every coordinate | The key is `00000000000` for every record a deserializer refused, because a record that never deserialized names no account. All such records therefore land on one partition, which is the correct trade for a topic whose volume is the platform's failure rate |
| The notification service's raw dead-letter producer pins acknowledgement from all replicas and idempotent production, like every other producer on the platform | Leave the two settings at their client defaults on this one template; set them globally and let the template inherit | This is the producer of last resort: the record it carries is the only remaining evidence of a delivery the service could not process. At the client default a leader acknowledgement is enough, so a leader failover between the acknowledgement and the replication loses exactly that record. The template is hand-built and inherits nothing from the bound producer block, which is why the two settings had to be named on it | Two more settings on one template, and a send that now waits for the followers. The template sends only on a terminal failure, so the cost is paid on the failure path alone |
| No deployment file widens a producer send past the budget its relay sweep is bounded by | Raise the relay budget in the deployment that needed the wider send; leave the override and accept an overrunning sweep | The card service's Compose block pinned `delivery.timeout.ms` to 120 seconds against a sweep bounded at five. One slow send therefore held the scheduled thread for twenty-five sweeps' worth of time, and the bound the setting names was decorative. Every service's `application.yml` already pins a send window that fits inside the sweep, so the override was the only thing breaking the invariant and deleting it restored the shipped values | The invariant now lives in a test rather than in a comment. `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest.noDeploymentOverrideWidensASendPastTheRelaySweepBudget` reads the effective value of every producer timeout in `docker-compose.yml` and in the six Kubernetes Deployments. It was proven to fail by restoring the three deleted lines before being kept |
| A publisher checks the topic each event type is bound to, for every event type it can publish | Check the business event's topic and let the dead-letter route pass unchecked; drop the check and rely on the serializer | The card publisher held one configured topic and compared every send against it. A deployment that renamed the dead-letter topic therefore made every terminal diagnostic refuse, which is the one send whose failure loses the record. The authorization publisher already held a map from event type to configured topic, and the card publisher now holds the same two-entry map | A publisher that gains a third event type has to gain a third entry. A missing entry fails the send loudly rather than sending to an unbound topic, which is the failure this prefers |

### What names one card on a request line

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every route that names one card names it by that card's token, and no card number reaches a request line anywhere on this platform | Keep the number in the path and redact it in every log the deployment owns; move the number into a request body and make the read a `POST`. Keep the number and add a token-keyed route beside it | Three routes carried a full Primary Account Number in their path: `GET /cards/{cardNumber}`, `PUT /cards/{cardNumber}` and `GET /notifications/{cardNumber}`. A path is written to a container access log, to a reverse proxy log, into a distributed trace as the span name and into a browser history by the client. Not one of those four is reachable by this application's redaction. Masking a number in a response body while putting it in the request line minimised nothing. The token was already the right shape and already stored. `card.card_token` and `statement_transaction.card_token` are `CHAR(64) NOT NULL UNIQUE`, so one token reaches at most one row, exactly as `KEYS(16 0)` at `app/jcl/CARDFILE.jcl:L54` makes one number reach at most one row. Each route still addresses exactly one card, as its source transaction does. A body-carried number would have made the read a `POST` and published two shapes for one row, which an earlier pass had already tried and withdrawn | A caller needs the token before it can call, and the number no longer supplies it in the request. Three places carry one: the `nextCursor` of a `GET /cards` page, a granted `SCOPE_CARD_` authority, and the derivation `onboarding.md` gives. A token belongs to one `CARD_TOKEN_SECRET`, so a rollover invalidates a written-down token, and every guide derives rather than publishes one |
| Token-to-number resolution happens inside the card service and the source card-number edit still runs on the resolved number | Change `CardUpdateService` to take a token; drop the source edit as unreachable; resolve the token in a shared library both services call | `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L193-L194` edits a card number for sixteen digits and `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L180` prompts when none was given. `CardUpdateService.updateCard(String, CardUpdateRequest)` therefore keeps taking a number, `api/CardController` reads the row the token names and hands that row's number in, and both edits stay reachable and equivalence-tested in `domain/CardUpdateServiceTest`. Resolution lives in the one service that owns the card table, so the number never leaves it | A stored number always satisfies both edits, so a caller of the route reaches neither text. The route answers the same `CARD_NOT_FOUND` outcome for a token naming no row that the service answers for a number naming no row. That keeps the seven outcomes seven, and tells a caller nothing about which lookup missed |
| A card token holding no statement row answers `404`, and the masked number in a history body is read from a row rather than derived from the path | Answer `200` with an empty history and a `null` card number; answer `200` and echo the token in place of the number; fall back to `notification_log` for a masked number | The masked number was derived from the path value while the path carried a number. That let a card with no row still name its card, as `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L318-L325` does in its statement header. A path carrying a token cannot reproduce that, and the alternatives each publish something untrue: a `null` in a required member, or a storage key in a display member. The enumeration risk the old `200` defended against is closed by the token itself. A caller cannot construct one for a card it was not given, and `SCOPE_CARD_` ownership is checked before the read | A status now distinguishes a card this service has posted nothing for from one it has. A caller must already hold the token to ask, and an identity without the matching authority reads `403` first, so the answer discloses nothing it did not already hold. The notification walkthrough starts the ledger, because `transaction.posted` is what fills that read model |
| No exception message of this platform carries an identifier read from an event | Keep the account identifier in `AccountRowMissingException` and rely on the listener stripping it; redact exception text in one place at the logging configuration. Declare the message a governed surface and document who may read it | An exception message reaches a log line written by whatever caught it, a span attribute written by an instrumentation agent, and an error-reporting integration. None of those three is a surface this service governs. The account identifier resolves to a named cardholder through this service's own customer record, so it is pseudonymous rather than absent. Nothing is lost by leaving it out. The identifier is the message key of the delivery and the `aggregateId` of the envelope, so `config/KafkaConsumerConfig` carries it into the dead-letter record beside the source topic, partition and offset. That is a surface this platform does govern | A reader of a raw stack trace no longer sees which account failed and reads the dead-letter record instead. Every sibling failure path on this platform already behaved this way, so the exception was the one exception |

### What a consumer does with a version it cannot act on

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Both notification listeners account for a contract version they cannot act on and acknowledge it, rather than refusing it | Keep refusing and let the retry cycle dead-letter it; derive the missing card token from the masked number or the account identifier. Establish a replay boundary and start each group at the latest offset; run a migration that republishes retained version 1 records at version 2 | Both of this service's tables are keyed on the card token, which only version 2 carries, and a version 1 event is nonetheless governed and valid against its own schema document. Refusing it in the listener spent three delivery attempts and put a valid event on the dead-letter topic as though it were poison.
