Every consumer group here starts at the earliest offset, so a group added to a topic that still retains version 1 records met that route on every one of them. Deriving a token is the one thing that cannot be done. Masking discards twelve of the sixteen digits the derivation reads, and two cards sharing their last four digits mask to one value. A derived key would merge two cardholders under a value no card resolves to. Starting at the latest offset would discard current records as well as old ones, and a republishing migration would have to invent the same missing values. The ledger's `ledger-reject` group already answers a version 1 or 2 decline exactly this way | A replay of only older records now finishes quietly with an empty read model. `carddemo.notification.events.unapplied` is what makes that visible, and it is a series of its own precisely because a consumed count with no alert and no failure was the ambiguity to remove |
| No duplicate-delivery marker is written for a delivery nothing was applied for | Write the marker so a redelivery is silent; write the marker and count it as a duplicate | `processed_event` guards side effects, and there are none to guard, so a marker would assert that the event had been applied. Counting a redelivery as a duplicate would report an event that was never applied as one that had been | A redelivery increments the unapplied series again. That is truthful — the delivery did happen again — and it costs one counter increment and one log line |
| A fraud alert is rendered and stored nowhere, and the guide says so rather than implying a row | Store the row with a placeholder card identity; widen `notification_log` to admit a row with no card; drop the fraud alert altogether | `notification_log` requires a card token and a masked card number, and `FraudFlagged` carries neither: it names a transaction, an account, a score and the rules that fired. A placeholder identity would put a row under a card that does not exist, and widening the table would let a reader count alerts that name no card. The guide previously said both alerts were rendered "straight into `notification_log`", which was true of the authorization alert and false of the fraud one | A fraud alert leaves no trace in any table, only a counter increment and a log line. That is now stated in the service guide beside the limitation the whole table carries, which is that no row anywhere in it is evidence a cardholder was told anything |

### Where each request identity is delivered

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The acquirer verifier is delivered to the authorization service and to no other, on both deployment paths | Keep it in the shared anchor and the shared Secret as before; keep the shared delivery and rely on the role check to make it harmless. Give every identity its own per-service binding | `services/authorization-service/src/main/resources/application.yml` is the one file on the platform that reads `ACQUIRER_USERNAME` or `ACQUIRER_PASSWORD_HASH`. The identity it verifies reaches every card the platform holds. `POST /authorizations` names its card in the request body, so no path variable carries an identifier an ownership scope could be compared against. A bcrypt digest mounted into five services with no code that reads it is a value an attacker can take away. Whoever reaches any one of six containers can grind it offline at leisure. The role check protects the route rather than the digest. The delivery is scoped where each path actually injects. It moves out of the Compose `x-service-environment` anchor and into the authorization service's own block, and out of `carddemo-identity-secret` and into `carddemo-authorization-identity-secret`, which `40-authorization-service.yaml` alone pulls. The card-token key was already scoped this way to two services, so the shape is the one this platform already uses | The administrator, cardholder and monitoring hashes still reach all six, because all six verify against them. `app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD` defines one `USRSEC` dataset the whole region reads, so a platform-wide identity store is the faithful shape. Four hashes now live in two Secrets rather than one, so an operator replacing placeholders has two documents to visit, and `31-secret.example.yaml` says so where the values are. `ComposeEnvironmentIsolationContractTest` fails the build if any other service block or Deployment reaches the acquirer pair again |

## Published-figure and oracle-guard decisions

A quality pass read the run figures `docs/equivalence-results.md` publishes against the reports the
build writes, and read its description of the oracle guard against the harness. Four figures had gone
stale and one asset was protected differently from the other thirteen. Each row below states what
changed and what it costs.

### What holds a published count to the build

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The published run totals are asserted from the reports of the run that produced them. The reactor figure is checked as the published module figure plus what the other projects measured | Restate the four figures and leave them unasserted; capture the counts with a build plugin; drop the totals from the document | Nothing had read a report, so tests added to other modules moved the real figures and the published ones stayed as written. The arithmetic runs on the difference, because this module's own reports are still being written while the assertion runs. The report for the asserting class does not exist yet, and the integration phase has not started. A plugin would add a pinned version outside the dependency bill of materials for a number one test can read | A figure this module alone moves is caught by the per-class closure and by the platform guide having to state the same number, not by the report comparison. The document says which figures the build asserts rather than implying it asserts all of them |
| A report kind no module wrote is left to the closure checks instead of failing the build | Require both report kinds always; skip the whole assertion whenever anything is missing; use a test assumption | One continuous-integration stage runs the reactor to the test phase, which writes every module's unit reports and no integration report anywhere. Demanding both kinds would fail that stage for a reason unrelated to the documents. A kind written by some modules and not others still fails, naming the modules that are missing | A single-module invocation compares no total. Every stage that runs the reactor does compare, and no assumption is used, so this class reports the same case count however it was invoked |
| The two attribute sums are removed from the counting-method row rather than restated | Restate them beside the case counts; publish this module's attribute sums too so the difference can be checked | They existed only to explain why the `tests` attribute must not be summed, and that explanation survives without them. Restating two more absolutes that nothing can assert would have reproduced the defect being fixed | A reader who wants the size of the under-report has to measure it. The row still names the cause, which is a report listing every nested case and counting only its own |
| `scripts/check-published-test-counts.sh` runs after the build rather than inside it, and is a script rather than a test | Assert the figures from a test; derive one published figure from another; publish no figures at all | A count is evidence, so a count nobody measures is a claim. A test cannot measure the run it is part of: the report for its own class does not exist yet and the integration phase of its own module has not started, and every in-build attempt ends up subtracting one published figure from another, which passes whenever both move together. After the build every report is complete, this module's own included, so each published figure is compared against a measured one and nothing is derived from anything else this repository wrote | The check is outside the build, so a run that skips the script publishes unverified figures. The workflow runs it as its own step, and the results document names the command a reader can repeat |

### What proves an oracle row was read

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `posting-summary.csv` is read through the shared `ExpectedOutcomes` reader by both of its consumers, and the whole-feed comparison answers every row of it from the run | Correct the prose to describe the bespoke loader; keep the loader and add a row-count assertion beside it | It was the last asset with a reader of its own, so it was the only one with no record of which rows an assertion looked up. A row added to it went unread, and only the documentation inventory noticed. One reader for fourteen assets also means one column contract, one quoting rule and one duplicate-key refusal | The comparison now lives in the test that owns the database, because three of the twenty rows are only measurable there. That test grew rather than a new one appearing, which keeps the published per-class counts unmoved |
| `posting.ledger_rejected_transaction_count` is answered by counting reject rows between the authorized stream and the declined stream | Delete the row as unused; assert it after both streams and change the expected value to thirty-eight | The row states that the posting consumer's own reject path writes nothing for this feed, which is true and worth proving. After the declined stream the same table holds thirty-eight rows, so that is the only point in the run where the claim is measurable. Deleting it would have removed a fact rather than checked it | The assertion depends on the two streams staying in that order inside one test. The failure message says so, and the order is the one `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L211` gates |
| The documents and the reader's own documentation now describe an end-of-test assertion rather than an `@AfterAll` method | Add `@AfterAll` methods so the prose becomes true; leave the wording as it was | No equivalence class has ever had one. The assertion closes the test that walks the rows, which is stronger than a lifecycle method. It holds however the runner orders the class, and it names the walking test in the failure | A reader who knew the old wording has to notice the new one. The mechanism is stated in one place, the [asset consumption](equivalence-results.md#asset-consumption) section, and repeated nowhere |

## Picture-field ceiling QA-remediation decisions

Runtime testing authorized two amounts that each fit `DALYTRAN-AMT PIC S9(09)V99` and whose sum did
not fit `TRAN-CAT-BAL PIC S9(09)V99`. Both authorizations were approved and published, and the ledger
then answered SQLSTATE 22003, rolled the posting back, exhausted its retries and dead-lettered the
event. One approved transaction reached no balance, no accumulator, no category balance and no
`TransactionPosted`. The rows below state what changed.

### Where a store that exceeds its field is expressed

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every store into a COBOL monetary picture field keeps the low-order digits of the sum and its sign | Widen the four columns and declare the deviation; reject an amount that would overflow; leave the database error as the outcome | None of the five `ADD` statements at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L508`, `:L527`, `:L547`, `:L549` and `:L551` carries an `ON SIZE ERROR` phrase. The phrase appears in none of the twenty-eight programs under `app/cbl/`, so the source store drops the digit and continues. Rule T7 requires a source defect to be reproduced and flagged. Widening is what `V3__velocity_total_headroom.sql` did for `velocity_window.total_amount`, and that column has no copybook field behind it while all four of these do. Section 0.3.1 derives `NUMERIC(11,2)` from `PIC S9(09)V99` and `NUMERIC(12,2)` from `PIC S9(10)V99`. Rejecting the amount would decline a transaction the source posts | A cardholder past the ceiling sees a balance smaller than the postings sum to, which is the defect the source has. Register item 66 of [business-rule flags](business-rule-flags.md) carries the flag, and `V7__category_balance_ceiling.sql` carries it on the columns themselves |
| The category-balance truncation is written into the statement as `MOD`, and the account figures are truncated in Java | Read the row, truncate in Java and write it back; add a database constraint; keep one mechanism everywhere | The sum for `category_balance` is formed inside the single `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` that closes the create race. The store is therefore in the statement, and the truncation belongs there with it. Reading first would reopen the window a consumer group of more than one instance meets. The three account figures are summed in Java, so `CobolDecimal.truncateToPictureField` is the store there, as `notification-service` already used it for `WS-TOTAL-AMT` | The divisor is a literal in native text that no mapping check reads. `TransactionCategoryBalanceRepositoryTest` reads it back from the annotation against `PicClause` and reads the stored value back from a real database |
| A dropped digit is reported at warning level where the sum is formed in Java, and nowhere on the statement path | Warn on both paths by reading the row back after the statement; warn on neither; raise the level to error | A statement cannot write a log line. A follow-up read to discover what it stored would be a second round trip on the posting path, whose answer another instance could already have moved. Error level would page an operator for behaviour the source has. The line names the integer-digit capacity and withholds the figure, matching the account identifier withheld from every other ledger line | The category-balance path drops a digit silently at runtime. The migration comment, the register item and the two tests are where a reader learns it can happen |
| The two authoring prompts that say the ledger narrows nothing are read as statements about a `MOVE` between equal widths | Follow them literally and leave the ceiling as it was; change them | Both bullets rest on operand and target holding the same width, which settles a `MOVE` and not an `ADD` whose sum is wider than either operand. The runtime evidence is that the sum does exceed the field. The narrowing helper is called at the store rather than on an operand, so nothing is narrowed on the way in | A reader comparing the delivered classes with those prompts finds this row rather than an unexplained difference |

### What the oracle carries, against a prohibition that admits no invented case

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `synthetic-boundary-cases.csv` gains one case, `SYN-CATBAL-CEILING`, and one `COVERAGE` row that measures why the fixtures cannot reach it | Assert the ceiling only in the two service test suites; add the case to a fixture-derived ledger; leave the oracle as it stood | The file's own `PROHIBITION` topic reads `no_additional_cases_may_be_invented`, and the discipline behind it is that a case must be a behaviour the nine fixtures cannot produce. The new `COVERAGE` row reports how many seeded category balances come within one integer digit of the ceiling, which is zero, so the case qualifies on the file's own terms. Every expected value in it is computed from `PicClause` and `CobolDecimal` or measured from `app/cbl/`, including a census of the `ON SIZE ERROR` phrase | The prohibition row now stands beside a case that was added after it was written. This row is the record of that, and a `SUMMARY` count of the new family keeps the case ledger closed against its own arithmetic |

## Stored text width QA-remediation decisions

Runtime testing read a seeded customer and an updated one through the same route and got two widths
for the same field. A submitted five-character given name reached `first_name VARCHAR(25)` as five
characters, while the seeded twin held twenty-five. The row below states what changed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every submitted text component reaches its column at the width its Picture clause declares, and an absent component stays absent | Change the fourteen customer columns and the two account columns to `CHAR(n)` and let the database pad; record the unpadded pass-through as a deliberate deviation instead | `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4010-L4059` moves each screen field into the fixed-width field of `CUSTOMER-RECORD` and `:L4086` rewrites the whole 500-byte record, and `:L3962-L4002` with `:L4066` do the same for the 300-byte account record, so a stored value in the source always occupies its whole field. The mapper already brought the identifier, the three dates and the three social-security parts to their declared widths, so the fourteen text components were the inconsistency rather than the rule. Padding at the mapper keeps one place responsible for the width, where `CHAR(n)` would move that responsibility into six migrations and leave the response shape depending on the column type | A caller that submits a value and reads it back sees trailing spaces it did not send, which is what the source stores and what a seeded row already answered. Change detection is unaffected: `domain/ConcurrentChangeDetector` and `domain/AccountUpdateService` bring both operands to the declared width before comparing, and `UsPhoneNumberValidator` and `UsStateZipPrefixValidator` pad and trim their own operands |

## Executive-deck review-remediation decisions

A static review of `presentation/executive-summary.html` returned fifteen findings. Two concerned
figures the deck reports from its own evidence documents and had drifted from. Five concerned colour
and semantics a screen reader or a low-vision reader depends on. Four concerned the order in which
the deck starts its own libraries and pins their bytes, and one concerned the test that guards all of
it.

The rows below record the choices that were open. A figure corrected to match the document that
owns it is not among them, because that correction had no alternative.

### What the deck states about its own evidence

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The risk slide states that source defects are reproduced for parity and points at the register, instead of counting them | Move the count from two to the current number; drop the risk row entirely | A count of reproduced defects is a second copy of a figure the register already owns, and it had already drifted once while the register grew to sixty-six items. A sentence that points at the register cannot drift | A reader of that slide alone learns that defects are reproduced but not how many, and has to open the register for the figure |
| Every figure the deck reports from an evidence document is compared against that document on each build | Re-read the deck against its sources at release time; annotate each figure with its source in a comment beside it | Both drifted figures were correct when they were written. Only a check that reads both sides survives the next edit to either side, and a comment records provenance without detecting divergence | The check reads six artifacts, so an intended edit to any of them fails the presentation test until the deck is moved with it |

### The deck's accessible colour and type treatment

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The title eyebrow keeps its teal and gains a navy pill behind it | Darken the teal; move the eyebrow off the hero gradient; accept the shortfall because the text is decorative | The teal measures 3.23 to 1 on the hero gradient's lightest stop and 13.17 to 1 on the navy. Rule 4 enumerates both colours, so the readable pairing was already in the palette and only the surface beneath the text had to change | The pill is a shape the hero did not have, and its block padding moves the whole title block down by nine pixels |
| `--blitzy-text-muted` stays defined and colours no text | Delete the property; keep it on the legends and metric labels; redefine it as a darker grey | Rule 4 enumerates the property, so deleting it would break the brand block the specification fixes. Every surface it could sit on in this deck is light, where it measures 2.85 to 1 | A reader of the style block finds a defined property with no use, and the reason is in this table rather than in the file |
| Each table names itself with a visually hidden `caption` instead of an `aria-label` | Keep the `aria-label`; show the caption; name each table from the heading above it | A caption is the element table markup provides for the purpose, and it is announced as part of the table. An `aria-label` on a table replaces the content a screen reader would otherwise compute from it | The caption sits in a clipped one-pixel box, a technique a later reader could mistake for a layout defect |
| The keyboard focus ring is a teal outline inside a deep-violet halo | Restore the browser default outline; use one colour; leave the reveal.js default in place | The reveal.js stylesheet removes the outline from its own controls, so a keyboard reader had no indicator at all. Two layers stay visible over both the light surfaces and the dark gradients, which no single colour does | The ring extends six pixels beyond the control it surrounds, which crowds the control cluster at the smallest viewport |
| Motion is disabled in the stylesheet and the reveal.js transition setting is untouched | Set the transition to none when the preference is set; remove the transition from the configuration | Rule 4 fixes the transition value, so changing it would fail the enumerated configuration. A media query changes the rendered result without touching what the specification pins | Reveal continues to compute a transition that the stylesheet then suppresses, so the setting and the behaviour disagree by design |

### The deck-local scale

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A named type, spacing, radius, icon and diagram scale replaces sixty raw literals, each snapped to its nearest existing neighbour | Keep the literals; move every value onto a strict eight-pixel grid; take a scale from a library | Sixty literals across ninety-three uses carried no relationship a reader could see, and the deck may hold no local dependency, so the scale has to live in the file. Snapping rather than regularising kept the rendered result within two pixels, which a comparison of all sixteen slides confirmed | Ten values stay inline because they are relative to their own text rather than to the scale, so the scale is not the only source of dimension in the file |

### Diagram layout and lifecycle

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Figure 1 keeps a left-to-right outer graph with top-to-bottom subgraphs and one invisible link | Three measured alternatives: a top-to-bottom outer graph with left-to-right subgraphs, with and without the link; and a left-to-right graph without the link | All four were rendered into one panel and measured. The chosen one is the only candidate with no overlap and no dead space, and its effective type size is twelve pixels against nine, seven and eleven. One alternative painted a node over most of a word in the subgraph label | The invisible link exists only to order two subgraphs, so a reader of the diagram source meets an edge that means nothing in the architecture |
| A rendered diagram asks reveal.js to lay its stage out again, and nothing writes the stage transform | Keep writing the transform and restoring a captured value; render every diagram once before the deck starts; leave the stage alone and accept a mis-scaled diagram | Writing the stage transform to a fixed scale stranded the entire deck at that scale whenever a resize arrived during a render. Asking reveal.js to lay out again produces the same refit through the call reveal.js provides for it | The refit runs once per diagram slide instead of once for the deck, so a deck with more diagrams asks for more layouts |
| Mermaid loads through an import expression whose rejection is caught, and a failed load leaves a readable sentence in each frame **(the loading half SUPERSEDED by "Every library the deck runs is digested, and so is the deck’s own module"; the sentence still ships)** | A static import in the same module; a second module; no failure path at all | A static import that fails aborts the whole module, and that module also held the call that starts reveal.js, so one unreachable library took every slide down. An import expression fails in isolation, and the sentence names the two documents that carry the same views | The library is requested after the module begins rather than with it, so the first diagram appears marginally later The library is now fetched by a script element the module creates, which is asynchronous in the same way and carries the digest an import expression could not. Verified in a browser with a tampered digest: the fetch was refused, every slide still navigated, all seventeen icons drew, and all three frames carried the sentence. |
| A blocked reveal.js degrades to a static reading mode driven by one class on the document body | Report the failure and stop; retry the load; leave the failure unhandled | Reveal.js adds the classes that give the slide container its height, so without it the page painted nothing at all. The reading mode restores the height and renders every slide in document order, which keeps the content reachable | The reading mode is a second layout over the same markup, so a change to the slide structure has to be considered in both |
| Sections in the reading mode take pointer events again | Leave the reveal.js rule in force; disable that rule for every section in every mode | The reveal.js stylesheet stops pointer events on any slide that is not the present one, and in the reading mode no slide is present, so nothing in the document answered a pointer. The override is scoped to the reading mode and matches nothing when reveal.js starts normally | The measurable effect is hit testing rather than text selection, because the engine this deck was measured in already allows selection through that rule. The deck carries no links today, so the override guards a future one rather than enabling anything now |

### Supply chain

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The three classic assets carry a SHA-384 digest and an anonymous origin; the diagram module and the font stylesheet carry neither **(SUPERSEDED by "Every library the deck runs is digested, and so is the deck’s own module")** | Pin all five; pin none; copy the libraries into the repository and serve them locally | A digest attaches to an element that has the attribute, and an import expression has none. The font stylesheet is generated per user agent, so its bytes differ between readers and no one digest describes it. Serving locally is unavailable because the deck may hold no local dependency | Two of the five resources are pinned by version and origin alone, which is what the comment in the document head states A security review then found the unpinned module carrying nine advisories, and the diagram library moved onto an element that does carry the attribute. The font stylesheet is still the one asset pinned by version and origin alone. |

### What guards the deck

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The presentation test reads each figure from the artifact that owns it and compares the two, and locks no implementation detail | Keep asserting fixed strings; assert nothing about the figures; generate the deck from the evidence documents | The previous guard required two strings this pass removed, so it would have failed the corrected deck, and it held a dead-letter topology the services had already moved past. It also accepted a class that existed only in the stylesheet, because it searched the whole file for the class name instead of the markup | The test parses markup, stylesheet and script separately and reads a service configuration, so it is longer and a change to any of those shapes reaches it |

## Read-order and published-bound remediation decisions

A review read the paged assessment route of the fraud detection service against the document that
publishes it and against the index that serves it. It found three statements of one contract that
did not agree. The rows below state what changed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The paged assessment read orders by `assessed_at` descending and then by `transaction_id` descending, and the index that serves it carries the same three columns | Order on the instant alone, as before; add a surrogate sequence column and order on that; replace offset paging with keyset paging over the two columns | `assessed_at` is a timestamp this service stamps, so two assessments of one account share it whenever two authorizations arrive inside the same microsecond, and one burst on one account produces exactly that. A sort with ties is not a total order, so SQL leaves a tied pair's order unspecified and offset paging then repeats one row and skips another. `transaction_id` is already the primary key: unique, never updated, and present in every row, so it makes the order total at no storage cost. A surrogate sequence would add a column to carry a property the key already has. Keyset paging is the better answer for a large table and a larger change than this route's contract has: the published parameters are `page` and `size`, and changing them would break every caller of a demonstration surface | The tie is broken by an identifier rather than by anything a reader would call meaningful, so two assessments of one instant are ordered by their transaction identifiers and not by anything about the transactions. `V6__assessment_paging_tiebreaker.sql` rebuilds the index rather than adding one beside it, so a database applying it pays one index build |
| Which way a tie fell before was decided by the plan, so the guarantee is asserted at the finder name rather than at an observed row order | Assert only the observed order of a tied pair; assert the plan; leave it to the physical index | A tied pair came back in the wanted order in this environment both before and after the change, because the index supplied an order SQL did not promise. A test that only reads the rows therefore passes on a defect, and it would keep passing until a planner chose a sequential scan or a sort on a larger table. The declared finder name is the contract, and reflection over it fails the moment the tiebreaker is removed | The assertion reads a method name, so a finder that expressed the same order through a `Sort` argument instead would pass it without ordering anything. No finder on this platform does that, and the paged read of this route is the only one the assertion covers |
| The published `page` and `size` shapes carry the bounds the handler enforces, and a test drives the pattern and the handler's constants through one value set | Publish numeric `minimum` and `maximum` and change the parameters to numbers; leave the shapes loose and describe the bounds in prose alone; tighten the handler to refuse the non-canonical forms a pattern cannot express | The published shapes admitted a seven-digit page and a three-digit size while the handler refused anything above 1000000 and above 200, so a generated client accepted values the route answers 400 to. Numeric parameters cannot be used: these are declared as text precisely so that `?page=` is refused rather than silently taking a default, and `minimum` and `maximum` do not apply to a string. Prose alone is what the document already had. Tightening the handler would change what a caller receives for `+7` or a padded value, which no finding asked for | The pattern describes digit forms, while the handler strips surrounding whitespace before it reads the number, so a value with spaces is accepted by the route and not described by the shape. Both descriptions say so, and the contract is a subset of what is accepted rather than a superset of it |

## Cardholder-alert fidelity and log-rendering remediation decisions

A review read the notification service against `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL`, against the document that
publishes its one route, and against the decision that governs how a card token may be logged. It
found a bounded alert that omitted the transaction it reported. It also found three statements of a
contract that described behaviour the code does not have, and a rendering that disclosed a linkable
identifier. The rows below state what changed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A rendered alert carries the card's most recent `MAXIMUM_STATEMENT_ROWS` rows, read from the newest end of the key and reversed for presentation | Stream every row of the card as the source did and drop the ceiling; keep reading the oldest rows and add the triggering transaction to them separately; keep the oldest rows and describe the alert as a history extract | The read was ascending under a ceiling of 200, so past row 200 every alert omitted the transaction that triggered it, totalled a subset the cardholder had already been shown, and still recorded a rendered-alert row under the identifier it had left out. Dropping the ceiling is the source's own behaviour, but `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L458-L504` wrote one record at a time to a sequential file while a renderer here assembles every row in memory before joining them, and AAP section 0.2.2 keeps full statement generation out of scope: this service replaces the customer-facing alert tail alone. Reading from the newest end costs nothing and makes the ceiling honest, because an identifier is minted from one monotonic sequence and left-padded so its text order is its allocation order, and `TransactionPostedConsumer` writes the row before it asks for the alert — so the triggering transaction holds the highest identifier of its card and any ceiling of at least one row includes it. Appending it to an oldest-rows read would have put a row out of key order and double-counted it whenever the card held fewer rows than the ceiling | The alert is a recent-activity extract rather than a full statement, and its total is the total of the rows it shows rather than of the card. That is a declared additive divergence from `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L417-L432`, which totalled every row between two key breaks. `GET /notifications/{cardNumber}` answers the whole history with no ceiling, so nothing is unreachable |
| The unlimited history read stays, and `V7__statement_read_bounds.sql` corrects the schema comment that denied it | Bound the history route and keep the comment; add paging to the history route; edit the `V1__schema.sql` comment in place | `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L429` totals every row of one card between two key breaks, so the unlimited read is the source-faithful one and the comment claiming every read names a limit was the untrue half. Paging was tried and retired on this route already, recorded under [The notification history route](#the-notification-history-route). Editing `V1__schema.sql` would change the checksum of an applied migration, which Flyway compares at every start | The correction lives on the constraint comment in the catalogue while the stale line comment stays in the `V1__schema.sql` text, so a reader of that file alone sees both. The V7 header names the file and the lines it supersedes |
| The published `500` description states the diagnostics the handler actually writes | Add the fault message and stack trace to the log so the document becomes true; leave the description as it was | The handler records the route template, the status and the class names of the fault and its causes, and deliberately records neither the message nor a trace, because a message quotes the value that caused the failure — a constraint violation, a query timeout or a broken connection would put a row value, a statement or a data-source URL into an ordinary log. Making the document true by adding the logging would undo that, and the finding said not to | A reader learns the failure class and no more, so diagnosing further needs the datastore's or the platform's own telemetry. The description now says that rather than implying a trace exists |
| The wording says an alert is rendered and recorded, never sent | Build a delivery port with real outcomes so "sent" becomes true; leave the heading | This service reaches no mail, message, webhook or push gateway, `ck_notification_log_outcome` admits `RENDERED_NOT_SENT` alone, and the constructor writes that constant rather than accepting one. A heading reading "both sent" is a claim a customer-facing document could repeat. A delivery port is a capability rather than a wording change and it is carried as a task in [suggested next tasks](suggested-next-tasks.md) | The platform still renders alerts nobody receives, which is the demonstration's actual posture. Every statement of it now says so |
| A log rendering reports the card token as present and withholds its value, using the one platform marker | Render the token; render a prefix of it; drop the field from the rendering | The token discloses no digit of a card number and it names one card for as long as its key stands, so a log line carrying it lets any reader follow that card across every request that touched it, and a prefix of a keyed code is equally linkable. That is the decision already recorded under [Report a card token as present or withheld in a log rendering, never in full](#what-names-one-card-on-a-request-line), and this rendering was the last place on the platform that did not follow it. Dropping the field would hide that a row carries a token at all | An operator cannot correlate two rows of one card from a log line, and locates a row by transaction identifier instead, which the rendering still carries |

## Card ownership, source-fidelity and status-domain remediation decisions

A review read the card service against `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl`, against the two documents that publish
its three routes, and against the decision that governs what a lost race answers. It found a replica
table no delivered path read or wrote, while a migration comment said card events kept it current. It
found a refusal that answered a name in a case the source does not store, and a column whose domain
lived in one writer alone. Four further statements described a route, an event and a serialization
path the code does not have. The rows below state what changed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `domain/CardCrossReferenceReconciler` reconciles this service's `card_xref` row against the card row on every update that commits, inside the same transaction, and counts the three outcomes it can reach | Leave the table seeded and unread and carry the reconciliation as an open task, as the guide did; drop the table, the entity and the repository; consume a `CardUpdated` event and refresh from it; run a scheduled sweep over the whole table; create the missing row from the card | The plan lists `card_xref` among the tables this service owns, so removing it needs an owner-approved plan change rather than a code change, and a table nothing reads is a copy that diverges silently. An event cannot do the work: `CardUpdated` carries a masked card number and both tables are keyed on the full sixteen characters, so no event this platform publishes can name a row. `CARD-ACCT-ID PIC 9(11)` at `app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L6` is the mapping this service owns, so the card row is the authority and a card update is the one moment this service holds both copies of it. Running inside the caller's transaction under `MANDATORY` propagation makes the card row, the replica and the queued event one unit, which is the property `outbox/OutboxWriter` already has. A sweep and the reconciler answer different questions, and the sweep is the larger one | A card nobody updates is never compared, so this closes divergence on the path this service owns and not everywhere; `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries that residual. A card number holding no replica row is counted on `carddemo.card.xref.missing` and no row is invented, because `XREF-CUST-ID PIC 9(09)` at `app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy:L6` is mandatory and the card record carries no customer identifier. A correction moves a mapping without publishing anything, so a reader of the replica learns of it from the meter and the log line rather than from an event |
| A lost race answers the refreshed embossed name folded to upper case, which is the value the source moves | Keep answering the stored mixed-case value, as an earlier revision did; fold for the comparison and answer the unfolded value with a note; change the decision record to match the code | `9300-CHECK-CHANGE-IN-REC` at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1499-L1501` runs `INSPECT CARD-EMBOSSED-NAME CONVERTING LIT-LOWER TO LIT-UPPER` on the record area itself, so the field is folded in place before `MOVE CARD-EMBOSSED-NAME TO CCUP-OLD-CRDNAME` at `app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1513` refreshes the saved value. The refusal therefore carries the folded name, and this log already said so. The account service folds a copy rather than the record area at `app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4109-L4193`, which is why its own row decides the opposite and both rows are correct | A caller that submitted `Ward Jones` reads `WARD JONES` back from a refusal, which looks like an unrequested change until the source is read. The published example and both member descriptions now carry the folded form, and `CardChangeDetectionTest` asserts it with the source locator in the message |
| The `active_status` domain is enforced in three places: `ck_card_active_status` on the column, one shared helper on the read contracts, and the inbound request record that already held it | Leave it to the update path alone, as before; declare a PostgreSQL enumerated type or domain for the column; annotate the response components with Bean Validation; declare the pair on `entity/CardEntity` | `V1__schema.sql` declared the column `CHAR(1) NOT NULL` and constrained nothing, so a direct load could store a third value and a read answered it inside a schema that enumerates two. A `CHECK` holds every writer including a load that bypasses this service, which is the writer the API could not see. A dedicated type would change the column type in a migration that has run and would reach the entity mapping for no additional protection. Bean Validation binds a request and never an outbound record, so an annotation there would enforce nothing. The pair is declared on `api/dto/CardSummary` rather than on the entity so that a response contract does not depend on the persistence layer to know what it may answer | A row already outside the domain would fail the migration rather than being corrected by it. None exists: `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt` carries `Y` and `N` only, and `CardRepositoryIT` asserts that over the loaded table. A read now refuses a row it would previously have answered, which is the intended trade: a 500 naming the domain is better than a body contradicting its own schema |
| The card outbox payload is written through `CardUpdated.toValidatedJson()`, the shared publish-side gate, rather than through a mapper the writer owns | Keep the private mapper and correct the configuration comment to describe it; keep the private mapper and delete the gate as unused; move every service's outbox writer onto its gate in one change | The configuration comment, the event record's own documentation and the two tests that assert the wire form's privacy properties all described the gate, and the writer used a private `ObjectMapper` with a schema check of its own, so the properties those tests proved belonged to a text production never produced. The gate checks the same document and adds two screens the writer had not: a forbidden property, and a card number or government identifier in a free-text property. Every message it writes names JSON pointers, keywords and property names and no value, so nothing about a refusal becomes less safe | The gate reports a refusal as a Kafka `SerializationException`, which names a concern this transaction has not reached, so the writer wraps it as the `IllegalArgumentException` its contract declares and keeps the cause. The account service still writes its two events through a mapper of its own, and its configuration describes that accurately, so two shapes now coexist on the platform. Its own `toValidatedJson` methods remain exercised by tests alone, and their claim to be the only path from those events to a topic is the statement a later checkpoint has to settle one way or the other |

## Rule 1 rationale-location decisions

A review read every executable file of this platform against Rule 1. That rule makes this log the
single source of "why" and allows a code comment to carry mechanics and provenance. It found decision
essays in twenty-four migration headers and in Java comments, and fifteen Javadoc blocks separated
from the declaration they described. The rows below state what moved and where the decisions now live.

### What identifies one delivery of one event

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A delivery is identified by the event and the topic it arrived on, so `processed_event` is keyed on `(event_id, consumed_topic)` in all six services | Keep the key on `event_id` alone, as `V1__schema.sql` declared it; key on the consumer group; key on the listener or service name; give each listener its own marker table | Every producing service assigns its own event identifiers and none knows what another assigns, so two different events on two topics may carry one identifier without either producer being at fault. Keyed on the identifier alone, the second of that pair read a marker its own topic never wrote, concluded it had already handled the event, and applied nothing — which is the right outcome for a redelivery and a silently dropped effect for a different event, with nothing raised and nothing dead-lettered. The topic is what a delivery carries in its own `RECEIVED_TOPIC` header, so a listener records what it observed; a key built from configuration would change identity whenever a group was renamed. Duplicate suppression within one topic is unchanged, which is the property every consumer relies on | A marker written before the change may carry a null topic, and a primary-key column cannot. Those rows take the sentinel `(no topic header)`, which `entity/ProcessedEventEntity.NO_CONSUMED_TOPIC` also declares and which no Kafka topic name can match because a topic name holds only `[a-zA-Z0-9._-]`. Six migrations carry the change: account `V6`, authorization `V6`, card `V3`, fraud `V4`, ledger `V5` and notification `V3`, which made it first |

### Where rationale lives, and what that cost

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The rationale essays are removed from the twenty-four migration headers that carried them and from the Java comments that argued a choice; each file keeps what it declares, its source locators and a pointer to this log | Leave the essays and rely on this log for the same content; add a new migration per service announcing that rationale moved; keep the essays and drop the duplicated rows from this log; leave the shipped headers and apply the rule to new files only | Rule 1 makes this log the single source of "why" and treats an unexplained deviation as a defect, and a decision recorded in two places drifts: the six topic-key headers argued a decision this log did not carry at all, which is that drift already realised. A new migration cannot resolve it, because the essay is repository text and never reached a `COMMENT ON` statement — nothing in any database says it. Grandfathering the shipped files would leave the rule enforced nowhere, since every migration on the platform is already shipped | Removing text from a migration changes the checksum Flyway stores for it, so a database that had already applied one of the twenty-four refuses to start with a checksum mismatch. Every test database is created empty and applies the whole history in one run, and no deployment of this platform exists; a developer holding an older demo volume resets it with `docker compose down -v` or runs a Flyway repair, which `docs/onboarding.md` now records under its pitfalls. The statements of `V13` and `V14` are still untouched and still superseded by `V15`, because that half of the discipline is about what a database was told |
| Fifteen Javadoc blocks moved onto the declaration each describes, and three were dropped as duplicates of the text below them | Merge each pair into one block; delete the orphan and its content; leave them and rely on the reader noticing | A Javadoc block followed by another Javadoc block documents nothing: the compiler and every generated document attach the second, and the first is read as belonging to a declaration it does not describe. Three of the fifteen restated the block beneath them, one described a helper that no longer exists, and one still called `CardUpdated` a version 2 event | The review named three sites and the same defect stood at fifteen. Fixing three would have left twelve, so the whole set moved; nothing about behaviour changes and no test asserts a Javadoc |
| The guard also reads the line rather than only the heading: nine argument shapes fail wherever they appear in a shipped comment, and the pointer form stays exempt | Widen it to every `because` and every `rather than`; keep the heading rules and rely on review for the rest; forbid multi-sentence comments outright | A later review found rationale in five artifacts that never wrote a heading: the composition argued its transport posture, alternatives and accepted risk over thirty lines, the namespace manifest argued a pinned admission version, the workflow justified a stage, the deck's script justified how it loads its renderer, and the aggregator justified a raised dependency. Every one of those arguments was already in this log, so each was a second copy of a decision that can drift from it. The nine shapes are a decision being defended — a deliberate choice, not an oversight, what makes it acceptable, the reasoning behind it, alternatives considered, a risk accepted, a first-person choice, an announced count of reasons, and a value named because of something — and a comment stating behaviour, a locator or an operational fact matches none of them. Reading every `because` was measured first and rejected: it fires on 30 comments that describe what code does, and a guard that flags mechanics is a guard somebody switches off | Nine shapes are not every shape. An argument written in words none of them name still passes, so the guard raises the floor rather than closing the class, and the review that reads prose remains the second line. The pointer exemption is deliberately narrow: the log has to be named on the same line or the next, because a log named thirty lines later answers nothing a reader met |
| Both rules are held by a build check rather than by review: `equivalence-tests/RationaleLocationContractTest` reads every migration, every demo overlay and every java file of the platform | Leave the rules to review; check only the files this remediation touched; check the migrations alone and leave javadoc to a reader | A rule enforced nowhere decays at the file somebody adds next, which is how twenty-four headers and fifteen orphaned blocks accumulated in the first place. The check also reads each pointer, so a file that sends a reader to the log names it at one path that resolves. Three detectors could pass by matching nothing, so a fifth test runs all three over text carrying each defect and over text carrying none | The detectors read text, so a rationale essay written without one of the headings they look for still passes. The check names the shapes a review actually found rather than claiming to read intent, and its own file is skipped because it holds the samples the detectors are proved against |
## Configuration-review remediation decisions

A configuration and infrastructure review read the deployment surfaces against the sources that bind
them. Three of its findings concerned how a relay timing reaches a service, six concerned the
cluster bootstrap and six concerned commands the guides print. The rows below state what changed
for each group. The environment-inventory
findings from the same review are recorded under
[Configuration truthfulness and published-key decisions](#configuration-truthfulness-and-published-key-decisions),
and the builder-stage finding under [Container packaging](#container-packaging-decisions).

### How a relay timing reaches the code that uses it

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `messaging/KafkaEventPublisher` in the authorization and card services carries no stereotype and reads no property placeholder; `config/KafkaProducerConfig` builds the one instance from the bound properties record | Keep the scanned component and add the positivity check to the record alone; keep the component and inject the whole properties record into its constructor; annotate the constructor parameter with a Spring Expression Language reference to the properties bean | `carddemo.outbox.relay.publish-timeout` was bound twice: once as a component of `Outbox.Relay`, which the record's constraints govern, and once as a constructor `@Value` in the publisher, which they do not. The publisher read the second one, so a constraint added to the record would have governed a value nothing used. Building the publisher from the record makes the binding that validates the value the binding the publisher reads, and it is the shape `account-service/config/KafkaProducerConfig` already had. Injecting the record into the publisher would put a configuration type in the messaging package and make every unit test assemble a properties tree to construct one publisher; a Spring Expression Language reference would keep the validation but depend on a bean name no reader would think to check | The publisher is no longer discovered by component scan, so a context that registers `KafkaProducerConfig` and not the publisher would have neither. Both services register it, `outbox/OutboxRelay` injects the port rather than the class, and the constructor signature is unchanged, so every existing unit test constructs the publisher exactly as before |
| Zero and a negative duration are refused for both relay timings, in the record's compact constructor, in all five relay-owning services | Refuse them with a Jakarta constraint annotation; document the requirement and leave it unchecked; check the value at the first send | `@NotNull` on a `Duration` rejects an absent value and nothing else, and no standard annotation expresses "positive duration", so the check has to be written. Neither value has a working meaning at zero: a claim timeout of zero strands every claim the moment it is taken, so one sweep recovers the batch the previous sweep is still publishing, which is the double publish the claim exists to prevent; a publish timeout of zero fails every send the instant it is issued, so the row stays unpublished and each sweep claims it again. Both failures are silent — a timeout per attempt and rows that never leave the table — where a start-up failure names the property. The fraud service had no check and the other four did, so one variable set to zero switched the guarantee off in that service alone | The compact constructor throws rather than reporting through the validator, so the message reaches a reader inside the binding failure rather than as a constraint violation list. That is the shape the four services that already refused a claim timeout used, so the two new checks read like the existing ones |
| Every relay ships a claim horizon of two minutes | Leave the fraud service at thirty seconds and document the divergence; lower the other four to thirty seconds | `.env.example`, `docker-compose.yml` and `deploy/k8s/30-configmap.yaml` each carry two minutes, so every deployed path already ran the fraud relay at two minutes and the shipped default described only the four other services. The divergence was reachable exactly where nothing set the variable — a test, or the service started directly — which is where a reader measures behaviour. A default that four services share and a fifth does not is worse than no default, because the number a reader carries from one relay is wrong for the next | Nothing deployed changes, since all three deployment files already set the value. A test that relied on the fraud relay recovering a claim after thirty seconds would now wait two minutes; none does, because the relay tests construct their own `Relay` record with an explicit horizon |

### How the cluster is bootstrapped, and what the manifests declare about themselves

Six findings concerned `deploy/k8s/`. Four of them were commands a reader would have run and been
refused by, and two were manifests describing themselves inaccurately to tooling that acts on the
description.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `kubectl apply -k deploy/k8s` is the only apply command any document states | Keep the filename-ordered glob and add `kustomization.yaml` to the exclusion; move the kustomization out of this folder; apply each file by name in a list | The glob listed `deploy/k8s/*.yaml`, filtered out `31-secret.example.yaml`, and piped what was left into `xargs -n1 kubectl apply -f`. It therefore reached `kustomization.yaml`, which is not a Kubernetes API object: the run fails on it with "no matches for kind Kustomization" after applying part of the platform, leaving a half-applied namespace. The `-k` entry point cannot make that mistake, because the resource list is data rather than a directory listing, and it excludes the Secret template by simply not listing it — the same protection the `grep -v` was there to provide, expressed once instead of in every copy of the command | The apply path now depends on the kustomization staying in step with the folder, so `DemoBootstrapContractTest` asserts every yaml on disk is either listed, the template, or the kustomization itself. A reader who runs `kubectl apply -f` out of habit still overwrites the Secrets, which is why all four documents now state the `-k` form and none states the glob |
| `deploy/k8s/load-images.sh` is the authoritative image step, and the cluster README's step 1 invokes it rather than restating a build loop | Keep the loop in the README and add a sentence about loading; document per-runtime import commands beside the loop; drop the script and tell readers to use a registry | The README's loop ran `docker build` and stopped there. That leaves the six images in this machine's Docker daemon, which is not where a kind node or a minikube profile looks: each keeps its own containerd store, and Docker Desktop is the only runtime where the two are the same. A reader following that step got `ErrImageNeverPull` from a page that had just told them the images were built. One procedure that builds, loads for the detected runtime, and reads the node's image list back cannot half-succeed silently | Step 1 is now a script a reader has to trust rather than a command they can read. The script prints what it is doing per service, and the README states what it does, so the trust is recoverable |
| `IMAGE_TAG` is checked against the tag `kustomization.yaml` requests and the run stops when they differ | Obey `IMAGE_TAG` and let the operator remember to retag the manifests; rewrite `kustomization.yaml` from the script; drop `IMAGE_TAG` altogether | `IMAGE_TAG` affected only what was built and loaded. The kubelet asks for the tag the manifests set and, under `imagePullPolicy: Never`, runs that or refuses the Pod — so a tag override produced the same `ErrImageNeverPull` as loading nothing, from a run that reported success. Refusing is better than rewriting because the manifests are the reviewed artifact: a script that edited them would move the decision out of version control. The same check catches `kustomization.yaml` drifting from the version in `pom.xml`, which is the tag Compose and the pipeline build, and it refuses six entries that disagree with each other | Changing the tag is now two steps rather than one, and the refusal has to name the `kustomize edit set image` command or it is merely an obstacle. Each of the three refusals was exercised against a perturbed copy of the manifests and prints the remedy |
| The restart the script prints names the six application Deployments | Keep `rollout restart deployment`; use a label selector; restart nothing and let the reader work it out | With no argument, `rollout restart deployment` restarts every Deployment in the namespace, which includes the broker and the database. That drops every consumer group and every open connection to reload an image neither of them uses. A label selector would work and would depend on a label staying correct; six names depend on nothing | The list has to grow when a seventh service appears. The script builds it from the same `services` array it builds images from, so it grows with that array rather than separately |
| The Pod Security profile version is pinned to `v1.25` on all three modes | Leave `latest`; pin to the newest release; pin `enforce` and leave `audit` and `warn` at `latest` | `latest` means whatever the running control plane currently defines `restricted` to be, so a cluster upgrade can tighten the policy under a manifest nobody edited and the six Deployments begin failing admission for a reason that is not in this repository. `v1.25` is named because it is where `restricted` began keying its Linux-only controls on `.spec.os.name` and where Pod Security admission became generally available, so it is the oldest version expressing the policy these manifests were written against. Pinning only `enforce` was rejected because `audit` and `warn` exist to predict what `enforce` will refuse, and they cannot do that while judging against different policy content | A policy tightening that a later Kubernetes release introduces for good reason is now something a human has to adopt deliberately. `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the review of the pin |
| The encrypted business port is named `https`, and each Service targets it by name | Keep `http` and add a comment; remove Transport Layer Security so the name becomes true; name it `web` or `api` to avoid the claim | `30-configmap.yaml` sets `SERVER_SSL_ENABLED`, so what answers on 8080 is Transport Layer Security while the port was named `http`. A port name is metadata other tooling acts on — service meshes, ingress controllers and scrapers read it to decide how to speak to the port — so the failure lands somewhere other than the manifest that caused it. `appProtocol: https` was added beside it because that is the field designed to carry this, where `protocol: TCP` states only the transport. Removing encryption was rejected outright: the manifests exist to show the deployed shape, and that shape is encrypted. Targeting by name follows `10-kafka.yaml`, which already targets `internal` that way, and leaves the port number declared in one place | A tool that matched on the literal name `http` stops matching. Nothing in this repository did, and `KubernetesDeploymentContractTest` now asserts the name, the target and `appProtocol` on all six so the three cannot drift apart |

### What a guide prints, and who owns the value it prints

Six findings concerned instructions. Each had the same shape: a guide restated something a committed
script or the build already owned, and the copy drifted from the original. The fix in every case is
to name the authority rather than reproduce it.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service guide calls `scripts/generate-env.sh` instead of printing a credential block | Add the two missing assignments to each of the five blocks; print the block and a warning that it is incomplete; move the whole procedure into onboarding and link to it | Each of the five blocks set fourteen passwords and three hashes, then printed "all 17 values are set" beside a paragraph promising nineteen. The two it never set were `CARD_TOKEN_SECRET` and `ACQUIRER_PASSWORD_HASH`, and both are values a service refuses to start without, so the reader who followed the block reached a refusal the block said could not happen. Adding two lines to five copies was rejected because the sixth divergence would arrive the same way the first did: the script reads the names out of `.env.example`, so a credential added there needs no edit anywhere else. Running it was measured to fill all nineteen and leave no placeholder | A reader now runs a script rather than reading what it does. The script prints one line per group as it works, and the guides state what it fills, so the behaviour is still visible without opening it |
| The four bcrypt writes in onboarding are single-quoted | Leave them bare and rely on the reader; write the hash to a temporary file; hash inside Compose | A bcrypt value starts `{bcrypt}$2a$10$` and Compose expands `$` in an unquoted dotenv value. Written bare, the hash reaches the container truncated at the first expansion and every sign-on answers 401, with the value on disk looking correct. `scripts/generate-env.sh` and the pipeline already quote these, so the guide was the one copy that did not. The corruption was reproduced with `docker compose config` before and after the fix | None. The quoting is inside a command a reader copies whole |
| The three module test commands are `verify` rather than `test` | Keep `test` and add a sentence; add a Surefire include for `*IT.java`; drop the coverage claims from the guides | Failsafe's default pattern matches `**/*IT.java` and Surefire's does not, so `mvn -pl <module> -am test` finished without four integration classes in the authorization service, three in the ledger service and six in the fraud service, and reported nothing missing. The ledger guide then credited its duplicate-delivery proof to a run that never executed it. Making Surefire run them was rejected because they start PostgreSQL and Kafka through Testcontainers: they belong in the phase that owns container-backed tests, and `verify` is where the pipeline runs them | `verify` needs Docker where `test` did not, so each guide now says which half needs what |
| Every path a guide prints resolves from a working directory the same block names | Make every block use one convention; print absolute paths; add a `cd` to every block | Three paths were in the `../../` form, which resolves only from a service directory no section names, so they resolved from nowhere a reader would be standing. Two conventions are legitimate here and both are in use — a block run from the repository root writes `app/...` and `card-platform/.env`, and a block run from `card-platform/` writes `../app/...` and `.env` — so the fix was to put each token under a base its own block states. A contract test now resolves every such token against the filesystem, which is what makes this checkable rather than reviewable | A reader who runs a block from the wrong directory still fails, and now fails on the first line rather than partway through |
| The card-service start command carries `--wait` and names its dependencies | Leave the command and delete the sentence about waiting; add a sleep; poll the health endpoint in a loop | The command started one container and the next line requested its health endpoint, while the surrounding text claimed the start had waited. The other five guides already named `--wait` and their dependencies, so this was the one copy out of step. `--wait` exits non-zero when a container does not become healthy, which turns a race into a reported failure | A slow first start now blocks the command rather than failing the health request. That is the behaviour the sentence already described |
| Docker Engine, Docker Compose and OpenSSL are published as floors, and the language level and Maven stay exact | Publish exact versions for all five and refresh them; publish ranges for all five; drop the versions and name only the tools | The enforcer plugin refuses a build outside `[25,26)` and `[3.9.16,3.10.0)`, so those two are requirements and a newer Maven fails rather than passes. Nothing in this repository constrains the other three, so an exact value for them goes stale within weeks of being written and tells a reader to downgrade for no reason. Publishing ranges for all five would have hidden the two that genuinely refuse a build. Each guide now states which of its versions are refusals and which are the versions the delivered stack was exercised on | A floor does not say what the ceiling is. Nothing here has one, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the image and toolchain review that would find one |
## Correlation identity and structured log context decisions

An observability review found that a demonstration could see one authorization produce one event and
three consumers react, and still not join those records to each other. Every event identifier was
generated at the point of construction. The structured log format carried no stable field naming the
service or the event, and a dead-letter record kept only the failed event identifier. The rows below
state what carries correlation now, and what deliberately does not.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Correlation and causation travel in two Kafka record headers and the logging context, never in an event payload | Add `correlationId` and `causationId` to `EventEnvelope`; add them to each of the nine event records; publish a sixth envelope field | Section 0.3.1 of the plan fixes the envelope at five fields, and every one of the fourteen schema documents, the round-trip test and the backward-compatibility test are written against that shape. A header is invisible to a schema, so no consumer contract changes and no schema version increments. The review's own compliance matrix accepts a field, a header or a logging context for this requirement | A consumer that reads only the payload cannot see the correlation identifier. Every consumer in this platform reads it through the record interceptor instead, and `equivalence-tests` holds the two header names to a closed set |
| A row written outside any scope adopts its own event identifier as its correlation identifier, and a relay does the same for a row that records none | Publish no header when a row records no correlation; refuse to publish such a row; generate a fresh identifier at publish time | Every published record is then joinable, including one whose row predates the two columns. A fresh identifier at publish time would join nothing, because nothing else would ever carry it. Refusing to publish would turn a missing diagnostic field into lost business data | A root event's correlation identifier equals its event identifier, so the two read alike in a log. A causation header stays absent on such a row, which is what distinguishes a root event from a caused one |
| The two columns are nullable and added by one migration per producing service | Backfill every existing row; make the columns `NOT NULL` with a sentinel default; store the pair in the payload column | A row already committed has no correlation to recover, so a backfill would invent one and a `NOT NULL` default would make an invented value indistinguishable from a real one. Nullable states the truth: this row was written before the platform recorded it | Two nullable columns per outbox table, and a reader has to treat absence as "written before this was recorded" rather than as an error. The relay's fallback above means absence never reaches the wire |
| Each of the six services carries the same structured member set, and the service name resolves through `spring.application.name` with a literal default | Hard-code the service name in each file; add the member in a shared library; leave the field to the log shipper | A shipper-added field cannot be asserted by a test, and a hard-coded name drifts when the application is renamed. The placeholder carries a default because `ShippedConfigurationContractTest` requires every placeholder in a shipped file to resolve with no environment set, and a credential is the only exception it permits | The default duplicates the application name inside the same file. The contract test that forced the default is also what would catch the two disagreeing |
| The listener side reads the headers through one `RecordInterceptor` per consuming service rather than through eleven listener signatures | Add a header parameter to each `@KafkaListener` method; read the headers inside each handler; use a `ThreadLocal` set by the container's own thread | An interceptor opens and closes the scope around the whole delivery, so a line written by any collaborator the handler calls carries the same context. Changing eleven signatures would put diagnostic plumbing into eleven business contracts, and reading headers inside each handler would leave the framework's own lines outside the scope | The scope is opened by infrastructure rather than by the handler, so a handler under unit test carries no context unless the test opens one. The scope helper is public for that reason, and the consumer tests open it explicitly |

## Metric and log truthfulness at the transaction boundary decisions

An observability review found counts and log lines that described durable state from inside the
transaction that would produce it. A rollback then left the count and the line behind, reporting work
the datastore never kept. The rows below state where each measurement sits now.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every outcome count is raised after the transaction that produced it has closed, and the class that does the work returns its outcome instead of counting it | Register a transaction synchronisation callback; count inside and decrement on rollback; leave the count beside the work and accept the drift | The consumer already owns the transaction in each case, so the value travels out on a return and is counted where the commit is observable. A synchronisation callback would put the count further from the code that decides the outcome, and a decrement would make the series non-monotonic, which a counter is not allowed to be. Reaching the count now means the row, the marker and the outbox row all committed | An outcome enumeration crosses a method boundary that a void return used to. That is the cost of making the count observable at the commit, and it is the same shape both consumers now use |
| `domain/RejectRecorder` takes no meter at all, and `messaging/TransactionDeclinedConsumer` raises the reject counter in its `RECORDED` arm | Keep the meter injected and unused; keep counting there and document the drift; pass a flag back | A class that records nothing takes no recorder, and leaving the collaborator injected would leave a Javadoc naming a counter it never raises. Removing the parameter is what makes the change visible to a reader rather than only to a rollback. The reflective assertion in `RejectRecorderTest` is there because a counter added back would pass every behavioural test in the class | One constructor signature changed, and one test fixture with it. The alternative was a field nothing reads |
| A truncation is reported where the arithmetic happens, worded as an attempt rather than as a stored fact | Move the report to the post-commit boundary; drop the report; return a truncation flag to the caller | Truncation is a property of the add and is true whatever becomes of the transaction, so the detection belongs at the add. What was untrue was the claim that the record and the event agreed, which asserts a store. Naming the capacity and withholding the figure is unchanged, so no monetary value reaches a log line | A reader learns a figure was truncated before learning whether it was stored. The commit itself is reported separately, and a rollback is reported as a failure, so the two together are unambiguous |
| The line stating that a posting moved the balance is emitted by the consumer after the commit, not by the service inside it | Keep it in the service and reword it as attempted; drop it | Unlike the truncation line it carries no information the arithmetic uniquely holds: the transaction identifier is available to the consumer, and the fact worth stating is that the change committed. Moving it makes the line true rather than merely careful | The line now sits one class away from the arithmetic it describes. It reads better there, because that is where the commit is |

## Framework throwable rendering decisions

Every rule this platform applies to its own logging governs a call it writes. Spring Kafka reports a
delivery it has given up on through a call this platform does not write, and that call carries the
exception. The rows below state how the rendering was closed and what was rejected on the way.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| All six services withhold the `stack_trace` member from every structured record | Limit the rendering's depth or length; supply a printer that omits messages; silence the framework's logger category; rely on the sanitized record each service writes | The member is the only one the logstash format renders a throwable into, and it renders the exception message ahead of the frames. Depth and length limits were measured and still emit the header line carrying the message, so they do not close it. Silencing the category discards the record instead of sanitizing it, and would hide genuine container faults. Withholding the member drops the rendering and nothing else: the exception still propagates, so the container still retries and still routes the record | A genuine stack trace is no longer available from a running service without changing configuration for a run. Each service already reports a failure as a type chain with the record coordinates, which is the record an operator should read, and the failure counters carry the classification |
| The declaration sits in each service's `application.yml` rather than in a shared file or a logging back-end configuration | One shared imported configuration fragment; a `logback-spring.xml` per service; a customizer class registered in code | `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest` forbids a logback file in this platform, and a shared fragment would put a security-relevant declaration one indirection away from the file an operator reads.
