Six literal declarations are six places a reader can see it, and one test holds all six to the same value | The value is repeated six times. The contract test is what keeps the six from drifting, and it names the service that drops it |
| Depth zero was rejected even though it also closes the leak | Set `logging.structured.json.stacktrace.max-throwable-depth` to zero | It expresses "print no frames" rather than "never render a throwable". A contributor raising it to one to debug something would silently reopen the exposure, and nothing about the property's name would warn them | A reader unfamiliar with the format may not know the member is the only throwable carrier. The declaration carries a comment saying so |
| The framework's own record formatter is left alone | Replace it through `KafkaUtils.setConsumerRecordFormatter` | Its default output was measured to be `topic-partition@offset`, carrying neither the record key nor its value, so there is nothing to close. A replacement would add a platform-wide mutation of framework state to defend against a leak that does not exist | The sufficiency of withholding the member depends on that default staying as it is. `FrameworkThrowableRenderingTest` formats a record whose value holds a card number and asserts neither the value nor the key is rendered, so a change to the default is reported |
| The proof runs the framework call in a separate process, twice, and one arm asserts the leak | Assert only the sanitized arm; capture logs in the test process by replacing the console appender's stream; build a synthetic logging event and drive the encoder directly | Installing a logging configuration replaces it for the whole virtual machine. Capturing in-process would therefore change what every later test in the same fork writes, and make the result depend on test order. A synthetic event proved brittle against the logging back-end's internals. The arm that asserts the leak is what proves the tests can fail, so deleting a declaration is reported rather than passing quietly | Eight child processes, about twenty-four seconds. The alternative was a test that could pass after the thing it tests was removed |

## Readiness listener expectation decisions

Readiness compared the listener registry against itself, which an empty registry satisfies, so a
service whose listeners never registered reported ready. Correcting it meant reintroducing a declared
count, which this platform had already been bitten by once. The rows below state how both failures are
avoided at the same time.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Each service declares how many listener containers it has, and readiness reads that number | Compare the registry against itself, as before; count the listener endpoints the registry knows; require a running container per consumed topic read from configuration | An empty registry satisfies every rule that reads only the registry, so no such rule can separate "nothing is broken" from "nothing is there". The endpoint registry is populated by the same registration that fails, so it cannot supply the expectation either. The number has to come from outside the registry, and the service's own code is where it is true | A number lives beside the listeners it counts and can disagree with them. That disagreement is what the build-time guard below exists to catch |
| The declared number is a floor, not an exact match: readiness needs at least that many registered and every registered container running | Require exact equality, which is what the review's guidance describes | Exact equality reproduces a failure this platform has already had. A count of two against three declared listeners left a service permanently unready, failing its compose health check and both Kubernetes probes for good. A floor cannot do that, because a listener added without an edit still reports ready once it runs. It closes the reported defect in full, since zero and short registries both fail a floor | A service running more containers than it declares reports ready rather than reporting the anomaly. The build-time guard rejects that state before it can ship, and a running listener is not a reason to refuse traffic |
| The count is held against the module's own sources by a build-time contract test | Trust review to keep the two in step; assert it in each service's own test; derive it by scanning sources at run time | The failure mode of a declared count is drift, and drift is only dangerous where it surfaces late. A test that reads the constant and counts the `@KafkaListener` methods of the same module turns a deployment failure into a build failure. Scanning sources at run time would put a source tree on the runtime path, which no container carries | One more contract test, and a service that adds a listener has two files to touch instead of one. The second file is named in the failure message |
| The health detail carries `declared` beside `registered` and `running` | Report the status alone; name the missing listeners | Three integers let an operator tell the three states apart without reading the code: none there, some there, all there but stopped. Naming the missing listeners would put endpoint identifiers into a document served without credentials, and the identifiers add nothing a count does not | A reader has to know that `declared` comes from the code rather than from configuration. The field names are the ones the tests assert, so they are stated in one place |

## Metric lifecycle decisions for the account update and the card reads

Two series described less than their names promised. An update latency timed only the calls that
returned, and a card failure counter never moved for a read that could not reach its table. The rows
below state where each records now.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Account update latency is recorded in one `finally`, so every exit is timed once | Add the recording to the rollback arm as well; time only the calls that returned, as before; wrap the call in a timer aspect | Timing only the arms that returned made the series describe the work that succeeded. A datastore failing every write slowly showed a falling call count against an unchanged latency, and the measurement that would have named the slowness was the one discarded. Adding a third recording site would have worked and left three places to keep in step; one site cannot disagree with itself. An aspect would hide the measurement from a reader of the method | A rollback now contributes to the same latency series as a success, so the series mixes both. The failure counter separates them, and a rollback that is slow is exactly what the series is worth reading for |
| The applied and validation counters no longer travel with the elapsed time | Keep passing the duration into the outcome recorder | The recorder exists to touch meters only after commit, and the duration was the one thing it carried that had nothing to do with the commit. Removing it leaves the record with one job | One method signature changed, and the tests asserting the recording order changed with it. The order they now assert is the order the code performs |
| The card handler counts an unreachable table and an internal fault, and counts no refusal | Count every answer this handler gives; count in the read services instead; leave the log line as the only record | A read that cannot reach the card table moved nothing an operator watches, which is the gap. Counting refusals as well would make the series rise whenever a client sent a bad request, so the number would describe callers rather than this service. Counting inside the read services would need the same increment in each, and the handler is the one place every read failure arrives | The counter now rises from two layers, the handler and `domain/CardUpdateService`. They cannot double count, because a write that failed while holding the row answers through its own outcome chain and never reaches the handler |
| The handler takes the counter through its constructor | Inject the registry and look the counter up; use a static holder; make the handler a component that reads a facade | The class had no constructor at all, so a constructor states the one collaborator it gained. Every other consumer of this counter in the service takes it the same way, by qualified name, so the wiring reads alike. A static holder would make the counter unassertable in a unit test | Four test call sites construct the handler and each supplies a counter. Those tests can now assert which outcomes move the series, which none of them could before |

## Producer failure reporting decisions

Every failed send was reported twice at error: once by the service's producer listener and again by
the relay for the same send. So an operator counting error lines counted each lost event twice, and a
retried attempt was reported as loudly as an event this platform gave up on. The rows below state who
reports what now.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A failed send is reported at warning, not error | Leave it at error; report it at info; report it at debug | The event stays on its outbox row and the relay attempts it again, so an attempt is not a loss. At error an unreachable broker wrote one error per attempt per row. Nothing then told a retry storm from a permanent loss, which taught an operator to skip the level. Info and debug would hide a real symptom, and debug is below the shipped default | A reader who alerts on error alone no longer sees individual attempts. Abandonment is still reported at error, and that is the event worth alerting on |
| The producer listener owns the per-attempt report, and the relay's line for the same send was removed | Keep the relay's line and silence the listener; keep both at distinct levels; keep both and accept the duplication | The listener is the one place every send of every template passes through. So it also covers sends no relay makes, such as the dead-letter template of the notification service. Silencing it would leave those silent, and `ProducerFailureLoggingContractTest` requires it to report. Keeping both is the defect | The removed lines carried the attempt number and the retry schedule, which the listener cannot know. The abandonment line still reports the total attempts and the row carries its own attempt count. The relay still reports a claim it recovered and a sweep that ran out of time |
| The relay keeps every line that reports something the listener cannot | Remove all relay reporting of failure and rely on counters | Four distinct facts remain, and none is a failed send. A claim was recovered from a stopped instance, a sweep reached its deadline, a row's type named no topic, or a row was abandoned. Removing them would have traded a duplicate for a blind spot | A reader has to know that a send failure and a row abandonment are reported by different components. Each line names which, so the two are distinguishable in the log |
| The contract test reads the level instead of naming one | Keep the pattern fixed at error; drop the level from the contract entirely | The pattern named one level, so it was two rules wearing one shape: what a line may carry, and how loudly it says it. Correcting the level would have looked like relaxing the sensitivity rule. Reading the level separates them, and the level is now asserted to be warning, so a service reverting to error is reported | A widened pattern could match more than intended. A further test therefore drives a bare throwable, a key read, a value read and the sanctioned form through it, asserting each is still classified correctly |

## Outbox backlog visibility decisions

Five services relay an outbox. Each one reported nothing about the work waiting in it, so a stopped
relay looked exactly like an idle one until the first row was abandoned. These rows record how that
was closed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The backlog is published as two gauges rather than a counter | Count each row as it is enqueued and again as it is published; publish only the count; publish only the age | A backlog is a state, and a counter cannot fall. Subtracting two counters gives the size but never the wait, and the wait is what separates a service working through a burst from a stopped relay | Two series rather than one to scrape. A gauge is sampled, so a burst shorter than the scrape interval is not seen |
| Neither gauge carries a tag | Tag by event type; tag by aggregate; tag by relay state | A backlog belongs to the service as a whole. A tag naming an event type or a row would make the series unbounded, which is what a gauge must not be | A reader cannot tell from the gauge which event type is waiting. The outbox table answers that when it is asked |
| An abandoned row stays the only condition that fails readiness | Fail readiness above a backlog threshold; fail readiness above an age threshold | A backlog is usually the broker, not the pod. Removing the pod from service on a broker hiccup would take away the only component still recording what happened. This service already lost a deployment to a readiness count that named a number the code did not match | A service can be deeply behind and still be reported ready. The threshold instead names the state in the readiness detail, so an operator sees it without a probe acting on it |
| The threshold names the state rather than setting the status | Publish only the raw numbers; add a custom degraded status; fail readiness | A raw number needs a reader who knows what is normal for this service. A name drawn from a closed set of two is readable in a demo and in an alert rule alike. A custom status would change what the probe endpoint answers | The two thresholds are constants rather than settings, so tuning them is a code change. That keeps the shipped configuration inventory unchanged, which a contract test enforces |
| Both a size threshold and an age threshold are read | Read only the size; read only the age | The two say different things. A burst raises the size while the age stays small, and a stopped relay raises the age while the size may not move at all. Either alone reports one of those two as healthy | Two thresholds to keep true rather than one. A test reads each boundary from both sides |
| The backlog is reported as readiness detail beside every reading | Report it only when it is large; report it in a separate endpoint | The reading is cheap and the detail is where an operator already looks. Reporting it only when large makes its absence ambiguous | Two more indexed reads per readiness poll. Both are bounded by the index on relay state and next attempt |
| The gauges are registered outside the observability configuration | Register them beside the counters; register them in the relay; register them in the readiness configuration | The counter class takes the meter registry alone, and its own tests load it on that basis. A repository there stops that class loading by itself, which removed the assertion that its declared meters are the only ones it registers | One more configuration class per service. A contract test requires it to stay separate, so the reason cannot be lost |
| A datastore that cannot answer yields not-a-number | Let the failure escape; report the last value read; report zero | A gauge that throws takes the whole scrape with it, so one unreachable datastore would remove every other series of that service at the moment they were needed. Zero would read as an empty outbox, which is the opposite of the truth | Not-a-number has to be read as absent rather than as a value. It is a truthful reading in a way that a stale value or a zero is not |

## Meter declaration decisions

A series that is declared in two places is declared once as far as the registry is concerned, and the
second declaration is dead. These rows record how the authorization service was reduced to one
declaration per series.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One accessor method per shared series, called by the binder and by every recorder | Keep two registration helpers and delete one; keep both and make the descriptions agree; give each recorder its own builder | The name, the tag and the description are then written once. Two helpers that agree today drift tomorrow, and nothing fails when they do | An accessor call rather than a builder chain at each recording site. The call is shorter and it cannot omit the description |
| The description kept is the one the registry already held | Adopt the richer description from the dead block; write a new description for each series | Micrometer keeps the meter that registered first, so the surviving text is what every scrape has carried. The service guide already quotes it. Changing it would edit published metadata while claiming to remove duplication | The per-outcome wording from the dead block is lost. It described the same two outcomes the tag already names |
| Recording sites resolve through the accessor rather than build their own counter | Leave each recorder building its own; inject a counter bean per series | A builder without a description registers the series without one whenever that component is constructed before the binder, and neither controls the order. Injecting six counter beans would change five constructors to fix a description | A recorder can no longer name a series the configuration does not declare. That is the property being bought |
| An unknown diagnostic outcome is refused rather than counted | Accept any outcome; fall back to a default outcome | That tag has exactly two values by design, and both are named in the configuration. A third would be a typo, and counting it would create a series no reader expects | A caller passing a new outcome fails instead of recording. The decision counter takes any outcome, because a reject reason this service has not seen still needs counting |

## Consumer exit instrumentation decisions

A listener that returns early recorded nothing, so a stream arriving entirely as tombstones or
mismatched keys read as a stream that had stopped. These rows record how every exit came to be
counted.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The consumed count and the timer start before the first guard, and the latency is recorded in one `finally` | Record after the guards; record per guard; leave the early exits uncounted | A guard is work the service did, and a delivery it refused is a delivery it received. Counting after the guards made a refused stream indistinguishable from an absent one, which is the reading an operator acts on first | A tombstone now contributes to the latency series, and it is fast. The series therefore mixes refusals with applied work, and the outcome counters separate them |
| Each early exit is classified rather than counted as one kind of failure | One failure counter for all exits; no classification | A tombstone, a key naming another aggregate and an unreadable payload need different responses. Summing them into one number names none of them | One more tag value per classified exit. Every value is a literal in code, so the series count stays known |
| A duplicate delivery is counted on its own name, `carddemo.fraud.duplicates.skipped` | Count it as a failure; count it as a normal delivery only; leave the debug line as the only record | A replay is expected traffic, and it is the one outcome that writes nothing and raises nothing. Read beside the consumed count it separates a quiet service from one working through a redelivered backlog | One more series in this service. It is untagged, so it adds exactly one |
| The listener key guards keep the names they had | Rename them to read alike across services | A delivery-guarantee contract test recognises the guard by one of three exact spellings. Renaming would have left the guard in place and the test unable to see it, which is worse than an inconsistent name | The three services spell one idea two ways. The contract test is what keeps every guard present |

## Category-balance overflow visibility decisions

The store that reproduces a COBOL `ADD` with no `ON SIZE ERROR` phrase was silent at runtime. The
balance was then wrong by a known amount and nothing said so. These rows record how it came to
report itself.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The upsert reports the wrap through `RETURNING`, comparing the row as it stood against the row as it stands | Read the row before the write; read it again after; compare the stored value against a ceiling | A read before the write reopens the window the single statement exists to close. A read after cannot tell a wrap from a small balance, because the prior value is gone. The comparison inside the statement runs under the row lock the conflict arm already holds | The statement now answers a flag rather than a row count. A missing answer still fails the call, because the mapping requires exactly one row |
| The wrap is counted and reported after the transaction commits | Count it where it is detected; log it inside the transaction | The detection sits inside the transaction, and a rollback would remove the row the count described. The caller already reports its outcome after the commit, so the flag travels back to that boundary | The flag is carried through two return values to reach the boundary. That is visible in the signatures rather than hidden in a field |
| The wrap is reproduced, never prevented | Refuse the store past the field; widen the column; saturate at the maximum | The source stores the same sum with no `ON SIZE ERROR` phrase, so refusing or widening would change a posted balance and break the parity this platform is measured on | A balance can still be wrong by exactly one billion. It is now counted and named, and widening the column is recorded as an owner decision |
| The line names the two low-cardinality codes and the event, and no account identifier | Name the account identifier; name the amount; name the stored balance | A log-hygiene contract forbids an account identifier in an ordinary line, and no ledger line names one. The event identifier reaches the row through the correlated record without publishing a cardholder identifier | A reader has one hop to make from the event to the account. The correlation members on the same record make that hop a filter rather than a search |
| The wrap has its own meter rather than a fourth posting outcome | Add an `outcome` value to the posting counter; reuse the failure counter | The posting succeeded, so a fourth outcome would break the three that are mutually exclusive. It is not a fault either, because the source stores the same value | One more series in this service. It is untagged, so it adds exactly one |

## Performance-review remediation decisions

A performance review read the whole delivered platform and raised twenty-one findings, none of them a
security fault and none of them a wrong answer. Every one was work the design did not need to do. A
payload was parsed four times to publish it once, and a rate ceiling scanned ten thousand counters to
admit one request. A relay held a broker inside a database transaction, and an event stream read one
record at a time on a topic of three partitions. The rows below state what changed, grouped by
the part of the platform each group touches.

### What one publish and one consume parse

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A payload is parsed once on publish and once on consume, and the parsed tree is what the type check, the sensitive-field screen, the schema validation and the binding all read | Cache the mapper alone and leave the four parses; drop the schema check on one of the two ends; validate the text and bind the text as before | Publishing one event parsed the same characters four times: once to write them, once for the envelope type, once for the screen, and once inside schema validation, which built a mapper of its own on every call. The validator admits a parsed tree directly, so nothing had to be weakened to remove three parses. Consuming parsed three times for the same reason. The screen and the validation now read the tree the serializer already built, so a payload cannot be checked in one form and published in another | The serializer holds a tree for the length of one publish, which is memory the previous version did not hold at that moment. It is one event, and it was already holding four copies of the text |
| One static mapper serves every serialize and deserialize | A mapper per call, as before; a mapper per serializer instance | A mapper is thread safe once configured and expensive to build, and the previous version built one inside the validation helper on every single call. A mapper per instance would leave the same cost on the validation path, which is static | The configuration of that mapper is now shared, so a future setting that needs to differ per service would need a second one. No such setting exists on this platform |

### What a rate ceiling does when it runs out of room

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The expiry sweep of each request-rate ceiling runs at most once per tenth of a window, and exactly one caller runs it | Sweep on every unseen key once the map is full, as before; sweep from a scheduled task; remove the map bound and let it grow | At capacity every request naming an unseen key walked all ten thousand counters, so the ceiling that exists to make a refusal cheap made it the most expensive request the service served. Throttling the sweep keeps the bound and pays for it once per interval. A compare-and-set on the last-sweep stamp means a burst of arrivals runs one sweep between them rather than one each. A scheduled task would put a second thread on a map every request touches | Between sweeps the map can hold expired windows, so it is bounded by its ceiling rather than by the live key count. The overflow bucket already absorbs an arrival past the ceiling, so the ceiling still holds |

### One event of one account in flight, and every other account beside it

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every relay divides one pass into a committed claim, sends outside any transaction, and one short transaction for each result | Keep the whole pass in one transaction, as four of the five did; shorten the pass budget instead; move the sends to a second thread and leave the transaction open | Four relays wrapped the whole sweep, so every broker acknowledgement was waited for with a database transaction open and every claimed row locked. A broker that answered slowly therefore held connections and rows for the length of the pass, and the retention sweep and the listener contended with a transaction that was waiting on a network. The authorization relay already had the division, so this makes five relays read alike | A pass is several transactions, so a process that stops between the claim and the result leaves rows claimed. Every relay already recovers a stranded claim after its timeout, and that recovery is what makes the division safe |
| The claim of every relay answers with the due head row of each account and never two rows of one account | Claim every due row and stop the pass at the first failure; claim every due row and reorder on retry | Recording each result separately means an older row can fail while a newer row of the same account succeeds, and the retry would then reach the topic behind it. The account identifier is the message key, so that is a reordering every consumer of that account would see. Two of the five relays already claimed this way; the ledger, card and fraud relays claimed every due row | A burst on one account drained at one event per pass, which is one relay interval each, until a performance review made one pass repeat its claim; see [what one pass may drain](#what-one-pass-may-drain-and-what-a-pass-that-runs-out-of-time-leaves-behind). Unrelated accounts are unaffected either way, which is the point of the claim |
| The sends of distinct accounts are issued together, bounded by the producer's own in-flight window of five, and awaited against the one deadline the pass already had | Send one row at a time and wait for each, as before; issue the whole batch at once; raise the producer window to the batch size | One send at a time made every account wait behind whichever account was publishing, which is a serialization the ordering guarantee never asked for: order is per account, and the broker holds it per partition. Bounding the group at the producer's own window means the relay never has more outstanding than the producer will admit, so the bound needs no second setting | A pass can now have five sends outstanding, so a broker failure can affect five rows rather than one. Each row records its own outcome and backs off on its own, which is what the per-result transaction is for |
| All five outbox schemas ship the partial index the head claim reads, as a forward migration each | Add the index to the two schemas whose claim already needed it; create it in the original migration; leave the correlated check unindexed | The claim asks for the earliest undelivered row of each account, which is a correlated check over the rows of one account. Without an index it re-read an account's backlog for every candidate row. The index is partial on the two relay states a pending row can hold, so it covers the working set and not the published history. Editing a migration that has already run is not an option Flyway leaves open | Five more indexes to maintain on a table every event passes through. Each is narrow and partial, and the write it costs is the write the claim it serves would otherwise pay for many times over |
| A refused send backs off its own row and leaves the rest of the pass to run | Stop the whole pass at the first refusal, as the ledger and card relays did; abandon the row immediately | Stopping the pass meant one unpublishable account held every other account's events until its row was abandoned ten attempts later. Backing the row off is what the attempt count and the next-attempt time already exist for, and the head claim keeps that account's later rows behind it | A pass can now end having published some accounts and refused others, so a single failure reads as a partial pass in the logs rather than as a stopped one. The counters separate the two |
| A repository finder that holds a pessimistic lock is read inside a transaction of its own | Drop the lock from those finders; open one transaction around the whole pass again; call the finder from inside the claim transaction | Two such reads sat outside every transaction, and a locking read with no transaction raises rather than reading. The card relay's stranded-claim recovery raised on every pass, so that service published nothing at all until the read was given a boundary. Dropping the lock would let two instances claim one row | A short transaction of its own for a read, and two instances can name the same row twice from separate reads. The obligation each row carries is cleared only against a broker acknowledgement, so a duplicated diagnostic is a duplicate on the topic and not a lost one |
| The ledger relay abandons a row whose event type names no configured topic without publishing a diagnostic for it | Publish the diagnostic to the dead-letter topic, as three services do; refuse to start with such a type configured | A row with no topic has no source topic to name in a diagnostic, and the ledger dead-letter route derives the destination from the source. The service reports the unconfigured type at error level with the row identifier instead. This is the behaviour that service already had, and the review did not ask for it to change | The two services with no durable dead-letter obligation report such a row in logs alone. Their guarantee is that the abandonment rolls back, so the row is offered again rather than lost |

### What reads a partition, and what runs on the scheduler

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service reads one consumer thread per partition, three, from `spring.kafka.listener.concurrency` | Leave the framework default of one thread, which four services did and the fraud service declared; one thread per topic; a value chosen per listener; a placeholder that tracks `KAFKA_TOPIC_PARTITIONS` so the count follows the broker | Three partitions read on one thread is three accounts' events applied in sequence for no ordering reason: Kafka assigns a partition to exactly one consumer of a group, and the account identifier is the message key, so a thread per partition reorders nothing. A per-listener value would need a container factory for each listener. Tracking the variable would raise the thread count wherever an operator raised partitions, and each service's connection pool is sized against this number | A service now holds up to three consumer threads for each topic it reads, and the notification service reads four topics. Every pool is sized for that. Concurrency above the partition count would leave threads unassigned, so the value is documented as a ceiling as well as a default |
| Each service gives its scheduler one thread for each method it schedules, two where a relay and a retention sweep both run | Dedicated schedulers for the relay and the sweep; keep one thread and shorten the per-table drain ceiling; move the sweep off the scheduler onto an executor of its own | The framework's pool holds one thread and five services put two tasks on it, so an hourly drain bounded at thirty seconds a table delayed every relay pass that fell inside it. A sweep whose batch bound exists to keep out of the relay's locks was blocking the relay's thread instead. Two threads is the smallest change that removes it, and a dedicated scheduler for each task would be two beans and one more name to configure per service | Two different tasks can now run at once, so both hold a connection at once. Fixed-delay scheduling measures the next run from the end of the previous run, so no task overlaps itself however many threads the pool holds, and the notification service keeps the framework default because it schedules one task |
| Each datasource pool is sized for the work that holds a connection at once, with a small idle floor | Leave every pool at the default of ten; size each pool to the in-flight request ceiling of sixty-four; raise the connection ceiling of the database instead | Twelve listener threads against a pool of ten is a listener waiting three seconds for a connection and then failing a delivery that had nothing wrong with it. Each pool is now its listener threads plus its scheduled tasks plus a margin for request handling, and the six maxima together stay under what one server admits. Sizing to the request ceiling would need six times that. The idle floor matters because a pool keeps its maximum idle unless told otherwise, so six services would hold every connection they might ever need while nothing was happening | A request arriving when the pool is exhausted still waits and is then refused, which is the behaviour the connection timeout already defined. A burst pays to open connections above the idle floor, which is one handshake each |

### What the hot path reads twice, what a sweep leaves behind, and what an index nothing reads costs

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A request naming only an account carries the cross-reference row it already read into the rule chain, so `card_xref` is read once per call rather than twice | Cache the row across requests, keyed by card number or account; read the single `card_number` column instead of the row; leave the second read, on the grounds that a primary-key lookup is cheap | The row the account branch reads and the row the first rule reads are the same row: `card_number` is the primary key of `card_xref`, and on this branch that key came out of the row already in hand. Carrying it removes a read whose result was already known. A cross-request cache was rejected outright and not merely deferred: the two replica tables are refreshed by two listeners, so a cached row is a row that can be wrong, and correctness of the four decline rules is the one thing this service exists to get right. Selecting one column would have removed the second read too, but the row is what the rules that follow need | The rule now has two paths, one of which does no read, so a future change that seeds a row for a different card would silently skip the lookup. The rule therefore compares the carried row's own card number against the card the context names and reads the table when they differ, and two tests hold that behaviour. Nothing is held between requests, so the only lifetime involved is one call's |
| The authorization retention sweep repeats each bounded delete until it comes back short or the table's own deadline passes | Raise the batch ceiling; shorten the interval; run one unbounded delete per table; leave one bounded statement per table per pass | A bound without repetition is a fixed removal rate, and this one was four thousand rows an hour across four tables. Any arrival rate above that grew the schema for ever while every individual statement stayed small and fast, which is why nothing about the defect ever looked slow. Raising the ceiling or shortening the interval moves the threshold without removing it; only repetition makes the rate follow the arrivals. The deadline is what keeps the repetition finite, so one large table cannot hold the scheduled thread and starve the three behind it | A pass can now issue many statements instead of one, so a sustained backlog keeps the scheduler thread busy for up to the per-table ceiling. That thread is the sweep's own, not the relay's, because this service schedules two methods and sizes its scheduler for two. A table whose backlog exceeds what the ceiling clears is still not fully drained in one pass, by design: the remainder goes to the next pass rather than to an unbounded loop |
| The two unread composite indexes on `authorization_decision` are dropped rather than kept for a query that might arrive | Keep both against future use; keep them and add the endpoints that would read them now; keep the read methods and drop only the indexes | Both indexes were maintained on every authorization and read by nothing: the four repository methods that would have used them had no production caller. An index with no reader is pure write cost on the hot path. Adding the endpoints instead would have meant designing a bound, a page contract and an authorization model for an operator query nobody has specified, which is a larger change than the finding asks for and would have been unreviewed. The questions both indexes answered are worth answering, so the task is recorded rather than dropped, and the index belongs in the same change as the query that reads it | An operator who was querying this table directly in SQL loses two index-supported access paths, and a large table would answer those ad-hoc queries by sequential scan until the endpoint lands. `ix_authorization_decision_decided_at` stays, so the bounded retention delete is unaffected. The drops are written `IF EXISTS`, so the migration is safe on a schema where an earlier hand-run already removed one |

### What a history response reads, and what one posted event writes

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The history route serves a bounded keyset page and reads the count and the total whole, once per response, rather than per page | Keep the unbounded read; bound the page and take the count and the total from the page; bound the page and offer an offset; cap the response at a large number of entries and truncate silently | The route read every retained row of a card, mapped all of them and copied the list, so query work, heap and response bytes all followed how long a cardholder had been transacting. Taking the count and the total from a page instead would have been cheap and wrong: `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L429` totals every row of one card between two key breaks, so a figure over one page describes a statement the source never produced. Reading them as `COUNT` and `SUM` over the primary-key prefix kept them whole. A later review measured that this cost did follow the history, and [what a page of history costs](#what-a-page-of-history-costs-and-what-keeps-a-stored-total-honest) records what replaced it; the figures the route answers are the same. An offset page reads and discards every entry before it, which makes the last page of a long history the most expensive one to serve, so the walk is a keyset cursor over `(card_token, transaction_id)` — the same idiom the card service already ships. Silent truncation would answer a statement that is wrong with no way for a caller to tell | A caller that wants a whole history now makes several requests instead of one, and the entries can shift between two of them: retention can remove an entry a cursor has already passed, and a new posting appends beyond the page being read. The count and the total are read once at the start of a page, so a response can carry a count that a subsequent posting has already changed. Both are properties of any paged view of a growing table, and the alternative was an unbounded response |
| The whole-history total is a single `SUM` only where the sum of the magnitudes fits `WS-TOTAL-AMT`, and is otherwise accumulated row by row | Always sum in the database; always accumulate row by row; sum in the database and truncate the result once; sum in the database and accept the difference as a documented divergence | The source stores each addition into `WS-TOTAL-AMT PIC S9(9)V99` at `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L65`, so a running total above that field's ceiling loses its high-order digits at every addition, and `CobolDecimal.truncateToPictureField` reproduces that as signed modular truncation. One `SUM` truncates at most once. Signed modular truncation is not compositional when amounts differ in sign and a running total crosses the ceiling, and refunds mean they do differ in sign, so the two answers can genuinely diverge. What makes the substitution safe is a bound rather than an assumption: a running total never exceeds the sum of the magnitudes, so an `ABS` sum that fits the field proves no addition overflowed. `SUM(ABS(amount))` costs nothing beside the sum already being read. Accepting a divergence was rejected outright — equivalence with the source is the contract this platform is verified against, and the AAP admits no exception here | The fallback reads the card's rows in bounded pages, so in that one case the read is proportional to the history again. It is reachable only above a billion in absolute turnover on a single card, it is the case the source itself gets wrong, `docs/business-rule-flags.md` records the defect being reproduced, and a warning names it when it happens. Preferring a correct slow answer to a fast wrong one is the trade this makes |
| The page size and its ceiling are constants of the controller rather than configuration | Reintroduce `carddemo.history.default-page-size` and `carddemo.history.maximum-page-size` with the two environment variables that were retired with them; make only the default configurable | The ceiling is part of the published contract: `openapi.yaml` declares `maxItems` on the array and `maximum` on the parameter, and a contract test holds the document to the controller. A deployment able to raise the ceiling could publish a document its own service does not honour. The ceiling is also `NotificationRenderer.MAXIMUM_STATEMENT_ROWS`, so one number bounds a rendered alert and a page rather than two numbers that can disagree | A deployment that wants a different page ceiling changes code and republishes the document, which is the intent. `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest` polices a fixed set of environment keys, so adding none also keeps that inventory unchanged |
| One posted event writes its read-model row with a single `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` | Keep the read that chose a debug message and the merge that followed it; drop the read and keep the merge; make the entity `Persistable` so the merge inserts blindly | Every event performed a `findById` whose only effect was to choose between two debug lines, and the `save` that followed it is a merge for an entity carrying an assigned key, so it issued a select of its own: three round trips for one event, two of them asking the same question. The upsert is one statement, and it runs in the transaction that claims the processed-event marker, so the row and the marker still commit together. `ON CONFLICT` is also what makes a redelivery store the same values rather than raising on the key, where `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L562-L579` failed its status test and abended. Making the entity `Persistable` would insert blindly and fail on a redelivery, which is the behaviour being removed | The diagnostic no longer says whether the row was new, because one statement cannot report it without a `RETURNING` clause the modifying query cannot carry. That distinction was a debug line and cost a full select per event. The twelve non-key columns are all replaced, which is safe only because every one of them is copied from the event rather than accumulated |

### What a page costs at the far end of a history

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The assessment collection route pages by a cursor over `(assessed_at, transaction_id)` and refuses a page number outright | Keep the offset and lower `MAXIMUM_PAGE_NUMBER`; keep the offset and leave the ceiling; accept `page` and quietly serve the newest page; keep both and let a caller choose | The offset was bounded, and the bound was the defect rather than the fix: page 1,000,000 at 200 rows was a valid request that walked up to 200,000,000 entries to answer with 200, because an offset is reached by reading and discarding every row before it. Lowering the ceiling moves that cost without removing it and turns a legitimate deep read into a refusal. A cursor names a position, so the predicate is a range and the ten-thousandth page costs what the first one does. Accepting `page` and ignoring it was rejected for the reason `sort` is already refused here: a `200` for something the route did not do is worse than a `400`. Supporting both would have kept the expensive path reachable, which is the thing being removed | A caller that wants the tenth page now makes ten requests instead of one, and cannot jump. Nothing jumped before either — the tenth page was already ten pages of reading, charged to the server rather than to the caller. An offset caller is broken rather than silently changed, which is deliberate: the refusal names the header to send instead, so the failure is self-describing. Rows can shift between two pages of a growing table, which is a property of any paged view |
| The cursor carries the transaction identifier beside the assessment time, and travels in `X-Fraud-Cursor` | Carry the assessment time alone; carry an opaque encoded token; carry the cursor as a query parameter; sort by the primary key alone and cursor on that | `assessed_at` is not unique. One consumer batch records several verdicts, and `TIMESTAMP(6)` lets them share a microsecond, so a boundary drawn on time alone falls inside a group of equal times and repeats one row on the next page while skipping another. The primary key makes the order total and the boundary exact, which is also why the index carries three columns. Cursoring on the key alone would have been total but would have ordered by identifier rather than by time, and newest-first is the order this route answers in. An encoded token hides a value that is not a secret — it is two ordering columns of a row the caller was just shown — and would have made a demonstration harder to read for nothing. The header keeps the value out of the access logs that record a query string, and matches `X-Card-Cursor` and `X-Notification-Cursor` | A caller can compose a cursor by hand and read from an arbitrary position of an account it is already entitled to, which is what a cursor is for. Every read is bound to the `accountId` of the request rather than to the cursor, so no cursor reaches another account's rows. An unreadable cursor is refused rather than repaired, and the refusal names the shape and never the value, because the value holds a transaction identifier |
| `ix_fraud_assessment_account_cursor` replaces the two indexes that carried a leading part of it, rather than joining them | Add the three-column index and keep both; add it and keep the single-column one; widen `ix_fraud_assessment_account_assessed_at` in place under its existing name | Both withdrawn indexes are leading prefixes of the new one, so it answers every query either of them answered, and the only other reads of this table are by primary key and by `assessed_at` alone, which `ix_fraud_assessment_assessed_at` still serves. Keeping a prefix index beside its superset is write cost for no read, on a table that takes one insert per authorized transaction. Reusing the existing name would have meant a drop and a create of one name in a single migration, which reads ambiguously and which the schema-parity harness resolves by version rather than by line order, so a distinct name states the change plainly | An operator with a query planned around a single-column account index loses it, though the wider index serves the same predicate. The drops are written `IF EXISTS` so the migration is safe where a hand-run already removed one. The entity declares the new index and neither old one, so a start-up validation would catch a schema that disagrees |

### What an update reads twice, and who owns a table nothing deletes from

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| An account update answers with a snapshot read off the locked row, so the controller does not read the account again after the transaction | Return the managed entity; return a detached copy of the entity; answer with the values the caller submitted; keep the second read | The transaction already held the values it committed, so reading them again asked a question it had just answered — and answered it as of a later instant, which never made the second read the more truthful of the two. The submitted values are not the committed values: `applyAccountFields` stores each amount at the scale of its Picture clause, so a balance submitted with three decimals commits with two, and reporting the request back would have described a row the table does not hold. A managed entity handed out of a transaction lets a caller trip a lazy load or mutate a row through a setter, and a detached copy is indistinguishable from the managed kind at the call site; `AccountSnapshot` is a record, so neither is possible. `viewOf(AccountEntity)` now routes through `AccountSnapshot.of`, so the read path and the update path map those eleven values in one place rather than two that can drift | `AccountUpdateService.updateAccount` answers `AccountUpdateOutcome` rather than `EditResult`, which moved every call site — one in production and about a hundred in tests. `EditResult` was deliberately not widened: it is the verdict type of every edit in the service, used in seventy-six places that have no account to carry. The snapshot is null on a failing verdict, because a refused field, a lost race and a missing relationship each leave no row to report |
| A card update is handed the row the controller already resolved, through a third parameter rather than a changed signature | Change the two-argument method to take the resolved row; have the controller pass the entity instead of the number; delete the service's own read and require a row from every caller | Resolving a token to a card is the only way that route can name a card, so the row is in hand before the update side is called, and it was reading the same key again. Requiring the row from every caller would have made three of the service's own answers unreachable — the absent-number edit, the search-key edit and its own not-found answer — and deleting reachable validation is a larger change than removing a duplicate read. Passing the entity in place of the number would have done the same. The two-argument form therefore stays, behaves exactly as it did, and delegates | Two overloads exist where one did, and in production only the three-argument form is called. The row supplied is compared against the padded key before it is used, so a caller that resolved one card and named another falls back to the read rather than editing the wrong row, and a test asserts that. The locked compare-and-swap read is untouched: a row read before the transaction opened cannot stand in for a row held under a lock |
| The ledger transaction table is given a named archival owner rather than a retention window | Add a `retention=` window with a default period and sweep it; partition by month now; leave `V1` as it stood | `V1` recorded that no timer deletes a financial record, which is correct and is what the source does — `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L562-L579` writes the record and nothing in `app/cbl/` removes one. What it never recorded is who owes the decision it was deferring, and a comment stating only an absence reads to the next person as a question already settled. Choosing a period here would have been the wrong kind of decision to make in a migration: how long a posted transaction is kept is fixed by the jurisdiction the deployment operates in, and deleting one early is a worse failure than keeping it too long. Partitioning now would commit to one of the two mechanisms before the owner has chosen. So `V9` assigns the obligation, names `proc_ts` as the range key either mechanism would scan, and records the task | The table is still unbounded, which is the correct behaviour for a financial record and the state the review found. `purge_key=none` is kept deliberately: `RetentionSweepContractTest` requires a sweep for any table whose comment names a real purge column beside a window, so writing a period without a schedule would fail the build rather than mislead a reader. The obligation now sits in three table comments and in `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md`, where an operator planning capacity will meet it |

### What a stage repeats, and what it starts that nothing reaches

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Each test stage runs only the suites it owns, drawn by `-DskipUnitTests` on the integration stage and by that flag plus `-DskipServiceIntegrationTests` on the equivalence stage | Leave every stage building its dependencies with tests enabled; collapse the three stages into one reactor `verify`; hand class files between stages as an artifact | Asking Maven for a module asks for the modules beneath it, and building those ran their tests too, so the equivalence stage re-ran every Surefire test in the platform and every service integration suite: the two stages before it, repeated for no new information. Collapsing the three would lose the separate report artifacts and the parallelism the `needs:` graph buys. Each suite now runs once, in the stage named for it | Two properties now change what a stage runs, so a stage command read on its own no longer tells a reader which suites it will run. `ContinuousIntegrationWorkflowContractTest` asserts which stage carries which flag, and that the integration stage carries no flag that would skip the suites it exists to run |
| The two skips are Maven profiles keyed on properties of their own, not a binding of Surefire's `skipTests` parameter | Bind Surefire's skip to a new property in the parent build; pass `-Dsurefire.skip=true`; give the plugins separate skip parameters permanently | Surefire and Failsafe read the same `skipTests` property, so no flag skips one and leaves the other. Binding Surefire's skip permanently would carry that binding into every build: `-DskipTests` would stop skipping Surefire, and `mvn -DskipTests package` - run by the compile stage, the container stage, `scripts/start-demo.sh` and every service README - would execute the whole unit suite instead of none of it. A profile is inert until its property is named | A profile that nothing activates is a profile nobody notices is broken. The contract test names both properties, asserts neither profile keys on `skipTests`, and asserts each of the six service descriptors declares the one the equivalence stage names for it |
| The service integration skip lives in the six service descriptors; the unit skip lives in the parent | Put both in the parent; put both in each module; put the unit skip in each module too | The parent is also `equivalence-tests`' parent, and that module's Failsafe classes are the equivalence stage's entire purpose. A parent-level Failsafe skip would switch off the stage it was added to speed up. The unit skip is safe in the parent because no stage needs Surefire where it names the flag | The two skips sit in different files, which reads as inconsistency until the reason is known. The contract test asserts the parent does *not* carry the service skip, so the asymmetry cannot be tidied away without a failing test explaining why |
| The integration and equivalence stages start no Compose stack | Keep the database and broker they started; start the whole stack in each; point the tests at the Compose services | No test reached that stack. Every integration class provisions the PostgreSQL and Kafka it needs through Testcontainers against the runner's own Docker daemon, so the pair those stages started, and the fourteen credentials each generated to start them, were a minute of setup a stage serving nothing | Nothing in those two stages now reads `docker-compose.yml`, so a change that breaks the file is caught one stage later. The container stage starts the whole stack from that file and reads every health endpoint, which is the check that the file runs; the contract test asserts it is the only stage that copies the environment file |
| The container stage packages the archives it builds images from, rather than restoring them from the compile stage | Upload the six archives from the compile stage and download them here; cache the reactor output; build the images from a compile inside the Dockerfile | Handing archives between jobs needs a download action, and every `uses:` key in this workflow names a full commit rather than a tag - a pin this repository has to be able to verify rather than copy. Nothing in the run depends on those archives except the six images built from them in the same job, so they are produced where they are consumed | The reactor is packaged twice in a run: once to check it packages, once to produce the archives the images carry. That is the smaller of the two duplications this review found, and it is bounded by one job. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the hand-off |

### What a container costs when every test class starts its own

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One PostgreSQL container per module, shared by every test class in that module through a single owning facility | Leave each class starting its own; one container for the whole reactor; Testcontainers' reuse flag | Fifty class-level declarations started fifty database servers, ten of them alive at once in one module, each paying its own start and migration. One per module is one start per fork. A single container for the reactor cannot work: the plugins fork per module, so there is no process to own it. The reuse flag is exempt from the resource reaper, so a container it starts is removed by nothing | A container shared by a module is a container a slow class holds. Each class still pays its own schema migration, which is where most of the time went, so the saving is the server start rather than the whole setup |
| Each test class is given its own database inside the shared container, keyed on the class name | Share one database and one migrated schema; give each class its own schema; clean tables between classes | The schema name is fixed: `application.yml` names it for both Flyway and Hibernate's validation, so it cannot vary per class. A database can. Every class previously had a container to itself, which is a database to itself, so a database per class preserves exactly the isolation that existed and changes no test's meaning. A shared database would make every class order-dependent | Databases accumulate for the life of the fork, and a class whose name collides with another's hash would share one. The name carries six hex digits of the fully qualified name, and the facilities record every database they create |
| The eight broker classes keep a Kafka container each | Pool the broker as the database is pooled; rename topics per class; share one broker across a module | The integration classes assert the *shipped* topic names - the dead-letter routing test compares the record's topic with the name the service publishes to - and notification's two broker classes use the same topics and the same consumer groups. Sharing one broker would need per-class topic renaming, which would stop those classes proving the delivered names work. Only two forks hold more than one broker class, so the prize was two container starts | Eight brokers still start in a full run, which is most of the remaining container time. `TestInfrastructureOwnershipContractTest` pins the eight by name, so a ninth has to be declared deliberately |
| The fixture posting run is computed once per test class and shared by every assertion | Recompute per test; compute in a `@BeforeEach`; hold the result in a mutable static field | Thirty assertions each drove all three hundred fixture records through the posting path to look at one column of the outcome. The run is deterministic and nothing asserts against a mutated copy, so one run answers all thirty. A holder class initialised on first use keeps it lazy, and a digest checked after the class runs proves no test changed what a later test read | A shared result is a result one test could corrupt for the rest. The digest guard is what turns that from a silent wrong answer into a failure naming the class |
### What the deck draws again when a slide comes back

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A diagram block records the source it was drawn from, and a block whose record still matches its source is left untouched | Draw every diagram once at load; keep redrawing on each visit; hold a plain drawn/not-drawn flag | Returning to a diagram slide used to restore the raw source and draw it again, so a diagram was redrawn as many times as the deck was navigated over it. Recording the source rather than a flag means a block whose source ever changed would be drawn again, which is the behaviour a flag would silently lose. Drawing all three at load would delay the first slide for diagrams a five-minute talk may not reach | The record is written only after the run resolves, so a diagram that failed is drawn again on the next visit - a retry on every visit for a diagram that cannot be drawn at all. Measured in a browser: three draws on a first pass through the deck, and zero more across a backward and a forward pass over all sixteen slides |
| The icon guard asks whether the slide holds an element naming an icon that has not been given the library's own class | Test for the naming attribute alone; test for the placeholder tag name; pass the slide to the library; count icons per slide | The library copies the naming attribute onto the graphic it draws, so a guard reading that attribute alone matches finished icons, never reaches zero on a slide that has any, and rebuilds all seventeen icons in the document on every navigation. Verified against the pinned bundle, which builds its output with that attribute and always adds the class beside it. The class is therefore what separates a waiting placeholder from a drawn icon. The library cannot be given a container at all: it reads the whole document itself | The guard depends on a property of the library's output rather than on a documented option, so a major version that stopped adding that class would make the guard fire when it should not and leave an icon undrawn. `PresentationAndProseContractTest` pins the selector and the pinned library version together, so the two move as one. Measured in a browser: the first pass draws all seventeen, and forty-five slide changes afterwards call the library zero times |

## Security-review remediation decisions

A whole-project security review found sixteen items. Each decision below answers one of them, names
what was rejected, and states what the choice leaves open.

### The subject of a decision comes from a stored row

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **SUPERSEDED by "One event for the outcome whose card resolves nothing, keyed on the identifier this service minted" in the completeness-review section below, which restored the decline without restoring the declared account. Its reasoning about the subject is unchanged and is why that decision keys on a minted identifier. It read:** A card that resolves no cross-reference row is refused for every caller, before an identifier is drawn and before any row is written. **Supersedes "Resolve the subject of every decided call, and publish reject code 0100 like the other three"** | Keep the decline and validate the declared account against a stored row; keep the decline and key it on the transaction identifier again; keep the decline and key it on a sentinel account; refuse only a caller holding no ownership scope | The review found that the account came from the request body, and that no stored row of this platform ties that value to the card presented. An entitled caller — the acquirer identity this route exists for, or an administrator — could therefore pair an unknown card with any account and leave a durable decision, an audit row and a published decline against an account it had merely named. Validating the declared account against a stored row proves the account exists and still proves nothing about the card, which is the pairing the finding is about. Keying on the transaction identifier is the position two revisions ago, and it puts a decline on a partition no account owns. A sentinel account puts a false subject on the wire. Refusing only an unscoped caller leaves the two identities that actually reach this route unaffected. The source settles it: the feed record at `app/cpy/CVTRA06Y.cpy` carries twelve fields and no account, so `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L394` has to take the subject out of the resolved cross-reference row, and the synchronous ancestor at `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L625-L626` reports the card unknown and decides nothing | A caller sending an unknown card receives `422` with that source text rather than a decline body carrying reject code `0100`, which is a change to this route's answer. Reject code 0100 is now a batch outcome that this platform's synchronous path never publishes, so a consumer waiting for one waits for ever; `docs/event-flow.md` and the service README both say so |
| The entitlement comparison stays ahead of the refusal | Refuse the unresolved card first, since it needs no entitlement; refuse both in one branch | A refusal that runs before the entitlement check answers a caller with no entitlement whether a card exists, which is the card-existence oracle the review closed on the 403 path. Ordering the two makes an unentitled caller see one answer whatever card it sends | The comparison runs against a subject the caller may not own, so the refusal it precedes is reached only by a caller entitled to that subject |
| **In force as to the table. Its metric half is SUPERSEDED by "One event for the outcome whose card resolves nothing" below, which made the outcome a counted decision.** `unresolved_card_attempt` is dropped rather than left empty | Keep the table and stop writing it; keep it and write it without an account; keep the account column and leave it null | Every column of that table duplicated a column of `authorization_decision`, and its `account_id` column held the value the review objected to. A table nothing writes is a table a later reader has to work out the status of. The fact worth keeping is that an unknown card reached this service, and the `authorization_decision` row keeps it: one row per such call, naming the actor, the masked card, the amount and reject code `0100`, with `account_id` null. The failure-stage counter that briefly carried the fact instead is withdrawn, because the outcome is a decision and `carddemo.authorization.decisions` counts it under its own reject code | Rows written under the earlier contract go with the table, so the audit trail of those calls is the `authorization_decision` row alone. That row is not rewritten, and `V20__unresolved_card_attempt_withdrawn.sql` states as much |
| **In force. Its last column is SUPERSEDED by "One event for the outcome whose card resolves nothing" below, which gave version 2 a producer again.** `transaction-declined-v2` and `-v3` both stay released and on the classpath | Retire version 2 now that no reason-0100 decline is published; retire both and republish version 3 as version 4 | Deleting a released document is the one change a consumer cannot undo, and a record already retained on `transaction.declined` under either version stays readable. `EventEnvelope.AGGREGATE_KEY_PATTERN` admits both key forms on the read side. Keeping version 2 released is also what let the next revision restore the decline without declaring a new contract fork | Both versions now have a producer, so a reader of the schema folder cannot tell from the folder alone which reason travels which document. `contracts/released-contracts.json` states the posture of each version and each document's `$comment` says which fact it carries |

### A duplicate-delivery claim is permanent

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Nothing deletes a `processed_event` row. The horizon is withdrawn from all six services, together with the setting, the sweep step, the repository method and the index that served it | Raise the horizon to a longer window; keep the horizon and delete a claim only once every effect it guards has expired; keep the horizon and add a natural-key uniqueness constraint to every guarded effect | A horizon bounds how long the broker can redeliver, and a record can arrive from further away than that. An archive, a restored backup and an operator resetting a consumer group all deliver a record the guard has already forgotten. Every effect a claim guards outlives any horizon worth setting: a posted balance, a category balance, a cycle accumulator, a read-model row, a rendered alert. Tying the horizon to the longest-lived effect makes it unbounded in all but name. A uniqueness constraint per effect is the stronger answer and a wider change, and `account` carries no column a constraint could key on | The table grows by one row per consumed event and nothing reclaims the space. A row is 61 bytes of `CHAR(36)`, topic name and timestamp, so a million consumed events cost tens of megabytes. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the work of bounding what a permanent claim costs. It also records why range partitioning on `processed_at` is not that bound: PostgreSQL requires the partition key inside every unique constraint, so one claim would be unique per partition rather than for ever |
| `processed_at` stays on the row and stops being a purge key | Drop the column with the horizon; keep the column and keep the index | The column answers when a claim was written, which is what an operator reads while diagnosing a redelivery. Nothing scans it for a horizon any more, so the index on it maintained a write on every consumed event for a query no code issues. Each service's migration drops `ix_processed_event_processed_at` and restates the column comment as evidence rather than a purge key | The platform's index total falls from 47 to 41, and `docs/data-model.md` restates it. A future purge would have to add the index back in the same change as the query that reads it |
| Six forward migrations state the permanence in the catalogue, rather than an edit to the migration that created each table | Edit `V1` in each service; state it in the properties class alone; state it in the service README alone | A `COMMENT ON` lands in the catalogue, and an operator reads the catalogue rather than the Java source. Flyway compares a checksum on every start, so editing an applied file refuses to start against the demo volume that already applied it. Each migration writes `retention=permanent; purge_key=none`, which is the phrase `RetentionSweepContractTest` reads to tell a table nothing expires from one something does | Six files say the same thing, one per schema, because each schema is applied on its own. The account file is `V11` rather than `V8`: `V8__subject_request_posture.sql` already held that version, and two migrations of one version stop Flyway outright |
| The `ProcessedEvent` properties record is removed from all six configuration classes | Keep the record and ignore the value; keep it and bind it to a fixed unbounded figure; keep the start-up refusal and raise the required margin | A bound component is a value a deployment can set, and a value that changes nothing is worse than absent because a reader believes it. Removing the record is what makes the horizon unreachable from configuration. `PROCESSED_EVENT_RETENTION_HOURS` joins the retired keys `ConfigurationInventoryContractTest` refuses in any deployment artifact | A reused `.env` still carrying the assignment keeps it, reported by `scripts/generate-env.sh` and read by nothing. Six services each lost a start-up refusal, and each replaced it with a test that asserts no component and no nested record can reintroduce one |
| `KAFKA_LOG_RETENTION_HOURS` no longer reaches any service | Leave it in the shared Compose block; give each service its own copy again | The figure existed in each service so it could be compared against that service's marker horizon. No service compares anything against it now, so a value handed to a container that binds nothing is a value a reader can set with no effect. The broker still applies it, from its own environment block and from the ConfigMap | Broker retention and duplicate suppression are no longer related by any check, because they are no longer related at all. The relationship the check enforced was the finding |
| A natural key stays the second line of defence where one exists, and the paths where none exists are why the claim had to become permanent | Add a uniqueness constraint to every guarded effect and keep the horizon; rely on the natural keys alone and remove the claim | Three consumers already refuse a replay on a key. `TransactionEntity` and `FraudAssessmentEntity` both answer `isNew` true for every instance, so a second insert of one transaction identifier refuses and rolls the arithmetic beside it back. `statement_transaction` is keyed on card token and transaction, so a replay rewrites one row with the same values. Two paths have no such key: the account service adds an amount to a balance and a cycle accumulator with nothing to collide on, and `notification_log` takes a fresh identifier per render. Those two are exactly the double-processing the review named | A replay the natural key catches arrives as a constraint violation rather than as a skip, so it retries and then reaches the dead-letter topic. That is louder than the claim, which is why the claim runs first and the key is second |

### Moving a card token is an audited act, not a start-up rewrite

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `card.card_token` carries the key version it was taken under and whether this deployment derived it, in `card_token_version` and `card_token_provenance` | Infer the version by trying each configured key; keep one boolean "needs re-keying" column; keep no column and re-derive at every start-up as before | A card token is an `HmacSHA256` code over a card number, so a key or version change renames every card. Four places hold that name and only `card` holds a card number: `statement_transaction.card_token` and `notification_log.card_token` in the notification service, `authorization_decision.card_token` in the authorization service, and any granted `SCOPE_CARD` authority. A security review found the reconciler re-deriving its own column silently, which left all four naming a card nobody could reach. Two columns are the smallest state that separates correcting a checked-in literal from moving a live identity, and they are read without deriving anything | A row an operator edits by hand can claim a provenance it does not have. `ck_card_card_token_provenance` bounds the value to two words and cannot check the claim against the token |
| A `SEED` row is corrected without being asked; a `DERIVED` row stops start-up unless `CARD_TOKEN_ROTATION_ENABLED` is `true` | Rewrite both silently, as before; refuse both and make the first start-up an operator procedure; log a warning and rewrite anyway | The fifty seeded literals belong to the build-scope key in `pom.xml` and every deployment generates its own, so a first start-up always finds fifty drifted rows. Nothing outside the service can be holding one of those tokens: the reconciliation finishes before the readiness probe accepts traffic, and no event has carried a token derived here. A row this deployment derived is the opposite case, and rewriting it is what the review found. A warning that rewrites anyway leaves the same four stores broken and adds a log line | An operator who sets the statement and restarts without supplying the previous key gets a second refusal rather than a rotation. That is deliberate: without the previous key no mapping can be written, so the rewrite would be unmappable |
| A rotation writes one audit row per run and one mapping row per re-keyed card, both permanent | Log the mapping instead of storing it; publish a `CardTokenRotated` event per card; expose an admin endpoint that returns the mapping | The three stores holding a token hold no card number, so a re-key needs the pairing stated somewhere durable, and a log is neither queryable nor a thing an operator can `\copy`. Read in the other direction the same rows are the rollback. An event would need a released schema, a topic, broker access rules and two new consumers, for a message no consumer could act on without the same manual statements.
