An endpoint would return what the operator can already read, to a caller who already has database access | The mapping is a pairing of two tokens of one card, so a holder of either token learns the other. It holds no card number and no key material, and a token holder already knows the token they hold |
| `authorization_decision` records `card_token_version` and the notification read model records none | Add the column to both; add it to neither and infer staleness from the rotation window | The decision row is a diagnostic and is never rebuilt, so the version is the only thing that tells a reader whether a stale token names the card they think it does. The notification read model is rebuildable by replaying `transaction.posted` from the retained log, and the mapping re-keys it in place, so a column there would record what two other mechanisms already answer | A decision row written before `V22` carries the default version `1`. Where the key moved but the version did not, the column says which key only in combination with the rotation audit rows |
| The re-key of the other two schemas is an operator procedure in `services/card-service/README.md`, not a job | Have the card service reach into the other two databases; publish a mapping event each consumer applies; ship a migration in each service that reads the card schema | Each service owns its schema and no service reads another's, which is the boundary this platform is for. A cross-schema job would break it, and a mapping event would put a card-token pairing on a topic to solve a problem the operator solves with three idempotent statements. AAP section 0.2.2 also puts operational scaffolding of this kind out of scope for a demonstration | A rotation is not atomic across services. Between the card rewrite and the two re-key statements, a history request for a re-keyed card finds no rows. The procedure says to run the statements immediately and states this, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the automation |
| The previous key is not held to the published-key refusal that the current key is | Refuse the published key in both halves; drop the refusal from both | Rotating away from the published demonstration key is exactly the case that refusal exists to force, and it cannot be performed without deriving under the key being left behind. Holding both halves to it would make the one rotation this platform most needs impossible | A deployment can derive under the published key by naming it as the previous key. It cannot serve tokens under it, because the current key is still refused |

### One produce-side gate for every event

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every producer path writes its payload through one shared boundary, `PublishGate.checkedJsonOf`, and no writer holds a mapper of its own | Add the two cardholder-data screens to each of the five writers; move `JsonSchemaValidatingSerializer` onto the Kafka producers; screen on the consume side only | Two produce-side gates existed and they disagreed. `JsonSchemaValidatingSerializer` applied the class check, the topic binding, both screens, the document and the byte ceiling; the writers wrote their own text with a plain mapper and measured it against the document alone, and the fraud writer measured only the contract posture. A card number in a free-text property therefore committed with the business state, reached a topic, and was refused there by the consume-side screens: a transaction approved that could never post. One boundary cannot disagree with itself | The gate is now on the hot write path of six services, so a change to it changes every publisher at once. Its own tests carry the adversarial payloads, and every writer test asserts nothing is saved when the gate refuses |
| The gate returns the text it checked, and the writer stores exactly those bytes | Check the record, then write it again for storage; store the record and serialize in the relay | Writing the event a second time stores bytes no gate has seen, which is the defect being closed rather than a smaller version of it. Storing the record would move serialization behind the relay, where a refusal can no longer roll back the decision that caused it | The stored payload is now produced by the shared serializer rather than by a service-local mapper. The account service's own mapper produced identical bytes, because its monetary components carry `ToStringSerializer`, so no wire form changed |
| The two account-service records serialize through the gate as well, rather than through the serializer directly | Leave `AccountStateChanged.toValidatedJson` and `CustomerContextChanged.toValidatedJson` on the shared serializer | A second path to a topic is a second thing to keep in step. Both methods now apply the contract-posture check they were missing, and a caller reaching either one gets the screens | A refusal from either method now arrives as `IllegalArgumentException` rather than as Kafka's `SerializationException`. Nothing publishes at that point, so the Kafka type named a concern the caller had not reached |
| The produce side refuses an `extensions` object; the consume side keeps reading one | Refuse it on both sides; screen it on both and refuse it on neither | Every schema document declares `extensions` as the channel additive evolution travels through, so refusing it on read would refuse the enriched record a later version publishes. No record of this platform declares one, so a payload carrying it on the way out was built by something other than its own record | A future producer that legitimately needs the channel has to declare it on the record and lift the produce-side closure. The closure is one method, named, with a test either way |
| A narrative field takes a wider screen than a structured one: any unbroken run of nine digits or more, and a value that is nothing but three or four digits | Apply the wider screen to every screened property; apply it to none and keep only the labelled forms; detect on the label alone | An unlabelled verification code and an unseparated government identifier carry no separator and no label to key on, so only their shape gives them away. That shape is also a legitimate postal code and a legitimate credit score, so the wider screen is applied where a person writes prose and not where a pattern already holds the value. The fixtures settle it: no description in the three hundred records of `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` and no customer field in the fifty of `app/data/ASCII/custdata.txt` carries either shape, while `app/data/ASCII/custdata.txt` does carry `19852-6716` | A merchant reference that is nine digits of prose would be refused. `merchantId` is nine digits by its own pattern and keeps the structured screen, so the refusal falls only on a field a caller wrote by hand |
| The dead-letter diagnostic fields stay out of the narrative screen | Screen `reason`, `message` and `culprit` on the same terms as a description | A refusal inside the dead-letter path suppresses the dead letter itself, and the record it describes is then redelivered for ever. Those three values are written by this platform from a caught failure, not by a caller, and the structured screen still covers them | A platform-written diagnostic could carry a long digit run from a payload it is describing. The structured screen refuses a card number there, which is the shape that matters |
| An array reports the property that owns it, not the index of the element inside it | Report the element path; report the literal container name | A refusal names a property so an author can find the value that caused it. `triggeredRules[0]` and `extensions` both send a reader looking in the wrong place when the value sits in a named array | A caller with two offending elements in one array is told about one of them, and finds the second on the next attempt |
| Property names fold through Unicode compatibility normalization before they are compared | Compare the names as written; strip non-ASCII characters | A full-width property name is the same property to a JSON reader and a different string to a comparison, so a screen keyed on the written name can be stepped around with one substitution. Normalizing first closes that without widening what the screen refuses | Normalization runs on every property of every published event. The measured cost is one pass over a name that is already in the parser's cache |

### The stored card verification value keeps a formal exception

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `card.card_verification_value` stays, and `V11__card_verification_value_exception.sql` is the exception record it stays under | Drop the column, its mapping, the fifty seeded values and the preservation test by forward migration, which is what the review asked for first; keep the column and stop seeding it; replace the stored value with a token | The plan settles it. AAP section 0.4.1 requires this service to store the field, section 0.6.4 states that it is persisted because the card record defines it and is never emitted and that a test asserts the non-emission, and section 0.2.2 places payment-card industry controls beyond the one documented masking deviation outside the engagement. The plan is the frozen contract this work is measured against, so the review's own second route is the one taken. Dropping the seed values would also drop the fifty-row parity `CardSeedEquivalenceTest` reads out of `app/data/ASCII/carddata.txt`, which is an equivalence obligation the same plan carries | Three digits of cleartext authentication data stay at rest in every deployment. Nothing reads them and nothing needs them, so the value is liability alone until a project owner answers `business-rule-flags.md` entry D4 |
| Encryption at rest is the first of the four controls, declared on the claim that holds the column and bound by an overlay | Encrypt the column itself in the application; encrypt it in the database with `pgcrypto`; state the requirement in documentation alone | Column encryption needs a key the service holds, and a service that can decrypt a value it never reads has gained a reader rather than lost one. The volume is the layer that answers the threat the review named, which is a copied disk or an unprotected snapshot. `deploy/k8s/overlays/encrypted-storage` binds an encrypted class for both claims in one command, and both claims declare the requirement so a cluster applying the base without the overlay is auditable rather than silent | A base applied without the overlay binds whatever class the cluster has, and a bound claim's class cannot be changed afterwards. The demonstration Compose stack is unencrypted by design and holds synthetic fixtures only |
| Minimal access is asserted by a test rather than described in a comment | Add a package-private accessor for a future need; document the rule in the entity javadoc alone | A value with no accessor, `@JsonIgnore` on the field and a withheld marker in `toString` has four exits and all four are closed. `CardholderDataExposureTest` reads every one of them reflectively, so an accessor added later fails the build in the same commit rather than in a later review | A repository could still project the column through a native query. `RepositorySurfaceTest` reads the repository surface, and no query on this platform names the column |
| The audit is two tests on the two sides of the service boundary | One test inside the service; one contract test in `equivalence-tests`; a scanner rule in `.gitleaks.toml` | Inside the service the question is whether the object leaks the value, which needs the class. Outside it the question is whether the published exception, its reader inventory and its destruction procedure still describe the delivered tree, which needs the files. `CardholderDataExposureTest` answers the first and `SubjectDataGovernanceContractTest` answers the second. A scanner matches text and cannot tell a stored column from a comment about one | Two tests can disagree about what the exception says. Both read the delivered artifacts rather than a copy of the wording, so a change to the migration or the procedure fails one of them |
| Destruction is a published procedure in the service README, not a migration held in reserve | Ship the drop migration disabled; ship it behind a Flyway placeholder; leave destruction undocumented until it is asked for | A migration checked in and not applied is a file Flyway will apply the moment the version is reached, which makes the decision an accident waiting on a deployment. The procedure states the four edits and the one migration a deployment writes, and names every artifact each edit touches, so answering the question is an afternoon rather than a discovery exercise | The procedure is prose and can drift from the tree it describes. `SubjectDataGovernanceContractTest` reads it and holds each artifact it names to the delivered tree |
| The exception carries no expiry date | Set a review date one year out; set one at the next release; require the exception to be renewed each quarter | A date this engagement invented would be a date nobody agreed to, and an expired exception with no owner is worse than an open one with a named question. The trigger is an answer to `business-rule-flags.md` entry D4, which is a decision a project owner makes and this platform records | An exception with no clock can outlive the attention that opened it. The register entry, the migration header and the destruction procedure all name each other, so the open question is reachable from any of the three |

### A subject request is a procedure before it is an orchestration

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One documented operator procedure covers discovery, export and erasure across all six schemas, in `docs/data-model.md` under "Subject data: purpose, retention, export and erasure" | A subject-request endpoint per service; a `SubjectErasureRequested` event every service consumes; one cross-schema job in the account service; a seventh service owning subject requests | The platform's own boundary decides it. Each service owns its schema and no service reads another's, so an orchestration is six coordinated writes with no transaction across them, and a half-finished erasure is worse than a documented one. An endpoint per service still needs the caller that sequences them, an event needs six released schemas and six consumers for a message no consumer can act on alone, and a cross-schema job breaks the boundary this platform exists to demonstrate. AAP section 0.2.2 puts operational scaffolding of this kind outside a demonstration. The procedure is executable today, states the order and says why the order matters | A procedure is a person following steps, so it is slower than a call and it can be run wrong. It is also unaudited beyond what the operator writes down, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the audited orchestration that would replace it |
| Four migrations restate the catalogue comments that said no route existed | Leave the comments and add the section; edit the migrations that wrote them; state the correction in the READMEs alone | Six comments carried the sentence that no erasure or export workflow exists, and that sentence stopped being the whole truth the moment a procedure was published. An operator reads the catalogue, so the catalogue is where the pointer belongs. Flyway compares a checksum on every start, so editing an applied migration refuses to start against a volume that already applied it | Four more migrations to apply, and the sentence now appears in two forms across versions of the same comment. The last comment wins in the catalogue, which is the mechanism every correction on this platform uses |
| `notification.statement_transaction` is reclassified from `personal_data=no` to `pseudonymous` | Leave the classification and explain it in the section; drop the classification token altogether | The table is keyed on a card token, and a token that links every row of one card is a pseudonym rather than nothing. The earlier value would have let a reader of the catalogue skip the table during a request, which is exactly the discovery failure the review found | The token still names no card without the key that derived it, so the correction raises the classification of a table whose rows a holder of the token could already read |
| The customer context is not minimized, because parity forbids it | Drop the credit score from the projection; drop the postal address; hold only the name and the account | Measured rather than assumed. All ten columns of `cardholder_context` are rendered by `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L458-L504`, so removing any one of them breaks the statement parity the equivalence suite asserts. The projection already carries no government identifier, no electronic funds transfer account and no card number, which is the minimization that was available | Ten cardholder fields sit in a second schema, kept current by an event. The erasure procedure reaches them first for that reason, and the retained broker record is why a completed erasure waits out the log window |
| The completion record belongs in the deployment's case management, not in a platform table | Add a `subject_request` table to the account service; add one to every service; write the record to the structured log | A table inside a service is the one store an erasure cannot erase, and a record of the erasure has to outlive the rows it describes. A log line ages out with the log. The section states the six things one run writes down, so a deployment can hold them wherever it holds its other obligations | This platform ships no evidence that a request was fulfilled. That is the largest remaining gap, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries it as the orchestration's first requirement |
| A retained broker record and a backup age out rather than being erased | Delete the broker records of an erased subject; shorten the log window to zero; treat a backup as out of scope | Neither is reachable by a statement. A Kafka log is append-only, so an erasure inside a segment is a rewrite of the segment, and a window of zero breaks the replay every consumer's recovery depends on. A backup is a point in time and a restore undoes an erasure by definition, so the obligation is to know which backups predate a completed erasure and to re-apply it to any that is restored | An erasure is complete only after the broker window covering the last published event expires, which is 168 hours as shipped. A deployment that needs faster completion shortens the window and accepts the shorter replay |

### Encryption at rest is a declared requirement and a one-command overlay

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Both `PersistentVolumeClaim` manifests declare the requirement in annotations and still name no storage class | Name `carddemo-encrypted` in the base; name a class and document how to override it; leave the base unchanged and document the requirement in prose alone | A base that names a class no cluster has fails to bind, and the documented local demonstration runs on kind, minikube and Docker Desktop with no edit. An annotation is readable by a person and by a policy engine, so a cluster running the base unencrypted is auditable rather than silent. The annotations state what each volume holds, which is what makes the requirement arguable rather than decorative | The base still binds an unencrypted class on a cluster whose default is unencrypted, and nothing at apply time refuses it. A policy engine is the enforcement this platform does not ship, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries it |
| The overlay patches by `kind` rather than by name | Patch each claim by name; ship two patch files; ship a full copy of both manifests | A patch keyed on `kind: PersistentVolumeClaim` reaches a third claim added to the base with no edit here, and a patch keyed on a name goes stale the moment a manifest is renamed. The resource list is the base folder rather than a second copy of the eleven manifests for the same reason | A future claim that genuinely should not be encrypted would be encrypted by this overlay. Both claims that exist hold subject data, and a claim that holds none is a change worth noticing |
| The Compose stack keeps unencrypted local volumes, and says so where an operator reads it | Encrypt the Compose volumes; refuse to start without an encrypted mount; say nothing | Docker Compose has no storage-class equivalent, and a local volume's encryption is the host's disk encryption rather than anything a compose file can declare. The honest answer is the posture stated in the file: a single operator, on a loopback binding, holding synthetic fixtures alone. A refusal to start would make the documented five-minute demonstration impossible | A deployment that copies the compose file into a shared environment inherits an unencrypted volume. The volumes block, the environment template and the onboarding guide each state the posture, and the Kubernetes path is the deployed answer |
| Key ownership, rotation and the restore proof are documented obligations rather than delivered code | Ship a key rotation script; ship a backup job; ship a restore test | A storage key belongs to the cluster's key service and its rotation is that service's procedure, not an application's. A backup job and a restore proof are the high-availability and disaster-recovery work AAP section 0.2.2 excludes from this engagement. `deploy/k8s/README.md` states the obligations under "Encryption at rest" so a deployment inherits them explicitly | This platform proves no restore. The obligation is stated and unmet, and `suggested-next-tasks.md` carries the work |

### Forwarded headers are trusted in one profile, and only behind a named proxy

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every shipped `application.yml` states `forward-headers-strategy: none`, and a separate `application-trusted-proxy.yml` per service enables forwarding | Leave the setting unstated and inherit the framework default; enable `framework` forwarding in the shipped file; enable it and filter forwarded headers in application code | The default is already `none`, so stating it changes no behaviour and answers a different question: whether the value is a choice or an oversight. A review could not tell, and the two filters that depend on it are the reason it matters. Enabling forwarding in the shipped file would trust a header any caller can write on a container reachable directly, which is the arrangement the review named. A profile is inert until a deployment activates it | A deployment that puts a proxy in front of a service without activating the profile still keys ceilings on the proxy and compares an internal host. The profile is documented in the guide of every service and in the ConfigMap comment, and nothing at start-up can tell whether a proxy is present |
| The profile uses Tomcat's `native` strategy rather than the framework's | Use `framework`; write a servlet filter that reads the headers itself; parse `Forwarded` instead | The framework strategy honours the headers on every request whoever sent them, so a caller reaching the container directly can claim any address. The native strategy is Tomcat's own valve and it rewrites only for a request whose immediate peer matches `internal-proxies`, which is the property that makes the trust conditional rather than blanket | The valve trusts the first element of a header a compliant proxy overwrote. A proxy that appends leaves a client-supplied address in that position, and no setting here can detect it. The profile states the condition as the operator obligation it is |
| `TRUSTED_PROXY_ADDRESSES` carries no default, so an activated profile with no value stops start-up | Default to the private address ranges; default to the cluster pod range; default to a value that matches nothing | A default that matches the private ranges trusts every workload sharing the network, which on a cluster is every pod. A default matching nothing would activate a profile that silently does nothing, and a profile whose whole purpose is inactive is worse than a refusal. The variable is deliberately absent from `.env.example`, the composition and the ConfigMap, because a documented default is the thing being refused | An operator has to write a regular expression, and a wrong one either trusts too much or breaks the deployment it was added for. `deploy/k8s/30-configmap.yaml` names the profile and the variable together so the pair is discoverable |
| The profile ships per service rather than once | One shared profile file; a ConfigMap key each service reads; a Helm value | Spring reads a profile file from the classpath of the service that runs, so a shared file would have to be packaged into six images anyway. Six copies of nine lines drift, which is why `RequestSurfaceControlContractTest` reads all six and compares them | Six files to change together. The contract test refuses a service configured unlike its peers, so drift fails the unit-test phase |

### A refusal runs ahead of the password it would otherwise pay for

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Four filters declare an explicit rung ahead of the security chain: correlation at minus four, the rate ceiling at minus three, the body ceiling at minus two and the cross-site check at minus one | Leave the cross-site and body filters unordered, as shipped; order them after the chain explicitly; move both checks into the chain as an `AuthenticationEntryPoint` | An unordered `@Component` filter runs after Spring Security, so every refusal it makes was paid for with a bcrypt verification of a request the service was always going to turn away. That is the cost the review named, and an order is the only thing that moves it. The rungs are ordered by what each refusal costs, cheapest first, so the sequence is derivable rather than arbitrary | An order is a number in an annotation and nothing at run time proves the resulting sequence. `RequestSurfaceControlContractTest` reads all four rungs of all six services and refuses a duplicate or a missing one |
| The cross-site and body filters defer when the request carries no `Authorization` header | Order them ahead of the chain unconditionally; keep them after the chain to preserve the status; answer 401 from the filter itself | Ordering a refusal ahead of authentication moves the answer as well as the cost. An unauthenticated cross-site request answered 403 tells a caller that the route exists and that its credential was the only thing missing. The deferral keeps 401 the first answer, and there is no cost to defer: a request with no credential has no password to verify | Two filters now carry a branch that a reader has to understand before changing either. The contract test asserts the branch alongside the order, so removing one without the other fails. One detail did move: an authenticated state-changing request that omits the required header now receives the cross-site 403 rather than the route rule's 403, because the cheaper refusal reaches it first. The status is the same and the new text discloses less, and two route-security integration tests present the header so each still measures the rule it names |
| The rate ceiling deliberately does not defer | Defer for consistency with the other two; count only authenticated requests; count unauthenticated requests under a separate ceiling | A flood of unauthenticated requests is precisely the cost this ceiling exists to bound, so a filter that stood aside for them would bound nothing. The `authentication` stage is the ceiling that answers it, and it needs to see the request | The rate filter can answer 429 to a request the chain would have answered 401, which is a status a caller can observe that it could not before. A caller learns only that it is being throttled, which it can learn from an authenticated request anyway |
| The correlation filter runs first of the four | Run it last so a refusal carries no identity; run it inside the chain; leave it where it was at minus two | A refusal is the event most worth correlating, so the identity has to be attached before anything can refuse. Moving it from minus two to minus four keeps that property as two filters were inserted below it | Three more filters now run before authentication, so a request that will be refused still allocates a correlation identity. That is a map write against a bounded map, which is the cheapest thing on the ladder |

### Every Kafka wire ceiling is a rung above the envelope this platform governs

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Five ceilings form one ladder: the 8192-byte governed envelope, a 16384-byte producer `max.request.size`, a 32768-byte broker `message.max.bytes`, a 65536-byte consumer `max.partition.fetch.bytes` and a 262144-byte `fetch.max.bytes` | Leave every ceiling at its client default, as shipped; set each ceiling to the envelope exactly; raise the broker ceiling alone | Every default is orders of magnitude above the envelope, so a message this platform would never publish is one the transport would happily carry, and the only thing refusing it is application code. Each rung above the one below it leaves room for the envelope plus its record overhead and batching, so a message inside the envelope always fits and a message far outside it is refused by the transport as well | Five numbers in eight files have to move together if the envelope ever moves. `KafkaDeliveryGuaranteeContractTest` reads every one of them and asserts the ladder is monotonic, so a single edit fails the build |
| `EventWireBounds.MAX_EVENT_BYTES` stays the governing ceiling, and the transport ceilings sit above it | Make the producer ceiling the governing one; drop the application check now that the transport has one; govern in the schema alone | A transport refusal is a broker error a consumer cannot interpret, and it arrives after the outbox row is committed. The application refusal happens before the row is written, names the event and its size, and rolls the whole publish back. The transport ceilings are a second line, which is what they are good at | Two places refuse an oversized event and they refuse it differently. The application ceiling is the lower of the two by construction, so the transport ceiling is only reached by a defect in the application one |
| `ssl.endpoint.identification.algorithm: https` is stated explicitly in all six services | Leave it at the client default, which is the same value; state it only in the Kubernetes profile; disable it for the demonstration composition | The value equals the default today, and a future client release changing that default would silently drop hostname verification from a deployment using SASL_SSL. Stating it makes the property a decision the contract test reads rather than an inherited behaviour. The demonstration composition uses SASL_PLAINTEXT, where the setting is inert, so one value serves both paths | Six copies of one line. The contract test reads all six, so a service missing it fails |
| The broker ceiling is a literal in both deployment descriptions rather than a ConfigMap key | Add `KAFKA_MESSAGE_MAX_BYTES` to the ConfigMap and the environment template; derive it from the envelope at start-up | The broker ceiling has to agree with the four application ceilings, and a key an operator can override is an invitation to break that agreement in one place. The value belongs to the ladder rather than to a deployment, so it is written where the broker is declared and read from there by the contract test | An operator who needs a larger broker ceiling has to edit two manifests. That edit is exactly the one that should fail the contract test if the rest of the ladder does not move with it |

### A secret scan reads the change under review, and the whole history on a schedule

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A push and a pull request scan the commit range under review, and the whole history moved to a weekly schedule and a manual run | Keep the full-history scan on every event; drop the history scan entirely; scan only the working tree | The full-history scan reports 113 findings over 2,580 commits, so every push failed before an image was built. A gate that can never pass is a gate somebody switches off. The range is where a secret this change introduces would appear, and that is the gate a push now answers to | A secret introduced outside the reviewed range waits until the Monday run to be reported. The working-tree scan still reads every file on every event, so a secret present in the tree fails immediately whichever commit added it |
| The 113 findings are recorded in a committed baseline rather than excused by an allowlist rule | Add an allowlist pattern per finding; allowlist the paths they sit in; rewrite the history to remove them | A baseline matches by fingerprint, which is the commit, the path, the rule and the line. It excuses exactly the finding that was reviewed and nothing that resembles it, where an allowlist pattern excuses every future value of the same shape. Rewriting the history of a shared sample repository is not a change this engagement may make | A reviewed baseline can grow into a habit. The scheduled scan fails on any finding the baseline does not name, and a canary step proves the baseline suppresses only its own entries |
| Every baseline entry is redacted, so the file records which finding was reviewed and never what it held | Commit the values so a reviewer can judge them; commit the file outside the repository; keep no baseline file | The baseline is committed and readable by anyone with the repository. A finding is identified by its fingerprint, so the value adds nothing to the comparison and would publish whatever the history holds. A workflow step counts the entries carrying a value and fails on the first one | A reviewer cannot see what an entry held without checking out the commit it names. The triage below is what stands in for that, and the entries are grouped so a reader can follow it |
| The triage found nothing this engagement authored and nothing this branch can reach | Treat every finding as a live credential and rotate; treat every finding as noise without reading it | All 113 findings sit on 39 commits dated 2025-10-25 to 2026-08-08, none reachable from this branch and none inside the range this engagement wrote. They belong to unrelated branches of a shared sample repository, which the scanner reads because it scans every ref. The paths confirm it: most of the directories they name do not exist in this tree | A finding on an unreachable ref is still a finding in the repository. Nothing here can rotate a credential belonging to another branch, and suggested-next-tasks.md carries the request to the repository owner |
| The nine Kubernetes-manifest findings and the 72 bearer-token findings were read rather than counted | Accept the scanner's classification; reject every base64 value on principle | Each of the nine decodes to printable placeholder wording carrying 'change' or 'demo', which is what a template holds. The 72 are Authorization header examples in documentation, 23 or 39 characters long, which is shorter than any token those examples imply. Reading them is what makes the baseline a review rather than a suppression | A future placeholder that decodes to something real would look identical to the scanner. The working-tree scan and its two planted-secret canaries are what cover the tree as it stands |

### Two pinned infrastructure images carry a dated exception rather than an unpinned upgrade

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The two pinned infrastructure images are scanned in the same stage as the six this project builds, and gated the same way | Scan only the images this project builds; scan the two and report without gating; scan them in a separate stage | A green image stage said nothing about the broker or the database, which is what this platform deploys beside its own images. One gate over both kinds means a fixable finding fails the run wherever it sits | The stage now depends on two public registries rather than one local daemon. Both references are already pulled by the integration stages of the same workflow |
| Each image reference is read out of docker-compose.yml and deploy/k8s rather than written into the workflow | Write both digests into the workflow; write them into the exception file; scan the moving tag | A digest written again in the workflow is a third copy, and the copy that goes stale is the one nothing deploys. The step also requires the two deployment paths to name one digest, because a scan of one proves nothing about the other. Scanning the tag would scan whatever the registry serves that morning | A rename of either manifest breaks the step. That is the failure worth having, because the same rename would otherwise leave an unscanned image deployed |
| The 28 fixable findings carry a dated exception rather than a version bump | Raise both image versions; rebuild both images from a patched base; drop the severity threshold; remove the gate | Section 0.5.1 of the plan pins postgres:18.4 and apache/kafka:4.2.1 and states why each newer image is deliberately not used, so raising a version is not a change this engagement may make. An exception is what the delivered gate accepts in its place, and the review named it as the second route | Twenty-eight known-fixable findings stay in a deployed image. The demonstration posture is what they are accepted against, and every entry states that rather than implying a reachability argument it cannot make |
| Every entry names one package or one path, never an image or a whole scan | Ignore by identifier alone; ignore by image; suppress the severity band | An identifier ignored outright excuses the same vulnerability in a package nobody reviewed. Fifteen entries are scoped to usr/local/bin/gosu and thirteen to a package URL, so the same identifier elsewhere in either image still fails the stage | A finding that moves to another package inside the same image reappears and fails the run. That is the intended behaviour and it will look like a regression to whoever meets it first |
| Every entry expires on 2026-11-30, and trivy is what enforces the date | Leave the exceptions open-ended; expire each on its own date; track the expiry in a document | Trivy stops honouring an entry the day after its expiry, so the finding returns and the image stage fails. That is what makes the date a decision rather than a comment. One shared date means one review answers all 28 rather than 28 reviews spread over a year | All 28 exceptions lapse together, and the run that meets it fails on 28 findings at once. The failure names the file and the task in suggested-next-tasks.md describes the three ways to close it |
| A canary step re-scans the broker with no exception file and fails if it finds nothing | Trust the exception file; assert the entry count in a test; scan without exceptions and report only | A clean scan under the exception file says the exceptions hold and says nothing about whether the gate would fail without them. The secret scan already carries a planted-secret canary for the same reason, and this stage lacked one | The canary fails once the upstream image ships the fixed packages, which is the day the exceptions become unnecessary. The failure text says so and names the file to empty |

### Every library the deck runs is digested, and so is the deck’s own module

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **SUPERSEDED by "The deck loads the Mermaid version Rule 4 fixes" below, which returned the pin to the version the Rule names. It read:** Mermaid moves to the 11.16.1 line | Stay on 11.4.0 and rely on the diagrams being static; drop the diagrams; render them to images | The pinned 11.4.0 carried nine advisories. The three diagrams are authored text and htmlLabels was already false, which lowers exploitability and does not remove it. Rule 4 governs the pin, and the review authorised the move within it | A minor line moves the renderer under a deck whose layout was measured against the old one. All sixteen slides were walked in a browser afterwards and all three diagrams drew |
| The single-file bundle replaces the module graph, so one digest covers every byte | Keep the ES module and digest its entry through a modulepreload link; keep it and add an import-map integrity entry; accept the entry-only coverage and document the rest | The module entry is 30 KB of a 2 MB graph and the chunks it imports carry no digest, so digesting the entry would have verified one part in seventy. The bundle is 2.6 MB in one file and a digest covers all of it. Neither bundle evaluates a string, measured with a search for both eval forms across both files | The deck transfers 2.6 MB where the graph fetched less, and it fetches every diagram type where the graph fetched the flowchart chunks alone. The transfer is asynchronous, so the first slide is unaffected |
| The bundle is fetched by a script element the module creates | Write the element into the head; keep the import expression; fetch it and verify the digest in code | An element carries an integrity attribute where an import expression has none, which is the objection the earlier decision recorded. An element the script inserts is asynchronous, so the first slide is still drawn without waiting, exactly as the import behaved. An element written into the head would have made the whole talk wait for 3.5 MB | The digest sits in the module rather than in markup, so a reader looking for it in the head does not find it. The head comment says where it is |
| The policy admits the deck’s own module by the digest of its text | Admit it with unsafe-inline; move the module into a file beside the deck; use a nonce | Unsafe-inline would admit any script injected into the document, which is the thing worth refusing. A file beside the deck breaks Rule 4's single self-contained file. A nonce has to be fresh per response and a static file has no response to vary. Verified in a browser: with one character of the digest changed, Chrome refused the module and reported the refusal as enforced rather than report-only | A digest over a script is a value that goes stale the moment the script is edited, and a stale one blanks the whole deck silently. PresentationAndProseContractTest recomputes it from the shipped text on every build, so the drift fails there instead |
| Style is the one relaxation the policy makes | Digest the deck's own style block and forbid the rest; drop the policy because style cannot be closed; move the styles into a file | reveal.js writes element styles and Mermaid appends a style element beside every drawing, so neither can be digested ahead of time. Closing script, object, base, frame and form while leaving style open still refuses every path a script reaches the document by, which is what the finding was about | An injected style element runs, and a style can read a layout and exfiltrate through a background request. Connect and image sources are closed to everything but the one origin and data URIs, which is what bounds it |
| The diagram library runs at its strict security level, stated rather than inherited | Leave it unset and inherit the default; set it to loose to keep any future HTML label; set antiscript | The level was unset, so the deck depended on a library default a minor release could change. Strict escapes the labels it draws and refuses a click binding. Confirmed live in a browser through the library's own configuration reader, with all three diagrams drawn under it | A future diagram needing an HTML label or a click binding will not get one, and the failure is a diagram that does not draw. The three shipped diagrams need neither |
| The font stylesheet stays the one asset with no digest | Digest it against one fetched copy; copy the fonts into the file; drop the web fonts | The stylesheet is generated per user agent, so its bytes differ between readers and one digest would fail for some and pass for others. Rule 4 fixes the three typefaces and the deck may hold no local dependency | One of five resources is pinned by version and origin alone. Its origins are the only two the policy admits for style and fonts, and a stylesheet cannot execute |
| The deck loads the Mermaid version Rule 4 fixes, 11.4.0, and holds every hardening the 11.16.1 revision added | Stay on 11.16.1 and record the Rule as unmet; drop the diagrams; render them to images and embed them; ask the owner to amend the Rule | Rule 4 enumerates the version of each content-delivery pin, and a review read the earlier move off 11.4.0 as a deviation the Rule does not permit. Rule 4 outranks a scanner heuristic here, so the pin returns to 11.4.0 and the four measures taken alongside the move all stay: the single-file bundle, its SHA-384 digest over all 2,571,838 bytes, the policy that admits the deck's own module by digest, and the diagram library at its strict level with htmlLabels false. The bundle was fetched and digested at the version named, and its tail assigns globalThis.mermaid, so the script element loads it the same way | 11.4.0 carries nine published advisories, which the digest does not address: a digest proves the bytes are the ones reviewed and says nothing about what those bytes contain. What bounds it is a deck one presenter opens from disk, three diagrams authored as text in this repository, no HTML label and no click binding. suggested-next-tasks.md carries the advisory-review task and names this as the exposure it closes |

### A local stack states its transport posture and binds it to one machine

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The composition states one posture in one place, and the environment template and the platform guide state the same sentence | Leave the three per-site comments as the whole statement; state it in the guide alone; state nothing and rely on the deployment path being obvious | Each transport already carried a comment explaining itself, and three explanations are not a posture: a reader who meets one of them learns why that value is set and not what the stack as a whole is for. The three files are the three places an operator arrives at, so the same sentence is in all three | One sentence in three files drifts. A contract test reads the phrase and the loopback figure out of all three, so a change to one of them fails the build |
| The posture is enforced by the binding and read back by a test, not asserted by the comment beside it | Trust the comments; check the bindings in a review; refuse to start when a binding is wrong | A published mapping written without an address listens on every interface of the host, which puts an HTTP Basic credential and a broker password on whatever network that host is attached to. Fourteen mappings ship and every one names the loopback address today. A test that reads all fourteen is what keeps the fifteenth honest, and a container cannot check its own published mapping from the inside | The test reads text rather than a running stack, so a mapping overridden on a command line is outside it. The floor of fourteen means a scan that silently matched nothing fails rather than passing |
| An authenticated Transport Layer Security profile is designed and not delivered | Deliver it now; deliver it and make it the default; deliver a self-signed variant with verification switched off | Every value such a profile needs is already a pass-through variable, and none can be filled without a local authority, a certificate per container and a trust store each client mounts. This repository publishes no private key on purpose. Section 0.2.2 of the plan excludes production hardening and 0.8.5 asks for minimal, swappable infrastructure, and a demonstration that opens by minting seven certificates is neither. A variant with verification switched off would encrypt and prove nothing, which is the position the database is already in | The local stack stays unencrypted on three transports until someone does that work. suggested-next-tasks.md carries the procedure and the check, and the posture states the case it does not cover rather than implying there is none |
| The database keeps sslmode=require rather than verify-full | Generate an authority for the compose database; disable encryption entirely; ship a certificate in the repository | The server signs its own certificate on first start, so there is nothing to verify it against and verify-full would refuse the connection outright. Require still encrypts, which is worth more than nothing on a bridge. The cluster path asks for verify-full because a cluster has an authority and a mounted root | An impersonated database on the same bridge would be accepted. The bridge has one operator on it and the fixtures describe nobody, which is the whole of what makes that acceptable |

### The delivered set is every path this engagement creates or updates

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The inventory command names one file at the repository root as well as the two directories | Keep the two directory arguments and excuse the root paths in prose; count the whole tree minus `app/`, `diagrams/` and `samples/`; count tracked paths only | Scoping the command to `card-platform` and `.github` left the root `README.md` outside a closure that claimed the whole delivered tree, and a review measured the gap. A `.gitattributes` was named here too and has since been withdrawn, so the command names one root path. Naming it is the smallest change that makes the claim true, and it keeps the command something a reader can run and get the published figure from. Subtracting the legacy directories would have swept in four files this engagement never touched | The command grows a path each time work reaches a new file outside those two directories. `DocumentationContractTest` compares the command's output against the rows both ways, so a path the command misses would be invisible rather than reported. A second test in the same class closes that gap: every top-level entry git resolves is either covered by the command or named in the test as outside it, with the reason it is |
| The four untouched root files carry no row | Row every file at the repository root; row them and mark them unchanged; drop the root from the set again | `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `LICENSE` and `NOTICE` are byte-identical to the baseline commit, so a row for any of them would claim an operation that was never performed. Rule 1 closes over what this engagement delivers, and an unchanged file is not a delivery | A reader counting files at the root finds six and the inventory names two, so the difference has to be read out of the paragraph that states it rather than inferred from the table |
| The root readme is classified `Rule 3 update` rather than as a document this engagement created | Classify it as a Rule 3 document alongside the eight it joins; add a fourth column naming the operation; leave the operation unstated | It is the one pre-existing file this work modifies, and a review asked for that operation to be visible. A label carries it without a new column, which two tests parse as three cells. Sharing the Rule 3 document label would have hidden the distinction that matters, since every other file under that label was authored here | The vocabulary grows from seven labels to eight, and one of them describes exactly one path. The alternative was a label that reads as a creation for the only file that is not one |
| A withdrawn path is recorded below the closure rather than inside it | Keep a row for it in the backward tables; leave the deletion unrecorded; keep the record in a separate document | The row set is the delivered tree, and a row for a path the tree no longer carries would inflate the count and fail the closure that compares the two. Recording the eight withdrawals in their own section keeps a deletion traceable and keeps the arithmetic above it honest | The two directions no longer read from one table, so a reader following a withdrawn file has to reach a second section. That section names what replaced each path, which a row in the closed set could not have done |
| The module-level tally is stated as the rows those two tables carry | Leave the published 52 and treat the difference as intent; drop the figure; bind it to a test | The two tables carry 53 data rows, so the published 52 was one short of what a reader counts. A figure a reader can check has to be the figure they get. It is not test-bound because those tables resolve modules and uniform groups rather than paths, so no set difference defines it | The number is maintained by hand, so a row added to either table has to be counted again. The closure beneath it is test-bound, so the figure that carries Rule 1's claim cannot drift the same way |

## Completeness review

A whole-project completeness review found three items. Two were scope and hygiene, recorded under
[The delivered set is every path this engagement creates or updates](#the-delivered-set-is-every-path-this-engagement-creates-or-updates)
and under the repository-boundary section below. The third was the one behavioural finding, and it
asked a question the two earlier revisions of the same code had each answered by giving something up.

### One event for the outcome whose card resolves nothing, keyed on the identifier this service minted

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **In force. Supersedes "A card that resolves no cross-reference row is refused for every caller, before an identifier is drawn and before any row is written".** A card that resolves no cross-reference row is decided: one `authorization_decision` row with `account_id` null, one `outbox_event` row under `transaction-declined-v2`, both in one local transaction, keyed on the sixteen-character identifier `transaction_id_seq` issued for that decision | Keep the refusal and accept that one call produces no event; restore the caller-declared account, which the security review withdrew; put an eleven-zero sentinel in `accountId`; declare a version 4 carrying the nine descriptive values without an account; publish the decline on a topic of its own | The refusal satisfied the security review by giving up the event, and AAP transformation rule T4 gives one authorization call exactly one event. Both obligations hold at once as soon as the subject is a value this service issues rather than one it is handed: the identifier is authoritative, deterministic under retry, and names no cardholder, account or card. `transaction-declined-v2` already declares that shape — no `accountId` property, `aggregateId` retyped to the sixteen printable characters an identifier occupies — and it was already released and governed, so promoting its posture from RETAINED to PUBLISHED declares a producer for a document rather than forking a new one. A sentinel account puts a false value on the wire and on a partition a real cardholder owns, which the log already rejected. A version 4 would declare a second knowingly-broken-consumer fork, which this platform's own policy makes an owner's decision. A separate topic splits one outcome across two subscriptions for no gain | Reject code `0100` is a decline body again on the synchronous route, carrying `accountId` as JSON null, so a client that assumed the field was always a string reads null for one outcome. `openapi.yaml` declares that as a fourth `oneOf` branch pinning the field to the null type. A consumer holding version 1 or 3 requires `accountId` and dead-letters a version-2 record rather than reading it; the ledger reads every version and takes its no-detail path for this one. The declared account never becomes the subject, the aggregate or the key, which `Outcome`, `AuthorizationResponse`, `TransactionDeclined` and `OutboxWriter` each enforce and named tests hold in both directions |
| The identifier is allocated before the branch, so the decline and the three that follow a resolved account draw from one sequence | Allocate inside each branch; allocate lazily only where a row is written; keep a separate sequence for the account-less decline | One allocation site is one place where identifier semantics live, and every decision this service takes now carries an identifier from it. A probe sending unknown card numbers consumes sequence values, which is the cost of the outcome being a decision rather than a refusal, and the source agrees that a reject is expected traffic: `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L229-L230` ends a run that rejected records with return code 4 rather than an error | A caller repeating unknown cards advances the sequence and writes a decision row per call. `authorization_decision` is bounded by the same decision horizon every other outcome is, and the rising count is visible on `carddemo.authorization.decisions` under reject code `0100` |
| The entitlement comparison stays ahead of the allocation and the branch | Allocate first, since the branch needs an identifier; check entitlement only where an account resolved | This is the security review's ordering and the reason for it is unchanged: a branch that runs before the entitlement check answers a caller with no entitlement whether a card exists, which is the card-existence oracle that review closed. `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest` asserts the order of the three steps by name | An unentitled caller receives the same 403 whatever card it sends, so it learns nothing, and an entitled one reaches an outcome that names no account anyway |
| `V24__unresolved_card_decline_is_decided.sql` changes no schema and only restates catalogue comments | Add a column recording that the decision resolved no account; re-create `unresolved_card_attempt`; ship no migration and let the comments of `V20` stand | Every column the outcome needs already exists and every constraint already admits it: `authorization_decision.account_id` is nullable, its approved-account check only binds an approval, and `ck_outbox_event_aggregate_id` admits both key widths. So there is nothing to alter and something to correct — the comments `V20` wrote describe a refusal, and an operator reads the catalogue. A recreated attempt table would duplicate `authorization_decision` again, which is what the security review dropped it for | A reader of `V20` alone meets a comment four migrations out of date. `V24` names `V20` in its header, the service README lists both, and the folder now holds six files arguing successive positions on this one question |
| Reject code `0100` paired with an account is refused by the two `TransactionDeclined` factories that take one, and not by the canonical constructor | Refuse it in the canonical constructor as well; refuse it only in the authorization service; leave the library permissive and rely on the service | A producer reaching for the familiar factory is how the withdrawn pairing would come back, and a refusal in the shared library closes it for all six services rather than one. The canonical constructor stays permissive on purpose: deserialization runs through it, and records published under the earlier revision carry reject code `0100` with an account. Refusing them there would make history unreadable and send those records to a dead-letter topic | The rule lives in two layers and a reader has to know which layer refuses what. Each factory's `@throws` says so, and `EventSerdeSecurityTest` asserts both factories refuse while the read path still accepts a historical record |

### The whitespace policy is carried by the check invocation, not by a file at the repository root

The same review read the AAP's scope boundary against the tree. The plan admits writes to
`card-platform/`, the root `README.md` and `.github/`, and one delivered path sat outside all three.
Removing it had to leave two things standing: the legacy guide's bytes, and the reason the file was
written in the first place.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **In force. Supersedes "Declare the root guide's carriage-return line endings in a root `.gitattributes`" under [Documentation, configuration, and presentation](#documentation-configuration-and-presentation).** The root `.gitattributes` is withdrawn, and the whitespace check is documented as `git -c core.whitespace=cr-at-eol diff --check` | Keep the file and argue it is hygiene rather than scope; move it to `card-platform/.gitattributes`, where git would not apply it to a path above; convert `README.md` to line-feed endings; leave the check reporting the guide and read past the noise | The AAP admits three write paths and this file was in none of them, which a mechanical check on changed paths reports rather than debates. The setting the file declared per path is available per invocation, where it covers every file at once and needs no path list to stay current. A file under `card-platform/` would declare nothing about a path above it, so it would be inert and still misleading. Converting the guide is not available: `RuleThreeDocumentationContractTest` holds its legacy bytes against a hash, and those bytes are carriage-return terminated. Measured on this tree, the default invocation reports 85 added guide lines as defective and exits 2, so reading past the noise means reading past a non-zero exit | The setting now has to travel with the command wherever the command is written, which is a documented string rather than a tracked file. `docs/onboarding.md` and this log both carry the whole invocation, and `everyCarriageReturnTerminatedFileIsCheckedUnderADocumentedInvocation` fails the build if either drops it. That test also refuses an attributes file anywhere in the delivered set, so the withdrawal cannot quietly reverse |
| The one trailing space the check reported is removed rather than declared | Add a whitespace exemption for the file; leave it, since one space renders identically | The space sat at the end of a prose line in `docs/traceability-matrix.md` and carried no meaning, so the check was right and the file was wrong. With it gone the documented invocation exits 0 across the whole review range, which makes the check a gate rather than a list of known noise | A future prose edit can reintroduce one, and only this invocation catches it. No pipeline stage runs it, so it is a review-time check rather than an enforced gate. `everyCarriageReturnTerminatedFileIsCheckedUnderADocumentedInvocation` keeps the command documented where a reviewer looks for it |

## Final acceptance gate remediation decisions

A final cross-model acceptance review read the delivered platform against the plan and the five rules.
It withheld approval over one stale explanation and four smaller items. Each entry below records what
was found, the routes weighed against the one taken, and what the choice leaves open.

### Reject reason 0100 is explained by the version the code publishes it under

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every file that explains the reason-`0100` outcome is rewritten to the behaviour `V24__unresolved_card_decline_is_decided.sql` settled, and `UnresolvedCardDeclineProseContractTest` holds them there | Correct the two files the review named and stop; delete the explanations and point every reader at the migration; revert the code to the refusal the prose described | The outcome was reversed twice, and each reversal left prose describing the position before it. The review named two files. A search for the claim found six more: `card-platform/README.md` in three places, the authorization `openapi.yaml`, the repository-root `README.md`, and the javadoc of `EntitySchemaMappingContractTest`. A maintainer reading any one of them would have concluded that a live event stream did not exist, which is the one documentation error that costs an incident. Reverting the code was not available: AAP transformation rule T4 gives one authorization call one event | The test reads prose, so a rewording that keeps the meaning and drops the version number fails it. That is the intended cost: the version is the fact a reversal moves, so naming it is what makes the prose falsifiable |
| The two outcomes the stale text had merged are stated as two | Keep one paragraph covering both; describe only the decided one, since the refusal is rare | A card that resolves no cross-reference row is decided and publishes. A request naming an account that holds no cross-reference row is refused before a decision by `AccountNotFoundInCrossReferenceException`, allocates no identifier and writes nothing. The withdrawn javadoc described the second and attributed it to the first, and cited `app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L625-L626` — the card limb — for a refusal the account limb at `:L591-L592` raises | Two paragraphs where there was one. The alternative is a reader who meets a null account identifier and cannot tell which outcome produced it |
| The detector flattens comment markers and line breaks before it searches | Search the raw bytes, as the review's own search did | The claim that survived longest was invisible to a raw search. `V19` wraps `records no decision and publishes no event` across two comment lines, and a javadoc wraps a sentence across three. A detector that reads raw bytes finds a claim only where its author happened to fit it on one line | Flattening can join two unrelated lines into a phrase that matches. Each literal is a clause rather than a word, which makes that unlikely and would be a false failure rather than a false pass |
| The append-only history keeps its withdrawn claims, and the exemption that lets it is itself asserted | Rewrite the two superseded migrations; drop the superseded log rows; exempt every migration | An applied Flyway migration cannot be edited, and a superseded log row exists to record what it replaced. `V24` names both `V19` and `V20`, so the chain reads forwards from either. Exempting every migration would have hidden the next stale header, so three paths are named and `theAppendOnlyHistoryStillRecordsWhatWasWithdrawn` fails if any of them stops carrying a withdrawn claim | A reader who opens `V19` alone meets the withdrawn position. Its own header names the two readings it replaced, and `V24` names it, so the path forwards is one file away |

### The masked card number of a posted transaction is held by the catalogue as well as the model

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `V12__transaction_card_number_is_masked.sql` adds `ck_transaction_card_number`, holding `transaction.card_number` to twelve mask characters and the last four digits | Leave `entity/TransactionEntity` as the only enforcement; add a database trigger; drop the column and read the mask from the event each time | This was the one masked column of the platform with no catalogue check behind it. `ck_rejected_transaction_masked_card_number` covers the reject block of the same schema, `ck_notification_log_card_number` the notification read model, and `ck_authorization_decision_masked_card` the decision row. A model check binds one writer; a catalogue check binds every writer, including a repair statement typed at a console during an incident. No row of this table is written outside the entity today, so the constraint validates against every existing row without a data fix | A check constraint is one more object a schema comparison reports. It costs a regular-expression match per insert on a table that takes one insert per posted transaction |
| The pattern admits four digits and not the sixteen-mask form its wider sibling admits | Reuse the wider pattern of `ck_rejected_transaction_masked_card_number`, which also admits sixteen mask characters, as the review suggested | `cobol.PanMasker` answers a card it cannot read with sixteen mask characters, and that value reaches a refusal record and a decision row. A posted transaction exists only for a card that was read into a cross-reference row, so every row here carries four digits. The wider form would have admitted a shape the table cannot hold, and `schemas/transaction-authorized-v2.json` and `TransactionEntity.MASKED_CARD_NUMBER_PATTERN` both declare the narrow one | A future path that posts a transaction for an unreadable card would be refused by the database. That is the intended answer, and it arrives at the write rather than in a report |
| The column keeps the name `card_number` | Rename it to `masked_card_number` in the same migration, which the review offered as an option | The name is what `card-platform/docs/traceability-matrix.md` maps `TRAN-CARD-NUM PIC X(16)` at `app/cpy/CVTRA05Y.cpy:L15` to, and the same field name appears in the reject block's byte offsets. A rename would touch the entity, the matrix row, the offsets and several documents, and would change no behaviour. The constraint and the refreshed column comment say what the name does not | A reader of the column name alone could expect a full card number. The catalogue comment is one query away and now names the constraint that makes the masking certain |

### Two domain services take a clock instead of reading the machine one

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `domain/PostingService` and `domain/RiskScoringService` take a `Clock` through a package-private constructor, and their public constructors supply `Clock.systemUTC()` | Leave both reading the static factory; declare a `Clock` bean in each module and take it as a fourth argument on the one public constructor; pass the instant in as a method argument from each consumer | These were the last two domain services reading a clock statically, and each read produced a value the service stores and publishes: the processing timestamp of a posted transaction and the moment a fraud assessment reports. Neither could be named by a test, so the one field of a posted row nothing asserted was the one the source stamps at `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L437`. The two-constructor shape is what `notification/domain/NotificationService` already uses, so no new bean and no new configuration is introduced. Passing the instant from the consumer would move the decision to four call sites and let two of them disagree | Two constructors make the injection point ambiguous, so the public one carries `@Autowired`. `RiskScoringServiceTest` asserted a single constructor and no annotated injection, and both assertions described the shape rather than a requirement, so both are restated |
| `PostingService` converts at `ZoneOffset.UTC` rather than calling `LocalDateTime.now(clock)` | Call `LocalDateTime.now(clock)`, which is shorter and honours the clock's zone; set the container's zone in every deployment file | `CobolDecimal.formatProcessingTimestamp` takes a `LocalDateTime`, and the previous call read the machine's default zone. Two containers in two zones stamped one instant two ways, and nothing in the repository set a zone. `domain/RetentionSweep` in the notification service already converts at `ZoneOffset.UTC` for the same reason, so this is the platform's existing answer rather than a new one. A test now passes a clock in another zone and asserts the same stamp | A deployment that wants local-time stamps cannot get them by setting a zone. That is intended: the column is compared against `app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L414-L420` as text, and a stamp whose meaning moves with a zone cannot be compared at all |
| `Clock` leaves the list of types `RiskScoringService` must not hold | Keep the list as it was and inject the clock as a method argument; drop the list check | The list names the four observability types the consumer owns, and it named `Clock` only because the class had none. A test that forbids the fix the same review asked for is a test describing yesterday's shape | The list is one item shorter, so a clock added for another purpose would pass it. The two clock tests name the value the clock produces, which is what a wrong clock would change |

### The Tomcat pin names the advisory it does not answer

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **In force. Supersedes "`tomcat.version` is described as precautionary rather than as a security floor" under [Supply-chain floors](#supply-chain-floors).** The comment on `tomcat.version` names CVE-2026-66299, states that 11.0.24 does not fix it, and states why this platform is outside its affected surface | Raise the pin to 11.0.25; leave the comment as it was; drop the pin and take the bill of materials version; call it a security floor | The comment said no advisory affecting the 11.0.x line had been identified, and CVE-2026-66299 was published on 28 July 2026 covering 11.0.0-M20 through 11.0.24. Raising to 11.0.25 is unavailable: Maven Central publishes nothing above 11.0.24 on that line, which `curl` against the artifact metadata confirms. The advisory's subject is the WebSocket chat sample of the examples web application, which an embedded Tomcat does not ship and which no service here declares, so the affected surface is absent rather than mitigated. Saying so is what a reader needs; silence would read as an absent advisory | An advisory covering the pinned version stands unfixed for as long as 11.0.25 is unpublished. Nothing in this platform is within its surface, and the watch item in `suggested-next-tasks.md` names the version to move to |
| The nine module names read `CardDemo Card Platform :: <Module>` | Leave the five conventions as they were; use the artifact identifier for every module; drop `<name>` and let Maven print the identifier | Five conventions were in use across nine modules, and one reactor listing carried all five: two bare artifact identifiers, two `CardDemo <Service>` forms, one `CardDemo Platform ::` and one `card-platform ::`. A reactor summary is the first thing a reader of a failing build sees, and inconsistent naming there costs nothing to fix and reads as inattention. The chosen form is the root `<name>` followed by the module, so the parent and the children agree | A reactor line is longer than the artifact identifier alone. The notification service's OpenAPI document title still reads `CardDemo Notification Service`, which is a document title rather than a reactor name and stays as it is |
| The two comment blocks in `card-platform/pom.xml` and the image block in `deploy/k8s/kustomization.yaml` keep their mechanics and lose their weighing | Leave all three as they were; delete them and rely on this log alone; move them and leave no pointer | Rule 1 puts mechanics, provenance and locators in a comment and the decision in this log. The three blocks argued their choices in place: one explained why a fixed date beats a commit-derived one, one weighed a compatibility raise against a security floor, and one opened with a capitalised banner arguing why six images carry no digest. Each now states what the value does and where the argument lives, and the rows in this section carry the argument. `RationaleLocationContractTest` reads no comment format these three use, so the placement was correct only by the detector's silence | A reader of the build file follows one link to reach the reasoning. That is the cost Rule 1 accepts, and the pointer label is one line |

### An unreferenced closeable resource is named `_` rather than suppressed

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every try-with-resources whose resource is opened for its side effect names it `_`, the unnamed variable | Add `@SuppressWarnings("try")` at each site, which the review suggested; enable `-Xlint:try` in the build and accept the warnings; reference the resource in the body to satisfy the compiler | A probe settles it: `@SuppressWarnings("try")` on the resource declaration does **not** suppress the warning, and `-Xlint:all` still reports it. The unnamed variable produces no warning at all, because that is what it was added for. It also says what a suppression only implies, which is that the resource is opened for its effect and never read. Referencing the resource to satisfy the compiler would add a statement that does nothing | The unnamed variable needs Java 22 or later; this platform pins 25 and the enforcer refuses anything below it. A reader unfamiliar with the form has to look it up once |
| All 26 sites are converted, not the four the review named | Convert the four named sites; convert only production sources and leave the ten in tests | The same condition held at 26 sites across all six services and both libraries, so converting four would have left the identical finding at 22 places and made the four look arbitrary. The javadoc example in `CorrelationScope` is converted with them, so the documented idiom is the one the code uses | Twenty-six mechanical edits, each verified by the compiler. Compiling all 325 production sources with `-Xlint:try` now reports zero, and re-inserting one named unreferenced resource makes it report one, which is how the check was shown to be measuring something |

### The browser-simple refusal sits wherever a state change is reachable

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The authorization and card services take the `isBrowserSimpleStateChange` refusal the account service already carried, and the three read-only services do not | Add it to all five services the review named; leave it in the account service alone and record why; delete it everywhere and rely on `config/CrossSiteRequestFilter` | Three of the six chains name only `GET` routes. Their `.anyRequest().denyAll()` already refuses every state-changing method, so the rule could never fire in any of the three, and a rule that cannot fire is one a maintainer has to rule out before changing anything. The two that name a state change — `POST /authorizations` and `PUT /cards/{cardToken}` — could reach a handler with a form encoding, and now cannot. The rule is therefore uniform on a statement a reader can check: the refusal is present exactly where a state change is reachable | Three of six chains still differ from the other three. What makes that a decision rather than an oversight is that a test now holds it: `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest.BrowserSimpleRefusal` fails if a chain naming a state-changing method lacks the rule, if the rule sits after a route rule, or if a read-only chain carries it |
| The refusal is placed ahead of every route rule in both chains | Place it beside each state-changing route; place it after the route rules | A refusal after a route rule is a refusal a route rule can pre-empt, and a refusal beside one route leaves the next route added uncovered. The test asserts the index of the rule against the index of the first state-changing method, so the order is held rather than reviewed | A reader meets a deny rule before the rules that grant.
