The comment above it says why, in three lines |
| The eight assertions the account service already had are duplicated into both services rather than shared | Extract them into a shared test fixture in one module; assert the behaviour once and trust the copies | Each `config/SecurityConfig` is per-service by the shape AAP section 0.3.1 fixes, and no service module depends on another, so a shared fixture would have to live in a library every service test depends on for one check. The platform already duplicates the six security configurations for the same reason | Three copies of eight assertions. `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest` reads all six sources, so a copy that drifts from the declared content-type set or the method set fails there |

### The first posted contract stays PUBLISHED and says why its branch still stands

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `TransactionPosted` version 1 keeps posture PUBLISHED, and its note now states that the branch reaching it is reachable only from a retained `TransactionAuthorized` version 1 record | Retire it to RETAINED, which the review offered first; delete the conditional branch and drop version 1 records; leave the note as it was | RETAINED means this platform no longer writes the version. `TransactionPosted.forAuthorized` still writes it, for an authorized record carrying no card token, so RETAINED would be false while the branch stands. What the note lacked was the other half: nothing produced today reaches the branch, because the authorization service publishes `TransactionAuthorized` version 2 alone and version 2 requires `cardToken`. Both halves are now stated, so a reader can tell a live path from a replay path | A posture of PUBLISHED on a version no current traffic produces reads as busier than it is. The note is the answer, and the alternative is a false posture |

### What the Rule 4 word ceiling is measured on

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The Rule 4 word ceiling for a slide is measured on the slide's body prose, and excludes the eyebrow index, the heading, the Rule 2 legend and tabular or list content | Measure every rendered token and cut the three figure slides to fit; drop the legends from the three figure slides; drop the eyebrow index from every slide | Counting every rendered token puts slides 3, 5 and 14 at 41, 46 and 37, and slides 10, 12 and 15 at 40, 40 and 39. The body paragraph of each figure slide is 12, 18 and 12 words. The tokens that push the three figure slides over are the ones Rule 2 requires: every diagram must carry a title and a legend, and a legend is not prose a reader wades through. Cutting the legend to satisfy the ceiling would breach Rule 2 to satisfy Rule 4, and cutting the eyebrow index would break the numbering convention Rule 4 itself sets | A reader counting words on a rendered slide reaches a higher number than this row states. The row states which tokens are counted, and the three figure slides carry a diagram rather than more prose, which is what the ceiling exists to achieve |
| The Mermaid connector colour stays `#999999`, at 2.85 to 1 against white | Darken it to the `#5B39F3` the node borders use; darken it to a grey that clears 3 to 1; drop the theme variable and take Mermaid's default | Rule 4 enumerates the five Mermaid theme variables by value and `lineColor: "#999999"` is one of them, so the value is the specification rather than a choice this work made. Measured, it is 2.85 to 1 on white, 2.53 to 1 on the primary node fill and 2.51 to 1 on the secondary, all below the 3 to 1 floor WCAG 1.4.11 sets for a graphical object. What limits the cost is that `primaryBorderColor` `#5B39F3` measures 6.32 to 1 on white, so every node boundary is discernible and only the connector between two discernible nodes is faint | A reader with low contrast sensitivity may not follow a connector at a glance, on three slides that also carry the same relationships in prose. This is a rule-driven residual and the only one the deck carries; a project owner who wants it closed has to change Rule 4's enumerated value first |
| The deck's fixed 1920 by 1080 canvas is left as it is, with no responsive rules added | Add media queries for narrow viewports; make the canvas fluid; add a mobile stylesheet | The presentation framework scales its fixed canvas to whatever viewport it is opened in, which is what the framework's own configuration is for, and Rule 4 fixes the dimension. A media query would fight the scaling rather than add to it | A viewport far from sixteen by nine letterboxes the deck. That is what a slide deck does, and the artifact is a deck for a human audience rather than an application interface |
| Fourteen expected-output files is the count, and the fourteenth is `posting-summary.csv` | Publish thirteen and treat the extra as accidental; delete it; fold its rows into another asset | A checkpoint scope statement counted thirteen. The directory holds fourteen and `DocumentationContractTest` asserts fourteen, so the code and the test were right and the statement undercounted. The fourteenth is `posting-summary.csv`, whose 20 rows carry the two evaluation models' run totals and four `sha256` digests that `PostingEquivalenceTest` and `AuthorizationDecisionEquivalenceTest` both read. Deleting it would delete the only asset that states a whole-run total rather than a per-record one | Nothing. The correction is to a statement outside this repository, and `equivalence-results.md` already publishes the inventory the test measures |

## Final performance-profiling remediation decisions

A final performance profiling read every route, every relay, every consumer group and the deck, and
raised six findings. None is a wrong answer and none is a security fault. Every guarantee held under
the load the review put on it. Each finding is work the design does more of than it needs to, or a
bound the design never set. The rows below are grouped by the part of the platform each touches.

### What a response costs to deliver, and what a borrowed connection may do

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every service compresses a JSON or problem+JSON body of at least two kilobytes, and nothing else | Leave every body whole, as all six did; compress every media type the framework lists by default; compress with no size floor; put a compressing proxy in front of the six | The review measured a 69,959-byte history page and a 37,299-byte assessment page leaving the connector byte for byte with no `Content-Encoding` header, and not one of the six files declared a compression block. The two media types are the only ones this surface answers with, so naming the framework's eight would name six it never sends. The floor is the container's own and it reads a declared content length, so on a surface that answers chunked it filters nothing today and holds for a deployment that declares one. A proxy would add a container the demo has to explain in order to compress the same bodies | Every compressible answer costs one compression pass on the thread serving it, short ones included: the floor reads a declared content length and every route here answers chunked. Measured, the history page leaves as 1,993 bytes of 69,959 and a 182-byte card answer as 147. Transport security is off in the shipped file and on in the cluster, so a deployment that put a secret in a response body would be compressing it under transport security; no response of this platform carries one |
| Every pool sets `statement_timeout`, `lock_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` on each connection it opens | Set them on the server for every database; carry them in the JDBC URL; set a persistence query timeout per repository method; leave them unset, since no hang was ever observed | The review found all three at `0` on the server and no ceiling in the application or the driver either, so a connection already acquired could run or wait for a lock without limit while every other caller queued on the three-second connection timeout. These are session settings, so one initialisation statement applies them to every connection the pool opens, Flyway's included. The URL was rejected because `docker-compose.yml` and `deploy/k8s/30-configmap.yaml` replace `SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL` whole and would drop anything the file had put in it. A per-method query timeout would bound statements one annotation at a time and would not bound a lock wait at all. The values sit far above measured behaviour rather than tight against it: statements answer in single-digit milliseconds, and a forty-way concurrent update of one account cleared its lock waiters in about two seconds | A statement that legitimately needed more than thirty seconds would now fail rather than finish. The longest this platform issues is a bounded retention batch. A lock wait past ten seconds now surfaces as a failed request instead of a slow one |
| A connection is retired for age or for idleness, and one held past two minutes is reported | Leave all three at their library defaults; report a held connection at a threshold near the statement ceiling; leave leak reporting off | The review named `max-lifetime`, `idle-timeout` and `leak-detection-threshold` unset, and that is a finding whether or not the library defaults happen to be reasonable: a bound nobody states is a bound nobody can audit. Declaring them makes the pool's lifetimes as visible as its timeouts already were. The reporting threshold sits above the idle-transaction ceiling, so a connection is named only once neither a running statement nor an open transaction could still explain the hold. The longest legitimate hold on this platform is Flyway's, which owns one connection for the length of the migration run at start-up | Leak reporting writes a warning carrying a stack trace, so an environment slow enough to hold a connection for two minutes gains log noise it did not have before. It refuses nothing |

### What a page of history costs, and what keeps a stored total honest

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The whole-card count and totals a history page carries come from one maintained row of `statement_card_total`, not from an aggregate over the card's retained rows | Leave the aggregate and index `(card_token, amount)` to cover it; page the figures with the page, so each answer describes 25 rows; drop the figures from the response; compute them in the service by reading every row | The review measured the aggregate on a card holding 21,299 rows: a sequential scan of 1,228 buffers and 7.775 ms on every request, and the page size moved neither figure. A covering index would still scan every row of the card and would grow with the retention window. Paging the figures was refused because `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL:L429` totals every row of one card between two key breaks, so a total over 25 rows would describe a statement the source never produced. Dropping them would drop the overflow proof of `WS-TOTAL-AMT` with them. A service-side loop moves the same scan into the heap | One more table, and one more object a reader of the schema has to understand. The figures are now a stored value rather than a derivation, so a defect in a writer could answer a wrong figure where an aggregate could only ever answer a slow one. Two `CHECK` constraints and a repository test are what that risk bought: `transaction_count >= 0` and `absolute_total >= 0` fail a write that drifted, and `StatementTransactionRepositoryTest` asserts the row equals an aggregate over the rows it summarises |
| The row is maintained by delta inside the two statements that already write the read model, and no other writer may touch the read model | Recount the card after each write; maintain the row in a second statement of the same transaction; maintain it in a trigger; rebuild it on a schedule | A recount would move the cost this removes from the read path to the write path, and the write path runs per posted event. One statement is what makes the summary commit with the rows it describes, so the marker `TransactionPostedConsumer` claims cannot commit beside rows the summary disagrees with. A second statement would leave a window where a crash committed one and not the other. A trigger would put the rule somewhere no reader of the repository looks, which Rule 1 refuses. A scheduled rebuild would answer stale figures between runs. The delta is taken from the row as it stood before the statement, so a redelivery that rewrites one transaction adds no count and only the difference between the two amounts | Both statements are longer and each carries a common table expression a reader has to follow. Any future writer that moved a row without moving the summary would answer a wrong figure, which is why `StatementReadModelWriterContractTest` fails the build if a production call reaches an inherited `save` or `delete` on that repository, and why the retention delete decrements the summary of every card it removed a row from before it removes them |

### What one pass may drain, and what a pass that runs out of time leaves behind

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One pass repeats its claim-and-publish cycle until nothing more is due, a cycle publishes nothing, or the pass deadline arrives | Leave one claim per pass and shorten `fixed-delay-ms`; raise `batch-size`; claim every due row of an account at once; run several relay instances | The claim answers the due head row of each account, so a pass that claimed once published one event per account and the scheduled delay then set the rate. A review measured it: about two events a second on one account against 171 a second across 49, with `batch-size` at 100 the whole time. Shortening the delay buys a linear multiple of two and spends a claim query on every step of it. Raising `batch-size` buys nothing at all, because the bound that applied was one row per account rather than the batch. Claiming every due row of an account would reorder that account on any retry, which is the reordering the head-row claim exists to prevent, and more instances multiply the same per-account limit rather than removing it. Repeating the cycle takes the head each account has once the previous head is published, so ordering is the guarantee it always was | A pass can now issue many claim queries instead of one, and a busy pass holds the relay's scheduler thread for up to `max-duration-ms`. `batch-size` still bounds the pass rather than the cycle, so the work of one pass is bounded in rows as well as in time. A pass claims any one row at most once, so a broker refusing everything costs one batch of attempts rather than a deadline's worth |
| A pass releases every row it claimed and did not attempt, rather than leaving the claim to expire | Leave the claim and let `claim-timeout` recover it, as the code did; shorten `claim-timeout`; record a failure on the unattempted rows; claim no more rows than one pass can attempt | The claim query passes over a claimed row, so a row a pass claimed and never reached was invisible to every later pass until the lease expired. The review measured what that costs after a broker outage: a plateau of about 48 seconds, and 132 rows carrying `last_error = 'ClaimExpired'` with no backoff behind it, because the stranded row was its account's head and the whole account waited on it. Shortening the lease weakens the protection the lease exists for, which is a dead instance's claim. Recording a failure would spend one of the row's ten attempts on a send nobody issued, and ten such passes would abandon a row no broker ever refused. Claiming less would give back the throughput the row above just recovered | The release is a second write on the rows a pass could not reach, in one transaction, and it happens only on a pass that ran out of time. A released row is due immediately, so the next pass may claim it and run out of time again; each such pass leaves the row exactly as it found it, and `claim-timeout` still covers the case it was written for, which is an instance that stops holding a claim |

### What the deck fetches before it draws

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Both library scripts are deferred, and the deck's own module keeps its place behind them | Leave both parser-blocking at the end of the body, as they were; mark them `async`; inline the smaller one; drop the icon library and draw its seventeen icons as markup | A profile of the shipped file named both scripts render-blocking, and the icon library is the heaviest asset the deck fetches: 83 kilobytes over the wire and 356 decoded. Every slide is already in the markup, so neither library has to run before the parser reaches the end of the document. `async` was refused because it abandons document order, and the module below reads `window.Reveal` and `window.lucide`. A deferred classic script still runs in order, and still runs before a module, so the ready and slide-change hooks and `Reveal.initialize` keep the sequence they had. Inlining would put a pinned digest out of reach. Drawing the icons by hand would trade one request for seventeen hand-built drawings | Both libraries now run once the parser has finished rather than during the parse, so the deck answers a key a moment later than its markup is complete. The static path already covers the case where neither arrives: an absent `window.Reveal` adds `deck-static` and draws every slide in document order |
| Both stylesheets stay on the critical path, and only the two scripts leave it | Take the typeface stylesheet off the path at print priority and adopt it on load; preload it as a stylesheet and promote it on load; preload the three face files themselves; self-host the faces; drop the web faces for the system stack; leave all four requests blocking, as they were | The profile named four render-blocking requests, and the two scripts are 112 kilobytes of the 126. Both ways of moving the typeface stylesheet aside were built and measured, and both were refused for the same result: a face is only fetched once its declaration is registered, so a stylesheet off the critical path registers the faces after the first paint and the title heading is redrawn a size larger under the reader. Print priority shifted it in one run of fourteen, and a preload promoted on load shifted it in one of fourteen again, because priority governs how fast a request is served and not when it may be issued. Preloading the face files themselves would pin paths Google Fonts generates per user agent; one of those paths was found serving a 404 during this work. Self-hosting would add files to a deck Rule 4 requires to be a single one. So the rule is one rule: a stylesheet that decides the first paint stays, and a script the first paint does not need goes | The typeface stylesheet and `reveal.css` stay render-blocking and a profile will keep naming both, together a measured 13,196 bytes of the 125,167 the profile started with. Returning the typeface stylesheet to the path cost about 14 milliseconds of first paint and kept four fifths of the gain, because the scripts were the bulk of the bytes. That is the price of a first paint that shows one slide, at the size it keeps |

## Automation remediation decisions

A final operational review ran the pipeline against unmodified HEAD and found three of its ten stages
red. None of the three was a fault in the platform itself, and each was localised to one file. The
behaviour they exercise was already the behaviour the guides describe.

A guard held a generated file to a rule meant for inputs, a severity gate could not record an accepted
finding, and a smoke request omitted a header. The rows below state what changed in each case and what
the change gives up.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A path a guide prints is checked two ways: a fixture under `app/data/` has to exist, and a printed `.env` has to resolve to the one destination the credential step writes | Drop the `.env` token from the pattern; create `.env` inside the unit-test stage; reorder the guide so credential generation runs before the build | `.env` is the output of a command the guide itself prints and `.gitignore` keeps it out of the tree, so the guard failed the very first command the guide gives a reader. Dropping the token would have left eight of fifty-three printed paths checked, which stops the guard reading the guides at all. Creating the file inside the stage would have turned the pipeline green and left the guide's own order red on a developer machine. Holding the token to `card-platform/.env` keeps the check that found three tokens written from a directory no section names, because such a token resolves somewhere else | A guide could print `.env` in a block that never creates it, and the guard would not notice. The eight fixture paths are inputs and are still held to existence, and the census floor still fails if the pattern stops matching what the guides write |
| `java/spring-disabled-csrf-protection` is excluded in `.github/codeql/codeql-config.yml`, and the severity gate passes over a result the analysis reports as suppressed | Suppression comments at the twelve sites; the analysis action's newer inline configuration input; raising the gate threshold above the result's severity; enabling a forgery-token repository the API has no session to hold | The twelve results are the two stateless filter chains in each of the six services. Both create no session, authenticate with HTTP Basic and read no cookie, so a forgery token has nowhere to live and there is no ambient credential a foreign site could spend, and `config/CrossSiteRequestFilter` refuses a state-changing request that declares a foreign site or origin or carries no non-simple header. A configuration file is where a query filter is documented, and `config-file` has been an input of that action since its first version, which the newer inline form has not, so the file is the form whose acceptance is not in question. Raising the threshold would have retired the gate for every high result rather than for one. The gate also counted suppressed results, so dismissing an alert at its source could not have worked either | The exclusion is by query rather than by site, so a genuinely unprotected chain added later is not reported by this query. The refusal filter is covered by unit, slice and integration tests in all six services, the exclusion is the only one in the file, and the gate still fails on any other result at security severity 7.0 or higher |
| The container stage's authorization smoke sends `X-CardDemo-Request` | Admit the continuous-integration caller without the header; exempt the smoke path inside the filter; assert the refusal the request produced | Every state-changing route refuses a request that carries no non-simple header, and that is the control the guides tell a caller to satisfy. The stage omitted it, so the smoke read a refusal and the three consumer assertions behind it had never run once. Sending the header makes the stage exercise the same contract a reader follows. Exempting the stage would have made the pipeline prove something the platform does not do | The stage now depends on the header name, which the workflow guard and `RuleThreeDocumentationContractTest` both pin. Nothing else in the step changed, so its outbox and `processed_event` assertions are exercised for the first time rather than rewritten |


## Container refusal, stored width and alert-format remediation decisions

A runtime review drove the six services through a browser and a command line, and raised five
findings. Two concerned what a caller reads when the container refuses a request target before any
route runs. Two concerned a declared width the account update accepted and stored short. One
concerned an alert format the notification service documented and never rendered. The rows below
state what changed.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A request target the container refuses is answered with the problem document its service publishes, in `application/problem+json` carrying `type`, `title`, `status` and `detail` | Leave the framework body and the container page the review found; answer in each service's own error record; set `server.error.include-path` alone and keep the framework shape | The framework body copied the resolved request path into a `path` member, and a path of this platform carries a card token or an account identifier, so a refusal echoed a caller value into a body and into any log built from it. Tomcat's own report valve answered a target it refused with an HTML page, from routes that publish JavaScript Object Notation alone. The four members are the ones `config/SecurityConfig` already writes for `401` and `403`, so a caller that parses a security refusal parses this one too. A service error record could not carry it: its `route` member names a route template, and a target refused before mapping matched none | Two writers render this document, the error-dispatch filter and the replacing valve, and both call one `document` method so they cannot diverge. A caller of the card, notification or authorization service parses two failure shapes rather than one, which it already did for `401` and `403` |
| The container's error-report valve is removed from the host pipeline and replaced, and every `server.error.include-*` key is set to the value that reveals nothing | An MVC error controller; Spring Security's `RequestRejectedHandler`; a servlet error-page declaration; leaving the valve and accepting the HTML page | A request target Tomcat rejects is refused before a context is mapped, so no filter, no controller and no error page of the application runs, and only a valve on the host pipeline can answer it. `RequestRejectedHandler` is wired through `WebSecurityCustomizer`, which `ApiSurfaceSecurityContractTest` forbids platform-wide, and it would answer the firewall's own refusals rather than Tomcat's. An error controller would carry an MVC annotation and grow that same contract's pinned endpoint inventory by six files that serve no route | The replacement subclasses `ErrorReportValve` and overrides `report`, so it rests on one protected method of the container rather than on a framework interface. The customizer is ordered last so it runs after the framework's own Tomcat customizer, and a reordering would leave two reporters on the pipeline. `ContainerErrorDocumentTest` asserts exactly one remains and that it is not the container's own |
| The rate-ceiling refusal writes `X-Content-Type-Options` and `X-Frame-Options` itself, as the container refusal does | Leave both headers to the security chain; move the rate ceiling behind that chain so the chain writes them | Both refusals are written before the security chain runs, so the header writers of that chain never reach them, and a `429` was the one answer of each service a browser could sniff or frame. Moving the ceiling behind the chain would spend a bcrypt verification on the request the ceiling exists to refuse | Two header names are now written from three places in each service rather than one. Each `RequestRateCeilingFilterTest` asserts both on every refusal it measures, so a copy that stops writing them fails the unit phase |
| An account or customer value longer than the column that stores it is refused with `422` and a message naming the field | Truncate to the declared width, as the mapper did; widen the columns past the copybook; leave the value to the database and answer `500` | `app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy` and `app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy` fix every width, and the screen fields at `app/bms/COACTUP.bms` cannot submit a longer value, so the source has no truncating path to reproduce. Truncation stored a value the caller did not send and reported success. Widening a column would break the width parity every entity, migration and fixture holds | A caller that received `200` for an over-long value now receives `422`, which is the finding's own expected outcome. The refusal text follows the platform's existing held-width wording rather than a source message, because the source raises none |
| Every notification listener renders one alert per rendered format, so one delivery writes one `notification_log` row for each | Select one format by configuration; render the second format only on request; leave the second renderer unreachable and correct the documents instead | `app/cbl/CBSTM03A.CBL` declares one output file per format at lines 45 and 47, and one run writes both, so rendering both is the source behaviour. The service README, the data model and the event diagram all described two formats while one was unreachable. A configuration key would make the enumeration and the shipped value two sources for one list | One delivery writes twice the alert rows it wrote before, and the rendering metric counts one per format. The idempotency marker and the read model stay at one row per event, which the duplicate-delivery tests assert |

## Automated-test gate remediation decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks accepted |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `.env` is held in the guides as a generated path: the tracked template and the documented copy command are asserted, and the file itself is not | Create `.env` in the three test stages of the workflow; assert the template alone and stop counting the token; keep asserting the file and document that `mvn clean verify` needs a preparatory step | `card-platform/.gitignore` ignores `.env` and `docs/onboarding.md` states it does not exist until it is copied, so the assertion contradicted the guide it validates and failed on every clean checkout. It failed inside the module whose Failsafe phase runs the equivalence suite, so the suite the plan calls non-deferrable never ran there. Creating the file in the workflow would leave `mvn clean verify` needing a step no guide names, which Rule 3's no-questions standard rules out | The test no longer shows that a developer's `.env` exists, because at that point none does. It shows that the template is present and that the guide printing the path carries the command producing it, so a guide that stops carrying that command fails |
| The published-count comparison reads which reports this invocation wrote, from the session start the build hands the test | Skip the comparison when the reactor holds fewer than ten projects; judge each module's Failsafe reports against that module's own Surefire reports; leave directory presence as the test | A directory an earlier command left behind is not evidence about this run. Presence failed a module built on its own with `clean`, and it let a stale directory stand in for a phase that never ran. A project count settles neither: `mvn -pl equivalence-tests -am test` holds all ten projects and runs no integration test anywhere | The comparison now depends on one system property. Its absence fails the test loudly instead of passing quietly, which is the only reading that keeps the fail-open closed |
| Both test-skipping profiles activate on the value `true` rather than on their property being named at all | Leave activation on presence and document the trap; bind the unit-test plugin's own skip parameter to a new property; remove the profiles and let each stage run every suite again | `-DskipUnitTests=false` activated the skip, so an operator asking for the suite was given a green build over nothing. A flag named with no value still activates it, because Maven assigns `true`, so the workflow and every documented command behave exactly as before | A command passing some other spelling of yes now runs the suite rather than skipping it. That is the safe direction to fail in, and no command in this repository passes one |
| The card-width refusal is held against the served texts and the absence of a decision property, rather than against the whole body | Strip the timestamp before searching the body; parse the body and read one property; assert the status code alone | An error body carries a moment taken from the clock, and about one rendered instant in sixteen hundred holds the four characters of the reject code among its nanoseconds. The bare search therefore answered the clock as well as the endpoint and failed for a reason the test is not about | The case reads three expectations where it read two. A reject code appearing somewhere in an error body other than its texts would not be caught, and no error body carries one |
| Coverage measurement and a cluster-level deployment check are recorded as tasks rather than added to the build | Add a coverage agent now and publish a figure; add a stage that applies the eleven manifests to a throwaway cluster on every run; record neither and say nothing | Section 0.5.1 pins every dependency this build resolves and names the four it excludes deliberately, and section 0.2.2 excludes production hardening from this engagement. A coverage agent instruments every test JVM and a cluster is new runtime, and no requirement asks for either. The Compose path is read on every run already, because the container stage starts the six built images and follows one authenticated authorization to all three consumers, so what no run exercises is the eleven manifests and `scripts/start-demo.sh`. `docs/suggested-next-tasks.md` carries both tasks, with the procedure and the check each needs | Line and branch coverage stay unmeasured, and the manifests and the advertised bootstrap script stay held as text. Mutation sensitivity across sixteen planted defects, the container stage and the demo walked by hand are what stand in for them |
